                                 Preface

ATP 3-92 discusses the roles, functions, structure, organization, and operations of the corps. This publication
discusses the corps as a tactical formation and operational headquarters during operations. This publication
contains copyrighted material.

This publication is most applicable to corps commanders and staffs. It provides relevant information
regarding the corps organization and operations for commanders and staffs at subordinate divisions and
brigades, corps, theater armies, other Service headquarters, and combatant commands. The organizational
figures and associated staffing levels are derived from an amalgamation of corps and main command post-
operational detachment modified tables of organization and equipment, and may vary to some degree from
individual modified tables of organization and equipment for different corps.

The principal audience for ATP 3-92 is all members of the profession of arms. Commanders and staffs of
Army headquarters serving as joint task force or multinational headquarters should also refer to applicable
joint or multinational doctrine concerning the competition continuum and joint or multinational forces.
Trainers and educators throughout the Army will also use this publication.

Commanders, staffs, and subordinates ensure that their decisions and actions comply with applicable United
States (U.S.), international, and in some cases host-nation laws and regulations. Commanders at all levels
ensure that their Soldiers operate in accordance with the law of armed conflict and applicable rules of
engagement. (See FM 6-27 for more information on the law of war.) They also adhere to the Army Ethic as
described in ADP 6-22.

ATP 3-92 uses joint terms where applicable. Selected joint and Army terms and definitions appear in both
the glossary and the text. ATP 3-92 is not the proponent publication (the authority) for any terms. For other
terms defined in the text, the term is italicized, and the number of the proponent publication follows the
definition.

ATP 3-92 applies to the Active Army, Army National Guard of the U.S., and U.S. Army Reserve unless
otherwise stated.

The proponent of ATP 3-92 is the United States Army Combined Arms Center. The preparing agency is the
Combined Arms Doctrine Directorate, United States Army Combined Arms Center. Send comments and
recommendations on DA Form 2028 (Recommended Changes to Publications and Blank Forms) to
Commander, United States Army Combined Arms Center and Fort Leavenworth, ATZL-MCD (ATP 3-92),
300 McPherson Avenue, Fort Leavenworth, KS 66027-2337; by email to
usarmy.leavenworth.mccoe.mbx.cadd-org-mailbox@army.mil; or submit an electronic DA Form 2028.

                              Introduction

This revision of ATP 3-92 articulates nonprescriptive ways and methods a corps accomplishes missions,
performs functions, and executes tasks. ATP 3-92 expands on corps foundations and tenets found in FM 3-94
and incorporates changes to the Army’s operational concept found in FM 3-0.

ATP 3-92 addresses the corps’ role in Army operations. It describes how the corps performs as a tactical
formation and as a headquarters.

ATP 3-92 nests with FM 3-94 on key topics while expanding on specific topics. These topics include
convergence; the corps’ role and function as a tactical formation; the corps’ operational environment (OE);
the corps’ structure and capabilities; the operational variables of political, military, economic, social,
information, infrastructure, physical environment, and time; the mission variables of mission, enemy, terrain
and weather, troops and support available, time available, civil considerations, and informational
considerations of [METT-TC (I)]; and operational-level headquarters considerations.

Corps have been a critical part of Army operations since the Civil War. Since that time, corps demonstrated
their value in both World Wars, Korea, and Vietnam. Ably commanded and properly organized, corps
demonstrated their value as maneuver forces capable of significant independent action during large-scale
combat operations. Starting in the 1970s, as the Army sought to improve command and control (C2) by
realigning and reducing headquarters, the responsibilities and functions of the field army were divided
between the theater army and the corps. It was during this period that the corps became capable of operating
at the tactical or operational level depending upon the mission. While they are organized, manned, trained,
and equipped as a tactical formation, corps were routinely called upon to become joint headquarters for
operations. Between 1975 and 2004, Army corps were called upon to operate as the senior tactical
headquarters in large-scale combat or as joint headquarters in various limited contingency operations.

Today, corps must possess the ability to execute current warfighting doctrine in contact with a peer threat
capable of contesting their operations in all domains. An Army corps is an echelon of command and tactical
formation that employs divisions, multifunctional brigades, and functional brigades to achieve objectives on
land (JP 3-31). Corps are the largest tactical formation in the Army. They must be flexible enough to conduct
simultaneous operations throughout their assigned operational area with a faster tempo than previously
required. They are also the echelon best positioned and resourced to achieve convergence with Army and
joint capabilities. Corps design their operations on land to take advantage of the opportunities created by
other components of the joint force, preserving combat power to maximize their ability to exploit future
opportunities and create future opportunities for the joint force.

ATP 3-92 contains seven chapters and two appendices:

Chapter 1 provides an introduction of a corps and its operation as a tactical formation and operational
headquarters. First, it provides an overview of the corps. Next, it discusses the corps as a senior tactical
formation and its associated functions. The chapter concludes with a discussion of the additional roles that a
corps could fulfill for the joint force in operations other than large scale combat.

Chapter 2 describes how a corps is structured. It begins with an overview of a corps headquarters. Next, it
discusses corps assigned and provided units. The chapter concludes with a discussion of joint capabilities to
support corps operations.

Chapter 3 discusses the fundamentals of corps operations. It begins with discussion on the challenges to a
corps as the senior tactical formation during large-scale combat operations, followed by command and
leadership considerations. Next, the chapter discusses the operational framework and considerations for
movement and maneuver. This chapter concludes with a discussion on other considerations for corps
operations.

Chapter 4 expands on the application of the deep, close, and rear operations framework introduced in chapter
3. It provides an overview of general friendly and enemy challenges facing a corps during operations and

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  xi


---

Introduction

     describes how a corps synchronizes deep, close, and rear operations to overcome those challenges. The
     chapter concludes by providing considerations for synchronizing deep, close, and rear operations during
     offensive and defensive operations.

     Chapter 5 addresses considerations for a corps when conducting offensive and defensive operations. The
     information in this chapter pertaining to corps operations is meant to augment FM 3-90, not summarize it.
     The chapter begins with an overview of the offense and vignette, followed by a discussion of the operational
     framework in the offense. Finally, it discusses the defense.

     Chapter 6 covers enabling operations. It begins with an overview of enabling operations. Then, the chapter
     discusses reconnaissance, security, and troop movement, followed by relief in place. Next, the chapter
     discusses passage of lines, countermobility, mobility, and tactical deception. The chapter concludes with a
     discussion of linkup operations.

     Chapter 7 addresses what a corps is required to do to start consolidating gains while still fighting. This chapter
     begins with an overview of stability operations and consolidating gains followed by a discussion of the
     challenges inherent in consolidating gains during combat operations. It then discusses the types of activities
     and operations that a corps would be responsible for in their area of operations. The chapter concludes with
     some planning considerations when planning for these operations.

     Appendix A discusses the role of the ARFOR. While an ARFOR controls forces operationally, this appendix
     discusses the ARFOR’s administrative and support responsibilities to subordinate Army forces and to other
     Services in a joint force.

     Appendix B describes the types of Joint Task Forces, the formation authority, and planning considerations
     when transitioning a corps headquarters into a Joint Task Force Headquarters.

     Appendix C expands on the discussion from chapter 1 by describing corps considerations when performing
     other roles.

     Appendix D provides additional context for specific content in the manual and illustrates key concepts.

                                                 Chapter 1

                                   The Corps

    This chapter introduces a corps starting with an overview. Next is a discussion of a corps as a
    senior tactical formation and its associated functions. The chapter concludes with a discussion
    of the additional roles that a corps could fulfill for the joint force.

SECTION I — CORPS OVERVIEW AND STRATEGIC CONSIDERATION

             [The corps] can engage on an extended front and can carry on a battle until a decision is
             reached… In actions of long duration, the divisional units are relieved by fresh units; but
             the corps remains until a decision is reached or the strategical plan is changed.

                                                                                   A Manual for Commanders of Large Units

     1-1. The Army’s primary mission is to organize, train, and equip its forces to conduct prompt and sustained
     land combat to defeat enemy forces and seize, occupy, and defend land areas. It supports four strategic roles
     for the joint force: shape operational environments, counter aggression on land during crisis, prevail during
     ground combat, and consolidate gains. The Army provides forces for joint campaigns to fulfill these roles.
     The corps is a critical contribution capable of conducting sustained combat operations, leveraging
     multidomain capabilities in pursuit of objectives, or providing the nucleus of a joint headquarters for short
     duration or limited contingency operations.

     1-2. An Army corps is an echelon of command and tactical formation that employs divisions, multifunctional
     brigades, and functional brigades to achieve objectives on land (JP 3-31). The Army maintains and employs
     corps for two reasons. Primarily, corps exist to prevail during large-scale combat operations against peer
     threats. Secondarily, the employment of an Army corps offers civilian policy makers and senior military
     leaders options to compete effectively against threats, above or below the threshold of overt armed conflict.

     1-3. Corps are the largest tactical units in the Army. Corps are both an echelon of command and a combined
     arms formation capable of sustained, independent operations. They are the instruments by which higher
     echelons of command conduct operations at the operational level. They are the Army’s most versatile
     echelon, functioning as one of the principal integrators of landpower into joint campaigns. When a field army
     is constituted, a corps is the primary link between the operational- and tactical-levels of warfare.

     1-4. Corps operations are inherently combined arms operations distinguished by their scope and scale. Corps
     tactics use simultaneous and sequential attacks across a broad front to break the enemy’s cohesion and disrupt
     their entire defensive system. A corps focuses on shaping conditions for its divisions by using its assets and
     enablers and by leveraging joint capabilities to disrupt the enemy in depth. Corps integrate capabilities from
     multiple domains and echelons against multiple decisive points achieving convergence outcomes that their
     subordinates can exploit.

     1-5. Corps commanders and staffs focus on operations in their deep areas to shape future close operations
     while protecting their rear operations. The corps headquarters synchronizes maneuver, fire support, and
     effects with protection and sustainment to set conditions for subordinate forces, and it accomplishes tactical
     objectives to achieve operational and strategic objectives. Command at this echelon requires operational art,
     tactical expertise, and complementary employment of Army and joint capabilities. In addition to shaping the
     environment for their subordinate formations, corps commanders ensure the timing of division and brigade
     actions, commit reserves, and modify current and future operations to adjust to the situation. The corps
     commander prescribes the tactics for the corps in their concept of operation and statement of their intent.

     1-6. Operational and mission variables determine how a higher echelon tailors or task organizes a corps with
     specific capabilities to achieve its assigned objectives. Corps operations possess several key characteristics.
     Corps operations are conducted in multiple domains, requiring the synchronization of joint and multinational
     capabilities. They require forces tailored during force projection in response to crisis situations. Corps
     operations are conducted across the competition continuum from competition below armed conflict, through

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  1


---

Chapter 1

     crisis and ultimately armed conflict. The corps is the senior tactical echelon where the commander integrates
     and synchronizes joint and Army capabilities.

     1-7. The corps headquarters is flexible and adaptable, and it requires an experienced, capable staff. It needs
     the ability to expand and contract its structure and modify it to create forward operational elements as needed.
     The corps staff is a cross functional team, integrating warfighting functions to generate combat power at
     multiple decisive points to achieve objectives. (See Chapter 3 for more information on warfighting functions.)
     The Army intended corps to be highly versatile, designing them with senior, experienced, and varied staffs
     and making corps capable of performing different tactical- or operational-level roles.

     1-8. There are currently four corps in the Army. They are—
          • I Corps.
          • III Armored Corps.
          • V Corps.
          • XVIII Airborne Corps.

             Note. Each corps has particular focuses and characteristics, but each can exercise command and
             control (C2) over any division type, and each can control operations anywhere in the world.

     1-9. Each type of corps conducts operations to set conditions across multiple domains for its formations to
     exploit. Setting the right conditions for success is critical. When fighting a peer adversary, the corps must
     focus its available combat power on those operations that maximize its divisions’ ability to execute close
     combat. The corps headquarters conducts and coordinates operations to provide divisions with the freedom
     to maneuver ground forces by employing intelligence, surveillance, and reconnaissance (ISR),
     electromagnetic warfare (EW), space operations, and cyberspace operations. The corps employs air and
     missile defense (AMD), engineers, and other capabilities to preserve combat power as it maneuvers its
     divisions and other subordinate formations to execute the corps’ scheme of maneuver to achieve objectives
     and defeat enemy forces. Combat power is the total means of destruction and disruptive force that a military
     unit/formation can apply against an enemy at a given time (JP 3-0).

CORPS OPERATIONS IN MULTIPLE DOMAINS

     1-10. The Army’s operational concept is multidomain operations. Multidomain operations are the combined
     arms employment of joint and Army capabilities to create and exploit relative advantages to achieve
     objectives, defeat enemy forces, and consolidate gains on behalf of joint force commanders (FM 3-0). Army
     forces, enabled by joint capabilities, attack enemy forces and protect friendly forces through all domains.
     Army forces provide the lethal and resilient landpower necessary to defeat threat standoff approaches and
     achieve joint force objectives.

     1-11. Below the threshold of armed conflict, multidomain operations are how Army forces set advantageous
     conditions for future conflict, demonstrate readiness, and deter adversaries while assuring allies and partners.
     During conflict, multidomain operations are how Army forces close with and destroy enemy forces, defeat
     enemy formations, seize critical terrain, and control populations and resources to deliver sustainable political
     outcomes. (See FM 3-0 for an expanded discussion of multidomain operations.)

     1-12. Corps are designed to conduct sustained operations against a peer enemy as part of a larger joint force.
     An operation is a sequence of tactical actions with a common purpose or unifying theme (JP 1, Volume 1).
     Operations vary in many ways based upon the physical environment, the scale of forces involved, their
     objectives, and their duration. With appropriate support, corps can plan and conduct multiple successive
     operations against an enemy force, allowing them to create an expanding cycle of opportunities. Corps align
     their operations on land to take advantage of opportunities created by the effects generated by other
     components of the joint force. This allows corps to preserve the combat power essential to exploiting future
     opportunities over time. Corps likewise design and conduct operations that create opportunities for the joint
     force to exploit.

     1-13. Enemy forces able to contest the joint force and allies or partners in all environments means that the
     corps cannot rely on capabilities in only a few domains. A corps commander and staff leverage all available
     capabilities to overcome threat advantages during competition or conflict. By employing capabilities from

2  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                                        The Corps

     multiple directions and domains, Army corps present threats with multiple interrelated dilemmas that are
     difficult to overcome.

     1-14. Echeloned Army forces provide the landpower component necessary for the depth of maneuver
     required to defeat threat standoff approaches. As a tactical headquarters, a corps headquarters employs
     divisions simultaneously to overwhelm enemy forces or in sequence to extend the duration of operations. A
     corps provides the C2 and enabling capabilities required for the endurance to achieve necessary depth for the
     land component. A corps integrates and synchronizes joint and Army capabilities, fighting as a formation to
     create opportunities for the joint force and its subordinate formations. Corps commanders employ joint and
     Army capabilities to seize, retain, and exploit the initiative with their divisions.

OPERATIONS IN THE ARMY STRATEGIC CONTEXTS

     1-15. Joint doctrine describes the strategic environment in terms of a competition continuum describing three
     broad categories of relationships—cooperation, competition below armed conflict, and armed conflict. At
     the national strategic and theater strategic levels, cooperation, competition, and armed conflict commonly
     occur simultaneously in different parts of the world.

     1-16. The Army strategic contexts generally correspond with the joint competition continuum with two key
     differences. Army doctrine considers cooperation a part of competition because it is generally conducted with
     an ally or partner to counter an enemy or adversary. Additionally, Army doctrine adds crisis to account for
     the unique challenges facing military forces that often characterize transitions between competition and
     armed conflict. Army forces at the corps echelon and below typically conduct operations in one context at a
     time. The following discussion will briefly discuss the Army strategic contexts from the perspective of a
     corps’ contributions. (See FM 3-0 for more information on the strategic contexts.)

COMPETITION BELOW ARMED CONFLICT

     1-17. Competition exists when a specific adversary’s national interests are incompatible with U.S. interests.
     An adversary is a party acknowledged as potentially hostile to a friendly party and against which the use of
     force may be envisaged (JP 3-0). An adversary operates just below the conflict threshold to achieve its aims,
     and the tension between adversary and friendly groups creates potential for violent escalation. During
     competition, a corps trains and prepares for large-scale combat operations. Preparation includes activities
     such as building interoperability with allies and partners and training to execute the contingency plans that a
     corps is likely to support. When directed, a corps supports a theater army in executing missions and tasks in
     support of a combatant commander’s (CCDR's) campaign plan. Corps preparation for large-scale combat
     operations contributes to deterring adversaries, since exercises and training events serve as demonstrations
     of capability and will. During competition, a corps focuses on consolidating gains won on land during
     previous conflicts and ongoing security cooperation activities to maintain an operational environment (OE)
     that is advantageous to U.S. interests.

     1-18. When directed, a corps may participate in operations and activities intended to promote regional
     stability in a particular area of responsibility (AOR) and set security conditions favorable to U.S. interests.
     These activities include military engagement, security cooperation, and forward presence to promote U.S.
     interests, to develop allied and friendly military capabilities for self-defense and multinational operations,
     and to provide U.S. forces with peacetime and contingency access to a host nation. A corps enables theater
     armies to set their theaters by using existing partnerships or developing new ones to improve interoperability,
     enable forward basing or theater access, assure partners and allies, or other purposes in support of the CCDR’s
     campaign plan and the theater army’s campaign support plan. (See the vignette DEFENDER-EUROPE and
     V Corps for a description of a corps’ contribution to shaping an OE.)

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  3


---

Chapter 1

                                DEFENDER EUROPE and V Corps

          DEFENDER-EUROPE is an annual large-scale U.S. Army Europe and Africa-led,
          multinational, joint exercise designed to build strategic and operational readiness and
          interoperability between U.S., North Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO) allies, and
          other partners. DEFENDER-EUROPE 21 included more than 30,000 multinational
          forces from 27 nations conducting nearly simultaneous operations across more than
          30 training areas in a dozen countries. The 2020 activation of V Corps headquarters
          provided the needed level of command and control (C2) focused on synchronizing U.S.
          Army, allied, and partner-nation tactical formations operating in Europe. The exercise
          enhanced the capabilities of U.S. Army Europe and Africa, and U.S. European
          Command as they worked alongside allies and partners to promote regional stability
          and security.

CRISIS

     1-19. Regardless of the capabilities and forces employed by a corps, there are two broad outcomes from a
     crisis. Either the corps maintains deterrence and the situation is deescalated, or armed conflict occurs. A crisis
     is an emerging incident or situation involving a possible threat to the United States, its citizens, military
     forces, or vital interests that develops rapidly and creates a condition of such diplomatic, economic, or
     military importance that commitment of military forces and resources is contemplated to achieve national
     and/or strategic objectives (JP 3-0). During crisis, there are indications and warnings that conflict is a distinct
     possibility that requires rapid response by forces prepared for conflict. A corps may be deployed during crisis
     to provide C2 as the senior tactical formation in support of a joint commander. The corps serving as the senior
     tactical echelon of Army forces in a theater will be responsible for ensuring effective support to the other
     Services, especially in the initial stages of a conflict. Corps also provide force packages as part of joint
     flexible deterrent options or flexible response options. During a crisis, a corps may be tasked to form the core
     element of a joint task force (JTF) or joint force land component commander (JFLCC) headquarters at the
     direction of the combatant commander. If this happens, the corps requires significant joint and Army
     augmentation.

     1-20. Army operations to prevent conflict or escalation of conflict include all activities to deter an adversary’s
     undesirable actions. Corps commanders usually emphasize the protection of friendly forces, assets, and
     partners when conducting operations to prevent conflict and to indicate U.S. intentions to execute subsequent
     phases of a planned operation. In December 1989, General Manuel Noriega announced the Republic of
     Panama was in a state of war with the United States. This was a buildup of tensions involving election fraud,
     harassment of U.S. military personnel and their families, and continued corruption in Panama. General
     Noriega increased anti-American rhetoric and communications with the Soviet Union. General Noriega’s
     comments and role in drug trafficking and treaty violations set the tone for U.S. military intervention in
     Panama. (See the vignette OPERATION JUST CAUSE — Crisis Intervention on page 5 for a description of
     the intervention in Panama.)

             Note. While the invasion of Panama seems contrary to preventing conflict, conventional forces
             were necessary to prevent further aggression and corruption and to protect U.S. interests. The
             focus of the vignette is on the corps’ ability to set the theater, conduct planning, and develop
             tailored force projection to prevent further escalation on terms favorable to the adversary.

4  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                                        The Corps

                      OPERATION JUST CAUSE — Crisis Intervention

          The military intervention in Panama had four objectives: to safeguard the lives of
          Americans, to defend democracy in Panama, to combat drug trafficking, and to protect
          the integrity of the Panama Canal treaty.

          XVIII Airborne Corps planners were uncomfortable with OPERATION PLAN 90-1
          because its deliberate buildup of forces assumed provocation by General Noriega,
          which clearly threatened American lives and the security of the Canal Zone. The
          planners revised OPERATION PLAN 90-1 to develop OPERATION PLAN 90-2 (after
          an attempted coup) to increase the speed of deployment. They assigned the 82nd
          Airborne Division as the lead assault force and the 7th Infantry Division as the
          secondary assault force. As tensions continued in Panama, U.S. Southern Command
          commander and XVIII Airborne Corps planners realized any military action would have
          to be swift and decisive.

          Units in the United States and Panama increased training tenfold; OPERATION SAND
          FLEA training exercises demonstrated the U.S. resolve to ensure the treaty rights
          affecting the Canal Zone and U.S. installations were preserved. Numerous command
          post (CP) exercises with the corps headquarters (and division headquarters and
          special operations forces [SOF]) elements) allowed the commanders and their
          deputies to play specific roles in a training exercise replicating a joint task force (JTF)
          deployed overseas. The CP exercise was a replication of the actual military operation
          in Panama. The training exercises in the Canal Zone desensitized the Panamanian
          Defense Forces to U.S. movements, and they allowed U.S. forces to see on the ground
          their future objectives in OPERATION JUST CAUSE.

          XVIII Airborne Corps, established as JTF South, exercised both tactical and
          operational level command. The JTF divided its subordinate elements into four
          conventional ground forces against 27 different objectives. The thorough planning,
          training, and deliberate use of military power enabled the planners to set conditions
          favorable to a swift and decisive strike through surprise and rapid tempo. At the end of
          OPERATION JUST CAUSE, the corps had secured all 27 objectives, protected U.S.
          interests, and removed General Noriega from power.

ARMED CONFLICT

     1-21. Armed conflict occurs when a state or nonstate actor uses lethal force to achieve its political goals.
     Armed conflict can occur as a limited contingency operation or progress into large-scale combat. Large-scale
     combat operations are extensive joint combat operations in terms of scope and size of forces committed,
     conducted as a campaign aimed at achieving operational and strategic objectives (ADP 3-0). In a large-scale
     conflict, corps are generally the senior tactical formations on the battlefield under the control of a land
     component commander (LCC). Corps are required to conduct offensive and defensive operations to close
     with and destroy enemy forces, exploit success, and break an enemy force’s will to continue to conduct
     operations.

     1-22. During large-scale combat operations, Army forces focus on the defeat and destruction of enemy forces
     as part of the joint team. Corps close with and destroy enemy forces in any terrain, exploit success, and break
     an enemy force’s ability to resist. Corps attack, defend, perform stability tasks, and consolidate gains to
     accomplish national objectives. During large-scale combat operations, corps operate as a formation,
     controlling the operations of two to five divisions and multiple functional and multifunctional brigades,
     enabled by supporting theater-level organizations. A corps can transition to serve as an operational-level
     headquarters (for example, the land component or JTF). However, using a corps as the basis of an LCC or
     joint headquarters will render them incapable of function simultaneously as an Army tactical headquarters in

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  5


---

Chapter 1

     combat operations. The ability to prevail in ground combat is a decisive factor in breaking an enemy force’s
     capability and will to continue a conflict.

     1-23. Army operations to consolidate gains are activities to make enduring any temporary operational
     success and to set the conditions for a sustainable security environment, allowing for a transition of control
     to other legitimate authorities (ADP 3-0). Corps conduct a combination of offensive, defensive, and stability
     operations to consolidate gains. Consolidation of gains is an integral and continuous part of armed conflict,
     and it is necessary for achieving success across the range of military operations. At the conclusion of hostile
     activities in OPERATION JUST CAUSE, JTF South forces faced an even larger problem with a limited
     government, infrastructure, and civil support. Enabling legitimate leaders and opening commerce were
     critical to sustain Panama’s economy and protecting U.S. interests. XVIII Airborne Corps worked with the
     Panamanian Security Forces (many of whom were former Panamanian Defense Forces just a few days prior)
     to conduct policing operations and establish security. (See the vignette OPERATION PROMOTE LIBERTY
     — Transition to Stability Operations for a description of stability operations in Panama.)

              OPERATION PROMOTE LIBERTY — Transition to Stability
                                                Operations

          Upon the completion of major combat operations in Panama, Lieutenant General
          Stiner and his team (JTF South and the XVIII Airborne Corps) had a unique challenge.
          Most of the Panamanian people supported removing General Noriega from power, but
          they needed to rebuild the entire government with a focus on training, stability, and
          safety.

          The first order of business was reopening the Panama Canal, the Bridge of Americas,
          and the associated roads to allow personnel and supplies to move. Before this could
          happen, XVIII Airborne Corps conducted a rigorous campaign to remove any
          remaining Panamanian Defense Forces that affected stability.

          XVIII Airborne Corps planners discussed consolidation plans, but more as an
          afterthought to ousting General Noriega and restoring Panama Canal operations. The
          JTF carefully selected targets to limit destruction of critical infrastructure to protect the
          roads, economy, and local population. Upon cessation of combat activities, infantry
          Soldiers assumed policing missions within the cities because there simply were not
          enough other security forces available to restore order.

          The next order of business was to establish order and security; the JTF reconstituted
          some Panamanian Security Forces to serve as police liaison officers (LNOs) and
          collaborated with military police from the 16th Military Police Brigade for patrols.

          Planning for all phases of the operation proved critical. OPERATION JUST CAUSE
          created a displacement issue; the 96th Civil Affairs Battalion arrived with approximately
          100 personnel to establish a camp and coordinate relief resupplies for displaced
          civilians. XVIII Airborne Corps and JTF South rapidly evolved to conduct consolidation
          activities that facilitated the transition of power and responsibility to legitimate local
          authorities while protecting U.S. interests.

THE CORPS’ ROLES

     1-24. The effective integration of landpower across the competition continuum requires an expeditionary,
     scalable, and operationally flexible headquarters able to exercise command in a variety of roles. The corps is
     foundational to the joint and multinational forces’ ability to conduct sustained operations on land because it
     provides the JTF commander capabilities and capacity that exist nowhere else. The roles of the corps include
     operating as the—

6  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                                        The Corps

          • Senior Army tactical formation in large-scale combat, commanding two to five Army divisions
                together with supporting brigades and commands. (See Chapter 1 Section II along with Chapters 3
                through 7 for an in-depth discussion of this role.)

          • ARFOR (with augmentation) within a joint force for campaigns and major operations when a field
                army is not present. (See paragraphs 1-52 and 1-53 and appendix B for a detailed discussion of this
                role.)

          • JTF headquarters (with significant augmentation) for crisis response, limited contingency
                operations, or for campaigns and major operations when a field army is not necessary. (See
                paragraphs 1-54 through 1-57 and appendix A for a detailed discussion of this role.)

          • Joint force land component command (with significant augmentation), commanding Army, United
                States Marine Corps (USMC), and multinational divisions together with supporting brigades and
                commands when a field army is not present. (See paragraphs 1-58 through 1-63 and appendix A for
                a detailed discussion of this role.)

SECTION II — SENIOR TACTICAL FORMATION

     1-25. Large-scale combat operations will likely require a corps headquarters to function as a tactical
     headquarters within a land component command or subordinate to a field army equivalent. An example of
     this would be the relationship between a corps and a Republic of Korea Army if war recurred on the Korean
     peninsula. In this case, the corps would operate in a theater of war or theater of operations as a tactical
     headquarters under a joint command or land component. Recent historical examples of Army corps operating
     as a senior tactical headquarters include OPERATION DESERT STORM and OPERATION IRAQI
     FREEDOM I.

     1-26. When operating as a tactical echelon, the corps formation is task-organized for an operation. The corps
     commander and staff consider the operational variables and mission variables when planning and conducting
     operations. The number and type of additional divisions or brigades assigned or attached to a corps is
     dependent on the relative combat power of the enemy formations that the corps engages.

     1-27. A corps executes the following three tasks to accomplish tactical objectives and meet mission
     requirements and the commander’s intent:

          • Engage enemy forces to set conditions in the corps’ deep and close areas with available Army and
                joint capabilities.

          • Exercise C2 over Army divisions and other formations to gain positions of advantage, close with
                and defeat enemy forces, and compel their surrender or withdrawal in the corps’ close area.

          • Initiate, execute, implement, ensure, and support consolidation activities to set conditions for
                transition to a sustainable security end state.

     1-28. A corps receives its mission and tactical direction in operation plans or other formats, both verbal and
     written. An operation plan provides details and tactical directions necessary for a corps to execute the first
     phase and prepare to execute the subsequent phases of a campaign plan. A corps commander translates the
     strategic objective of the tactical action into tactical objectives and phases for accomplishment.

     1-29. A corps can conduct continuous and simultaneous tasks (including offensive, defensive, stability
     operations, or defense support of civil authorities) through a series of synchronized division and separate
     brigade operations. These operations create and exploit relative advantages across multiple domains to defeat
     an enemy force and achieve the operation’s overall purpose. Chapters 3 through 7 discuss various operations
     a corps conducts, and they include an in-depth discussion of the corps’ role in large scale combat operations.

SECTION III — FUNCTIONS

     1-30. In each of the roles described in Section II, a corps enables subordinate formations to achieve assigned
     objectives. As a JTF, a corps develops the scheme of fires to support the scheme of maneuver and employs
     joint fires to create conditions to allow freedom of maneuver. (See Chapter 3 for more information on setting
     conditions.) As the land component, a corps employs joint fire support systems and reconnaissance and
     surveillance assets in accordance with the JTF’s mission objectives. As the senior tactical formation, a corps

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  7


---

Chapter 1

     plans, prioritizes, and employs capabilities from multiple domains in its efforts to create opportunities for
     subordinate formations to exploit.

     1-31. During large-scale combat operations, a corps will likely fill its role as the senior tactical formation in
     a land component command or be subordinate to a field army equivalent established under a multinational
     force (including an alliance or coalition). Within this role, the corps synchronizes the employment of joint
     capabilities in conjunction with ground combat operations to achieve assigned objectives. Corps operate in
     depth over long distances requiring substantial support to support and extend land operations.

     1-32. Corps accomplish their assigned missions and achieve their objectives by performing some
     fundamental functions. The six primary functions of a corps are to—

          • Set conditions in the corps’ assigned area.
          • Employ joint capabilities.
          • Allocate resources and set priorities.
          • Task-organize and employ divisions and brigades.
          • Integrate and synchronize operations of divisions and brigades.
          • Mass effects at decisive points.

     1-33. Corps will usually fight as part of a larger multinational coalition or alliance with multinational forces
     task organized as part of the corps. Corps must understand the national caveats for their employment along
     with their capabilities to effectively employ them in combat operations.

SET PRIORITIES

     1-34. Corps employ Army and joint capabilities to achieve objectives and set conditions for subordinate
     echelons’ success according to the corps commander’s priorities. Corps planners arrange the employment of
     these capabilities based on the commander’s visualization of the operation. From that visualization, the
     commander, with the staff’s assistance, sets priorities regarding main and supporting efforts, objectives, fires,
     information collection, movement, protection, and sustainment. These priorities account for all joint and
     multidomain capabilities available to the corps.

     1-35. Subordinate unit commanders and their staffs, in conjunction with the corps planners, recommend
     priorities to the corps commander. The corps fire support element (FSE) develops a fire support concept and
     prioritization that includes supporting joint fires capabilities. Corps staff disseminates a close air support
     (CAS) distribution decision to provide guidance for the use of air to ground fires in its area of operations
     (AO). The corps protection cell identifies and establishes protection priorities and synchronizes and integrates
     protection tasks, systems, and methods that prevent or mitigate detection, threat effects, and hazards. This
     preserves combat power and enables freedom of action. The corps engineer cell assesses engineering
     capabilities against the terrain, mission, and requirements for prioritization and employment. The assistant
     chief of staff, logistics (G-4) recommends sustainment priorities that align with the corps’ main effort. These
     assessments enable the corps commander to employ combat power and determine any gaps in capabilities
     (including joint capabilities).

     1-36. Setting priorities provides clear visualization and direction to staff members, allowing them to continue
     planning, preparing orders, or making timely decisions within their warfighting functions. Staff members
     shift efforts to facilitate phase changes, transitions, or new missions. (See Chapter 3 for more information on
     visualization.)

TASK-ORGANIZE AND ALLOCATE RESOURCES

     1-37. The exact task organization of subordinate divisions and multifunctional brigades depends on corps
     mission requirements and what the main or supporting efforts are during an operation. Corps do not typically
     distribute all available combat power to the divisions. Commanders retain control of some assets, such as
     aviation, fires, engineers, and intelligence units, to allow corps to conduct deep operations or maintain
     flexibility for future operations. Corps commanders adjust throughout the course of operations as necessary,
     allocating Army and joint capabilities to accomplish objectives that achieve their higher echelon
     headquarters’ purpose. They weight the main effort and exercise economy of force where appropriate.

8  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                                        The Corps

     1-38. Commanders task organize corps resources to subordinate divisions and brigades based on mission
     requirements. Corps resources may include fire support assets, cyberspace capabilities, and attack aviation;
     EW capabilities; protection capabilities such as AMD, military police, explosive ordnance disposal (EOD),
     and chemical, biological, radiological, or nuclear (CBRN) elements; Army space support teams; sustainment
     capabilities; and engineering capabilities.

     1-39. The corps staff must identify unique, scarce capabilities and determine how best to task-organize to
     maximize effectiveness. Resource allocation balances requirements for deep, close, and rear operations to
     achieve objectives and accomplish missions. As the corps commander and staff develop and refine their plan
     and task organization, they may significantly reduce the combat power of one formation to weight the main
     effort. The corps commander reviews the task organization as the situation evolves and forces flow into
     theater to make sure that currently available resources align with operational priorities. This applies to
     functional or service components and the capabilities of joint, interorganizational, and multinational partners.

     1-40. During contingency operations, a corps submits support requests to the theater army when mission
     requirements exceed its capabilities. As the corps headquarters directs its divisions, the theater army
     apportions additional capabilities to ensure the corps can accomplish its tasks. During large-scale combat
     operations, a corps submits requests to the JFLCC or JTF when mission requirements exceed its capabilities.

             Note. The theater army is the senior Army command in an area of responsibility. The theater army
             provides the corps’ force and sustainment requirements, regardless of the corps’ role. Depending
             upon the situation and command-support relationship, the corps may address those support
             requests through the CCDR or directly to the theater army.

ENABLE SUBORDINATE DIVISION AND BRIGADE FREEDOM OF
ACTION

     1-41. As corps task-organize their subordinate divisions and brigades and allocate them resources, they also
     consider how to enable freedom of action for those forces. A corps enables freedom of action by employing
     the mission command approach to C2, employing corps, joint, and subordinate formation capabilities,
     orchestrating division and supporting brigade operations across the operational framework, and through the
     effective use and distribution of additional available resources.

     1-42. While corps commanders and their staffs develop plans to maximize a subordinate’s ability to use
     initiative and take advantage of opportunities, they also identify phases of operations where more detailed
     control is necessary. River crossings, passages of lines, the commitment of the corps reserves, and
     employment of aviation battalions and brigades are examples of operations that require tight control to ensure
     their success. (See Chapter 7 for more discussion of these operations.)

EMPLOYING ARMY AND JOINT CAPABILITIES

     1-43. A corps integrates and synchronizes all available capabilities during operations because it is the senior
     Army or joint headquarters, and it is best suited to effectively employ the full range of possible capabilities
     to greatest effect. As a corps combines Army and joint capabilities for employment using two or more defeat
     mechanisms, the complementary and reinforcing nature of each friendly capability presents multiple
     dilemmas for enemy forces. This produces an overall effect that is greater than the sum of each individual
     effort.

     1-44. Corps employ capabilities from multiple domains in ways that defeat or disrupt enemy forces long
     enough for friendly forces to effectively exploit created opportunities. Corps balance the principles of mass,
     objective, and economy of force, massing combat power on some parts of an enemy force while employing
     different techniques against other decisive points to create cumulative effects that an enemy force cannot
     overcome.

     1-45. During large-scale combat operations, a corps must be able to simultaneously and continuously execute
     operations in depth. The degree to which a corps integrates Army and joint capabilities in an operation
     depends on how well the commander and staff can—

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  9


---

Chapter 1

          • Develop an understanding of the enemy system, its capabilities, requirements, decision processes,
                and vulnerabilities through effective surveillance that provides mixed, redundant, and overlapping
                coverage.

          • Employ Army and joint capabilities at the echelons, times, locations, or in the domains where they
                are most effective.

          • Consider all domains and redundant methods of attack to increase the probability of success.
          • Assume risk and rapidly exploit the opportunities provided by layering Army and joint capabilities

                at decisive points.

     1-46. Corps enable their divisions by integrating Army and joint capabilities to defeat enemy integrated air
     defense systems and portions of the enemy’s integrated fires command according to the joint force
     commander’s (JFC’s) plan and priorities. Corps commanders apply capabilities to achieve combinations of
     sequential, simultaneous, and enduring effects to maintain tempo and consolidate gains. They do this by
     conducting deep operations against appropriate corps targets (for example enemy armored formations) to
     shape the conditions for their subordinate brigades and divisions. (See Chapter 3 for more on the corps’ deep,
     close, and rear operations.)

ORCHESTRATING OPERATIONS

     1-47. An operational framework is a cognitive tool used to assist commanders and staffs in clearly visualizing
     and describing the application of combat power in time, space, purpose, and resources in the concept of
     operations (ADP 1-01). Corps organize and synchronize their deep, close, and rear operations. A corps then
     assigns areas (AOs, zones, or sectors) to its subordinate formations. Finally, commanders designate a main
     effort, supporting effort, and reserve for the operation. All three models are commonly used to build an
     operational framework. However, commanders and staffs may use any model they find useful as long as they
     remain synchronized with their higher headquarters’ operational framework. (See FM 3-0 for more
     information on the operational framework.)

     1-48. Corps commanders conduct the operations process to understand, visualize, and describe an OE and
     end state. The corps staff integrates and synchronizes the operations of its assigned divisions and brigades
     through targeting priorities, effects, and timing. With the help of the corps staff, commanders then direct,
     lead, and assess military operations that accomplish the mission. Corps commanders express their vision of
     how the corps orchestrates operations through their concept of the operations and associated commanders’
     intent. The concept of operations is the simplest guide to assigning missions to subordinate units binding
     everything together and establishing the relationship between deep, close, and rear operations.

     1-49. All echelons above brigade conduct deep operations designed to set favorable conditions for their
     subordinate formations in their close area. Corps destroy enemy systems, such as components of the enemy’s
     integrated fires command or integrated air defense systems, their reserves and lines of communication, to
     facilitate current and future operations and to ensure protection. Additionally, corps use a combination of
     joint fires and other capabilities such as counterfire, AMD, movement capabilities, and route allocation to
     reinforce efforts and enable freedom of action for their divisions. Corps position subordinate units to exploit
     the results of convergence and the dilemmas these results cause the enemy. Success during operations usually
     depends on how quickly and effectively friendly forces can create and exploit relative advantages over enemy
     forces. (See Chapter 4 for an in-depth discussion of the relationship between corps deep, close, and rear
     operations.)

MANEUVER DIVISIONS AND OTHER COMBAT FORMATIONS

     1-50. As combined arms formations, each of the echelons above brigade direct the broad scheme of maneuver
     of their subordinates. Corps commanders maneuver to seize objectives, using divisions and separate brigades
     to gain positions of advantage, to close with and defeat enemy forces, and to control terrain. Through its
     actions, a corps compels enemy surrender or withdrawal, synchronizing the maneuver of divisions and
     brigades to reduce friction and coordinating the simultaneous maneuver of multiple formations in time and
     space.

     1-51. Corps maneuver divisions and separate brigades to avoid enemy strengths and create opportunities to
     bring greater Army and joint firepower on enemy forces. The objective of maneuver at the corps level can
     be to—

10  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                                        The Corps

          • Exploit weaknesses or vulnerabilities in enemy dispositions.
          • Increase the amount of combat power applied against an enemy force at specific decisive points.
          • Exploit the effects of convergence with a division or divisions.
          • Position divisions for close operations.

     1-52. Effective maneuver allows the corps commander to dictate the time, manner, and tempo of operations
     to the enemy. While the corps’ subordinate combat formations focus on planning and executing tactical
     movement and maneuver, corps must focus on operational movement and maneuver in addition. Corps must
     position divisions and separate brigades to achieve a decisive outcome in the context of a campaign or major
     operations. Once forces are deployed into an operational area, corps maneuver their forces through the depths
     of their assigned area for offensive and defensive purposes. (See Chapter 3 for more on movement and
     maneuver.)

SECTION IV — ADDITIONAL ROLES OF THE CORPS

     1-53. Corps are often called upon to fill roles other than, or in addition to, senior tactical command. The
     nature of an operation and level of augmentation required dictate whether a corps can simultaneously perform
     more than one role. Generally, a corps serving in the JTF headquarters role should not perform as a senior
     tactical formation, ARFOR, or joint force land component. As a JTF headquarters, the corps commands joint
     forces, transforms to a joint staff, and follows joint doctrine (see Appendix A). These changes require
     significant augmentation, and performing additional roles presents extreme challenges for the command.
     Nevertheless, with significant augmentation, a corps can perform the joint force land component and ARFOR
     roles during competition and crisis. With less augmentation, a corps can be the senior tactical formation and
     ARFOR. However, during large-scale combat operations, corps require significant additional augmentation
     to perform more than one role.

     1-54. A corps requires a specific planning and staffing process to transition to different roles. Transitioning
     a corps headquarters to a joint headquarters requires a deliberate plan by the corps’ higher headquarters.
     Filling a joint role requires augmentation and delegation of authorities to account for and mitigate corps
     capability gaps and limitations. Simultaneously conducting joint and Service roles during operations can
     quickly overwhelm a corps commander and staff. Appendix C provides a vignette during OPERATION
     IRAQI FREEDOM to show the complexity and challenges when a corps assumes more than one of these
     roles simultaneously.

ARFOR

     1-55. An ARFOR is the Army component and senior Army headquarters of all Army forces assigned or
     attached to a combatant command, subordinate joint force command, joint functional command, or
     multinational command (FM 3-94). As the ARFOR for the JTF or land component command, a corps serves
     as an operational-level headquarters, conducting land operations as the Service component headquarters. The
     theater army provides the corps with theater-level assets as required. Appendix B expands the discussion of
     a corps headquarters as an ARFOR and the United States Code (USC) Title 10 and Army support to other
     Services (ASOS) responsibilities. (See FM 3-94 for additional information on an ARFOR.)

     1-56. A corps does not require significant augmentation to operate as an ARFOR, but the commander must
     organize the formation to execute specific functions effectively. For example, when operating as an ARFOR,
     a corps headquarters assumes significant responsibilities during the onward movement phase of reception,
     staging, onward movement, and integration (RSOI). Additionally, corps commanders may task a subordinate
     element to execute the administrative control (ADCON) functions for its subordinate divisions, brigade
     combat teams (BCTs), functional brigades, and multifunctional brigades. (See Appendix B for more on the
     corps as an ARFOR.)

JOINT TASK FORCE HEADQUARTERS

     1-57. The corps headquarters is the optimal Army echelon to serve as a core element for forming a JTF
     headquarters. However, this requires significant training and joint augmentation, accomplished by adding
     joint, unified action partner, and additional Army capabilities. The resulting JTF headquarters controls
     actions during competition, crisis, and armed conflict.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  11


---

Chapter 1

     1-58. The staffing of a JTF formed around a corps headquarters normally follows the JTF headquarters design
     in JP 3-33. The corps-based command organization progresses to a joint structure to provide the required
     augmentation. The corps (as a JTF) receives many additional resources from the headquarters that establishes
     the JTF, such as the combatant command, to augment the corps. (See JP 3-18, JP 3-31, JP 3-33, JP 3-57, and
     JP 5-0 for more information on staffing a JTF.) The corps tailors its organization for specific situations that
     require augmentation, such as—

          • Joint and special staff sections and activities.
          • Specific functional area augmentation, such as psychological operations, civil affairs, or movement

                control.
          • Headquarters life support functions.
          • Communications support (including personnel and equipment).
          • Security support for the JTF headquarters.

     1-59. A JTF organizes into packages based on the nature of operations, the tempo of developments, and the
     existing capabilities of the corps headquarters designated as a JTF. These three packages are—

          • Quick response cells to augment the JTF headquarters with joint and operational area expertise
                during the early stages of a crisis.

          • Functional area augmentation to provide the JTF with expertise not organic to its staff.
          • Joint staff augmentation to provide the coordinating and special staff sections expertise in joint

                procedures and Service-unique capabilities.

     1-60. When serving as a JTF commander, the corps commander integrates and synchronizes joint force
     operations. The JTF commander exercises specified authority and direction over JTF elements in the
     accomplishment of JTF missions. When performing a joint role, the corps uses joint doctrine. (See Appendix
     D for more on the corps as an JTF.)

JOINT FORCE LAND COMPONENT COMMAND

     1-61. The JFC designates the corps commander as the JFLCC in a U.S. operation or as the multinational
     force land component commander (MNFLCC) in an allied or multinational operation when the Army
     provides most of the land forces. In these circumstances, the corps headquarters functions as a joint
     operational-level headquarters. When serving as a JFLCC, the corps commander has significant
     responsibility for operational C2 and is the single focal point to integrate and synchronize land force
     operations for a JTF. Upon approval of the land force plan, the JFLCC exercises specified authority and
     direction over land forces in the accomplishment of land force missions. The JFLCC’s responsibility is to
     control land operations to accomplish the JTF’s campaign and major operations objectives.

             Note. Large-scale combat operations normally involve multinational land forces. JP 3-16
             identifies a land component command which includes multinational forces as a MNFLCC.

     1-62. When a corps commander is designated as the MNFLCC, the corps commands multiple division-size
     or smaller formations and exercises control over USMC and multinational forces made available for tasking
     by the JTF. As the senior Army echelon within the JTF, the corps headquarters is the ARFOR headquarters
     with the additional responsibility of ADCON over all attached Army forces. As required by the JTF, the
     corps headquarters receives a special operations liaison element, and it supports special operations forces
     (SOF) throughout the land operational area. Control of multinational units is stated in the agreement between
     the United States and partner nations. Task organizations for multinational units vary, and they include all
     types of control. (See JP 3-16 for a discussion of the organization and FM 3-94 for additional information on
     the roles and primary tasks performed by a multinational force land component command headquarters.)

             Note. A JTF may place a small USMC element under the operational control (OPCON) of the
             corps. However, a JTF normally specifies a tactical control (TACON) relationship when the
             USMC element is a Marine air-ground task force (MAGTF).

12  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                                  The Corps

1-63. The land component of a JTF is seldom composed of purely U.S. forces. Thus, the corps headquarters,
as a multinational force command, functions as the MNFLCC. In most cases, the MNFLCC exercises
TACON over multinational brigades and divisions.

1-64. A corps headquarters has many functions suitable for transition to a land component command
headquarters for a JTF without significant augmentation, but it has additional responsibilities that may require
specific additional resources. The corps headquarters coordinates with other JTF-level commands,
coordinates with the Service component headquarters of the JTF establishing authority for administrative and
logistics support, plans and conducts operations within the JTF commander’s guidance and plans, and may
be required to provide liaison personnel to the JTF. The corps commander and chief of staff allocate key
positions so that all Services are appropriately represented. When a JTF forms a joint force land component
command using an Army corps headquarters, the deputy commander or chief of staff may come from an ally,
partner, or the USMC. The commander may repeat this practice of distributing C2 duties throughout the
headquarters staff, ensuring multinational staff members understand all unit capabilities and that all units and
nations are included in planning, preparing, executing, and assessing operations. However, this should not
be done to the extent that it puts unity of effort or shared understanding at risk.

1-65. A corps often partners with multinational forces and coordinates with interagency organizations that
bring a wide range of resources and capabilities which enhance the lethality of the corps. Command of
multinational forces is complicated and politically sensitive. Multinational force headquarters must create a
mission partner environment framework under which all multinational forces operate with U.S. peers, under
the condition of interoperability. Interoperability is the ability to act together coherently, effectively, and
efficiently to achieve tactical, operational, and strategic objectives (JP 3-0).

1-66. Commanders and staffs operating as a multinational headquarters must work to resolve interoperability
challenges because they often add a layer of complexity to planning and execution. Multinational exercises
and training with technical solutions at home station improve interoperability across the competition
continuum. (See JP 3-16 and FM 3-16 for more information on multinational operations.)

       Note. To improve interoperability during multinational and joint operations, the liaisons from the
       theater liaison detachment (TLD) command serve as the commander’s representative (at the corps
       headquarters) promoting interoperability and building a healthy mission partner environment.
       Chapter 2 provides information regarding the role and capability of the TLD.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  13
