                                                 Chapter 2

                            The Corps Structure

    This chapter describes how a corps is structured. It begins with an overview of a corps
    headquarters. Next is a discussion of corps units. This chapter concludes with a discussion of
    joint capabilities to support corps operations.

SECTION I — CORPS HEADQUARTERS

     2-1. A corps headquarters is organized and equipped for sustained combat operations. A corps is designed to
     operate in its primary role as a senior tactical formation, and the Army provides a corps headquarters with
     the CPs and staffs necessary to accomplish its missions. A corps requires augmentation to perform as an
     ARFOR, joint force land component command, or JTF headquarters.

CORPS HEADQUARTERS COMPOSITION

     2-2. A corps headquarters provides a corps commander with several capabilities. A corps headquarters can—
          • Plan, prepare, execute, and assess military operations.
          • Direct, control, and coordinate the operations of subordinate combat and combat support units.
          • Coordinate sustainment support with supporting sustainment organizations.
          • Plan, coordinate, integrate and synchronize maneuver, fires, intelligence, sustainment, and
                protection operations for the corps.
          • Install, operate, and maintain the corps’ network enterprise in support of 24-hour operations for
                corps-level functions.

     2-3. A corps headquarters is organized with a main CP, a tactical CP, and a rear CP. A corps can form a
     mobile command group and an early entry CP by task-organizing capabilities from the main or tactical CPs.
     The functions of the CPs are—

          • Building and maintaining the situational understanding necessary for effective decision making.
          • Controlling operations through coordination, synchronization, and integration of the actions of all

                the corps’ subordinate elements.
          • Assessing operations.
          • Coordinating with external organizations (including higher echelon headquarters and adjacent

                units).
          • Conducting knowledge and information management.

     2-4. The composition of the corps headquarters allows the corps commander and chief of staff flexibility to
     structure the organization according to the demands of the situation and plans and operations across the staff
     sections, cells, CPs, and echelons. Flexibility is enhanced by a network and suite of C2 information systems
     to enhance collaboration and synchronization. A corps commander and enabled key subordinates may control
     operations from any of the CPs. A corps headquarters receives its battalion-level life support and field-level
     maintenance from the headquarters and headquarters battalion. (See figure 2-1 on page 16 for a depiction of
     a corps headquarters organization.)

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  15


---

Chapter 2

                                    Figure 2-1. Corps Headquarters Organization

COMMAND GROUP

     2-5. The corps structure includes up to four general officers: the commanding general, deputy commanding
     general, chief of staff, and assistant chief of staff, operations (ACOS G-3). The corps commander uses the
     deputy commanding general and chief of staff to synchronize operations between the CPs in the corps’ close
     and support areas. The commander retains a mobile capability to exercise C2 on the move. The deputy
     commanding general serves as the commander’s second in command and often has oversight of rear area
     operations. The deputy commanding general does not have a staff but interacts with the chief of staff and the
     corps staff to maintain situational understanding to assume command when necessary. The chief of staff has
     supervisory authority over the staff and manages the staff actions to achieve the commander’s guidance and
     directives. The G-3 manages the corps movement and maneuver functional cell while synchronizing
     operations in the corps’ deep and close areas. (See FM 6-0 for more information on the duties of the staff.)

             Note. Corps headquarters typically have an additional general officer from one of the U.S.’s key
             allies. The presence of general officers from allied nations greatly enhances interoperability with
             North Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO) forces, bilateral affairs, host-nation coordination, and
             C2 during multinational operations. Depending on the circumstances during an operation, the
             commander may use this senior officer as either the deputy commanding general for operations or
             deputy commanding general for support.

     2-6. The corps commander’s location depends on the situation. A routine mission on the less violent end of
     competition—such as a joint or multinational training exercise—may place less of a demand on the corps
     commander for rapid decisions and necessary situational understanding. Conversely, large-scale combat

16         ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                          The Corps Structure

     operations may demand frequent guidance and dynamic decision-making. The level of trust the commanding
     general has in the staff sections and their level of training influences the commander’s location. Well-trusted
     senior leaders on well-trained staffs allow corps commanders greater latitude for deciding where to place
     themselves during operations.

HEADQUARTERS AND HEADQUARTERS BATTALION

     2-7. The headquarters and headquarters’ battalion provides the personnel and equipment used to support the
     corps CPs and mobile command group (MCG). A headquarters battalion operates as a tactical and
     administrative headquarters for two organic companies: a headquarters and support company and a signal,
     intelligence, and sustainment company. It also has a main command post-operational detachment (known as
     MCP-OD). Generally, Reserve Component personnel man the MCP-OD, but it may consist of Title 10 active-
     duty personnel in unique cases. Each company consists of staff sections organized into three deployable CPs:
     the main CP, tactical CP, and rear CP. The main CP and the tactical CP are supported by elements of the
     headquarters and headquarters’ battalion providing the administrative and logistics support to the CPs.

Battalion Command Group

     2-8. The battalion command group provides supervision and exercises administrative command functions
     over all personnel assigned to the corps headquarters. The command group consists of a commander, an
     executive officer, and a command sergeant major. The battalion command group functions are—

          • To provide supervision and C2 of the corps’ headquarters personnel.
          • To plan, direct, control, and supervise headquarters battalion operations.
          • To plan, direct, supervise, and assign the soldiers and resources of the command.
          • To allocate forces, assign missions, and subordinate areas of operation.

Headquarters Battalion Staff

     2-9. The headquarters battalion staff consists of six staff sections:
          • Battalion personnel staff officer.
          • Battalion intelligence staff officer.
          • Battalion operations staff officer.
          • Battalion logistics staff officer.
          • Maintenance.
          • Unit ministry, and battalion medical treatment.

     They provide administrative support, human resources, logistics support, religious support, and life support
     to the corps’ headquarters elements in garrison and during operations. When deployed, the battalion staff
     sections and companies are responsible for unit-level communications support, property accountability,
     transportation (including the mobility of the corps CPs), medical, food service, and maintenance support for
     the three CPs. The subordinate elements of each company report to their company chain of command. The
     company commander, in turn, reports to the headquarters and headquarters battalion commander.

Battalion Personnel Staff

     2-10. The battalion personnel staff section provides support to the battalion commander. This section—
          • Provides administrative and personnel (human resources) support to the headquarters battalion
                personnel.
          • Performs strength accounting, reporting, and the assignment of personnel.
          • Performs personnel actions for organic and assigned units.
          • Provides battalion-level legal assistance to the headquarters battalion commander with the
                disposition of offenses under the Uniform Code of Military Justice and adverse administrative
                separation actions.
          • Maintains liaison with human resource elements of the main CP.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  17


---

Chapter 2

Battalion Intelligence Staff

     2-11. The battalion intelligence staff officer and battalion operations staff officer sections provide support to
     the battalion commander. These sections—

          • Form the battalion’s tactical operations center to monitor the operational overview and situation.
          • Conduct liaison with the main CP’s command and tactical CP’s maneuver and intelligence elements

                to monitor the common operational picture and anticipate and adjust future planning requirements.
          • Establish priorities, allocate resources, and coordinate, integrate, and synchronize the efforts of all

                organic and supporting assets to support current operations.
          • Direct the management and dissemination of intelligence within the battalion staff and support

                activities.
          • Synchronize the functions of the battalion to support the CPs when deployed.
          • Prepare and issue warning and fragmentary orders to support garrison operations as the single

                planning element within the battalion structure.
          • Operate the operational network control station.

Battalion Logistics Staff

     2-12. The battalion logistics staff section provides logistics support to the corps commander. It—
          • Provides logistics support to the battalion.
          • Performs property accountability functions and organizational property books.
          • Maintains the nontactical vehicle and government service agency fleet vehicle accounts.
          • Provides oversight of international merchant purchase authority card programs.
          • Manages supply services for the battalion support and personnel.
          • Maintains liaison with logistics section in the main CP.
          • Operates the administrative and logistics log net control station.

Battalion Maintenance Section

     2-13. The battalion maintenance section provides maintenance support to the corps commander. It—
          • Coordinates maintenance support for all the equipment of the headquarters and headquarters
                battalion, main CP, the tactical CP, rear CP, and the mobile command group.
          • Coordinates with main CP, tactical CP, and rear CP maintenance sections to oversee field-level
                maintenance of vehicles, power generators, and air conditioners assigned to the CPs.

Unit Ministry Team

     2-14. The unit ministry team provides religious support to the commander. It—
          • Coordinates with the corps chaplain section to provide unit level religious support to all battalion
                and headquarters personnel assigned or attached to the battalion.
          • Provides unit level support to all nondenominational coverage and ministry for mass casualties and
                hospitalized members of the battalion.
          • Advises commander and staff on religious, moral, and Soldier welfare issues to include the impact
                of indigenous religions on operations.
          • Establishes liaison and maintains contact with the chaplain section of the main CP and other unit
                ministry teams from higher echelon and adjacent units.

Battalion Medical Treatment Section

     2-15. The battalion medical treatment section provides medical support to the corps commander. It—
          • Plans, coordinates, synchronizes, integrates, and provides force health protection to the corps
                headquarters.
          • Coordinates and conducts combat stress control training for the corps headquarters’ personnel.
          • Collects and records the social and psychological data of the corps headquarters’ personnel.
          • Provides emergency medical treatment, advanced trauma management, sick call services, and
                preventive medicine support to the main CP and tactical command post.

18  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                          The Corps Structure

     2-16. The headquarters and headquarters battalion are responsible for the tactical mobility of the corps
     headquarters. Mobility is a quality or capability of military forces which permits them to move from place to
     place while retaining the ability to fulfill their primary mission (JP 3-36). A field feeding company is assigned
     to the battalion for the feeding of the corps headquarters CPs and any additional units assigned to the
     headquarters. Based on the mission variables, a corps headquarters requires additional security elements to
     secure the headquarters and headquarters battalion and each of the corps’ CPs. The security elements require
     the mobility, survivability, and combat power necessary to secure all the CPs while moving and when
     stationary.

Headquarters Support Company

     2-17. The headquarters support company provides C2 and limited company-level administration and life
     support to the headquarters and headquarters battalion, corps headquarters, and organic staff cells of each of
     the corps’ CPs. The headquarters support company contains all members of the headquarters battalion staff
     in addition to the company headquarters. The functions and tasks of the company are—

          • The administrative, maintenance, legal, and supply functions in support of the corps headquarters.
          • Oversight, coordination, and synchronization of administrative and logistic support for assigned,

                attached, or OPCON units to the corps headquarters.
          • Task-organization to support the CPs.

Signal, Intelligence, and Sustainment Company

     2-18. The signal, intelligence, and sustainment company installs, operates, and maintains corps network
     enterprise communication and computer systems in support of 24-hour operations for corps-level functions.
     It also provides the planning, coordination, integration, analysis, and synchronization of the intelligence and
     sustainment operations of the corps headquarters. It contains the Soldiers assigned to the corps headquarters
     intelligence and sustainment functional cells. The organic functional intelligence and sustainment cells plan,
     coordinate, integrate, synchronize, and execute specified functions and activities across their warfighting
     functions and support the coordination and synchronization of warfighting functions and forces across the
     three planning-horizon integrating cells (current, future, and plans). The company headquarters primarily
     supports the tactical CP when it is deployed. The staff elements operate from the tactical, main, and rear CPs.

     2-19. The signal, intelligence, and sustainment company headquarters provides company level administrative
     and logistics support to Soldiers in the corps headquarters intelligence and sustainment functional cells, corps
     surgeon section, the corps assistant chief of staff, signal (G-6) staff section, and the two signal platoons that
     support the corps main CP, tactical CP, and rear CP. The company receives its ground vehicle and generator
     maintenance support from the battalion’s headquarters support company. The corps headquarters and
     headquarters battalion commander normally employ this company’s headquarters element as the base around
     which a task-organized life support organization is formed to support the corps tactical CP when that
     command node deploys.

Main Command Post-Operational Detachment

     2-20. The multicomponent nature of the corps imposes certain limitations on the corps’ capability to rapidly
     deploy and initiate large-scale combat operations. A corps has multiple Reserve Component organizations
     that require time to mobilize, deploy, and integrate into their respective roles and responsibilities. A main
     command post-operational detachment is required to staff the corps CPs. The main command post-
     operational detachment (MCP-OD) provides additional depth to the corps staffs for sustained operations.
     When a corps is preparing to deploy, commanders, staffs, and planners must take care to coordinate for
     Reserve Component mobilization with enough time to assemble the formation for deployment. Alternatively,
     a corps may lack adequate time, and then it must assemble the formation in theater to conduct operations
     before the Reserve Component is mobilized. This requires the corps to secure resources and capabilities from
     other formations or operate without the Reserve Component elements of the formation.

CORPS COMMAND POSTS

     2-21. Corps echelon their CPs to control deep, close, and rear operations appropriately for a specific
     operation, based upon mission variables and the specific functions the commander requires during the

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  19


---

Chapter 2

     operation. The Army organizes a CP staff section’s cross functionally into CP cells. A command post cell is
     a grouping of personnel and equipment organized by warfighting function or by planning horizon to facilitate
     the exercise of command and control (FM 6-0). Functional cells group personnel and equipment by
     warfighting function. Integrating cells group personnel and equipment by planning horizon.

                                           Figure 2-2. Corps Command Posts

     2-22. Generally, the main command post is the primary command post that plans and synchronizes the
     employment of corps combat power across the entire AO. The corps’ tactical command post usually focuses
     on controlling critical close operations so that the main command post may focus on deep operations and
     identifying opportunities to exploit. In certain situations, the corps commander may direct the tactical
     command posts to control a specific deep operation. The rear command post is deployed to assist the
     commander with controlling rear operations, managing terrain, and the overall support of the operations. The
     rear command post coordinates with the maneuver enhancement brigade (MEB) to ensure that critical assets
     in the corps support area are protected. The mobile command group provides the corps commander with the
     necessary situational awareness to make critical decisions when moving between command posts or
     subordinate units. These CPs and the MCG are discussed in paragraphs 2-23 to 2-40. (See figure 2-2 for a
     depiction of the corps CPs.)

MAIN COMMAND POST

     2-23. The main command post is a portion of a unit headquarters containing the majority of the staff designed
     to command and control current operations, conduct detailed analysis, and plan future operations (FM 6-0).
     The main CP is the primary CP that the corps uses to control operations, and it is positioned and physically
     distributed in ways that minimize its signature and vulnerability to enemy fires. The main CP is responsible
     for the sustained conduct of current operations, future planning, analysis for current and future operations,
     targeting, and other staff functions. The staff at the main CP operates under the general supervision of the
     chief of staff. The main CP can accomplish its C2 responsibilities without the tactical CP. Main CP
     responsibilities include—

          • Synchronizing corps operations with adjacent units and the higher echelon.
          • Controlling all corps operations.

20  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                          The Corps Structure

          • Planning operations.
          • Monitoring and assessing current operations for impact on future operations.
          • Executing supporting efforts in the deep and close areas.
          • Producing operation and contingency plans.
          • Preparing and maintaining corps running estimates, plans, and orders to support future operations.
          • Integrating intelligence operations into both current and future operations.
          • Coordinating and managing force structure, to include requests for forces and equipment.
          • Synchronizing a corps’ targeting process.
          • Managing airspace.
          • Preparing all reports required by higher echelon headquarters.

     2-24. The main CP plans and synchronizes the employment of capabilities across all warfighting functions.
     If the corps commander decides to deploy the tactical CP to control current operations in the close area, the
     main CP conducts the deliberate targeting process. It plans and executes deep operations, and it coordinates
     with the next higher echelon of command for joint and Army capabilities to support the corps’ operations.

     2-25. The main CP includes the command group and most of the coordinating, special, and personal staffs.
     The main CP contains five functionally focused cells (intelligence, movement and maneuver, fire support,
     protection, and sustainment) with broad responsibilities for controlling operations across the corps by
     warfighting functions. The movement and maneuver cell, augmented by subject matter experts from across
     the staff sections, organizes into the three integrating cells: the current operations integration cell, future
     operations cell, and plans cell. The integrating cells coordinate or unify actions across all other functional
     cells. The movement and maneuver cell also includes several specialized elements, including the airspace
     element. It also receives United States Air Force (USAF) elements structured to support the headquarters’
     assigned role. Because there is no C2 functional cell, staff elements responsible for C2 tasks either receive
     direction and priorities from the chief of staff or work within an integrating cell. Liaison elements from higher
     echelon, adjacent, and subordinate units locate at the main CP. The main CP also includes the corps surgeon
     section, which is responsible for advising the corps commander on the health of the corps and coordinating,
     synchronizing, integrating, planning, and assessing operational medical support within the corps. If provided,
     the SOF coordinating element also locates here.

TACTICAL COMMAND POST

     2-26. The corps tactical CP is resourced as a stand-alone capability replicating the main CP’s functionality,
     albeit with less depth and redundancy. It provides a place from which the commander can exercise C2 while
     the main CP deploys or displaces. The tactical CP should be physically offset from the main CP to provide
     redundancy in the event of an attack on the main CP. The tactical CP may be designated the primary CP to
     control division operations in the close area. The corps commander may task the deputy commander, but
     typically the corps G-3 controls operations from the tactical CP as directed by the commander.

     2-27. The tactical CP is designed for short-term combat requirements and is manned accordingly. The CG or
     corps chief of staff can position any staff necessary for the situation in the TAC.

     2-28. A corps employs a tactical CP to control specific operations. It contains many of the same C2 systems
     as the main CP. It maintains continuous communication with subordinates, higher echelon headquarters,
     other CPs, and supporting joint organizations. The G-3 section is usually responsible for its operation,
     supported by other staff sections as required by the mission. When not employed, the corps prepares for the
     next operation by establishing the tactical CP at a separate location.

     2-29. The tactical CP focuses on specific actions, activities, or tasks within a larger operation. It requires
     augmentation from the main CP to control continuous operations for periods of time extending beyond 48
     hours. Its potential roles include—

          • Controlling forces committed to a specified operation.
          • Temporarily controlling corps operations.
          • Serving as the headquarters of a multidivisional task force organized from corps units for a mission.
          • Distributing control of the main effort and supporting efforts.
          • Employing and deploying forces simultaneously with the main CP.
          • Augmenting the main CP.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  21


---

Chapter 2

     These roles are discussed in paragraphs 2-30 through 2-35.

     2-30. A tactical CP may control forces committed to a specified operation. Examples of these types of
     operations include gap crossings and passage of lines involving multiple subordinate units. A passage of lines
     is an operation in which a force moves forward or rearward through another force’s combat positions with
     the intention of moving into or out of contact with the enemy (JP 3-18).

     2-31. A tactical CP may serve as the headquarters of a multidivisional task force organized from corps units
     for a mission. For example, during an offensive operation, the corps commander may establish the tactical
     CP to control forces eliminating bypassed enemy forces along a line of operations, while the main CP
     maintains control (and focus) on other operations in the corps’ deep and close areas.

     2-32. A tactical CP may distribute control of the main effort and supporting efforts. A corps may distribute
     control of main and supporting efforts between CPs during complex operations. For example, the tactical CP
     may control a division and supporting units in a covering force mission, while the main CP controls defensive
     preparations in the main battle area (MBA).

     2-33. A tactical CP may employ and deploy forces simultaneously with the main CP. During protracted
     operations, the main CP can control the deployment of forces into an assigned area, while the tactical CP is
     forward in the corps’ assigned area controlling initial operations. These roles could be reversed over time,
     with the main CP controlling operations within the corps’ assigned area and the tactical or rear CP managing
     the onward movement and integration of forces into the corps’ assigned area.

     2-34. A tactical CP may augment the main CP. During protracted operations, the commander may combine
     the tactical and main CPs to increase the capacity to control particularly complex tasks or as part of
     reorganization after suffering losses.

     2-35. The tactical CP is mobile, and it can control corps operations for a limited time and form the core
     element of a forward-deployed early-entry CP (sometimes designated as an assault CP). Factors that influence
     the movement of the tactical CP include the flow of operations, the threat of enemy action, the ability to C2
     subordinate units, and the desires of the commander. Based on the threat environment and the typical
     proximity of the tactical CP to the close area, frequent displacements are typically required for survivability.

REAR COMMAND POST

     2-36. Corps commanders have a rear CP to assist in controlling rear operations. The rear CP disperses the
     corps signature and enables the corps commander to exercise C2 over disparate, functionally focused
     elements operating between the close area and the division and corps rear boundary that may exceed the
     effective span of control of the main CP. The corps deputy commanding general, or the corps commander’s
     designated senior staff officer leads the rear CP. The rear CP enables a corps by unifying the efforts of the
     various units, both under corps control and not under corps control, that operate in the rear area. When
     synchronized, these efforts enable a corps to maintain freedom of action, tempo, and operational reach. A
     rear CP performs six functions to C2 the rear area. The six functions are—

          • Managing terrain and controlling movement in the rear area.
          • Defeating threats in the rear area to support areas, other base camps or clusters, and lines of

                communication.
          • Enabling sustainment operations.
          • Enabling consolidation of gains.
          • Enabling transitions.
          • Coordinating and synchronizing rear area security.

     2-37. The complexity of elements in the rear area creates additional challenges for the corps rear CP. One of
     the most important tasks for the rear CP is the management of terrain and route/air corridors. The rear CP
     requires significant augmentation with personnel and equipment, and authorities and responsibilities must be
     clearly identified in the corps order or corps standard operating procedures (known as SOPs). Additionally,
     the planning and organization of the rear CP is critical to ensure protection and life support and to account
     for the corps signal structure. The rear CP also provides the corps commander an alternate command post
     and assists in integrating protection operations throughout the corps assigned area.

22  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                          The Corps Structure

EARLY-ENTRY COMMAND POST

     2-38. The early-entry command post is a lead element of a headquarters designed to control operations until
     the remaining portions of the headquarters are deployed and operational (FM 6-0). Commanders can establish
     an early-entry CP to assist them in controlling operations during the deployment phase of operations and to
     coordinate the RSOI of subsequent corps forces. The deputy commander, chief of staff, or G-3 typically leads
     the early-entry CP. The early-entry CP normally consists of personnel and equipment from the tactical CP
     with additional intelligence analysts, planners, and other staff officers from the main CP based on the
     situation.

MOBILE COMMAND GROUP

     2-39. The MCG enables the commander to command from anywhere in an AO. It normally consists of the
     commander, intelligence personnel, fires personnel, and maneuver personnel, as determined by the
     commander. It allows a corps commander to—

          • Provide personal leadership, intent, and guidance directly to subordinate commanders.
          • Circulate to make a personal assessment of situations on the battlefield.
          • Maintain situational awareness while moving around the corps’ assigned area.
          • Avoid being fixed in vulnerable fixed CPs.

     2-40. The MCG moves within the AO to interact with subordinate commanders and different staffs, assess
     the operation, make decisions, and issue guidance. What corps commanders learn and see helps them
     mentally visualize adjustments needed in current and future operations. The MCG is not capable of exercising
     C2 to the same degree as the CPs but is sufficient for providing subordinates direction and guidance. Its
     primary purpose is to assist the commander with maintaining the necessary situational understanding to make
     critical decisions while moving between CPs or subordinate units during the conduct of operations. The MCG
     requires a security force. Usually, a company-sized unit is tasked from a subordinate BCT to secure the main
     CP, headquarters and headquarters’ battalion, and the MCG. (See FM 6-0 for additional information on CPs
     and CP placement considerations.)

THE CORPS STAFF

     2-41. Staff sections assist commanders in planning, coordinating, and supervising operations. A staff section
     is a grouping of staff members by area of expertise (FM 6-0). Not all staff sections reside in one of the
     functional or integrating CP cells. These staff sections maintain their distinct organizations. They operate in
     different CP cells as required and coordinate their activities in meetings (including working groups and
     boards) established by the unit’s battle rhythm. For example, the corps FSCOORD ensures that the corps fire
     support system supports the corps commander’s guidance for fire support, meets joint force requirements,
     and reacts responsively to changing battlefield conditions.

     2-42. There are several key aspects of corps staff activities. First, the corps staff has extensive interface with
     multinational forces, host-nation forces and agencies, and other Services; there are multiple liaisons working
     at the corps. Second, the coordination with USAF, USMC, U.S. Navy, and SOF is critical at the corps level.
     Third, the corps continuously plans to set the conditions for large-scale combat operations by moving forces
     and assembling resources. The corps staff sections consist of the chief of staff, coordinating staff, special
     staff, personal staff, staff augmentation, command liaison, functional liaison, and other sections as required.

CHIEF OF STAFF

     2-43. The corps commanding general delegates supervisory authority of the corps staff sections to the chief
     of staff. As the key staff integrator, the chief of staff frees the corps commanding general from routine details
     of staff operations and the management of the headquarters. The chief of staff is responsible for the conduct
     of efficient and prompt staff actions within the corps headquarters. The secretary of the general staff and the
     corps headquarters and headquarters battalion commander, acting as headquarters commandant, assist the
     corps chief of staff in managing the corps headquarters.

     2-44. The chief of staff directs, supervises, and trains the staff sections. All corps principal staff officers—
     coordinating, special, and personal—conduct their staff functions under the supervision of the chief of staff.
     The chief of staff also serves as one of the commanding general’s principal advisors. An effective working

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  23


---

Chapter 2

     relationship helps the chief of staff, and the commanding general transmit and share information and insights.
     The chief of staff normally remains at the main CP. (See FM 6-0 for a list of the chief of staff’s
     responsibilities.)

COORDINATING STAFF

     2-45. Coordinating staff officers advise, plan, and coordinate actions in their areas of expertise. They also
     exercise planning and supervisory authority over designated special staff officers, as described in FM 6-0.
     The corps staff principals are responsible for integrating individual and small team staff augmentees into the
     corps’ existing staff structure to form the staff structure needed to support the corps mission. The coordinating
     staff should participate in all integrating processes as required. The corps coordinating staff are—

          • Assistant chief of staff, personnel (G-1).
          • Assistant chief of staff, intelligence (G-2).
          • Assistant chief of staff, operations (G-3).
          • Assistant chief of staff, sustainment (G-4).
          • Assistant chief of staff, plans (G-5).
          • Assistant chief of staff, communications (G-6).
          • Assistant chief of staff, financial management (G-8).
          • G-9.
          • Deputy fire support coordinator.
          • Chief of Protection

SPECIAL STAFF

     2-46. Special staff officers help commanders and other staff members fulfill their responsibilities. The
     number of special staff officers and their duties vary, and corps commanders organize them according to
     professional or technical responsibilities. The commander delegates planning and supervisory authority over
     each special staff function to a coordinating staff officer. The special staff should participate in all integrating
     processes as required.

     2-47. While special staff sections are not always integral to a coordinating staff section, they usually share
     areas of common interest and habitual association. Special staff officers routinely work with more than one
     coordinating staff officer. For example, the engineer officer is involved in planning and operations with more
     than just the protection cell. Mobility and countermobility are part of movement and maneuver, general
     engineering is part of sustainment, and geospatial engineering supports the intelligence warfighting function.
     The information operations officer coordinates with various other staff members, such as the psychological
     operations officer, public affairs officer, operations security officer, cyber electromagnetic warfare officer,
     and others to synchronize information activities. The information operations officer also contributes to the
     overall intelligence preparation of the operational environment (IPOE) by assisting the G-2 in identifying
     and evaluating threat information capabilities and the means to influence the population. The members of the
     special staff can change depending on the capabilities available to the corps commander and on the situation.
     (See FM 6-0 for additional information on special staff officers.)

     2-48. The corps special staff includes many different officers with different skills. They include the—
          • AMD officer.
          • Air liaison officer (LNO).
          • Aviation officer.
          • CBRN officer.
          • Civilian personnel officer.
          • Cyberspace electromagnetic warfare officer.
          • Dental surgeon.
          • Engineer.
          • Equal opportunity advisor.
          • EOD officer.
          • Force management officer.
          • Foreign disclosure officer.
          • Historian.

24  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026
