                                                                                                                          The Corps Structure

          • Information operations officer.
          • Knowledge management officer.
          • LNO.
          • Military deception (MILDEC) officer.
          • Operations research officer.
          • Operations security officer.
          • Personnel recovery officer.
          • Provost marshal. This officer is also part of the coordinating staff when designated as chief of

                protection.
          • Psychological operations officer.
          • Secretaries of the general staff.
          • Space operations officer.
          • Staff veterinary officer.
          • Staff weather officer.
          • Surgeon. This officer is also part of the commander’s personal staff.
          • Transportation officer.

PERSONAL STAFF

     2-49. Personal staff sections perform special assignments as directed by the corps commander. The personal
     staff sections advise the commander, provide input to orders and plans, and interface and coordinate with
     entities external to the corps headquarters. Army regulations and public law establish special relationships
     between certain staff officers and the commander. For example, AR 20-1, AR 27-1, AR 165-1, AR 360-1,
     and AR 385-10 require the inspector general, staff judge advocate, safety, chaplain, surgeon, and public
     affairs officer to serve as members of the commander’s personal staff. Additionally, the command sergeant
     major and aides are part of the commander’s personal staff. (See FM 6-0 for additional information on
     personal staff officers.)

     2-50. The corps personal staff includes the—
          • Aide-de-camp.
          • Chaplain.
          • Inspector general.
          • Internal review officer.
          • Public affairs officer.
          • Safety officer.
          • Staff judge advocate.
          • Surgeon.

STAFF AUGMENTATION

     2-51. Depending on the situation, teams and detachments with special expertise augment the corps
     headquarters. Available capabilities include civil affairs operations, additional Army space personnel, combat
     camera, operational law, detention, history, operational public health, and safety.

LIAISON ELEMENTS

     2-52. Command and functional liaison elements vary in size and overall staffing depending on the situation.
     The corps and higher echelon, lower, and subordinate headquarters exchange command liaison elements.
     Host nation, joint, multinational, and nonmilitary entities may also exchange command LNOs, such as the
     special operations C2 element. Functional liaison elements work with organizations that provide services to
     the corps such as intelligence, signal, and sustainment. The corps headquarters selects, trains, and equips
     liaison elements from the corps staff. The corps headquarters houses, provides life support, and trains liaison
     elements from outside the corps headquarters. (See FM 6-0 for more information on the roles and
     responsibilities of LNOs and liaison considerations when the corps conducts operations with unified action
     partners.)

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  25


---

Chapter 2

SECTION II — CORPS UNITS

     2-53. There is no standard configuration for a corps, although each corps has similar types of assigned forces.
     Corps are provided with additional Army and multinational forces and joint enablers dependent on mission
     variables in a specific theater.

     2-54. A corps consists of its headquarters battalion and assigned divisions, brigades, and a sustainment
     command. A corps formation may also include provided forces, such as attached divisions or attached
     functional support capabilities operating under OPCON or in direct support to the corps or its subordinate
     divisions. Functional support units operating in the corps AO as general support, but not assigned or attached
     to the corps, are not normally included in the corps formation.

SUBORDINATE FORMATIONS

     2-55. The Army typically assigns corps with divisions and other forces, such as field artillery, aviation,
     military police, engineers, CBRN, military intelligence, signal, and sustainment. Corps augmenting forces
     provide a capability to accomplish assigned missions. The corps commander exercises command, OPCON
     or TACON, over a mix of Service, multinational, and division headquarters and brigades appropriate for the
     mission. (See figure 2-3 for an example of corps forces and enablers. Paragraphs 2-55 through 2-108 include
     descriptions of forces potentially available to support a corps.)

     2-56. A corps routinely controls multinational forces in accordance with their individual national caveats.
     The organization, support, and sustainment capabilities of these units varies. The corps commander pays
     particular attention to the national capabilities, caveats, and limitations of each multinational partner. Some
     limitations are tactical while others may be political. Normally, a corps receives a TLD to facilitate
     multinational interoperability.

    Figure 2-3. Corps Forces and Enablers

26  ATP 3-92                               15 January 2026


---

                                                                                            The Corps Structure

ASSIGNED FORCES

2-57.   The corps routinely has units attached or assigned  to it. These  forces  include—
     •     Two to five divisions.                           (E-MIB).
     •     A combat aviation brigade (CAB).
     •     One or more separate BCTs.
     •     A maneuver enhancement brigade (MEB).
     •     An expeditionary military intelligence brigade
     •     An artillery brigade.
     •     An engineer brigade.
     •     A corps sustainment command (CSC).
     •     A military police brigade.
     •     A corps signal brigade.

2-58. A corps commander task-organizes the formation, assigns tasks, and determines appropriate command
and support relationships for subordinate divisions and brigades. A corps commander may retain a division
to consolidate gains concurrently with other operations.

Divisions

     2-59. Divisions are the major subordinate tactical units for a corps, and they are the Army’s principal tactical
     warfighting formation during large-scale combat operations. A division’s main role is as a tactical
     headquarters, employing brigades during offensive, defensive, and stability operations in an AO assigned by
     its higher echelon headquarters, normally a corps. A division commander task-organizes subordinate forces
     to accomplish missions. During large-scale combat operations, a division operates as a formation.

     2-60. In addition to its BCTs, each division controls a tailored array of multifunctional support brigades and
     functional brigades. Not all types of brigades are available for every division. In some operations, divisions
     control multiple brigades of the same type. They can also control functional groups, battalions, or separate
     companies, which are often task-organized to a brigade. Division organizations vary for each operation based
     upon context and whether they are the main or supporting effort. (See ATP 3-91 for more information on
     division organization.)

Brigade Combat Teams

     2-61. BCTs are normally subordinate units for a division; however, a corps may control a BCT or similar
     organization and task-organize it for a specific role such as a corps reserve or tactical combat force for area
     security. There are three types of BCTs: armored, infantry, and Stryker. BCTs maneuver against, close with,
     and destroy enemy forces. BCTs have organic combined arms capabilities, including battalion-sized
     maneuver, field artillery, reconnaissance, and sustainment units. Each BCT has organic Role 1 and Role 2
     medical support.

     2-62. A corps or division commander can task-organize a BCT by adding or subtracting its subordinate units.
     Higher echelon commanders may augment BCTs with additional combat power for specific missions.
     Augmentation might include aviation, armor, infantry, field artillery, air defense artillery (ADA), military
     police, civil affairs elements, military information support elements, engineers, CBRN elements, EOD
     elements, or other forces or capabilities. Some of this augmentation may come from other BCTs.
     Multifunctional and functional brigades can support BCT operations. (See FM 3-96 for additional
     information on BCT operations.)

Maneuver Enhancement Brigade

     2-63. Corps commanders typically assign responsibility for the support area to a MEB headquarters when
     available. When assigned the mission of supporting echelons above division, joint, or multinational forces, a
     MEB could be task-organized with other Service or national units and integrated staff augmentation to
     provide C2 for a variety of elements necessary to support those forces.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92                                                                   27


---

Chapter 2

     2-64. MEBs are multifunctional brigade headquarters designed to control forces from multiple branches, but
     especially organizations that conduct tasks enabling support area operations. Corps commanders organize
     MEBs for support area operations based on identified mission requirements. A MEB also performs doctrinal
     functions associated with AO responsibility.

     2-65. A MEB may support an Army, joint, interagency, or multinational headquarters. A MEB headquarters
     is staffed and optimized to conduct combined arms operations that integrate a wide range of functional
     branches and forces. A MEB organizes, provides, or employs battalion task force and company team
     combined arms technical experts to conduct tasks that enable maneuver across all OEs. A MEB may include
     a mix of CBRN elements, civil affairs elements, engineer elements, military police elements, ADA units, and
     a tactical combat force. With a tactical combat force, the MEB can provide command and control for assigned
     forces to defeat Level I, II, and III threats within an assigned AO.

             Note. The MEB can support specific missions outside of its AO or provide forces to complement
             or reinforce other formations when necessary. However, serving as a force provider for enduring
             periods risks its ability to conduct support area operations.

     2-66. The number and type of organizations placed under a MEB depends on the mission, threat, and number
     and type of battalions or companies operating in the brigade’s AO. A MEB provides staff planning and
     exercises command relationships with units conducting support area operations, especially for base camp and
     base cluster defense operations. Army force structure places all MEB headquarters in the Army Reserve and
     the Army National Guard. (See FM 3-81 for additional information on the MEB. See ATP 3-37.10 for more
     information on base camps.)

Expeditionary Military Intelligence Brigade

     2-67. The Army designed E-MIBs to support the emerging requirements of field armies, corps, and divisions
     conducting large-scale combat operations across all domains. An E-MIB conducts multidiscipline
     intelligence operations and provides processing, exploitation, and dissemination, analysis, and
     multidiscipline information collection capability and capacity to the corps and subordinate divisions’
     multidomain operations. This facilitates situational understanding for the corps and division commanders and
     critical pieces of the sensor-to-shooter linkage necessary for the corps to execute deep operations.

     2-68. E-MIBs are organized with a headquarters, one intelligence and electromagnetic warfare (IEW);
     battalion (at corps echelons), and one IEW battalion (at division echelons) per each priority division. The
     IEW battalions conduct and provide direct support multidiscipline intelligence analysis and processing,
     exploitation, and dissemination in support of the corps G-2; multidomain intelligence analysis and targeting
     support, combat assessments, processing, exploitation, and dissemination; and collection in support of corps
     multidomain effects. They also provide counterintelligence, human intelligence, signals intelligence, and EW
     capabilities to the corps. They also execute C2 of all allocated, nonorganic military intelligence forces
     assigned or attached to the G-2. Additionally, the IEW battalion (at corps echelons) conducts multidomain
     deep sensing, analytics, and effects capabilities in support of corps operations during large-scale combat
     operations. The headquarters is responsible for the training and readiness of the assigned IEW battalions and
     can serve as the senior military intelligence C2 headquarters for field armies or other deployed operational
     Army headquarters.

     2-69. The E-MIB staff works closely with the corps G-2 analysis and control element. The E-MIB
     commander and staff also assist the corps commander with information collection management. The IEW
     battalion (at corps echelons) is responsible for overseeing and executing ISR operations in support of corps
     operations. ISR operations personnel are co-located with the corps G-2 current operations on the current
     operations integration cell floor. (See FM 2-0 for more information on intelligence and ATP 2-19.3 for more
     information on E-MIB capabilities and operations.)

Corps Artillery Brigade

     2-70. The corps field artillery brigade’s primary task is conducting corps-level strike operations. It is the
     primary executor of Army long-range, indirect fires within the corps AO. The field artillery brigade gives the

28  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                          The Corps Structure

     corps or a designated supported commander the ability to plan, prepare, execute, and—with proper
     augmentation—assess long-range, indirect fires.

     2-71. The field artillery brigade can employ Army rocket and cannon fires and incorporate EW. It can detect
     and attack targets using a mix of its organic target acquisition and fire support capabilities, a supported
     organization’s information collection capabilities, and access to higher echelon headquarters information
     collection capabilities.

     2-72. Corps typically employ their assigned field artillery brigade as the force field artillery headquarters and
     the corps artillery brigade commander is the corps fires coordinator. A force field artillery headquarters is a
     battalion size or higher unit designated by the supported commander who specifies its duration, duties, and
     responsibilities (FM 3-09). However, in the case where there are two field artillery brigades assigned,
     attached, or OPCON to the corps and no operational-level field artillery command available, one field
     artillery brigade can serve as the counterfire headquarters, and the other can service the corps commander’s
     targeting priorities and support division operations. (See FM 3-09 and ATP 3-09.24 for additional
     information on field artillery brigade operations.)

Corps Engineer Brigade

     2-73. The corps engineer brigade controls the mission-tailored engineer battalions that are not task-organized
     to subordinate divisions and brigades. These battalions typically have capabilities from the three engineer
     disciplines: combat engineering, general engineering, and geospatial engineering. Their purpose is to enhance
     mobility, countermobility, protection, and sustainment.

     2-74. An engineer brigade develops plans, procedures, and programs for engineer support. These include,
     but are not limited to, requirements determination, mobility and countermobility, general engineering, power
     generation, area damage control, military construction, geospatial engineering, engineering design,
     construction materials, and real property maintenance activities. The corps engineer brigade integrates and
     synchronizes engineer capabilities across the corps AO, and it reinforces subordinate corps units in the
     execution of engineer tasks by allocating mission-tailored engineer forces.

     2-75. Engineer tasks shape terrain to overcome obstacles (including gaps); create, maintain, and improve
     lines of communications; create fighting positions; improve protective positions; and build structures and
     facilities (including base camps, aerial ports, seaports, and utilities). An engineer brigade is also capable of
     rapid deployment in modular elements to support the needs of the operational commander. These elements
     can provide a wide range of technical engineering expertise and support. (See FM 3-34 for additional
     information about engineer operations.)

Corps Signal Brigade

     2-76. A corps signal brigade is a headquarters and staff intended to design and control signal support for
     corps and division operations. In large-scale combat operations, the corps signal brigade will command one
     or more attached expeditionary signal battalions or expeditionary signal battalions-enhanced. Corps signal
     brigades provide communications transport to a corps headquarters, the corps’ subordinate units, and, as
     required, to joint and unified action partners throughout the corps AO. The corps signal brigade and its
     attached units install, operate, maintain, and secure their portions of the communications network. Each corps
     signal brigade leverages network extension and reachback capabilities to provide connectivity to CCDRs and
     subordinate commanders to support C2 and enable the other warfighting functions. (See FM 6-02 for more
     information on the corps signal brigade.)

Corps Sustainment Command

     2-77. The Corps Sustainment Command (known as CSC) integrates and synchronizes sustainment operations
     in the corps area. Assigned to the corps and task-organized with one or more sustainment brigades, it
     commands and controls sustainment units operating in the corps rear area. The sustainment brigades task
     organized under the CSC comprise functional and multifunctional units providing general or direct support
     within the corps' AO. (For more information on sustainment brigades, refer to ATP 4-92.) Sustainment is the
     provision of logistics, financial management, personnel services, and health service support necessary to
     maintain operations until mission completion (ADP 4-0). In addition to sustainment brigades, a CSC typically

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  29


---

Chapter 2

     includes enablers such as an Army field support brigade (which provides a corps logistics support element
     when deployed), petroleum group, and movement control battalion. Depending on missions it may also have
     an attached transportation brigade expeditionary and corps aerial delivery company as required.

     2-78. The CSC assists the corps sustainment cell with planning and coordinating sustainment, planning for
     near-term operations, and synchronizing operational-level sustainment operations to meet current operations.
     The CSC is dependent on the corps staff for long-range planning capability. It also relies upon a corps for
     additional security, signal, and medical support. (See FM 4-0 and ATP 4-92 for more information on the
     CSC.)

Army Field Support Brigade

     2-79. An Army field support brigade enables readiness and power projection in direct support of the corps
     and its subordinate organizations at home station through its Army field support battalions (on divisional
     installations) and logistics readiness centers. An Army field support brigade deploys a corps logistics support
     element OPCON to the corps when it deploys which provides a single interface to all Army materiel
     command, acquisition, logistics, and technology and materiel enterprise capabilities. These capabilities
     include expert technical support through logistics assistance representatives, the logistics civil augmentation
     program, Army pre-positioned stocks, and many Army Material Command call forward capabilities,
     particularly sustainment maintenance. (See ATP 4-98 for more information on the Army field support
     brigade.)

Military Police Brigade

     2-80. A military police brigade is a functional brigade capable of planning, integrating, and directing the
     execution of military police missions conducted by up to five mission-tailored military police battalions,
     integrating capabilities from all three military police disciplines (police operations, detention operations, and
     security and mobility support), and integrating police intelligence operations. It may also C2 other assigned
     or attached subordinate headquarters and units supporting military police operations. A military police
     brigade is required to allocate, synchronize, control, and provide technical oversight of military police
     operations when there is a requirement for more than two military police battalions within a corps or division
     AO. When designated, executes the duties of the Commander of Detainee Operations (CDO) and exercises
     command and control of all assets performing detainee operations. (See FM 3-39 for additional information
     on the military police brigade and military police operations.)

PROVIDED FORCES

     2-81. Based upon a corps’ role and mission, it can receive a variety of forces to assist it in matching
     capabilities to requirements. Forces provided to a corps may consist of functional brigades, multifunctional
     brigades, and smaller units and enablers. These forces can be attached, under OPCON, or under TACON of
     the corps. The forces commonly provided to corps are—

          • CAB.
          • Expeditionary CAB.
          • ADA brigade.
          • Civil affairs brigade.
          • Medical brigade (support) (MEDBDE).
          • SOF.
          • CBRN brigade.
          • EOD group.
          • Psychological operations group.
          • Army space support team.
          • Army space control planning team.
          • Military history detachment.
          • Mobile public affairs detachment (MPAD).
          • Cyber and Electromagnetic Warfare (CEMA) forces.
          • Security force assistance brigade.
          • Chaplain detachment Charlie.

30  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                          The Corps Structure

          • Theater liaison detachment (TLD).

             Note. SOF may have a supporting or coordinating relationship with the corps.

     2-82. A corps receives general support from the signal and air defense brigades commanded by the theater
     signal command and Army Air and Missile Defense Command (AAMDC), respectively. A corps commander
     employs subordinate brigades under corps command to reinforce divisions or to conduct deep operations,
     which set conditions for its maneuver forces. These brigades also support the corps. Depending on the
     situation, a corps may receive direct or general medical support from a MEDBDE or a theater medical
     command.

Combat Aviation Brigade

     2-83. A CAB synchronizes multiple aviation battalions’ operations to support corps or division operations.
     A corps commander can reorganize a CAB in response to the situation. It can provide tailored support to
     adjacent, supported maneuver commanders at the BCT echelon and below. A CAB commander is normally
     the senior Army aviation officer in the corps or division structure and the corps aviation coordinator. A CAB
     commander advises adjacent and higher echelon commanders on manned and unmanned aviation system
     employment.

     2-84. A CAB provides a corps or division commander with a maneuver advantage that can overcome the
     constraints of limiting terrain and extended distances. During large-scale combat operations, attack,
     reconnaissance, utility, and cargo aircraft may maneuver under corps or division control in the deep area or
     within an assigned AO with appropriate joint support from the air component. Alternatively, a CAB’s attack,
     reconnaissance, utility, and cargo assets may be under OPCON, TACON, general support, or direct support
     to another brigade as situationally appropriate. Furthermore, a CAB may receive OPCON of ground
     maneuver forces to conduct security or reconnaissance operations or to accomplish other economy of force
     missions. The use of aviation assets requires detailed planning of synchronized timelines, aviation task and
     purpose, and airspace management.

     2-85. A Gray Eagle company is organic to a CAB, and it is dependent on the CAB attack battalion or air
     cavalry squadron for staff planning, integration, and coordination in joint and combined arms operations. The
     Gray Eagle company provides dedicated multimission tactical unmanned aircraft systems capability and
     normally operates as a division or corps asset. The Gray Eagle company conducts surveillance; zone, route,
     and area reconnaissance; attack; battle damage assessment; and C2 support. (See FM 3-04 for additional
     information on Army aviation operations.)

Expeditionary Combat Aviation Brigade

     2-86. An expeditionary CAB is a multifunctional unit designed to air assault maneuver forces; position
     personnel, supplies, and equipment; evacuate casualties; conduct personnel recovery; and provide C2. The
     expeditionary CAB is designed to be modular and tailorable, and it is typically task-organized as required to
     support offensive, defensive, and stability operations in support of ground maneuver forces or during defense
     support to civil authorities.

     2-87. When task-organized with an attack battalion or air cavalry squadron, expeditionary CABs also provide
     accurate and timely information collection; provide reaction time and maneuver space; and destroy, defeat,
     disrupt, or delay enemy forces. An expeditionary CAB does not have organic attack helicopter assets. When
     augmented, an expeditionary CAB may perform additional tasks such as movement to contact, attack,
     reconnaissance, and security. An expeditionary CAB is an Army National Guard or U.S. Army Reserve
     organization. (See FM 3-04 for additional information on Army aviation operations.)

Air Defense Artillery Brigade

     2-88. ADA brigades are structured to perform several functions supporting the AAMDCs and designated
     CCDRs. ADA brigade functions include C2 activities, integration, planning, and liaison with joint, higher
     echelon units, and subordinate battalions. ADA brigades are the force providers for the AAMDCs, meeting
     the commander’s AMD objectives. ADA brigades must be prepared to integrate a mix of active and reserve

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  31


---

Chapter 2

     component forces. ADA brigades from the AAMDC support corps by providing air battle management of
     subordinate units. The ADA brigade commander is the corps AMD coordinator.

     2-89. ADA brigades deploy early to protect aerial ports of debarkation, seaports of debarkation, early arriving
     forces, and critical supplies. As a lodgment expands, ADA forces may reposition to better protect critical
     assets, communications, transportation, and maneuver forces. ADA brigades and any available joint and
     multinational AMD forces combine to form integrated defenses after completion of deployment operations.
     Echelon commanders, with staff support, designate their prioritized critical assets. The JFC and joint staff
     consider these prioritized critical asset lists during the development of an adjusted JTF-defended asset list. A
     JFC’s requirements and mission considerations may allow an ADA brigade to operate under the OPCON or
     TACON of a corps. Corps may assign or attach short-range air defense battalions to divisions or other
     headquarters to accompany maneuver forces and protect critical static assets. Support relationships, such as
     direct and general support, may enhance AMD of maneuver echelons. Corps may integrate ADA firing units
     and radars into their defense plans, ensuring that they integrate into local security measures and adjust them
     based on the results from the joint theater AMD board and working groups. (See FM 3-01 for additional
     information on Army AMD and ADA operations.)

Civil Affairs Brigade

     2-90. A civil affairs brigade mitigates or defeats threats to civil society and conducts actions normally
     performed by civil governments across the range of military operations. A civil affairs brigade engages and
     influences the civil population and authorities through planning and conducting civil affairs operations or
     enabling civil-military operations to shape the civil environment and set the conditions for military
     operations.

     2-91. The civil affairs brigade headquarters provides a control structure and staff supervision of the
     operations of its assigned civil affairs battalions or other attached units. Civil affairs force structure contains
     expertise in five functional specialty areas. These specialty areas include security, justice and reconciliation,
     humanitarian assistance and social well-being, governance and participation, and economic stabilization.
     Within each functional specialty area, civil affairs functional specialists advise commanders and help or direct
     their civilian counterparts. Civil affairs brigades can establish or support civil-military operations centers
     during operations. (See FM 3-57 for additional information on civil affairs.)

Medical Brigade (Support)

     2-92. A MEDBDE is a subordinate command organization of the theater medical command. It serves as the
     headquarters for all assigned and attached echelons above brigade operational medicine forces. The
     MEDBDE is not always subordinate to a theater medical command. It may be assigned or attached to a corps
     or division providing medical support to an operational commander for the full range of military operations.
     In an operational area where the theater medical command is not yet deployed, a MEDBDE may serve as the
     senior medical command.

     2-93. The corps-aligned MEDBDE serves as the corps medical command and provides a corps commander
     with a medical headquarters tasked to organize, resource, train, sustain, deploy, and support assigned and
     attached operational medicine forces. It is also responsible for coordinating with the corps surgeon section
     on the synchronization, coordination, integration, planning, and assessment of corps operational medicine
     support. (See FM 4-02 for more information on the MEDBDE and ATP 4-02.55 for Army Health System
     support planning.)

Special Operations Forces

     2-94. Within a corps’ AO, conventional forces and SOF operate near each other to accomplish the JFC’s
     mission. ARSOF provide capabilities that expand the options available to a corps commander. When
     deployed, Army SOF are directly subordinate to a joint theater special operations command or general officer
     or flag officer-level joint special operations task force, which may be established by the JFC to focus on a
     specific mission region within the joint operations area (JOA). During mission planning, the corps staff and
     SOF planners include options regarding how to integrate conventional forces and special operations ground
     elements. A corps may receive a special operations C2 element which provides the capability to coordinate

32  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                          The Corps Structure

     unilateral special operations with a conventional ground force headquarters and with a supported
     conventional force commander. (See JP 3-05 and FM 6-05 for more information on SOF integration.)

Chemical, Biological, Radiological, and Nuclear Brigade

     2-95. A CBRN brigade typically provides support to a corps. CBRN brigades consist of a headquarters and
     two to five battalions with a range of CBRN capabilities, which may include CBRN reconnaissance,
     decontamination, biological detection, and other specialized countering weapons of mass destruction mission
     capabilities. (See JP 3-40 and ATP 3-90.40 for more information on countering weapons of mass destruction.)
     With the advice of the staff and the supported unit CBRN section, the CBRN brigade commander evaluates
     and determines the CBRN unit support requirements of the supported unit. The CBRN brigade commander
     advises the supported unit commander on the employment and sustainment of CBRN assets. A CBRN
     brigade depends on other organizations for administrative, logistic, medical, and maintenance support. A
     CBRN battalion typically supports a division. However, if a CBRN brigade or CBRNE command is not
     assigned, a CBRN battalion may be assigned to or support a corps or echelon above corps. (See FM 3-11 and
     ATP 3-37.11 for more information on CBRN units.)

Explosive Ordnance Disposal Group

     2-96. An EOD group is typically assigned or attached to a corps. An EOD group commander exercises C2
     of a headquarters detachment, two to six Army EOD battalions, and enablers. An EOD group may also
     control joint and multinational EOD forces. The group directs EOD operations, including counter-improvised
     explosive device operations in an AO. The EOD group manages, integrates, coordinates, and synchronizes
     Army exploitation functions regarding detection, collection, processing, exploitation, and analysis of
     explosive hazards and associated components. An EOD group may also form the core of a specialized
     combined JTF with the mission to integrate protection and exploitation enablers to conduct counter
     improvised explosive devices, exploitation, or counter-weapons of mass destruction task forces. An EOD
     battalion typically supports a division. However, if an EOD group or CBRNE command are not assigned, an
     EOD battalion can be assigned to or support a corps or echelons above corps. (See JP 3-42, ATP 4-32, and
     ATP 4-32.1 for further information on EOD.)

             Note. When a corps is operating in its JTF, LCC, or ARFOR role, it may receive a CBRNE
             command. It provides force-tailored and task-organized CBRN and EOD force packages to
             conduct counter weapons of mass destruction missions, CBRN response, counter-explosive
             ordnance assets and operations, or other CBRN and EOD missions. (See ATP 3-37.11 for more
             information on the CBRNE command.)

Psychological Operations Group

     2-97. Psychological operations groups are trained, organized, and equipped to support corps-level operations.
     They support corps with military information support operations across the range of military operations. They
     can also support the staffing and augmentation of a psychological operations task force in a mature theater
     of operations. The psychological operations battalions are task-organized to support divisions. (See FM 3-53
     for more information on military information support operations.)

Army Space Support Team

     2-98. If requested, an Army space support team deploys to provide additional capacity for the corps organic
     space support element if the corps is serving as a JTF or joint force land component command headquarters.
     An Army space support team plans, coordinates for, and integrates joint space capabilities in support of corps
     lines of effort. (See ATP 3-14.3 for more information on Army space support teams.)

Space Control Planning Team

     2-99. Army space control planning teams deploy to a corps serving as a JTF with OPCON of space control
     systems. The primary function is target development and nomination to negate the adversary use of the space
     domain. They also coordinate with theater space components and U.S. Space Command to synchronize

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  33


---

Chapter 2

     timing and tempo of space control capabilities in support of corps C2, movement and maneuver, fires, and
     protection. Planning for space control capabilities should be conducted by space operations professionals
     who are subject matter experts on space control. (See ATP 3-14.3 for more information on space control
     planning.)

Military History Detachment

     2-100. Military history detachments are small teams of officers and enlisted Soldiers who carry out directed
     collection of historical material during combat and contingency operations for use when writing official
     history. They are trained and equipped to gather historical documents and materials, conduct oral interviews,
     photograph events, and advise supported units about preservation and proper disposition of historical
     information and potential artifacts. The military history detachment provides expertise, advice, and assistance
     to the designated corps historian or unit historical officer. (See ATP 1-20 for more details on the military
     history operations.)

Mobile Public Affairs Detachment

     2-101. An MPAD provides public affairs support to corps units during operations. An MPAD is assigned,
     attached, or under the OPCON of echelons above brigades. It may support the Army Service component
     command, theater sustainment command, or corps public affairs staff sections.

     2-102. An MPAD increases the public affairs capabilities of the corps organic public affairs section, and it
     can establish or support a media operations center in support of corps operations. An MPAD has limited
     capability to conduct independent public affairs activities, and it requires higher echelon command guidance,
     administrative support, and life support. Because of its limited capability, the corps public affairs officer
     exercises planning and supervisory authority over the MPAD. (See FM 3-61 for more details on public affairs
     operations.)

Cyber and Electromagnetic Warfare Forces

     2-103. The corps G-3 contains a CEMA section. The CEMA section plans, coordinates, and integrates
     offensive cyberspace operations, defensive cyberspace operations, and EW in support of the commander’s
     intent. The CEMA section collaborates with numerous staff sections to ensure unity of effort in meeting the
     commander’s total operational objectives such as collaborating with the G-2 to attain situational awareness
     and understanding of friendly, enemy, and neutral actors operating within an AO. The CEMA section is
     responsible for providing regular updates to the commander and staff on offensive cyberspace operations and
     other supported operations conducted in an AO. The CEMA section is responsible for synchronizing and
     integrating cyberspace operations and EW with the operations process and through other integrating
     processes.

     2-104. The cyber electromagnetic warfare officer is the commander’s designated staff officer responsible for
     integrating, coordinating, and synchronizing actions in cyberspace and the electromagnetic spectrum. The
     cyber electromagnetic warfare officer is responsible for understanding all applicable classified and
     unclassified cyberspace and spectrum-related policies to assist the commander with planning, coordinating,
     and synchronizing cyberspace operations and EW with combined arms. The corps commander selects organic
     EW capabilities to create desired effects on targets identified for electromagnetic attack. If the corps’ organic
     EW capabilities do not fulfill the targeting requirement to support the commander’s intent, or if the
     commander does not have the authority to employ a particular EW capability, the CEMA section requests
     support from the next higher echelon.

     2-105. Local network defenders assigned or attached to the corps conduct defensive cyberspace operations-
     internal defensive measures within their capabilities. A corps does not have organic capabilities to conduct
     defensive cyberspace operations-response actions or offensive cyberspace operations missions. The G-6 and
     the CEMA section coordinate to request defensive cyberspace operations internal defensive measures
     augmentation after determining that a threat in friendly cyberspace is beyond the scope of local network
     defenders. Defensive cyberspace operations-internal defensive measures are enablers for defensive
     cyberspace operations response actions. The CEMA staff may request defensive cyberspace operations-
     response actions upon deciding that threat activities in cyberspace require a defensive attack beyond friendly

34  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                          The Corps Structure

     cyberspace. Offensive cyberspace operations are used to create desired effects on targets identified for
     cyberspace attacks on the integrated target list. Defensive cyberspace operations-response actions and
     offensive cyberspace operations are similar except that defensive cyberspace operations-response actions are
     used to affect a threat offensive cyberspace capability, whereas offensive cyberspace operations are used to
     project power. (See JP 3-85 and FM 3-12 for more information on cyberspace operations and electromagnetic
     warfare.)

Security Forces Assistance Brigade

     2-106. Security force assistance brigades conduct, advise, support, liaise, and assess activities to achieve their
     missions of improving foreign security forces capacity and capability, generating multinational
     interoperability, and expanding the global landpower network. During campaigning, security force assistance
     brigades employ force packages of task-organized advising teams. Advising teams operate at the battalion,
     brigade, or division echelons, while brigade advising teams can advise up to corps level. The security force
     assistance brigades conduct advise, support, liaise, and assess activities to achieve their missions of
     improving foreign security forces capacity and capability, generating multinational interoperability, and
     expanding the global landpower network. Security force assistance brigades shape the security environment
     in ways favorable to U.S. interests in accordance with the CCDR’s intent.

     2-107. A security force assistance brigade can support a corps by providing liaisons with planning or
     functional expertise and communications capabilities. This provides the commander with representative
     leaders and accessible expertise about partner capabilities, facilitates planning, and improves joint force
     positional and information advantage. Through support activities, security force assistance brigades increase
     partner force access to joint fires and effects. They also increase a partner force’s lethality, survivability, and
     operational endurance by maximizing its inherent position as an inside force. A security force assistance
     brigade can also facilitate RSOI, and it may deploy as an advanced party to the host-nation or partner
     organization to conduct pre-deployment activities and coordinate necessary travel documentation and
     country clearances. This advance party establishes contact with U.S. and foreign security force commanders
     for final pre-mission planning activities. A security force assistance brigade establishes procedures to
     promote interagency cooperation and synchronization, and it develops relationships with foreign security
     force commanders. (See ATP 3-96.1 for additional information on the security force assistance brigade
     capabilities and employment.)

Chaplain Detachment Charlie

     2-108. The primary mission of the chaplain detachment Charlie is to provide augmentation to a corps
     chaplain section. The chaplain detachment Charlie normally locates with the corps chaplain section, and they
     may operate independently to provide support to religious support tasks and Soldier and leader engagements
     functions. Chaplain detachment Charlies provide unit and denominational religious support during
     multidomain operations and a liaison capability to faith-based nongovernmental organizations. The chaplain
     detachment Charlie provides assets required to enable the corps chaplain section to conduct 24-hour and
     split-based religious support planning, coordination, and execution. (See FM 1-05 for more information on
     religious support operations.)

Theater Liaison Detachment

     2-109. TLDs enable liaison and interoperability. They provide liaison capabilities between Army, joint, and
     multinational headquarters to ensure communications, mutual understanding, and unity of purpose. A TLD
     provides a coalition partner with the interoperability link to integrate U.S. C2 systems and synchronize the
     common operational picture. TLDs are assigned or attached to selected theater armies and further attached
     to the ARFOR for employment in support of corps and division echelons in a JOA. TLDs are normally
     comprised of 30 functional staff experts capable of aiding supported units and ensuring rapid and accurate
     communication between multinational headquarters.

     2-110. A TLD provides representation inside a coalition partner headquarters to assist in clarifying orders,
     interpreting the commander’s intent, and identifying and resolving problems involving plans, policies, and
     procedures. Guidelines of allocation distribute five TLDs per committed theater army for major operations,
     and one TLD per corps and division serving as a JTF headquarters. A corps may receive a TLD when serving

---

Chapter 2

     as a senior tactical formation based on its mission and TLD availability. (See ATP 3-94.1 for additional
     information on the TLD.)

SECTION III — JOINT FORCE CAPABILITIES

     2-111. Joint force capabilities are critical to providing most of the means available to a corps commander in
     setting conditions for division operations. Joint force capabilities allow a corps commander to apply specific
     assets and resources against adversary targets through a combination of activities in the air, space,
     cyberspace, or maritime domains. CCDRs apportion joint force capabilities directly to a corps when it is
     performing its JTF role or indirectly when a corps is the land component or senior tactical formation. The
     capabilities come from a mix of Services and combatant commands including the Air Force, Space Force,
     Navy, Marine Corps, Cyber Command, Special Operations Command, Space Command, and Strategic
     Command. (See JP 1, Volume 2 for more information on the joint force.)

U.S. AIR FORCE

     2-112. The USAF’s contributions to joint operations include a range of missions and capabilities that meet
     DOD mandated Service responsibilities and functions. Missions that directly affect corps operations
     include—

          • Counterair is a mission at the theater level that integrates offensive and defensive operations to
                attain and maintain a desired degree of control of the air and protection by neutralizing or destroying
                enemy aircraft and missiles, both before and after launch (JP 3-01). Suppression of enemy air
                defenses is part of counterair.

          • Air interdiction is conducted to divert, disrupt, delay, or destroy the enemy’s military surface
                capabilities before they can be employed effectively against friendly forces or to otherwise achieve
                objectives that are conducted at such distances from friendly forces that detailed integration of each
                air mission with the fire and movement of friendly forces is not required.

          • Close air support (known as CAS) is air action by aircraft against hostile targets that are near friendly
                forces and that require detailed integration of each air mission with the fire and movement of those
                forces.

          • Special operations to influence the accomplishment of strategic or tactical objectives through either
                overt, covert, or clandestine military actions.

          • Airlift to deploy, employ, and sustain military forces.
          • Reconnaissance and surveillance to collect information from airborne, space-based, and

                surface-based sensors.
          • C2.
          • Combat search and rescue.

     2-113. How the USAF interfaces with a corps depends on the corps’ role. As a tactical headquarters, a corps
     receives a corps tactical air control party (known as TACP). As a JTF or land component command in a
     limited contingency operation, a corps headquarters may not retain the tactical air control party, but instead
     it receives a joint air component coordination element as the joint force air component commander’s
     (JFACC’s) liaison to the JFLCC or JTF. The ability to conduct deliberate and dynamic targeting is a critical
     capability of a corps, regardless of its assigned role. A corps depends on joint fires and maneuver provided
     by fixed-wing aircraft to support its deep, close, and rear operations. This includes the air component
     functions of ISR, CAS, air interdiction, CEMA, and airlift missions.

     2-114. The USAF performs specialized tasks to enhance the execution of its missions. Tasks that most often
     enhance corps operations include—

          • EW to control selected parts of the cyber and electromagnetic spectrum in support of large-scale
                combat operations.

          • Warning, command, control, and communications to provide warning and characterization of an
                actual or impending enemy attack, and the C2 of forces through the sustained ability to communicate
                with those forces.

          • Intelligence to acquire, correlate, analyze, and apply intelligence data to provide essential
                information to the corps commander on when, where, and how to engage and attack enemy forces.

36  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                          The Corps Structure

          • Weather service to provide timely and accurate environmental information to support the decision
                process for employing forces in combat operations.

     2-115. As currently organized, trained, and equipped, USAF air support operations centers (known as
     ASOCs) support divisions. An ASOC resides in the current operations integration cell co-located with the
     FSE and other staff functions to form a joint air-ground integration center (known as JAGIC). Upon
     compliance with the requirements of the theater airspace control plan, a division may request a volume of
     airspace to control from the JFC’s designated airspace control authority. A joint air-ground integration center
     is capable of specific functions that include control of joint fires, airspace control, interdiction coordination,
     friendly force identification, and information collection in division-assigned airspace, but a joint air-ground
     integration center can be positioned to support corps operations as a tactical headquarters. A corps tactical
     air control party would continue its complementary planning and execution functions. The USAF does not
     position ASOCs subordinate to other ASOCs because they are decentralized execution nodes that directly
     coordinate with the air operations center (known as AOC). Positioning an ASOC at the corps echelon creates
     problems with its ability to efficiently control airspace and joint air operations across the corps AO in support
     of the corps and subordinate units. (See ATP 3-91.1 for more information on the joint air-ground integration
     center.)

     2-116. The commander, Air Force forces should consider co-location with the ground element most capable
     of integrating fires and effects and executing airspace control when determining ASOC placement. Placement
     of an ASOC is dependent upon the mission variables of METT-TC (I). Aligning an ASOC at the corps
     echelon to execute airspace management and control responsibilities requires detailed planning and analysis
     of capabilities, personnel, and equipment. This analysis includes systems interoperability, range capabilities,
     and the ability to manage and integrate large numbers of fire support and air missions. Generally, the tasks
     associated with controlling airspace at the corps level will overwhelm the staff and hinder operations at both
     the division and corps echelons. Therefore, maintaining the ASOCs within the divisions is typically the
     optimal arrangement during tactical operations and especially during large-scale combat operations.

U.S. SPACE FORCE

     2-117. The U.S. Space Force contributes to joint operations through the C2 of all DOD satellites and provides
     the force-multiplying effects of continuous global coverage, low vulnerability, and autonomous operations.
     Satellites provide essential in-theater secure communications; weather and navigational data for ground, air,
     and fleet operations; and threat warning. The U.S. Space Force is responsible for organizing, training, and
     equipping Guardians to conduct global space operations that enhance the way joint and multinational forces
     conduct operations while also offering decision makers military options to achieve national objectives.

     2-118. Surface-based and space-based systems monitor ballistic missile launches around the world to guard
     against a surprise missile attack on North America. A global network of ground and space surveillance
     sensors provide vital information on the location of satellites and space debris for the nation and the world.
     Maintaining space superiority is an emerging capability required to protect U.S. space assets from hostile
     attacks. (See JP 3-14 for more information on joint space operations.)

U.S. NAVY

     2-119. The Navy participates in joint operations and conducts a wide range of tasks. DOD-mandated Navy
     directives that most closely affect the corps include—

          • Providing maritime forces (including naval air) to conduct amphibious operations.
          • Providing sea-based AMD.
          • Providing close air support and naval support for land operations.
          • Conducting maritime superiority and sea denial to seize and retain sea lines of communications.
          • Providing sea movement of personnel and cargo through the Military Sealift Command.

     2-120. During planning and execution phases of amphibious operations, C2 facilities afloat are available to
     enhance or complement corps operations. These resources include—

          • Intelligence.
          • EW.
          • Naval gunfire support.

Chapter 2

    • Aerial reconnaissance and photography.
    • Airborne early warning.
    • Air transport.
    • Offensive and defensive air operations beyond CAS.
    • Special operations.

    2-121. The apportionment decision from the JFC provides the basis for naval aviation support to the corps
    during joint land operations. The JFC may apportion excess naval aviation assets to various missions or
    geographic areas to ensure unity of effort in the overall mission. The JFACC plans, coordinates, and tasks
    these sorties.

U.S. MARINE CORPS

    2-122. The USMC conducts operations to fulfill functional areas as mandated by the DOD. Operations that
    may complement or contribute to a corps’ effort include conducting land operations essential to the naval
    campaign and conducting amphibious operations. The USMC is the proponent for amphibious operations.

    2-123. Air and naval gunfire liaison companies support U.S.,    joint, and multinational divisions and
    subordinate elements. This support includes—                    and airborne EW and communications

         • Offensive air support, including CAS, deep air support,
              countermeasures.

         • Anti-air warfare to gain and maintain air superiority.
         • Assault air support.
         • Air reconnaissance.
         • EW.
         • Airspace management within the AO.
         • Special operations.
         • Intelligence.

    2-124. The USMC’s tactical aviation is an organic element of each MAGTF. The MAGTF commander
    retains OPCON of MAGTF assets during joint land operations. Air sorties in excess of MAGTF direct
    support requirements are provided to the JFC for JFACC tasking and to support joint operations and
    requirements or the joint force as a whole. This reapportionment or reallocation of USMC tactical aviation
    sorties occur when the JFC determines they are required for higher priority missions and to ensure unity of
    effort.

U.S. CYBER COMMAND

    2-125. The U.S. Cyber Command is a combatant command, but since it commands most U.S. cyberspace
    forces, it also acts like a Service. A subset of the overall Cyber Mission Force, the Cyber Combat Mission
    Force supports CCDRs with combat mission teams and combat support teams. These teams provide support
    to the corps upon receiving support requests, typically forwarded to the JTF by the JFLCC. Army Cyber
    Command is the Army Service component command for U.S. Cyber Command. (See Chapter 3 for more
    information on cyber support to a corps.)

U.S. SPECIAL OPERATIONS COMMAND

    2-126. SOF are organized, trained, and equipped military and paramilitary forces for special operations to
    achieve diplomatic, information, military, and economic objectives. SOF operate generally in unconventional
    means in hostile, denied, or politically sensitive areas. SOF conduct missions throughout the full range of
    military operations, either independently or with Service, joint, or multinational forces. Special operations
    differ from conventional forces by—

         • Their degree of acceptable strategic, physical, political, and diplomatic risk.
         • Their employment and operational techniques.
         • Their relative independence from larger echelon support systems.
         • Their dynamic interdependence on detailed operational intelligence and indigenous assets.

38                  ATP 3-92                                        15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                          The Corps Structure

     2-127. SOF allow a corps commander to maximize conventional forces’ and SOF’s core competencies and
     therefore optimize the efficiencies and effectiveness of the joint force. This level of interdependence requires
     ongoing integration and interoperability of both SOF and conventional forces communications, weapons,
     ISR systems, and other systems. SOF can operate independently or with conventional forces, performing
     critical, relatively small-unit missions that directly strike or engage the aim or object of their operational
     design. The Army Special Operations Command is the Army Service component command for U.S. Special
     Operations Command.

U.S. SPACE COMMAND

     2-128. U.S. Space Command conducts operations in, from, and to space to deter conflict and, if necessary,
     defeat aggression, deliver space combat power for the joint force, and defend U.S. vital interests with allies
     and partners. The Army Space and Missile Defense Command is the Army Service component command to
     U.S. Space Command.

U.S. STRATEGIC COMMAND

     2-129. The U.S. Strategic Command integrates and coordinates the necessary C2 capability to provide
     support with the most accurate and timely information for the President, the Secretary of Defense, other
     national leaders, and CCDRs. The mission of the U.S. Strategic Command is to deter strategic attack and
     employ forces, as directed, to guarantee the security of the U.S. and its allies. The command enables joint
     force operations, and it is the combatant command responsible for strategic deterrence; nuclear operations;
     nuclear command, control, and communications enterprise operations; joint electromagnetic spectrum
     operations; global strike; missile defense, analysis and targeting; and missile threat assessment. The Army
     Space and Missile Defense Command is the Army Service component command to U.S. Strategic Command.

                                                 Chapter 3

                 Fundamentals of Corps Operations

    This chapter discusses fundamentals of corps operations. The chapter begins with a discussion
    of the challenges to a corps as the senior tactical formation during large-scale combat
    operations followed by command and leadership considerations. Next, the chapter discusses
    the operational framework and considerations for movement and maneuver. This chapter
    concludes with a discussion on other considerations for corps operations.

SECTION I — LARGE-SCALE COMBAT OPERATIONS CHALLENGES

     3-1. Large-scale combat operations present unique challenges for a corps in its primary role of senior Army
     tactical formation. A corps overcomes these challenges through creative tactics and operational approaches,
     employing an operational framework, integrating its echelons to create relative advantage, and synchronizing
     the application of its combat power.

     3-2. A corps and its subordinate formations prepare for tactical-level operations in contested environments
     prior to and during armed conflict, including in the United States. Corps do this in accordance with the tenets
     and imperatives found in FM 3-0. Corps and their subordinate formations must be ready to deploy on short
     notice to austere locations and be capable of immediately conducting combat operations.

DEFEND AGAINST INITIAL ATTACKS

     3-3. Peer enemies will engage in armed conflict when they view that the rewards are worth the risk. They
     will employ combinations of methods to render U.S. military power irrelevant whenever possible and inflict
     unacceptable losses on the U.S., its allies, and its partners.

     3-4. During the initial phases of conflict, corps forces will likely contend with adversaries enjoying combat
     power, geographic, and other advantages. Forward-stationed and first-deploying units require the capabilities
     to defend themselves and continuously collect information on threat activities, as they provide reaction time
     and freedom of maneuver for follow-on forces. These forces may have limited joint support and will likely
     be under attack from enemy long-range fires.

     3-5. Forward-stationed and early-deploying corps elements may defend critical terrain alongside
     multinational forces in support of the theater commander’s response to enemy offensive operations. Some
     forces under corps control may defend joint bases to mitigate the impact of enemy attacks against strategic
     and operational lines of communications. In both cases, Army forces under corps control must be prepared
     to fight while relatively isolated in the early stages of an enemy attack.

DEFEAT ENEMY FORCES

     3-6. Corps and their subordinate divisions are central to the conduct of large-scale combat operations aimed
     at defeating enemy forces of a peer adversary. Defeat is to render a force incapable of achieving its objectives
     (ADP 3-0). Defeat has a temporal component and is seldom permanent; defeating an enemy attack does not
     always prevent a subsequent attack in a different location with other forces, for example. When developing
     an operational approach, corps commanders consider methods to employ a combination of defeat
     mechanisms and stability mechanisms. Defeat mechanisms relate to offensive and defensive operations;
     stability mechanisms relate to stability operations, and security. Both types of mechanisms are relevant to
     consolidating gains in an AO.

     3-7. Corps avoid attacking enemy strengths directly unless they enjoy overwhelming combat power
     advantages. They create their own advantages to defeat enemy forces in detail by sequencing the application
     of combat power to overwhelm portions of the overall enemy force using a series of rapid transitions. Corps
     seek to create cascading dilemmas and defeat the enemy’s operational approach through the employment of
     the units they control and the joint capabilities they are allocated.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  41


---

Chapter 3

     3-8. Corps attack, defend, and consolidate gains to accomplish objectives as part of a larger campaign. Corps
     set conditions for defeating enemy forces by reducing enemy integrated air defense and fire support systems,
     enabling subordinate formations to defeat or destroy enemy forces, and conducting exploitation and pursuit
     to achieve land component objectives. The corps has deeper target sets, operations planned deeper in terms
     of time and geography, and broader sustainment considerations than divisions do.

     3-9. The purpose of corps operations is to direct, sequence and sustain the battles and engagements of its
     subordinate formations linked to the attainment of operational and strategic objectives. Corps look deeper in
     time and space to extend the fight beyond the focus of divisions concentrating on the success of their brigades
     during close combat. The commander decides on the tactics that the corps will use and provides their
     subordinates with a concept of operation and commander’s intent. Then the corps task organizes divisions,
     coordinates for joint capabilities, and positions units in ways that anticipate the requirements of future
     operations. The corps prioritizes resources for its subordinate formations and ensures that the tasks it assigns
     are commensurate with the resources it allocates. The corps sets conditions for the success of its divisions
     and brigades by enabling their freedom of action.

     3-10. Corps operations seek to disrupt the enemy’s ability to synchronize forces, deceive enemy forces about
     the timing and location of the main effort, and delay the employment of enemy reinforcements against the
     main effort. Principally, corps sets conditions for the success of its main effort using Army and joint fires, as
     well as supporting efforts that prevent the enemy from fixing and massing effects against its main effort.
     When planning to set conditions, the corps looks beyond its assigned AO. Enemy formations and capabilities
     in adjacent areas that can influence the corps fight must be accounted for during coordination with friendly
     forces on the flanks and the land component command. Corps coordination with the appropriate headquarters
     is necessary to facilitate rapid adjustments and fire support during operations.

     3-11. Lethality is the capability and capacity to destroy (FM 3-0). Corps achieve and exploit lethality by
     maneuvering divisions and separate brigades into positions of relative advantage where they can employ
     weapon systems and mass effects to destroy enemy forces or place them at risk of destruction.

     3-12. Corps multiply their lethal effects as they execute the corps commander’s plan, altering it flexibly to
     maintain momentum and destroy enemy forces. They do this by employing combinations of capabilities to
     create, accrue, and exploit relative advantages—imposing multiple dilemmas on enemy forces and
     overwhelming their ability to respond effectively through employment of the defeat mechanisms. Doing so
     requires—

          • Integrating joint capabilities across echelons.
          • Employing capabilities in a combined arms approach to create exploitable opportunities.
          • Preserving combat power to maximize endurance and achieve depth.

INTEGRATE JOINT CAPABILITIES ACROSS ECHELONS

     3-13. The corps is the primary tactical echelon focused on integrating joint capabilities to enable operations
     on land and maneuvering land forces to support the objectives of the JFC. A corps creates conditions to do
     this by allocating resources, sequencing division maneuver, and incorporating joint capabilities in support of
     ground operations. A corps consolidates and prioritizes requests for joint capabilities and then apportions its
     allotment to the divisions based on the commander’s intent and concept of the operation.

     3-14. A corps contributes to the joint force by establishing C2 on land, countering air and missile threats,
     controlling key terrain, and defeating components of enemy integrated fires complexes. For example, corps-
     controlled fire support assets may suppress enemy air defense while friendly air operations target enemy
     long-range fires systems. Corps vary the focus of their operations on enemy systems, formations, or
     geographical objectives over time.

     3-15. Corps have organizations which support operations from different domains, such as engineer brigades,
     tactical air control party, E-MIB, space support elements (SSEs), and cyber support elements. These air, land,
     space, and cyberspace elements provide a corps mobility, intelligence, space, and cyber support and fire
     support that must be integrated into staffs, formations, and planning.

42  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                 Fundamentals of Corps Operations

COMBINED ARMS APPROACH

     3-16. Corps operations employ U.S. and multinational ground forces as part of joint combined arms team.
     While divisions and BCTs regularly synchronize and apply Army ground, aviation, and indirect fires
     capabilities in complimentary ways, corps are critical to exploiting joint capabilities from all domains. Corps
     maneuver their divisions in a manner that both supports and is supported by joint operations throughout a
     JOA. It maintains flexibility to exploit windows of opportunity by synchronizing and employing combat
     power from multiple domains at decisive points and times as part of the larger joint team.

     3-17. A corps headquarters is critical to synchronizing joint, cyber, space, or electromagnetic capabilities that
     are not typically found within subordinate formations. Understanding the strengths and dependencies of joint
     capabilities in each domain is fundamental to a multidomain, combined arms approach. For example, corps
     may synchronize the employment of cyberspace, space, or electromagnetic warfare capabilities to isolate
     enemy maneuver forces during close operations, while simultaneously integrating joint and Army long-range
     fires to attack the cohesion of enemy C2, integrated fires commands, and integrated air defense systems.

COMBAT POWER

     3-18. Corps commanders conceptualize capabilities in terms of how to generate and maintain combat power.
     Combat power is the total means of destructive and disruptive force that a military unit/formation can apply
     against an enemy at a given time (JP 3-0). A corps generates, sustains, and employs combat power to
     accomplish objectives during competition, crisis, and armed conflict. Combat power comes from a
     combination of dynamics that include leadership, lethality, information, mobility, and survivability. All
     warfighting functions contribute to generating and applying combat power.

     3-19. Leaders account for the requirement to build and preserve combat power while sustaining people,
     systems, and formations over the time and distance necessary throughout the depth of an AO. Although Army
     forces can respond rapidly to regional and global crisis, the ability to build and project corps combat power
     can take months. As forces deploy, corps employ sustainment and protection capabilities to build and
     preserve combat power.

     3-20. During large-scale combat operations, consumption rates of all classes of supply significantly increase,
     especially Classes III, V, VII, VIII, and IX. Corps planners must take these facts into account as they sequence
     and sustain battles over time. The consumption of precision long-range munitions will need particular
     attention. These challenges will be even greater as corps move further from theater sustainment nodes.

     3-21. A corps and its subordinate echelons align their operations on land with the opportunities created by
     the effects generated by the other service components of the joint force, preserving combat power to
     maximize their ability to exploit opportunities. One way corps preserve combat power is by maintaining
     dispersion to the greatest degree possible. Corps mass combat power and generate effects without
     concentrating forces any more than is necessary. (See FM 3-0 for additional information on combat power
     and the warfighting functions.)

SECTION II — COMMAND AND LEADERSHIP

3-22. Command requires corps commanders to exercise judgment, leverage experience, and use intuition

when leading their units. They provide leadership, delegate authority, allocate resources, and make decisions.

Corps leadership is executed through personal engagement

commanders. A corps commander influences and directs the         person. They add the power of their
corps staff to deliver clear, concise plans in line with the     personal example to maximize the
commander’s intent to support the divisions’ and separate        corps’ tactical effectiveness. They
brigades’ operations.                                            position themselves and their chief

3-23. A corps commander must be able to translate the strategic  assistants where their presence
or operational concept into understandable plans that            makes the greatest difference.

subordinate division commanders can execute. As conditions

develop and change, commanders use operational art to employ joint and Army capabilities as they direct the

tasks for their divisions.
