Chapter 3

OPERATIONS PROCESS

     3-24. Corps commanders synchronize actions and effects through C2 and the operations process. This
     includes how they understand, visualize, and describe their approach; how their corps sustains operations,
     synchronizes timelines, and sets conditions for division operations; moves and maneuvers its forces; and
     generates combat power. Operational environments are dynamic and contain vast amounts of information
     that can overload C2 systems and impede decision making. Corps commanders simplify information
     collection and decision making by focusing on how they see their corps, how they see enemy forces, how the
     enemy forces see themselves, and how the enemy see corps forces. This aids the corps commander with
     understanding the totality of the operational environment.

     3-25. Commanders and their staffs apply operational art throughout all phases of the operations process. As
     part of the operations process, Army leaders use different methodologies to understand and weigh options.
     Applying operational art and science is a key characteristic differentiating corps from divisions and lower
     echelons. Regardless of the role a corps is fulfilling, it operates at a level where the skillful application of
     operational art—and the science that informs it—is essential in accomplishing its assigned objectives.

     3-26. Operational art and science are interrelated. A corps cannot effectively generate sufficient combat
     power without them. The art of conceptualizing effective operations includes knowing capabilities and
     limitations of the force thoroughly and continually. The means for conducting warfare are the warfighting
     functions and combat power. Corps commanders, supported by their staffs, direct the collection of
     information and allocate capabilities to do so. They use operational and mission variables, intelligence
     preparation of the operational environment, and running estimates to help understand how capabilities in
     each domain affect operations on land and vice versa. Corps commanders must be comfortable with a certain
     degree of uncertainty and ambiguity within an operational environment—delaying action while seeking
     perfect understanding may prevent a unit from exploiting a fleeting opportunity or make it vulnerable to
     enemy attack.

COMMANDERS’ VISUALIZATION

     3-27. The commander’s visualization is the mental process of developing situational understanding,
     determining a desired end state, and envisioning an operational approach by which the force will achieve that
     end state (ADP 6-0). Corps commanders provide their visualization to their staff sections; the staff sections
     then start planning to develop force projection and arrangement of activities and forces in time, space, and
     purpose. Corps commanders should share their visualization with subordinate division and enabling brigade
     commanders early in the process. This helps the corps assesses its capabilities and the capabilities of its
     subordinate divisions and brigades to determine where the main and supporting efforts should be and how
     the corps can support the theater army or combatant command objectives.

             Note: While analysis of all domains and their impact on operations informs the development of
             the commander’s visualization, it is centered on a thorough analysis of the geography within a
             theater of operations and the terrain within the corps’ assigned area. Corps are land combat
             formations and the impacts of terrain on potential operations must be understood before
             incorporating the other domains.

     3-28. A corps allocates combat power to accomplish assigned missions. In determining the allocation of
     combat power to the divisions, a corps commander visualizes how each division can achieve assigned
     objectives within its AO. The corps commander’s visualization of the employment of combat power is
     essential to the planned allocation. The actual disposition of the divisions and their attachments and the
     planned employment of other support allocated to the divisions are the division commander’s responsibilities.
     However, the corps commander’s visualization and operational approach set the conditions for the success
     of those subordinate formations.

     3-29. Accounting for combat power—and the commander’s visualization for how to employ it—determines
     how a corps operates and its success. Visualization and the development of an operational approach allows
     a corps to close with and destroy enemy forces, defeat enemy formations, seize critical terrain, and control
     populations and resources. Finally, the commander’s visualization identifies decisions the corps commander

44  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                           Fundamentals of Corps Operations

     expects to make and focuses their staffs’ running estimates. (See ADP 6-0 for additional information on
     commander’s visualization.)

OPERATIONAL APPROACH

     3-30. It is through the elements of operational art that corps commanders develop and translate their
     operational approach into courses of action. An operational approach is the result of the commander’s
     visualization of what needs to be done in broad terms to solve a military problem. A corps commander
     considers all these elements (friendly and enemy centers of gravity, end state and conditions, lines of
     operation or effort, tempo, risk, basing, phasing and transitions, operational reach, and culmination) to
     understand the operational environment and determine courses of action. (See ADP 5-0 for an overview of
     planning and operational approach.)

     3-31. The operational approach is the main idea that informs detailed planning. When describing an
     operational approach, commanders—

          • Consider ways to defeat enemy forces in detail and potential decisive points.
          • Employ combinations of defeat mechanisms to isolate and defeat enemy forces, functions, and

                capabilities.
          • Assess options for assuming risk.

     3-32. Armed conflict requires the need to defeat enemy forces. When used as a desirable outcome for
     operations, defeat provides maximum flexibility to the commander in how to accomplish the mission. While
     defeat as a purpose may appropriately be given to a corps, it is too vague for tactical echelons below corps
     level, where more specific outcomes are necessary to ensure the overall intent of operations is met. During
     large-scale combat operations, peer threats, by definition, possess a scale and quality of warfighting capability
     that is too extensive to attack all at once. Therefore, commanders identify weaknesses in enemy formations
     and dispositions that provide opportunities to defeat them in detail.

     3-33. Defeating in detail requires evaluation of enemy forces in the context of all the relevant domains and
     dimensions of an OE. Corps commanders must understand the various parts of an enemy force and its
     vulnerabilities and then discern the best ways to apply combat power against those vulnerabilities. By
     comparing enemy weaknesses to friendly advantages, leaders begin to see opportunities and formulate
     options. Sometimes enemy vulnerabilities and friendly advantages intersect at a decisive place and time in a
     way that can be exploited to achieve objectives. Decisive points help commanders select clear, conclusive,
     attainable objectives that directly contribute to achieving an end state through convergence and other means.

     3-34. Defeat and stability mechanisms help corps commanders visualize how to employ their forces. A defeat
     mechanism is a method through which friendly forces accomplish their mission against enemy opposition
     (ADP 3-0). Therefore, defeat mechanisms are most relevant in combat operations against enemy forces. The
     four defeat mechanisms are destroy, dislocate, disintegrate, and isolate. Like stability mechanisms,
     combining them produces complementary and reinforcing effects not attainable through a single mechanism.

     3-35. A stability mechanism is the primary method through which friendly forces affect civilians in order to
     attain conditions that support establishing a lasting, stable peace (ADP 3-0). The four stability mechanisms
     are:

          • Compel,
          • Control,
          • Influence.
          • Support.

     Stability mechanisms are rarely used in isolation from each other. Combining them produces complementary
     and reinforcing effects helping to shape an OE more effectively and efficiently.

     3-36. All operations combine stability and defeat mechanisms to a greater or lesser degree. The corps
     commander and staff sections translate these mechanisms and desired effects into discreet tasks for
     subordinates arranged in time, space, and purpose to achieve the desired end state and objectives. During
     combat operations, most tasks focus on defeating enemy forces through an AO while initial stability tasks
     will be focused on the minimum-essential tasks and limited stability operations to set conditions for future
     operations as combat ends. Corps commanders and staffs do this by planning for the current operation while
     fully developing branches and sequels to their operations.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  45


---

Chapter 3

RISK

             It is my experience that bold decisions give the best promise of success. But one must
             differentiate between strategical or tactical boldness and a military gamble. A bold
             operation is one in which success is not a certainty but which in case of failure leaves one
             with sufficient forces in hand to cope with whatever situation may arise. A gamble, on the
             other hand, is an operation which can lead either to victory or to the complete destruction
             of one’s force.

                                                                                                    Field Marshal Erwin Rommel

     3-37. Commanders delegate risk acceptance to subordinate division and brigade commanders through their
     commander’s intent, orders, and commanders’ dialogue. Based on this guidance, refined as necessary by the
     commander and staff as the situation changes, leaders accept risk during operations by directing a single main
     effort at each echelon, creating exploitable opportunities by assuming economy of force measures in other
     areas. Weighting the main effort division may require task organizing it with capabilities taken from other
     divisions, providing it with priority of fires and other measures. The supporting efforts will be inherently less
     capable as a result, which means they cannot be assigned the same kinds of tasks as before. Assuming risk at
     corps level must be informed by a realistic appraisal of enemy capabilities and the mission variables.

     3-38. The corps commander must also consider the impact of the corps’ actions on higher echelons.
     Opportunities to exploit a relative advantage are fleeting during operations against a capable enemy that
     adapts quickly. Delaying action while setting optimal conditions, waiting for perfect intelligence, or
     achieving greater synchronization may end up posing a greater danger than swift acceptance of significant
     risk now. Shifting main efforts during execution is less risky than adhering to a plan that is not working.

     3-39. Tactical risks are those opportunities arising from planned events, anticipated events, unforeseen events
     and chance which, if taken, win a battle at hand. Contingency planning, the use of a reserve, and economy-
     of-force operations are three examples of how commanders accept and account for tactical risk while
     maintaining flexibility of thought and action. Taking risk must be seen in relationship to an advantage for the
     unit. The stakes involved in a particular conflict will influence what levels of tactical (and operational) risk
     are acceptable.

SECTION III — OPERATIONAL FRAMEWORK

     3-40. Corps commanders and staffs use the operational framework to visualize and describe the application
     of combat power in time, space, purpose, and resources to achieve their goals and objectives. The operational
     framework also provides the structure for integrating a corps’ primary function into the operations process.
     The framework illustrates the relative physical relationships between deep, close, and rear operations on land.
     As a visualization tool, the operational framework bridges the gap between a unit’s conceptual understanding
     of the environment and its need to generate detailed orders that direct operations.

     3-41. Corps commanders build their operational framework on their assessment of an OE, including all
     domains and dimensions. They may create new models to fit the circumstances, but they generally apply a
     combination of common models according to doctrine. The three models commonly used to build an
     operational framework are—

          • Assigned areas.
          • Deep, close, and rear operations.
          • Main effort, supporting effort, and reserve.

             Note. Commanders may use any operational framework model they find useful, but they must
             remain synchronized with their higher echelon headquarters’ operational framework.

ASSIGNED AREAS

     3-42. Once the JFC or LCC assigns a corps an AO, the corps commander subdivides that AO into subordinate
     assigned areas to best support the desired scheme of maneuver. The commander typically assigns areas to

46  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                              Fundamentals of Corps Operations

subordinates based on the mission variables. An assigned area that is too large for a unit to effectively control
or exceeds a unit’s area of influence increases risk, allows sanctuaries for enemy forces, and limits flexibility.
An assigned area that is too small constrains maneuver, limits opportunities for dispersion, and creates
congested lines of communication.

3-43. Most operations involve a combination of contiguous and noncontiguous assigned areas. Large areas
with small forces typically conduct noncontiguous operations which place greater demands on C2 and
sustainment. Commanders designate targeting priorities, effects, and timing within their assigned areas to
facilitate integration and synchronization. Within their assigned area, units use control measures to assign
responsibilities, prevent fratricide, facilitate C2, coordinate fires, control maneuver, and organize operations.

3-44. A corps commonly uses an AO, a zone, or a sector when assigning areas to subordinate formations.
Corps commanders designate the types of assigned areas for their subordinate formations based on the
primary mission, level of control required, and mutual support considerations. Commanders retain
responsibility for any area not assigned to a subordinate unit. (See FM 3-0 and FM 3-94 for further discussion
on assigned areas and figure 3-1 for the depths and frontages of AOs in an operational framework.)

                      Figure 3-1. Depths and Frontages of an Operational Framework

CONTIGUOUS AND NONCONTIGUOUS AREAS OF OPERATIONS

     3-45. The framework depicted in figure 3-1 portrays only one formation at each echelon. AOs can be either
     contiguous or noncontiguous, depending upon the situation. and they can display areas of influence and areas
     of interest. Figure 3-2 illustrates what a noncontiguous AO could look like for a corps.

     3-46. Contiguous AOs usually mean that divisions are in mutually supporting range of each other while
     avoiding gaps and seams in responsibility. Contiguous AOs may also provide additional security for
     headquarters, maneuver units, and support units. However, contiguous division AOs can be deceptive, since
     the forces within those division AOs are seldom themselves contiguous. The probability that an entire
     division is within mutually supporting range of another decreases as the size of their respective AOs increase.

     3-47. In noncontiguous AOs, subordinate divisions are not mutually supporting to each other, and there are
     gaps between their boundaries. Gaps between divisions and unassigned areas in the corps AO are the corps’

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  47


---

Chapter 3

     responsibility, and they can present added risks to an operation. This happens most often because geography
     prevents contiguous movement or positioning. (See FM 3-0 and FM 3-90 for more information on contiguous
     and noncontiguous AOs.)

     3-48. An area of influence is an area inclusive of and extending beyond an operational area wherein a
     commander is capable of direct influence by maneuver, fire support, and information normally under the
     commander’s command or control (JP 3-0). This area is bounded by the ranges of the commander’s assigned
     maneuver and fire support units. It is not dependent upon those capabilities that can be requested from higher
     echelons but are not assigned to the formation. A commander’s area of influence is normally larger than that
     commander’s assigned AO, but it is smaller than that commander’s area of interest.

     3-49. An area of interest is that area of concern to the commander, including the area of influence, areas
     adjacent to it, and extending into enemy territory (JP 3-0). This area also includes areas occupied by enemy
     forces that could jeopardize the accomplishment of the mission. An area of interest will vary in size and shape
     from operation to operation. It should include all enemy activities that might affect a friendly force throughout
     the duration of an operation. An area of interest will usually surround an AO, extending forward and to the
     flanks of the AO. Depending upon the operation, it may also extend rearwards. During offensive operations,
     portions of the area of interest may become part of an AO as friendly forces advance. (See FM 3-94 for more
     information on areas of interest.)

     3-50. Commanders generally consider noncontiguous, or the combination of both contiguous and
     noncontiguous AOs based on these factors: insufficient forces to cover the entire AO, available avenues of
     advance, terrain, or time constraints for completing the mission. The reasons for using a noncontiguous area
     vary, but they begin with geographic separation of important terrain from the remainder of the AO. A
     commander’s decision to deploy part of a force in noncontiguous AOs is derived from the commander’s
     analysis of operational and mission variables. In particular, the commander evaluates threat forces and their
     ability to mass sufficient combat power to threaten friendly forces. Second, the commander evaluates the
     ability of threat forces to sever lines of communication between the noncontiguous AOs. Finally, the
     commander assesses the risks of having isolated forces defeated in detail against what may be gained in
     mission accomplishment.

     3-51. While a commander can choose to use noncontiguous AOs based upon specific operational variables,
     the most obvious AOR requiring the use of noncontiguous areas of operations is the U.S. Indo-Pacific
     Command AOR. The geography and distances involved between island chains force a noncontiguous
     approach to land combat that relies heavily on joint capabilities. (See figure 3-2 for a depiction of a corps
     AO with noncontiguous divisions.)

48  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                           Fundamentals of Corps Operations

                               Figure 3-2. Corps AO with Noncontiguous Divisions

MUTUAL SUPPORT

     3-52. Commanders and staffs consider mutual support when considering how large an area to assign
     subordinates, when task organizing forces, and position units. Mutual support is that support which units
     render each other throughout joint operations, because of their assigned tasks, their position relative to each
     other and to the enemy, or their inherent capabilities (JP 3-31). In Army doctrine, mutual support is a planning
     consideration related to force disposition, not a command relationship. Mutual support has two aspects:
     supporting range and supporting distance.

     3-53. When two units are mutually supporting, their assigned areas are generally contiguous with each other.
     Units with non-contiguous areas are generally not mutually supporting. Supporting range is the distance one
     unit may be geographically separated from a second unit yet remain within the maximum range of the second
     unit’s weapons systems (ADP 3-0). Supporting range depends on available weapons systems, and it is
     normally the maximum range of the supporting unit’s indirect fire weapons, although certain capabilities
     employed via space and cyberspace can be used at much longer ranges. Terrain, visibility, and weather may
     limit the supporting range. If one unit cannot effectively or safely fire in support of another, the first may not
     be in supporting range, even though its weapons have the required range.

     3-54. Supporting distance is the distance between two units that can be traveled in time for one to come to
     the aid of the other and prevent its defeat by an enemy or ensure that it regains control of a civil situation
     (ADP 3-0). Supporting distances are influenced by the mobility of friendly formations, their assigned
     missions, the terrain, and the enemy situation.

     3-55. Communications are also a consideration when determining whether units are mutually supporting. If
     two units cannot effectively coordinate the use of indirect fire, then they may not be considered in supporting
     range of each other. Units are not within supporting distance regardless of their proximity to each other if
     they cannot communicate or are fixed by enemy forces. Communications should be rehearsed, especially
     when a partner division or brigade is part of the corps.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  49


---

Chapter 3

     3-56. Mutual support provides flexibility and options for the corps commander. In the offense, the corps
     formation can become over extended or compacted as movement progresses. Maintaining mutual support
     between divisions and separate maneuver brigades during maneuver requires the attention of corps staff and
     commanders at all levels. When units can support each other, commanders have more options to combine
     capabilities across all warfighting functions and multiple domains. A high degree of mutual support reduces
     the vulnerability of corps forces to defeat in detail. When the corps must operate highly distributed, the
     composition and positioning of the corps reserve is a critical consideration. The commander and staff task
     organize the reserve with the necessary mobility assets and then position it where it can maneuver in support
     of multiple formations if necessary.

ASSIGNED AREAS IN A MARITIME ENVIRONMENT

     3-57. Planning and training for the unique considerations of operations in maritime environments is critical,
     as is an integrated planning approach with the rest of the joint force. A maritime OE adds coordination and
     synchronization requirements for corps. Army and joint force planning must reflect an understanding of the
     dynamic nature of the threats and constraints to land forces in maritime regions. Corps movement and
     maneuver between land masses is almost entirely dependent on joint capabilities, and divisions within a corps
     may be hundreds of miles or further apart.

     3-58. Land forces play a critical role in littoral regions and along coastlines where they can seize and retain
     key terrain, as well as project fires to deny maritime maneuver. Control of critical land masses is essential to
     the sustainment and protection of joint forces in a maritime OE. This interdependence has been historically
     crucial to success during armed conflicts against peer threats in maritime theaters, and it continues to be
     crucial in the foreseeable future.

     3-59. Corps have the requirement to protect and augment the security of the other Services, which is critical
     in maritime theaters because of the relative isolation of the forces they position forward and the exposure of
     those forces to threat capabilities that could be employed with few indications or warnings. The following
     tasks directly support and are critical to the success of joint operations in a maritime environment:

          • Seize and secure key terrain.
          • AMD against manned and unmanned systems.
          • Littoral defense and seizure.
          • Area security (base and base cluster defense).
          • CBRN defense.
          • EOD support.
          • Detainee operations (detainee movement coordination)
          • General, combat, and geospatial engineering support.
          • Oversee the employment of Army watercraft operations.

DEEP, CLOSE, AND REAR OPERATIONS

     3-60. Corps commanders organize their operations in terms of time, space, and purpose by synchronizing
     deep, close, and rear operations. The degree to which a corps can set conditions for its subordinate divisions
     depends on its ability to synchronize close, deep, and rear operations among its subordinate echelons and
     with the joint force. Synchronization is the arrangement of military activities in time, space, and purpose to
     produce maximum relative combat power at a decisive place and time (JP 2-0).

     3-61. The deep area is where the commander sets conditions for future success in close combat (ADP 3-0).
     The close area is the portion of the commander’s AO where most subordinate maneuver forces conduct close
     combat (ADP 3-0). The rear area is that area within a unit’s AO extending forward from its rear boundary to
     the rear boundary of the area assigned to the next lower level of command. It is an area where most forces
     and assets are located that support and sustain close and deep operations. The support area is the portion of
     the commander’s AO designated to facilitate the positioning, employment, and protection of base
     sustainment assets required to sustain, enable, and control operations (ADP 3-0).

     3-62. Within these areas, corps conduct deep, close, and rear operations. Deep operations are tactical actions
     against enemy forces, typically out of direct contact with friendly forces, intended to shape future close
     operations and protect rear operations (FM 3-0). Close operations are tactical actions of subordinate
     maneuver forces and the forces providing immediate support to them, whose purpose is to employ maneuver

50         ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                           Fundamentals of Corps Operations

     and fires to close with and destroy enemy forces (FM 3-0). Rear operations are tactical actions behind major
     subordinate maneuver forces that facilitate movement, extend operational reach, and maintain desired tempo
     (FM 3-0). See Chapter 4 for an in-depth discussion on corps operations throughout the operational
     framework.

MAIN EFFORT, SUPPORTING EFFORT, AND RESERVE

     3-63. One way that commanders set priorities is by organizing operations by phases, modifying task
     organization, and designating the main effort, supporting effort, and reserve. Commanders designate a
     subordinate unit as a main effort when its mission at a given point in time is most critical to overall mission
     success. Commanders weight the main effort with additional combat power. Typically, commanders shift the
     main effort one or more times during execution.

     3-64. When commanders designate a unit as the main effort, it receives priority of support and resources to
     maximize combat power. Commanders establish clear priorities of support, and they shift resources and
     priorities to the main effort as circumstances and the commander’s intent require. The decision point to shift
     resources and priorities to a main effort division should occur before the decision point to shift the main effort
     occurs so that it is prepared for its assigned tasks.

     3-65. A corps task-organizes and echelons its forces based on the mission, concept of operations, available
     transportation assets, and other resources. Task organization is the process of forming combined arms task
     forces weighted with specific capabilities to achieve particular types of tasks. Corps typically task organize
     their divisions and separate brigades, but not the units inside those formations. Commanders resource
     supporting efforts with the minimum assets necessary to accomplish their assigned tasks, with the
     understanding that the success of the main effort often comes from the success of supporting efforts. A main
     effort in an earlier phase can be a supporting effort for a different main effort in a later phase.

     3-66. A proper task organization and command relationship provides the corps commander with flexibility
     and enhances synchronization efforts. Commanders retain flexibility by constituting a reserve and base the
     size of the reserve on the level of uncertainty in the current tactical situation. In determining the allocation of
     combat power to the reserve, the corps commander considers the mission of the reserve in relation to its
     scheme of maneuver. The appropriate allocation of combat power among corps units establishes a balance
     that enables a corps commander to retain the freedom of action to influence the conduct of the battle.

     3-67. As priorities shift through an operation, a corps uses brigade-size units as the basic building blocks for
     task-organizing its units. Division commanders can expect to receive a portion of the corps’ assets to
     accomplish their missions, depending on the division’s role and the corps commander’s intent. A corps can
     task organize divisions with additional maneuver units, AMD assets, CBRN assets, engineer assets, fire
     support assets, military police assets, signal assets, EOD assets, force health protection assets, civil affairs,
     and sustainment assets. A corps can also pull those same assets from one or more divisions for employment
     by another.

SECTION IV — CORPS MOVEMENT AND FORCE PROJECTION

     3-68. Corps-sized movements and deployments, which are only successful when anticipated, planned, and
     disciplined, demand command involvement at all echelons. The ability of corps commanders to posture their
     forces for large-scale combat operations depends upon their ability to move those forces. Successful corps
     operations depend upon the ability of the corps to project force, complete RSOI, and reconstitute their forces
     throughout the duration of an operation.

MOVEMENT OF LARGE UNITS

     3-69. The primary consideration of movements is to ensure that forces arrive at the proper place, at the proper
     time, in the correct formation, and prepared to accomplish their mission. As a result, the mobility of
     subordinate formations is a major concern for corps planners. Assured mobility is a framework—of processes,
     actions, and capabilities—that assures the ability of a force to deploy, move, and maneuver where and when
     desired (ATP 3-90.4). Additionally, the quality of roads, terrain, and transportation infrastructure shape how
     quickly a corps can move formations forwards, rearwards, and laterally inside its AO. Detailed planning that
     accounts for route use, synchronization, maintenance, refueling, and staging points is critical. Corps engineer,

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  51


---

Chapter 3

     military police, and sustainment forces are indispensable to making the corps mobile. They enable the corps
     to clear, secure, and maintain routes, control the movement of subordinate units, and resupply units transiting
     an AO. (See Chapter 6 for more information on mobility.)

     3-70. Operational movement and maneuver are the dispositions of joint or multinational forces to create an
     operational impact on the conduct of a campaign or major operation to achieve operational results. The land
     component headquarters coordinates operational level movements with the theater sustainment command
     (known as TSC), which in turn coordinates with whatever military or civilian entities control the routes to
     the corps’ assigned area.

     3-71. Corps direct the movement and maneuver of their subordinate divisions and other organizations.
     Movements can either be tactical or nontactical. The ability to move corps and their divisions during
     large-scale combat operations often determines the success or failure of operations. For example, the
     movement of VII Corps and XVIII Airborne Corps at the beginning of OPERATION DESERT STORM are
     landmarks in the history of large-unit movements. Such short notice repositioning of large forces to obtain a
     positional advantage and react to emergencies characterized mobile combat operations of the past and will
     characterize operations of the future.

STRATEGIC AND OPERATIONAL MOVEMENT

     3-72. Movement is generally broken down into two subsets. The first is strategic movement of forces and
     capabilities from strategic distances or intertheater movement. Intertheater movements involve the movement
     of forces from outside of an AOR into an AO to conduct operations. Planners consider intertheater
     movements when planning force projection and force tailoring in setting the theater. The second is
     operational movement or intratheater movement. Planners consider intratheater movement for positioning or
     repositioning and shifting forces to conduct tactical actions or operations inside an AO. The movement of
     corps and divisions over operational distances requires considerable planning and careful control during
     execution. The corps staff section coordination with movement control elements and staffs are crucial in
     shaping corps intratheater operational movement.

     3-73. Successful corps operations require the ability to move multiple division-sized elements into and
     through an AO. The subsequent maneuver of those divisions into close combat with enemy forces determines
     the outcome of an operation. Principally, a corps focuses on positioning its assigned forces in advance of
     operations to facilitate movement that supports several potential courses of action to gain an advantage over
     an enemy force. The corps headquarters develops schemes of maneuver that provides its divisions flexibility
     in close operations, accounting for movement times across variable terrain and axes of advance. The corps
     also provides the logistical and mobility support necessary to enable the necessary movements and overcome
     likely friction along the various routes.

     3-74. Positioning corps so that they can effectively maneuver requires sustained movement—often over
     extended distances—prior to the engagement of forces. This places a premium on developing and maintaining
     lines of communication and alternate movement routes, sustainment, and detailed planning to deconflict
     movements. Corps do not need to solve maneuver problems for their divisions, but rather corps ensure that
     divisions and BCTs are positioned to maneuver with the necessary support.

     3-75. The commitment of multiple corps with their divisions and supporting formations into AOs with
     limited road infrastructure makes successful integration of movement with maneuver over time and space an
     operational imperative. Successful planning accounts for the movement of field artillery, air defense artillery,
     aviation support, engineers, intelligence, and sustainment elements to ensure uninterrupted support to the
     commander’s scheme of maneuver. Limited and dispersed mobility corridors place a premium on a corps’
     abilities to synchronize the movement of large units and sustain them, things that must be trained outside of
     simulation to achieve proficiency.

     3-76. Operational movement establishes basic dispositions for forces and limits the range of possible
     subsequent movements and maneuver. At the operational level, movement involves establishing a base of
     operations and distributing forces along lines of operations to facilitate their future commitment. The
     availability of lines of communication (LOCs) exerts a dominant influence on the movement and operation
     of corps and divisions.

52  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                    Fundamentals of Corps Operations

MANEUVER

     3-77. Corps maneuver involves the movement of large ground forces in conjunction with fire support, both
     Army and joint. It is how the commander sets the terms of battle or acts to take advantage of tactical actions.
     While initial deployment is important, a corps commander postures for subsequent operations based upon an
     understanding of the higher echelon commander’s intent and changing enemy dispositions. Displacing
     divisions and high-demand functional and multifunctional brigades requires significant effort. Planners need
     time to effectively position units for subsequent operations.

     3-78. Corps-level maneuver demands anticipation, careful planning, and the synchronization of sustainment
     with operations. Corps sustainment sets conditions for maneuver and depends on preparation well before the
     movement or maneuver occurs. A corps commander considers the operational reach of the corps in terms of
     logistics capacity, distribution, evacuation, treatment of casualties, and personnel services. A corps
     commander and staff continually—

          • Assess the sustainment posture of their formation.
          • Position and protect logistics and medical units.
          • Establish priorities of support.
          • Secure and maintain lines of communications with Army and host-nation forces.
          • Manage movement of subordinate forces and coordinate across the formation for movement of

                sustainment units.

SUSTAINMENT DURING MOVEMENT AND MANEUVER

     3-79. The corps G-3 approves the selection of main supply routes (MSRs) and alternate supply routes (ASRs)
     to ensure maneuver and other operational issues are considered before detailed route synchronization
     planning. The corps G-4 and CSC facilitate the movement of divisions and separate brigades on the MSR
     and ASRs, synchronized in time and space with maneuver, fire support, and protection considerations. The
     corps G-3 coordinates with the G-4 and CSC on movement plan development and sequencing unit movement.
     This includes who goes first and how the CSC and G-3 collaborate to plan. To facilitate route
     synchronization, the G-4 and expeditionary sustainment command (ESC) (in collaboration with the corps G-
     3) develop and propose a corps movement control schedule. This schedule accounts for checkpoints, pass
     times, restrictions, and alternate routes. Movement schedules should assume friction.

     3-80. The corps G-4 and the corps ESC employ transportation capabilities to enable movement and
     maneuver. Sustainment planners consider various modalities to support movement, including road, air, rail,
     and water movement, and how to conduct movement control during large-scale combat operations.
     Movement control is critical because it facilitates the transition between unit movements and the maneuver
     of forces to their operational objectives. The G-4 and CSC establish movement control schedules and route
     movements according to priorities established by the corps in the corps close area (including division close
     and rear areas). The corps transportation officer (CTO) is essential to linking movement control at the corps
     with movement control at the division.

     3-81. The CTO is the primary technical advisor to the commander on transportation policy, modal operations,
     movement planning, in-transit visibility, and transportation automation systems. The CTO facilitates corps
     operational maneuver with reception and onward movement, retrograde, and replacement operations.
     Additionally, the CTO coordinates with the G-3 for maneuver planning, force tracking, and unit movements.
     A corps on the move can consist of 42,000 vehicles. The G-3’s planned operations and movements may
     require rapidly projecting forces over significant distances on MSRs. The G-3 and G-4, with the consultation
     of the CTO, establish priorities for MSRs and implementation of sustainment priorities. (See ATP 4-16 and
     ATP 4-92 for additional information on sustainment considerations.)

     3-82. Prior to crossing the line of departure, the corps G-4 assesses the committed divisions’ logistics needs,
     including consumption of Class 3 supplies (including petroleum and bulk items) from their last resupply
     point to the next supply point. The G-3 and G-4 identify critical points where restrictions could slow down
     or stop movement (including bridges, tunnels, checkpoints, border crossings, steep inclines and declines, and
     MSR and ASR geospatial capabilities) along with congested areas or choke points. These factors are
     considered and discussed during the corps movement control boards.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  53


---

Chapter 3

     3-83. In the corps rear area, the CSC or MEB commander regulates movement control on the corps MSR,
     ASRs, and connecting networks. The corps G-3 (in conjunction with the G-2, corps engineer section,
     protection cell, and CTO) determines and publishes MSR and ASR status. Corps typically use the technique
     of designating MSRs and ASRs with a color-coded status of green, amber, red, or black. (See ATP 5-0.2-1
     for more information on designating MSR and ASR status.) Commanders and corps staff sections consider
     the placement of ambulance control points, refuel and rearm locations, and combat and field trains in the
     context of synchronizing BCT and division sustainment in AOs and timing between operations.

SUSTAINMENT SYNCHRONIZATION

     3-84. The CTO, G-3, G-4, and CSC participate in movement control boards as part of the routine battle
     rhythm. The board’s members possess essential knowledge, based on their positions on the staff and their
     specific running estimates, which helps to broaden the situational understanding of the board regarding corps
     movement requirements. The board uses that understanding to make decisions that program movements
     according to the commander’s priorities and allocate the necessary assets based on current transportation
     capabilities. The movement control board provides the corps current operations and future operations sections
     with an opportunity to assess maneuver timelines against sustainment capabilities and develop a list of
     shortfalls. The corps G-4 and ESC coordinate with the theater army and the theater sustainment command to
     mitigate these gaps and make recommendations to the corps commander.

     3-85. Movement control boards include threats and trend analysis; validated transportation movements 24,
     48, 72, and 96 hours prior to an operation; convoy security and escort requirements and protection support;
     current and projected route and weather status; and retrograde and backhaul requirements. The movement
     control board is updated on the readiness status of the transportation capabilities (including equipment
     availability by type and licensed and trained operators). The CTO, CSC, G-2, G-3, G-5, MEB representative,
     and division transportation officer all participate in the theater movement control board (and the corps
     movement control board) to synchronize and deconflict sustainment timelines and support. (See ATP 4-92
     for more information on sustainment synchronization and boards.)

     3-86. At the tactical-level, sustainment remains the key to freedom of action. Properly integrated, the support
     provided by sustainment units allows corps formations to maneuver, concentrate their capabilities, and
     quickly adjust their task organizations. If poorly coordinated, sustainment becomes an unintended contributor
     to culmination. Corps staff sections integrate their sustainment units’ requirements into their concept of
     operations. This includes careful selection and security of support areas in their AOs and planning for their
     displacement based upon adjustments to the tactical AO, particularly during offensive operations. This
     displacement planning is based on movement, general engineering, security, and protection capabilities. The
     displacement of the support area normally entails more time and effort than shifting a subordinate formation.

FORCE PROJECTION AND INTEGRATION

     3-87. Force projection is the ability to project the military instrument of national power from the United
     States or another theater in response to requirements for military operations (JP 3-0). A corps typically
     deploys from the continental United States or forward locations to conduct operations requiring the C2 of
     multiple divisions.

     3-88. Corps deployments will be challenged by threat forces. Peer threats possess the capability and capacity
     to observe, disrupt, delay, and attack U.S. forces at any stage of force projection, including while still
     positioned at home stations in the United States and overseas. Commanders and staffs must therefore plan
     and execute deployments with the assumption that friendly forces are always under observation and in
     contact.

     3-89. Corps have an important role in planning and preparing for deployment operations. Based on the JFC’s
     guidance for establishing operational capabilities during the initial phases of force-projection operations, the
     corps commander recommends deployment priorities for subordinate units. Subsequently, the JFC, with the
     theater commander, develops required delivery dates from which to adjust or develop the time-phased force
     deployment list. The time-phased force deployment list includes assigned and supporting forces (including
     USAF airlift control elements and Army terminal operations units) that are to deploy to the operational area.
     The time-phased force deployment list establishes the joint force’s lift priorities.

54  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                              Fundamentals of Corps Operations

3-90. The final approved time-phased force deployment list becomes the basis for the corps’ development of
its marshaling and deployment schedules. During this stage of force-projection operations, the commander
might establish an intermediate staging base to pre-position communications and logistics assets, based on
the mission variables of METT-TC (I). The decision to establish an intermediate staging base may negate
strategic surprise because of global news organizations. Loss of strategic surprise does not necessarily mean
loss of the tactical surprise. Tactical surprise is particularly important during opposed-entry operations.

3-91. When the threat environment permits, engineer construction units may be required to prepare aerial
ports of debarkation, seaports of debarkation, and intermediate staging bases for operations. Construction
units also prepare for operations by training with potential host-nation engineer and sustainment units,
directing contract construction, and conducting home-station construction oversight. The corps engineer staff
is responsible for engineer-related requests for forces, coordinating engineer capabilities, establishing
engineer work lines, and producing the scheme of engineer operations supporting the corps. The corps
engineer staff ensures engineer units assigned are resourced. RSOI for task-organized engineers is
coordinated through the corps.

3-92. Requirements are critical to decisions about task organization. The corps staff determines if joint
capabilities or enablers are required, and it makes recommendations for their employment. Strategic lift
assets, host-nation capabilities, intermediate support bases and assets, and infrastructure requirements
influence corps decisions on the priority and timing of unit movements. Corps force projection cannot solely
consider maneuver forces. Sustainment and other enabling capabilities are essential to successful force
projection and often must be in position early during a deployment. The corps commander can then
task-organize in accordance with METT-TC (I).

3-93. The operational movement portion of power projection has three components: strategic deployments
into a JOA; conducting intratheater deployment and redeployment of forces; and the conduct of RSOI. RSOI
are primarily logistics functions. The integration of new forces into the theater is always an operational
function. In the RSOI process, the corps begins to integrate follow-on arriving forces into the C2 structure.
The corps staff manages the time-phased force deployment list as later stages of operations occur to determine
which forces are critical and how to allocate combat power to support the corps commander’s mission
objectives.

3-94. Rapidly introducing forces into an operational area requires timely, relevant, accurate, and predictive
intelligence. The intelligence staff determines the right combination of expeditionary and reach capabilities
to best support the corps’ mission. Expeditionary intelligence capabilities support the commander and staff
planning and decision-making processes to facilitate maneuver, precision engagement, and force protection.
Intelligence reach capabilities allow commanders and staffs to access the resources of national, joint, foreign,
and other military organizations and units. Detailed planning and training are critical to the success of both
expeditionary and reach operations. Requirements development begins as early as possible—in some cases
before receipt of mission, when only partial information about the general location or category of a mission
is known. Intelligence reach supports distributed analysis, processing, exploitation, and dissemination to
answer the commander’s critical information requirements and other priority intelligence requirements.
Intelligence reach also allows intelligence analysts to retrieve existing information, intelligence products, and
data that can support answering critical information requirements and other requirements from outside the
unit in a timely manner without waiting for an answer to a request for information or an information collection
task.

3-95. While deploying, the corps will initially be reliant on joint and allied information collection assets to
provide information to be processed into intelligence. The corps headquarters should consider placing its
intelligence staff early in force projection to quickly set up intelligence operations, receive collected
information, and support the corps commander. As soon as is feasible, the corps should exploit this initial
capability by deploying its organic, attached, and subordinate units’ information collection assets to decrease
its dependence on joint assets or use joint assets for other requirements. Allied and coalition partners may
possess an extensive array of human intelligence and counterintelligence assets to assist the corps, but
accessing their information requires significant coordination.

3-96. Anticipatory sustainment planning during this stage is key to successful execution of later stages.
Successful force-projection operations require tailorable, flexible logistics. The size of the deploying force,
the maturity of the theater, host-nation support capabilities, the availability of in-theater stockage, resources

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  55


---

Chapter 3

     pre-positioned afloat, and the existing theater infrastructure will all affect the logistics task organization. If
     there are no port facilities available to the corps, it may have to conduct logistics-over-the-shore operations,
     which will require early deployment of specialized engineer and logistics units.

     3-97. A corps typically requires the early or phased deployment of at least one engineer brigade headquarters
     to integrate engineer capabilities across the entire formation. Additional engineer brigades may be required
     to support the corps and its divisions, depending on METT-TC (I). The tailored engineer units are responsible
     for infrastructure planning, development, construction, and maintenance (See FM 3-0 and ATP 3-95 for more
     information on force projection and contested deployments).

RECEPTION, STAGING, ONWARD MOVEMENT, AND
INTEGRATION

     3-98. RSOI are critical steps for a corps commander in generating combat power. RSOI is a theater level
     responsibility, but the corps performs a critical role before deployment and during RSOI. Corps commanders
     typically shape the time-phased force deployment list to affect the force flow of corps units based on power
     projection and mission requirements. Intelligence, maneuver, and fire support may be prioritized initially,
     but sustainment, protection, and C2 elements are equally important. Commanders consider their mission
     objectives and design how they want to generate combat power in theater. The plan must be flexible to
     accommodate changes to the mission and the realization that building combat capability will be contested
     from home station to forward assembly areas.

RECEPTION

     3-99. Reception is a theater task, but this is where the corps commander’s units arrive in theater, and it
     includes the functions required to receive and clear personnel, equipment, and materiel through the point of
     debarkation. As the home station C2 element reports force projection, the early entry CP (derived from
     elements of the tactical CP) plans staging operations and subsequent onward movement. The corps, with its
     vehicles, equipment, and personnel, relies on the theater to receive and allocate space for staging. The corps
     early-entry CP is in constant communication with the corps home station C2 element to provide the corps
     commander with a visualization of forces and capabilities.

     3-100. The corps G-4 coordinates with the theater logistics base and establishes large unit supply operations.
     Divisions and brigades draw equipment and conduct necessary training and marshalling activities to link
     combat-ready elements with their equipment. Corps training during reception (and continued into staging)
     includes theater specific rules of engagement, status-of-forces agreements, local customs and basic language
     training, riot control, negotiation skills, and media interactions. Unit training and preparation also may
     include configuration of digital C2 systems and validation of communications and intelligence architecture
     across echelons.

STAGING

     3-101. Staging assembles, temporarily holds, and organizes arriving personnel, equipment, and materiel into
     forces and capabilities and prepares them for onward movement, tactical operations, or reintegration. The
     corps G-3 works closely with the corps G-4 and theater sustainment command to synchronize movement
     plans. The corps staff receive updates on units’ readiness to move forward. This is a critical point for the
     corps commander and staff to shape combat power capabilities.

ONWARD MOVEMENT

     3-102. The onward movement phase of RSOI is critical because it is typically the first point where the corps’
     personnel and equipment begin to assemble as a unit and move away from the port of debarkation. Onward
     movement is moving units from reception facilities and staging areas to the tactical assembly area or other
     theater destinations, moving nonorganic personnel to gaining commands, and moving sustainment supplies
     to distribution sites. While the theater army remains responsible for the process, the corps’ home station C2
     element and the early-entry CP inform it. Three primary factors affecting onward movement are movement
     control, transportation infrastructure, and security. The corps G-3, G-4, and CTO support the synchronization
     and development of the movement control plan with the theater transportation officer. This is the corps

56  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                           Fundamentals of Corps Operations

commander’s opportunity to redistribute combat power based on the expected environment in the     tactical
assembly area. The most important requirement during this phase is protecting the force through—

     • Movement control and transportation infrastructure.
     • Control and coordination measures.
     • Reconnaissance and security of the MSRs and routes within the corps AO.
     • Security.
     • Fratricide prevention.
     • Employment of air defense measures.
     • Exercising and reinforcing operations security (OPSEC).

Note. During large-scale combat operations, corps assembly areas may be in direct contact with
enemy forces. Close coordination between the theater army and corps is vital to force protection.
The intelligence staff estimate will support the commander’s decisions on movement orders, fire
support, and protection capabilities.

INTEGRATION

     3-103. Integration is the synchronized transfer of capabilities into an operational commander’s force prior to
     mission execution. It begins with corps units in the corps AO, specifically the assembly area for further
     movement to subordinate AOs. During integration, the divisions and separate brigades finalize preparations,
     such as completing any mandatory theater training and establishing communications and intelligence
     architecture. The transfer may require assimilation of provided forces, such as multinational units or
     additional Army capabilities to ensure they meet certain standards and understand corps standard operating
     procedures. Consequently, requirements for integration planning and coordination must occur early in the
     force projection process, and they are modified according to METT-TC (I) until achievement of force closure.
     Some integration tasks and requirements must be executed early in the RSOI process, particularly the
     coordination necessary for communications and intelligence architecture.

             Note. The arrival of units in an AO does not complete the integration cycle. Integration is a
             continuous process, and it is the final step before the corps assumes operations.

3-104. The corps commander plans for the employment of assigned forces to support the theater or CCDR’s
campaign. The corps commander integrates additional units as they become available and determines if
augmentation of supporting equipment or personnel is required. The initial force deployments must facilitate
the corps support to the theater army or combatant command objectives. The corps commander evaluates the
following factors in planning the integration of forces:

     • Probable weather conditions.
     • Terrain and hydrography in the corps AO.
     • Enemy situation, including the locations of main enemy forces and when contact is expected.
     • Complex terrain within, and adjacent to, the corps AO.
     • Capabilities of the enemy that may interfere with corps operations.
     • Time and space to employ available forces effectively.
     • Organization and employment of assigned, attached, or supporting forces, including corps air and

          naval liaisons.
     • Reconnaissance and security measures.
     • Sustainment support and the means, methods, and routes to support corps operations.
     • CCDR orders and any additional training requirements required prior to assuming operations.

3-105. The corps commander’s emphasis during integration is reestablishing C2. The corps commander also
plans for and considers multinational operations, host-nation forces and requirements, interoperability, and
liaison augmentation. For example, as security forces (including the military police brigade and MEB)
reestablish capabilities during staging and onward movement, the corps commander relies on joint or
multinational protection capabilities while integrating corps formations. Multinational or host-nation partners
may have different C2 systems, platforms, and capabilities that must be accounted for in planning and
conducting operations. Security force assistance brigades, TLDs, and regional support groups mitigate some

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92                                                                         57


---

Chapter 3

     of the C2 issues and facilitate interoperability with multinational forces. (See FM 6-0 for more information
     on liaison and interoperability; and see JP 4-18, ATP 3-35, and ATP 4-15 for more information on RSOI.)

TRACKING COMBAT POWER

     3-106. RSOI is not complete for the corps until the JTF commander determines the corps is ready to execute
     its mission. The corps commander assesses the corps status and submits a closure report to the theater army
     or combatant command. Corps commanders track the generation of combat power along warfighting
     functions. Tracking available combat power and force capabilities facilitates decision making. As the theater
     army processes the flow of units through RSOI, the corps assesses the progress and coordinates with the
     theater army when priorities change. The corps brings a tremendous application of force and combat power
     to synchronize efforts during large-scale combat operations. Corps commanders continually refine and
     reallocate resources while preparing for continued combat operations.

     3-107. In a maritime environment, RSOI activities may need to occur prior to onward movement into a JOA.
     The process of securing and uploading unit basic loads, addressing life support for personnel, or reconfiguring
     underway must occur prior to debarking directly into combat. Units that require extensive support for staging,
     including maintenance or cross-loading of supplies critical to survivability (including Class IV and Class V
     items) may require an intermediate staging base outside the JOA before continuing to their debarkation point.
     Additionally, units may execute onward movement out of sequence from reception, staging, and integration.
     Units may need to conduct integration during embarkation or during a reconfiguration at an intermediate base
     outside the JOA. (See JP 4-18, ATP 4-13, and ATP 4-15 for more information on watercraft operations.)

SECTION V — OTHER CONSIDERATIONS

     3-108. A corps may consider several things in addition to those previously discussed in this chapter. Corps
     consider planning tools and rehearsals. They incorporate effective knowledge management systems to
     synchronize corps operations. Finally, a corps must consider how information impacts its operations and
     develop the means to mask friendly actions from adversaries and seek to deceive them for its own benefit.

PLANNING AND REHEARSAL CONSIDERATIONS

     3-109. Corps commanders and staffs plan for the current operation while also developing or refining options
     for potential enemy actions and friendly opportunities. Several tools are available to the commander and
     planners to assist in adapting to changing circumstances. These tools include—

          • Decision points.
          • Branches.
          • Sequels.

     (See JP 5-0 and FM 5-0 for more information on planning tools.)

     3-110. The complexity of corps operations also requires deliberate rehearsals to shape future operations in
     line with the corps commander’s operational objectives. The size of a corps area, the potential branches,
     sequels, decision points, and the numerous elements related to operating over great distances in a corps area
     makes this a critical consideration for corps planners.

     3-111. The corps staff can conduct rehearsals collectively or by warfighting function. A warfighting function
     is a group of tasks and systems united by a common purpose that commanders use to accomplish missions
     and training objectives (ADP 3-0). Rehearsing operations via the integrating cells (in the movement and
     maneuver warfighting functional cell) provides the corps commander a way to assess the staff’s capabilities
     to operate and synchronize tasks across the warfighting functions in competition, crisis, and conflict.

     3-112. When preparing for defensive operations, the corps has a unique opportunity to execute rehearsals on
     the objective versus simulated terrain. As the corps transitions from defensive to offensive operations, the
     rehearsals on the battlefield may provide the corps commander (and subordinate division commanders) with
     an advantage over enemy formations. Corps rehearsals, including staff headquarters rehearsals, are critical
     to success in execution.

58  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                    Fundamentals of Corps Operations

KNOWLEDGE MANAGEMENT CONSIDERATIONS

     3-113. Knowledge management is the process of enabling knowledge flow to enhance shared understanding,
     learning, and decision making (ADP 6-0). Effective knowledge management helps the corps staff identify
     the necessary information and its relative importance to facilitate the corps commander’s decision making.
     Knowledge management is critical to improve efficiency and shared understanding during operations.
     Knowledge management uses a five-phase process to create shared understanding. The phases of the
     knowledge management process include—

          • Assess.
          • Design.
          • Develop.
          • Pilot.
          • Implement.

     3-114. Knowledge management officers contribute to time management, meeting management, content
     management, and report analysis. They also facilitate the integration of the corps battle rhythm for the corps
     chief of staff. The corps knowledge management working group helps the corps staff analyze and improve
     the battle rhythm, which is critical when synchronizing operations for multiple divisions and brigades.

     3-115. The corps’ battle rhythm supports the commander’s effective exercise of C2 and the commander’s
     style of decision making. Events occur in the different staff sections and cells of the headquarters to
     accomplish simultaneous activities, but they all support the commander’s overall battle rhythm. For example,
     the corps typically conducts a daily synchronization meeting and a battle update briefing to the commander
     or a designated officer to share the common operational picture and receive guidance.

     3-116. Battle rhythms nested by echelon let the corps’ subordinate commands offset their events to provide
     information needed by the corps headquarters. In addition, nested battle rhythms enable the corps CPs to
     supply necessary information to higher echelon headquarters and provide timely inputs to other joint force
     supporting components’ decision cycles. For example, the JFACC’s joint air tasking cycle dictates timely
     submission of Army preplanned air support requests and target nominations for resourcing on the air tasking
     order (known as ATO), and it requires the Army to digitally submit airspace coordinating measure requests
     for deconfliction of airspace coordinating measures on the daily air control order (known as ACO). (See ATP
     6-01.1 for a detailed explanation of knowledge management.)

INFORMATION CONSIDERATIONS

     3-117. A corps commanders’ approach to information changes depending on the role of the corps. Joint
     commands use joint doctrine in which information is one of the seven joint functions, and there are certain
     authorities, joint force capabilities, operations, and activities for leveraging information available to the JFC
     that are typically not delegated or provided to subordinate commanders. Army leaders ensure their
     information activities are nested with the joint plan while collecting, processing, analyzing, and using
     information to understand situations, make decisions, and issue orders. (See JP 3-04 for more information on
     the information function in joint operations.)

     3-118. Successful use of information can support corps operations, and commanders continually assess
     friendly and enemy forces in relation to each other and seek opportunities to exploit enemy weaknesses to
     achieve mission objectives. Actions are a form of nonverbal communication that have inherent informational
     aspects and are generally more impactful than verbal communications. Some examples of actions taken by a
     corps to use information to alter the enemy’s situational understanding are—

          • Making a feint or diversionary movement by a BCT.
          • Positioning divisions so that there is no obvious main effort and corps multiple courses of action are

                possible.
          • Presenting a false gap-crossing site.
          • Feeding false indicators to enemy sensors.
          • Placing physical and electronic decoys.
          • Jamming or dazzling ground, airborne, or space sensors to mask a movement or other action.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  59


---

Chapter 3

     3-119. A corps disintegrates enemy systems by corrupting or degrading their situational understanding,
     degrading their ability to C2 and sustain their forces, and forcing them to react to friendly actions. Depending
     on the capabilities employed, friendly forces can neutralize sensors or neutralize enemy capabilities to
     degrade friendly sensors. This can keep enemy forces in a disadvantageous position or deprive them of the
     time they need to recover from previous actions, while subordinate divisions and brigades maneuver and
     close with disaggregated enemy forces.

     3-120. While divisions and brigades are in contact with enemy forces, the corps continues to exploit
     information advantages by actively disrupting the enemy force’s ability to understand the situation, direct its
     formations, communicate, or employ joint fires. The ideal result is that enemy formations are isolated from
     their enabling capabilities and destroyed. Their destruction enables the corps to move on to the next echelon
     of enemy forces, enabling their ultimate defeat and a faster resolution to combat operations.

     3-121. Creative and innovative use of tactical deception (TAC-D), deception in support of operations security
     (known as DISO), space operations, cyberspace, EW, and integrated joint special technical operations
     (known as IJSTO) capabilities can conceal some important aspects and signatures of subordinate C2, fires,
     movements, and other operations or provide false signatures in different locations or times. The IJSTO
     program, commonly called special technical operations, includes the process and architecture to access, plan,
     coordinate, synchronize, and employ select joint, Service, and DOD agency special access programs in
     exercises and combat operations. It can create an information advantage across all Army warfighting
     functions, all domains, the information environment, and the electromagnetic spectrum. IJSTO represent a
     special access program portfolio, a security architecture, an information network, and a review and approval
     process for deployment and execution of special access programs.

OPERATIONS SECURITY

     3-122. Effective and disciplined OPSEC is employed during all corps operations. During rear operations,
     corps elements must consider camouflage, concealment, jamming, and other countermeasures to help prevent
     enemy or adversary reconnaissance and other information collection capabilities from gaining an advantage.
     OPSEC contributes to the corps’ offensive operations by enabling efforts that slow the adversary decision
     cycle and affect the quality of the enemy commander’s decisions. OPSEC supports the corps’ defensive
     operations by denying enemy forces information that could be used for targeting or attacking the unit.
     Commanders and staffs implement countermeasures and security measures to identify and protect essential
     elements of friendly information.

60  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026
