                                                 Chapter 4

                     Integrating Corps Operations

    This chapter expands on the application of the deep, close, and rear operations framework
    introduced in chapter 3. It provides an overview of general friendly and enemy challenges
    facing a corps during operations and describes how a corps synchronizes deep, close, and rear
    operations to overcome those challenges. The chapter concludes by providing considerations
    for synchronizing deep, close, and rear operations during offensive and defensive operations.

SECTION I — CHALLENGES FOR THE CORPS DURING OPERATIONS AND
    OVERCOMING THE CHALLENGES

     4-1. A corps fights to win battles, or a series of battles, as part of a larger major operation or campaign.
     During these battles, a corps overcomes the friendly and enemy challenges present during operations. The
     challenges arise from conducting operations on a large-scale with finite resources against capable, adaptable
     threats.

NATURE OF CORPS OPERATIONS

     4-2. Corps operations are expansive in terms of size, time, and space. They are expansive in nature due to the
     number of assigned and attached forces, the time required to employ those forces, and the space needed for
     subordinate formations to accomplish their objectives. At any given time, a corps employs multiple divisions,
     multifunctional brigades, functional brigades, and joint capabilities during operations. The integration of
     these forces and synchronization of their actions, on limited terrain and along restricted lines of
     communications, creates complexity which the corps manages. In addition to the friendly related challenges,
     peer threats create another set of challenges for a corps.

     4-3. Peer threats possess large operational formations, employ advanced long-range fires and air defense
     capabilities to provide stand off from friendly ground and air forces, and can typically contest a friendly corps
     in multiple if not all domains. Large formations provide the enemy with the ability to withstand friendly
     attacks against a single decisive point. The enemy can reposition, regenerate, or reconstitute forces to
     continue battles long enough to cause friendly culmination if the corps cannot account for how the threat
     might adapt.

     4-4. Enemy formations dispersed across large frontages and depths can position key capabilities like rocket
     artillery and AMD systems at extended distances from the corps' forward line of own troops. This makes it
     difficult for a corps to locate, maintain contact, and strike key enemy forces and capabilities without joint
     support. Additionally, the distances of enemy second echelon forces and long-range systems may be at
     distances greater than the range of a corps' land-based capabilities. These factors require a corps to integrate
     and synchronize the application of joint capabilities with corps capabilities through the LCC.

     4-5. The enemy's employment of deception, camouflage and concealment, and decoys limit the ability for
     friendly forces to locate potential targets in the corps’ deep area. The enemy's ability to hide, combined with
     the distances these forces are located, complicates information collection and targeting. To overcome this
     challenge requires the corps to develop and execute a robust information collection plan that incorporates
     joint ISR with the corps’ information collection capabilities.

     4-6. Long-range fires capabilities, supported by tactical unmanned aerial systems, allow enemy forces to
     observe and attack friendly forces throughout the corps’ AO. With these capabilities, enemy forces can
     disrupt the corps’ operation by attacking C2 nodes, logistics facilities, and friendly forces moving along lines
     of communication within the corps’ close and rear areas. Corps must assume that the enemy will maintain
     observation on every friendly asset of importance. These actions require friendly forces to reduce the effects
     of long-range enemy fires by dispersing forces, allocating protection assets to support key nodes and forces,
     and by detecting and destroying enemy forces operating in the corps’ rear area. In addition, the corps must

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  61


---

Chapter 4

     maintain open lines of communication to ensure the forward movement of maneuver and sustainment forces
     needed to support the close operations.

     4-7. Some peer threats have nuclear and chemical weapon capabilities, and they have expressed their intent
     to employ these weapons in certain situations. The enemy may choose to employ these weapons to disrupt
     friendly force deployment or to prevent defeat by friendly forces. Successful operations against CBRN
     capable threats require units to prepare for the employment of those capabilities and operations in
     contaminated environments. Planning includes active and passive measures for protection against the effects
     of these weapons and techniques for mitigating their effects to preserve combat power. This includes greater
     emphasis on dispersion, survivability, decontamination, and regeneration of communications.

OVERCOMING THE CHALLENGES

     4-8. Understanding the enemy is foundational to successful operations. The commander and staff develop an
     understanding of the enemy through IPOE, which informs the commander and staff of how an enemy force
     organizes, how it employs its forces, how it employs its long-range capabilities, and what the enemy’s
     possible objectives are. The commander and staff gain an understanding of how the enemy wants to conduct
     close operations, where the enemy may position its second-echelon forces, and the location of its long-range
     capabilities. Understanding the relationships and interdependencies between enemy echelons and capabilities
     provides information on vulnerabilities that the corps can exploit.

     4-9. Both offensive and defensive operations require a corps to attack and maintain pressure on the enemy
     force in depth. The corps attacks enemy forces simultaneously, or sequentially, when necessary, to deny
     enemy forces freedom of maneuver. The corps denies the enemy force sanctuary or respite to regroup,
     regenerate, or reconstitute. By attacking the enemy force in depth, the corps disrupts the enemy's ability to
     execute its plan and reduces the enemy force's ability to adapt. The corps synchronizes its attacks against
     enemy forces for a common purpose—to shape future close operations by its divisions.

     4-10. A corps synchronizes its deep, close, and rear operations to employ its forces in an effective manner.
     A corps links its deep operations with its operations in the close and rear areas to create multiple dilemmas
     for an enemy force. The linkage between the deep, close, and rear operations ensures that the associated
     objectives, timing, and activities are mutually supporting. One way a corps links these operations is through
     the employment of the defeat mechanisms in combination against parts of or all of the enemy force.

SECTION II — SYNCHRONIZING DEEP, CLOSE, AND REAR OPERATIONS

     4-11. The corps accomplishes its mission through either defensive or offensive operations, or a combination
     of both, as part of their higher headquarters’ plan. The corps must control key engagements. The corps must
     deny the enemy the ability to concentrate combat power by attacking the enemy’s follow-on forces during
     defensive operations or by attacking the enemy’s second echelons and mobile reserves during the corps’
     offensive operations. These actions deny the enemy the ability to interfere with current close operations while
     setting conditions for subsequent operations. Finally, the corps must conduct successful rear operations to
     sustain freedom of action during its close and deep operations. Continuous execution of deep, close, and rear
     operations is critical for the duration of the attack or defense, since the enemy will continually attempt to
     concentrate numerically superior forces and fires to defeat the corps. Figure 4-1 depicts synchronization of
     deep, close, and rear operations.

62  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                      Integrating Corps Operations

                 Figure 4-1. Synchronization of Deep, Close, and Rear Operations

THE COMMANDER’S VISUALIZATION AND CONCEPT OF THE
OPERATION

4-12. Deep, close, and rear operations require continuous synchronization. The commander and staff must

execute current operations while continuously formulating and revising a concept for future operations. They

continuously analyze their mission and maintain an updated estimate of the situation. The result of the staff’s

planning and coordination is an allocation of resources and

combat power to support deep, close, and rear operations     The conduct of corps operations is
based on the corps commander’s visualization and concept     based upon a clear, detailed
of operation.                                                statement of intent by the
                                                             commander. This is a prerequisite for

4-13. The commander must visualize the operations of the effective command and control.

corps and those of the enemy and project that visualization

into the future. Analysis of all domains and their impact on friendly and enemy operations inform the

commander’s visualization. Because corps are land combat formations, the commander first analyzes the

geography within a theater of operations and the terrain within the corps’ assigned area to assess their impact

on the operations. The impacts are due to the limited number of avenues of approach, mobility corridors, and

obstacles within the corps’ assigned area. Based on this analysis, the commander visualizes the size and types

of forces, tactics, and tempo required to achieve corps’ objectives.

4-14. The commander builds on the terrain analysis with analysis of the other domains. The commander
determines how friendly and enemy capabilities from the air, maritime, space, and cyberspace can impact the
corps operations. The commander then visualizes how the employment of friendly capabilities from other
domains can complement and reinforce the corps’ scheme of maneuver.

4-15. The result of the commander’s visualization is planning guidance that allows the staff to develop plans
to support a course of action and the corps commander to synchronize the operations of subordinate
commanders. It is particularly important that subordinate commanders clearly understand the corps
commander’s intent, so they can execute operations in the absence of further guidance.

4-16. The commander’s concept of operations describes how the corps will execute operations to achieve the
desired defeat mechanisms, the command relationships between units, and the relative priorities over time.
The concept of operations includes how the corps conducts deep, close, and rear operations and assigns on

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92                                                                63


---

Chapter 4

     order missions, priorities, and allocating combat power to each of the operations. It should prescribe tactics
     and forms of maneuver in detail for an operation’s first phase and provide guidance for all likely variations
     in maneuver and support necessary to complete the mission. Contingency and On Order modifications must
     be part of the initial plan if the corps is to maintain the initiative.

CLOSE OPERATIONS

     4-17. Deep and rear operations set conditions for the success of corps close operations. The measure of
     success of deep and rear operations is their positive impact on increasing the effectiveness of close operations.
     During large-scale combat, corps close operations will primarily be centered on offensive and defensive
     operations with stability operations limited to only what is required to be in accord with the law of armed
     conflict.

     4-18. Close operations for a corps include the deep, close, and rear operations of its divisions, and the
     operations of its separate brigades. Corps close operations comprise the efforts of its divisions to close with
     and defeat enemy forces during battles and engagements. Divisions and brigades combine direct and indirect
     fires with movement to destroy and defeat enemy forces. Close operations concentrate overwhelming combat
     power at the right time and place to create and then exploit windows of opportunity to achieve assigned
     objectives. In close operations, division commanders complete assigned tactical tasks as part of the corps
     plan and the corps commander concentrates overwhelming combat power at critical points throughout the
     operation.

     4-19. Activities are part of close operations if their purpose contributes to defeating committed enemy forces
     that are or will be in direct physical contact with friendly forces. For example, during close operations,
     engineers mainly facilitate mobility and countermobility operations. They support friendly movement of
     large, tactical units, and they control enemy movement. They may create time and space between friendly
     and enemy forces by restricting the enemy force’s ability to maneuver in large formations. Activities that
     comprise close operations include—

          • Maneuver of subordinate formations (including counterattacks).
          • Close combat (including offensive and defensive operations).
          • Fire support (including indirect fire, counterfire, close air support, electromagnetic attack, and

                offensive space and cyberspace operations against enemy forces in direct physical contact with
                friendly forces).
          • Information collection.
          • Sustainment support of committed units.

DEEP OPERATIONS

     4-20. Corps deep operations attack an enemy force in depth. Corps deep operations are inherently joint. A
     corps conducts deep operations to—

          • Limit the enemy force's freedom of action.
          • Alter the tempo of operations to create relative advantages at critical times and places.
          • Deny enemy forces the ability to concentrate and sustain their forces.
          • Isolate the close fight to enable the enemy’s defeat in detail.
          • Destroy the enemy force’s will to fight.

     4-21. The corps does this by integrating all available joint and Army capabilities to prevent the enemy from
     effectively employing its reserves, long-range fires, air defense systems, C2 systems, and logistics.
     Understanding and then attacking the enemy’s approach destroys its ability to synchronize operations or
     maintain a cohesive plan.

     4-22. Corps deep operations set conditions for success during close operations and subsequent engagements.
     The commander determines the conditions needed by the divisions to achieve their objectives. Those
     conditions are generally related to relative combat power, force ratios, and time. Based on the commander’s
     guidance, corps planners determine which enemy forces to attack, where and when to attack, and how to
     attack those forces to create the required relative advantages.

     4-23. A corps G-2 assesses the location, composition, and disposition of enemy formations and capabilities
     to determine their impact on close operations. The corps G-2’s assessment also accounts for enemy

64  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                             Integrating Corps Operations

     formations and capabilities located outside of the corps’ assigned areas which could impact corps operations.
     The G-2 focuses their analysis on the enemy’s second echelon forces, logistics assets, mobile reserves, air
     defense, and long-range fires systems. These enemy formations and capabilities are not all inclusive but
     represent the enablers that underpin how the enemy wants to fight.

     4-24. The corps focuses its attacks on the enemy vulnerabilities and capabilities most able to deny the
     objectives it assigns its divisions and other subordinate formations. The commander selects a defeat
     mechanism or combinations of defeat mechanisms to apply against those enemy forces. The staff then
     identifies decisive points critical to employing the defeat mechanisms based on the commander’s priorities
     and available assets. From this prioritization, the staff determines the most effective Army and joint
     capabilities to employ. The corps also accounts for those uncommitted forces and systems that an enemy may
     locate at distances greater than the maximum engagement range of the corps’ land-based capabilities. The
     corps staff recommends those distant targets to its higher echelon or to an adjacent formation for attack.

     4-25. The corps staff ensures friendly capabilities are available and prepared for employment continuously.
     To accomplish this task, a corps integrates and synchronizes the employment of friendly capabilities across
     multiple timelines to create effects against an enemy force. For example, a corps sequences joint air
     operations during the 24- to 96-hour joint air-tasking cycle, division maneuver on a 72- to 96-hour cycle, and
     sustainment operations on a 72- to 120-hour cycle. Accounting for these variances ensures conditions are set
     for continuous operations and the integration of the corps operations process with joint C2 processes.

CORPS ACTIVITIES DURING DEEP OPERATIONS

     4-26. Several activities are typically conducted as part of deep operations to achieve the desired defeat
     mechanism or mechanisms. They include—

          • Deception.
          • Information collection and target acquisition.
          • Interdiction.
          • Long-range fires.
          • Electromagnetic warfare.
          • Offensive cyberspace operations and space operations.
          • Military information support operations.

Deception

     4-27. Deception is an essential element of achieving surprise. There are multiple forms of deception that the
     corps must consider. Military deception are activities intended to mislead and cause adversary actions or
     inactions that are beneficial to the friendly force (JP 3-54). Tactical deception is a friendly activity that causes
     enemy commanders to take action or cause inaction detrimental to their objectives (FM 3-90). Deception in
     support of operations security is a deception activity that conveys or denies selected information or signatures
     to a foreign intelligence entity. Deception contributes to creating multiple dilemmas, achieving operational
     surprise, and maintaining the initiative.

     4-28. Deception efforts by a corps seek to delay enemy decision making until it is too late to matter or to
     cause an enemy commander to make the wrong decision. The corps deceives the enemy commander by
     concealing friendly locations, capabilities, and intent until a time and place of the corps commander’s
     choosing. Late or wrong decisions by the enemy commander creates friendly advantages, such as surprise
     and favorable force ratios, which the corps can exploit.

     4-29. The commander decides on the resources, location, and time to employ deception in support of
     operations. Deception plans may necessitate the dedication of significant effort and resources. The
     commander ensures the corps initiates deception measures in sufficient time to allow enemy forces to acquire
     false or misleading information, analyze the activity or inactivity, and react to the intended deception in the
     desired manner. The corps information operations officer coordinates with higher echelons to integrate
     TAC-D plans with the MILDEC plans established by the JTF or CCDR. (See Chapter 6 for more on MILDEC
     and TAC-D.)

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  65


---

Chapter 4

Information Collection

     4-30. Intelligence initially drives the selection of defeat mechanisms a commander aims to employ against
     enemy forces. The corps develops intelligence through information collection. Information collection is an
     activity that synchronizes and integrates the planning and employment of sensors and assets and the
     processing, exploitation, and dissemination systems in direct support of current and future operations (FM
     3-55). The corps’ information collection effort facilitates deep operations by identifying and tracking threats
     to the corps’ mission.

     4-31. A corps has access to joint ISR assets in addition to the corps' organic information collection assets.
     The joint ISR request process requires prior planning and approval. To receive joint apportionment and
     allocation for ISR, the theater army and corps intelligence staffs collaborate and coordinate early during the
     joint planning process. These staffs must understand the various joint ISR scheduling and collection
     management mechanisms, such as joint targeting, and the air tasking order.

     4-32. The corps G-2, with assistance from the E-MIB, manages an information collection effort that allows
     the corps to rapidly respond to changes in the threat and operational environment. During planning for deep
     operations, the intelligence staff builds its initial collection plan based on predicted enemy courses of action.
     As the corps’ operation unfolds, the intelligence staff adjusts its information collection plan to gather battle
     damage assessments, track the movement of enemy forces, and identify changes in the operational and
     mission variables that may impact close and deep operations. The information gathered to support these
     requirements must be timely and accurate to inform the commander’s decision to restrike a target or transition
     to begin collection against the next set of objectives for the corps.

     4-33. The corps intelligence staff maintains a broad perspective across the corps’ AO and continuously looks
     beyond the close fight. The intelligence staff focuses on information collection deeper, in time and space,
     than its divisions to identify and track the movement of enemy forces that are out of contact. However, the
     staff must frequently weigh the need to allocate joint and corps assets to answer subordinate requirements
     against the need answer requirements for corps deep operations. If the corps becomes overly focused on the
     close operations of its divisions, it increases the risk of surprise by enemy forces and reduces the corps’
     ability to set conditions for the next phases, branches, or sequels in the current operation.

     4-34. Clearly defined conditions or boundaries for intelligence handover synchronize information collection
     across echelons and organizations. These conditions or boundaries may be geographic, time-based, or
     delineated by target set. This allows predictable transitions as the corps and its subordinate divisions make
     progress during operations. It also enables the corps to look deeper towards the operational and strategic
     objectives as an operation continues. (See FM 2-0 for more information on intelligence handover lines.)

     4-35. The corps and other echelons may generate information collection efficiencies through en route
     collection and tasking subordinate maneuver elements or assets. For example, a corps may need information
     about the condition of a bridge. Rather than tasking corps assets, the corps can task the division responsible
     for the area containing the bridge to answer the request for information. Ultimately, a lower echelon element
     conducts a tactical reconnaissance task and then generates a report that flows back to the corps. When tasking
     subordinate formations, the corps must be clear about its specific information requirements and provide
     adequate time to collect the information. (See Chapter 7 for more information on reconnaissance.)

     4-36. Corps may also employ a geospatial intelligence cell to assist with information collection. The corps
     geospatial engineer in the main CP coordinates with the corps G-2 to form the cell. The geospatial intelligence
     cell includes geospatial intelligence imagery analysts and geospatial engineers. Combining these skill sets
     into one cell facilitates an environment for analysts and engineers to create the best geospatial intelligence
     products to support planning and operations. Geospatial intelligence cells provide a more complete picture
     of an OE’s physical environment and infrastructure, as well as analysis. (See ATP 2-19.3 for more
     information on the geospatial intelligence cell.)

Targeting

     4-37. Targeting is the process of selecting and prioritizing targets and matching the appropriate response to
     them, considering operational requirements and capabilities (JP 3-0). The FSCOORD leads the targeting
     working group and is the primary advisor to the commander on fire support.

66  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                             Integrating Corps Operations

     4-38. In addition to the FSCOORD, the targeting and airspace working groups involve a large portion of the
     corps staff. It includes the commander, chief of staff, operations officer, intelligence staff from multiple
     specialties, Army aviation commander, corps targeting officer, EW officer, chief of protection, and other
     staff members depending upon the situation. The corps G-2 provides the enemy's current situation, event
     templates, high value targets, and named areas of interest. The G-2 staff provides predictive analysis on the
     future enemy courses of actions to support targeting. The FSCOORD, working with the maneuver cell and
     FSE, develops and refines the target development plan for the targeting working group. The targeting working
     group generates target nominations, preplanned air support requests, airspace control means requests, and
     collection requirements. In addition, the airspace working group identifies airspace required to support corps
     operations and integrates subordinate unit airspace plans into the corps’ unit airspace plan.

     4-39. The targeting working group is a selection of predetermined staff representatives involved with
     targeting who meet to provide analysis, coordination, warfighting function updates, and running estimates to
     synchronize the targeting efforts and generate options presented to the commander at the targeting
     coordination board. The targeting coordination board is generally more formal and is focused on updating
     the commander, gaining new guidance, and obtaining approval of the collection plan and associated targeting
     actions. Targeting working group and coordination board sessions should be the minimum length required to
     present targeting information, situation updates, provide recommendations, and obtain decisions. The
     targeting coordination board is a temporary grouping of selected staff representatives with delegated decision
     authority to provide targeting decision recommendations for command approval. When the process or activity
     being synchronized requires command approval, a board is the appropriate forum.

     4-40. The corps provides Army inputs to the joint air tasking cycle as part of its operations process, normally
     at the conclusion of the targeting working group and airspace working group. The Army inputs support
     development of the joint air tasking cycle outputs (the air tasking order and airspace control order). The air
     tasking order is a method used to task and disseminate to components, subordinate units, and command and
     control agencies projected sorties, capabilities, or forces for targeting and specific missions (JP 3-30). The
     airspace control order is an order implementing the airspace control plan that provides the details of the
     approved requests for airspace coordinating measures (JP 3-52). The corps’ inputs into the joint air tasking
     cycle are critical to ensure the supporting air component understands the corps’ priorities for the use of joint
     airpower in its operational area. The details from the airspace control order are also critical to developing a
     concept of operations because they include airspace coordinating measures, fire support coordination
     measures, and air defense measures. (See JP 3-52 and FM 3-52 for more information on airspace control.)

     4-41. The corps synchronizes joint fire missions with corps current operations to ensure the corps employs
     joint and corps capabilities in complementary and reinforcing ways. Additionally, the corps identifies targets
     and requests for support that are beyond the capability or authority of the corps to engage. The corps passes
     those targets and requests for support to the higher echelon headquarters for action.

     4-42. Close air support is tasked to support ground forces based on the preplanned air support requests
     submitted to the air component, typically the JFACC. The JFACC provides close air support sorties to support
     ground forces based on the preplanned air support requests submitted from the LCC. The LCC and corps
     must submit preplanned air support requests based on the JFC apportionment decision to ensure JFACC
     allocation of close air support sorties. The FSCOORD, deputy FSCOORD, and air LNO are integral to the
     process, and they advise the commander on the close air support distribution for publication in the fire support
     plan. (See FM 3-60 for more information on targeting.)

Joint Fires

     4-43. Joint fires are fires delivered during the employment of forces from two or more components in
     coordinated action to create desired effects in support of a common objective (JP 3-0). The corps coordinates
     and synchronizes joint fires to shape enemy forces during deep operations to set conditions for their
     subordinate divisions. The JFACC, through consultation with other component commanders in the JTF,
     provides air apportionment recommendations to the JFC. Subsequently, the JFC makes decisions on
     apportionment and disseminates guidance and objectives to components providing support to the JTF. The
     results of apportionment for the corps are effects and sorties published through the air tasking order. Corps
     FSE planners develop a counterfire plan to enable effective weapons locating radar coverage over critical
     locations for the specific periods necessary for the successful conduct of operations.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  67


---

Chapter 4

     4-44. Typically, a USAF expeditionary air support operations group provides direct support to the corps. This
     group is OPCON to the commander Air Force forces and under tactical control to the JFACC. When the
     corps is a tactical headquarters, the Air Force provides it a tactical air control party. When the corps operates
     as a joint force command or MNFLCC, the JFACC provides a joint air component coordination element, as
     per the Army-Air Force memorandum of agreement. The joint air component coordination element is not a
     controlling agency, and it cannot perform certain functions, such as controlling airspace.

     4-45. When naval surface fire support is available, naval LNOs advise and assist the commander in the
     employment of this support. Naval surface fire support capabilities generally include 5-inch guns in littorals
     and precision guided missiles from extended ranges. The Navy designates ships to provide direct support to
     specific USMC battalions or furnish general support to Army brigades and divisions. A corps prioritizes
     which division receives how much support. A corps coordinates maritime surface-to-surface fires through
     the joint force land component command. Maritime surface-to-surface fires are typically the product of
     deliberate targeting through the joint targeting process which includes participation by the joint force land
     component command.

     4-46. A corps employs these assets primarily in that portion of the corps’ AO beyond the forward boundary
     of subordinate divisions. The corps FSE coordinates with joint or theater fires elements and synchronizes fire
     support to enable operational maneuver and destroy enemy forces and capabilities. The corps FSE, in
     conjunction with the corps field artillery brigades, develops fire support planning and employment
     considerations to integrate joint and Army fires capabilities. For example, the corps FSE coordinates
     maritime surface-to-surface fires through the joint force land component command or air effects through the
     air tasking order (known as ATO) to the JFACC. Air support and maritime surface-to-surface fires are
     typically the product of deliberate targeting through the joint targeting process.

     4-47. Corps can also plan and coordinate for the employment of space control and offensive cyber capabilities
     in conjunction with traditional fire support during deep operations. The commander's guidance provides
     specific attack criteria for supporting fire support assets, focusing planning, and execution efforts on critical
     times and events. (See ADP 3-19 for information on the fires warfighting function.)

Corps Fire Support

     4-48. Fire support is a rapid and continuous integration of surface-to-surface indirect fires, target acquisition,
     armed aircraft, and other lethal and nonlethal attack/delivery systems that converge against targets across all
     domains in support of the maneuver commander’s concept of operations (FM 3-09). The corps’ long-range
     fires assets enable the corps to disrupt, degrade, destroy, or neutralize enemy forces and resources. These
     enemy forces and resources include maneuver forces, C2 networks, sustainment nodes, long-range artillery
     and rockets, and air defense systems. Corps fire support capabilities include cannon, rocket, and missile
     systems found in the corps’ field artillery brigade and the divisions. Additionally, the corps relies on the
     USAF tactical air control party and support from the JFACC’s airspace control elements to support execution
     of the long-range fires and airspace management schemes.

     4-49. Corps, division, and BCT FSEs require nested procedures for identifying, prioritizing, and engaging
     planned targets and targets of opportunity that meet high-payoff target criteria, such as enemy C2 or
     long-range fire support systems. These procedures facilitate rapid clearance of fire support and assist in
     maintaining situational understanding across the joint force during target prosecution. (See FM 3-09 for an
     in-depth discussion of fire support procedures.)

     4-50. The field artillery brigade allocated to the corps augments the fire support of divisions; provides
     counterfire against enemy indirect fire capabilities and fire support against targets such as enemy CPs,
     logistics sites, and radars; and performs corps-wide target acquisition functions. Fire support planners at corps
     develop a counterfire plan to ensure weapons locating radar coverage. The types of missions assigned to field
     artillery brigades are general support, reinforcing, and general support reinforcing. Corps commanders may
     attach these units to one or more of their divisions or any BCT under their direct command.

     4-51. At the recommendation of the field artillery brigade commander, the corps commander assigns support
     relationships to the field artillery battalions of any divisions and BCTs in reserve or not yet committed to the
     fight. Commanders give priority field artillery support to their designated main effort. If an operation is
     phased, priority for fire support can vary from unit to unit. A corps will usually provide additional field

68  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                             Integrating Corps Operations

     artillery brigade battalions in a support relationship to the division artillery or field artillery battalions of the
     BCTs in the main effort. (See FM 3-09 and ATP 3-09.24 for information on the field artillery brigade’s
     support to corps operations.)

     4-52. The corps FSE recommends a task organization to the commander that best supports the conduct of
     successful operations by subordinate divisions. A corps commander may choose to retain OPCON of some
     field artillery assets to weight the main effort or set conditions for main and supporting efforts.

Cyberspace and Electromagnetic Activities

     4-53. Cyberspace electromagnetic activities (CEMA) are the process of planning, integrating, and
     synchronizing cyberspace and electronic warfare operations in support of multidomain operations (ADP 3-0).
     Cyberspace and the electromagnetic spectrum provide commanders at all echelons the ability to share
     information, communicate, integrate, and synchronize operations across warfighting functions and echelons.
     CEMA also provides corps commanders with the ability to gain and maintain an advantage in cyberspace
     and across the electromagnetic spectrum. The corps implements CEMA via the integration and
     synchronization of cyberspace operations, EW, and spectrum management operations during course of action
     development. It does so within the limitations and policies established by national or combatant command
     authorities.

     4-54. While units at echelons corps and below may be able to achieve desired effects in cyberspace through
     other military capabilities (suck as lethal fires, maneuver, or air support), cyberspace and electromagnetic
     attack may provide additional capabilities to augment traditional lethal and nonlethal fires. Conversely,
     cyberspace and the electromagnetic spectrum provide enemies with an effective, inexpensive means for C2,
     information warfare, electromagnetic attack, and sensor-to-shooter linkages. CEMA staffs provide corps
     commanders with the ability to gain situational awareness and create advantages in cyberspace and across
     the electromagnetic spectrum.

     4-55. Corps coordinate for offensive cyberspace operations support using the cyber effects request format. A
     corps G-3 receives cyber effects requests from its divisions, determines priorities of support, and then directs
     its CEMA section to submit the support request to the JFLCC. Requested cyber effects may include priorities
     of protection, the delivery of military information support operations against a specific target, cyber
     exploitation of a target, or a deliberate cyberspace attack in support of operation plan execution. Corps submit
     the cyber effects request format to their higher headquarters. The combatant command joint cyber center
     enter the request into the USCYBERCOM request for support portal. Corps prioritize cyber support to
     divisions in accordance with the specific requirements of their AO and the course of action. A corps may
     coordinate delivery of cyber effects against an enemy formation through cyberspace using social media while
     simultaneously employing cyber teams and units to engage with specific elements of a population. (See FM
     3-12 for more information on Army cyberspace activities.)

Space Operations

     4-56. A corps is the focal point for the planning and execution of Army space operations. The space domain
     is essential to operations because it supports and enables operations in all other domains. Each corps contains
     an organic space support element (SSE), usually assigned to the movement and maneuver cell. The SSE
     plans, coordinates, integrates, and synchronizes space capabilities into operations for corps, division, and
     subordinate units. All land component space planning coordination runs up to the corps headquarters. The
     SSE coordinates directly with other space staffs and space coordination authorities for support to meet corps
     requirements.

     4-57. The corps detects and targets enemy space capabilities to disrupt, degrade, and deny the enemy use of
     space capabilities. The corps employs on space- and terrestrial-based capabilities to detect enemy space nodes
     and counterspace capabilities for targeting during deep operations. Like friendly forces, the enemy relies on
     space capabilities for communications; position, navigation, and timing; intelligence operations; and
     cyberspace operations. So, the corps’ disruption, degradation, and denial of space capabilities may create
     advantages for the corps to exploit.

     4-58. Corps are directly dependent on joint space capabilities to plan and execute across their AOs and
     especially during deep operations. The corps G-3 SSE coordinates for joint space capabilities through

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  69


---

Chapter 4

     established theater space coordination authority to U.S. Space Command. The primary task for a corps G-3
     SSE is to retain freedom of access to the space domain while holding enemy use of the space domain at risk.
     The corps G-3 SSE receives space support requests from the division SSEs, determines priorities of support,
     and then submits them to the theater space coordination authority. Space capabilities are in high demand with
     limited capacity. Effective coordination, integration, and synchronization of space capabilities in all phases
     of planning is crucial to mission success. (See FM 3-14 for more information on Army space operations.)

Military Information Support Operations

     4-59. Military information support operations are planned operations to convey selected information and
     indicators to foreign audiences to influence their emotions, motives, objective reasoning, and ultimately the
     behavior of foreign governments, organizations, groups, and individuals in a manner favorable to the
     originator’s objectives (JP 3-53). Military information support operations (MISO) enable a corps to influence
     enemy formations through information activities. A tactical psychological operations (forces) (PSYOP)
     group normally supports a corps to conduct military information support operations. The planners within this
     group advise commander and staff on how to maximize influence activities and minimize the adverse impact
     and unintended consequences of friendly operations. Military information support operations focus on the
     following themes against enemy forces:

          • Degrading enemy combat power by encouraging surrender, desertion, and malingering.
          • Reducing the will of the enemy to resist.
          • Degrading the decision-making abilities and operational effectiveness of the enemy.
          • Exploiting and amplifying friendly successes on the battlefield.
          • Exploiting and amplifying enemy failures and actions on the battlefield.

     4-60. The military information support operations planners participate in the targeting process. They
     nominate specific targets and target audiences to influence and advise the corps commander on the effects of
     information activities on target audiences. The planners also assess how corps operations may affect decisions
     by the targeted population and the subsequent actions of those populations. During deep operations, military
     information support operations amplify the lethal effects against enemy formations, when properly planned,
     integrated, and synchronized.

REAR OPERATIONS

     4-61. A corps conducts rear operations to support its close and deep operations. Rear operations enable the
     desired tempo of combat and provides the corps with the agility and endurance to exploit opportunities. Rear
     operations typically include efforts that consolidate gains to make conditions created by deep and close
     operations more permanent.

     4-62. The corps’ rear operations generally occur within a location the commander designates as the corps’
     rear area. The corps’ rear area extends from the forward boundary of its next higher echelon to the rear
     boundaries of its divisions. The terrain and the organization of the corps dictates the size of the corps’ area.
     In most instances, this area will exceed several hundred square miles.

     4-63. The corps’ rear CP is typically responsible for rear operations. This responsibility includes establishing
     protection priorities within the corps’ rear area, securing forces operating within the corps’ rear area, and
     securing lines of communications.

CORPS ACTIVITIES DURING REAR OPERATIONS

     4-64. Successful rear operations by a corps conducting operations, within an area congested with friendly
     units and along limited lines of communication, center on its ability to conduct five broad activities. Those
     broad activities are the following:

          • Positioning and repositioning aviation, fire support, and AMD units.
          • Conducting support area operations.
          • Securing sustainment and C2 nodes.
          • Controlling tactical unit movement between the division or corps rear boundary and units

                conducting close operations.
          • Positioning and moving the reserves.

70  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                             Integrating Corps Operations

Positioning and Repositioning Aviation, Fire Support, and AMD Units

     4-65. Army aviation is typically positioned in the rear area, and it integrates into corps operations by
     conducting air-ground operations throughout the corps’ assigned area. Army aviation forces position
     maintenance, forward arming and refueling points, and unmanned aircraft system launch locations forward
     to enable close and deep operations, while employing protection measures against an enemy force’s long-
     range fires. Army aviation forces are positioned based on their mission requirements, speed, range, and the
     enemy threat to support corps operations. (See FM 3-04 for more information on Army aviation core
     competencies.)

     4-66. The corps AMD element plans and works with subordinate divisions and other headquarters to
     synchronize the placement of ADA capabilities and the use of the airspace and to protect the force. ADA
     assets may be further directed by the AAMDC or ADA brigade commander to protect facilities located within
     the corps’ AO. The senior officer in the corps AMD section advises the corps commander on how the corps
     can best counter air and missile threats and is also responsible for nominating the realignment of ADA forces
     to protect defended asset list assets to the joint theater AMD board. The corps AMD section is responsible
     for synchronizing and positioning AMD forces within the corps commander’s scheme of maneuver. (See FM
     3-01 for additional information on AMD operations.

Support Area Operations

     4-67. Support area operations are a critical part of rear operations. Support area operations are the tactical
     actions securing lines of communications, bases, and base clusters that enable an echelon’s sustainment and
     command and control (FM 3-0). A support area is where units position, employ, and protect base sustainment
     assets and lines of communications required to sustain, enable, and control operations. Support area
     operations include sustainment for the echelon and relevant security operations.

     4-68. A corps commander designates a support area within the corps’ rear area to frame a critical part of rear
     operations. The support area is where most of the echelon’s sustainment capabilities originate. During
     large-scale combat operations, and depending on the type of operation, commanders may position a portion
     of their sustainment capabilities in division AOs to facilitate flexibility and timeliness of resupply.

     4-69. Support area operations require detailed planning to coordinate among the various units providing
     sustainment, protection, and security. A maneuver enhancement brigade or BCT typically provides C2 for
     support area operations for a corps due to the level of security, planning, and integration required. The MEB
     is responsible for terrain management, movement control, mobility support, clearance of fires, and security.
     This allows sustainment units to focus on their primary function of sustaining corps operations. Commanders
     must balance their need to protect rear operations and tempo with the needs of main effort units committed
     to close and deep operations.

     4-70. Commanders consider several items when conducting support area operations. They include—
          • The requirements of the desired corps and division tempo.
          • Enemy maneuver and fires units that threaten current and future support area operations.
          • Additional forces required to protect the support area.
          • C2 nodes that will occupy the support area.
          • Command and support relationships between units in the support area.
          • Transportation networks (road, rail, inland waterways, and air) into and out of the area.
          • Protection from enemy indirect fire assets.
          • Survivability.
          • Dispersion, terrain management, and time-distance calculations between supporting-supported
                units.
          • Displacement considerations based upon the overall operation.
          • Movement control into, through, and out of the support area.

Securing Sustainment and Command and Control Nodes

     4-71. Sustainment and C2 nodes in the rear area are central to the conduct of rear operations. Every unit and
     node in the rear area is responsible for its own local security against level I and level II threats. Rear CPs plan

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  71


---

Chapter 4

     for and direct additional combat power to defeat larger threats. The corps’ rear CP also continuously monitors
     route security and adjusts the amount of combat power dedicated to route security as needed. If the corps’
     rear area is large enough, or if a significant threat emerges, route security may require additional forces to
     augment the tactical combat force (known as TCF) provided to the MEB or BCT assigned to C2 the support
     area. When the MEB does not have a tactical combat force, the MEB commander may organize its military
     police and combat engineers into a limited tactical combat force to defeat level I and II threats. Displacing
     sustainment and C2 nodes to ensure continued support for corps operations is a complicated process that
     requires detailed planning and synchronization for success.

Sustainment Nodes and Considerations

     4-72. Rear operations include the development and refinement of the concept of sustainment. The CSC, as
     the supporting unit, develops the concept of support. Sustainment headquarters at each echelon are also
     responsible for integrating additional sustainment assets they do not control, such as forward-positioned
     assets from higher echelon headquarters, into the overall plan. This is typically accomplished in conjunction
     with division rear CPs or the headquarters of a brigade support area, as these units may occupy a significant
     amount of terrain and will often require security assistance.

     4-73. Sustainment nodes are required to ensure sustainment remains responsive to operational demands. A
     node is a location in a mobility system where a movement requirement is originated, processed for onward
     movement, or terminated (JP 3-36). Nodes are large and small points of a distribution system or network
     connected by a line of communications (LOC) where a commander’s decision affects the actions of people,
     equipment, or supplies. Nodes and the LOCs that connect them require combat power to secure.

     4-74. Commanders position capabilities in support of protection, such as AMD units, and military police
     units to ensure uninterrupted movement of sustainment. Movement control, particularly in the corps and
     division areas, is vital, as distribution of needed materiel forward can be constrained by the availability of
     transportation or the capacity of the LOCs to accommodate the required traffic. Sustainment planners
     coordinate movement control with the G-3 to ensure movement plans are nested with maneuver. Constant
     coordination between the corps and division transportation officers, ESC, division sustainment brigades, and
     their supporting MEBs is required to prevent shortfalls of critical supplies in close operations. The corps
     chief of protection or provost marshal participates in the movement control board to facilitate decision
     making on throughput, capability, and security.

Command and Control Nodes and Considerations

     4-75. The corps’ main, rear, and tactical CPs synchronize maneuver operations across the three integrating
     cells for operations at 24, 48, 72, and 96 hours prior to an operation. The corps movement and maneuver cell
     coordinates with the other functional cells when synchronizing the different timelines to resolve resource
     issues and to prioritize specific capabilities and assets, including sustainment.

     4-76. Depending on the operation, the corps’ support area may contain multiple CPs and headquarters such
     as the corps’ main and rear CPs, the corps’ CSC, the corps’ MEB, and one or more BCTs. It is also likely to
     contain various companies and battalions with combat support or sustainment missions. These CPs,
     headquarters, and units may be dispersed across assembly areas within the general area. Conditions in a
     particular AO may require multiple dispersed assembly areas positioned in a noncontiguous manner
     throughout the rear area. The corps allocates combat power accordingly.

Controlling Tactical Unit Movement

     4-77. Rear operations require a controlled and steady flow of movement over LOCs such as road, rail, air,
     and waterways. Rear operations rely heavily on information and communications systems embedded in the
     distribution system. Assuring an uninterrupted flow of movement is essential to operational success. The
     system must be capable of rerouting and diverting traffic. Maintaining flexibility is one of the biggest
     challenges facing movement planners and operators during corps operations with shifting conditions and
     priorities. Establishing procedures for movements that cross boundaries and receiving and analyzing route
     statuses for corps MSRs and ASRs are essential.

72  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                             Integrating Corps Operations

     4-78. Corps military police units conduct route regulation and synchronization along the MSR and ASRs to
     facilitate freedom of maneuver and mobility support. The four key elements supporting MSR and ASR
     regulation are route reconnaissance, traffic control plan development, dislocated civilian control measures,
     and straggler control. Movement control is the dual process of committing allocated transportation assets and
     regulating movements according to command priorities to synchronize distribution flow over lines of
     communications to sustain land forces (ADP 4-0). Military police and sustainment units work together to
     facilitate movement control. The corps military police units contribute to the corps through prediction,
     detection, prevention, avoidance, and protection capabilities.

     4-79. Military police units plan for events that impede corps movement. Military police detect potential
     threats, providing insight for best use of available rear area reconnaissance and security assets. Military police
     units prevent the enemy from hindering corps movements, especially in contested or restricted terrain.
     Military police watch civilian movement patterns to prevent them from affecting corps mobility. Military
     police units also provide the corps with the ability to adjust movement and traffic control plans to mitigate
     the effects of obstacles or other types of interference.

     4-80. Working in close coordination with civil affairs elements, military police provide support to civil
     administration, manage civil disturbances, and establish roadblocks and obstacles to control traffic flow.
     Military police units enforce movement control through movement corridors to facilitate continuous flow of
     units, protect critical sites, and ensure local and area security. (See ADP 3-37 for more information on
     protection and ATP 3-39.30 for more information on mobility support.)

     4-81. Engineer resources retained by the corps, such as bridging, explosive breaching, sapper, and clearing
     units, are task-organized to divisions, the CSC, and brigades to support close and rear operations. During rear
     operations, engineers improve and maintain routes along corps lines of communication. Engineers also
     protect critical capabilities by emplacing protective obstacles and digging survivability positions.

     4-82. In the rear area, engineers mainly facilitate freedom of movement and enable survivability. Corps
     engineers build, maintain, and repair roads for ground movements and build airfields to support corps aviation
     units. As time permits, they replace tactical bridges with support bridging and construct LOC bridges.
     Additionally, they conduct route clearance missions. Engineers install obstacles to control traffic and restrict
     movement. They plan and execute situational obstacles, when necessary, to counter threat forces operating
     in the rear. When time and resources permit, corps engineers construct survivability positions for critical
     nodes and facilities. They coordinate with the Corps of Engineers and host-nation agencies to keep railroads,
     waterways, and transportation nodes like airports or seaports operating.

     4-83. The corps engineer also supports and collaborates with the theater engineer staff on the theater basing
     strategy and other facilities required to refine the planning and execution of construction and other general
     engineering requirements. The assistant corps engineer participates across the staff and planning horizons to
     synchronize the engineering disciplines. The corps engineer works closely with the theater engineer for
     requests for forces, capabilities, and capacities for mobility, countermobility, and survivability to shape
     operations for the division.

Positioning and Moving Reserves

     4-84. The location of units and capabilities does not determine how they align with corps deep, close, or rear
     operations. Commanders position forces as part of rear operations where they can be most lethal, effective,
     mutually supporting, and survivable. Corps commanders and staffs also consider branch plans, sequels, or
     changing priorities that will require the repositioning of supporting capabilities. Positioning the corps reserve
     or other uncommitted units is a planning consideration for branch plans and sequels.

     4-85. The commander generally positions the reserve where they can easily reinforce the main effort and,
     whenever possible, beyond the enemy force’s direct fire range. This positioning is to the rear of the divisions,
     in a location that provides maximum protection from enemy observation and fire. The corps considers several
     factors when determining the exact location for the corps reserve. These factors include—

          • Orientating on the most likely mission or their most important mission.
          • Response time to various planning priorities.
          • Access to MSRs.
          • Locations of probable enemy penetrations.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  73


---

Chapter 4

          • Terrain.
          • Availability of cover and concealment.

     4-86. A corps reserve is usually a division sized element requiring individual positions for every brigade-
     sized element. When the reserve is committed by the commander, the staff must make major changes in rear
     area movement and air defense extending into subordinate division rear areas as the corps reserve moves to
     its attack positions.

     4-87. Operations in a non-contiguous AO may pose a challenge for reserves due to intra-theater mobility
     concerns. When operating non-contiguously the corps should consider designating and positioning multiple
     reserves based on the terrain and threat. Positioning multiple reserves, when possible, ensures that employing
     a reserve is operationally feasible.

             Note. When constituted, the corps reserve is normally retained under the control of the corps
             commander. Circumstance, such as a major threat to the coherence of the defense (such as the
             commitment of additional enemy forces threatening a penetration) could arise requiring the corps
             reserve, originally designated for decisive commitment deep, be used to ensure success in the close
             or rear operations or retain the integrity of the defense.

WARFIGHTING FUNCTION CONSIDERATIONS DURING CURRENT
OPERATIONS AND FUTURE OPERATIONS

     4-88. At any given time, a corps may have one or more on-going operations and have other operations in
     various stages of planning. The corps must therefore ensure the success of the current operation while
     accounting for the requirements needed to set conditions for success during future operations. Future
     operations include those operations that are in various stages of planning within the corps, intended to be
     executed in the days or weeks ahead.

COMMAND AND CONTROL

     4-89. An effectively organized C2 system allows the commander to command from any point on the
     battlefield and allows the staff to perform the operations process from the corps’ dispersed command posts.
     This approach allows the commander to monitor the current operation while maintaining contact with the
     staff planning future operations. The commander designates the roles and responsibilities among the main
     command post, tactical command post, rear command post, and mobile command group. The commander
     balances the need to have redundancy within command posts against the need for dispersion and any
     unnecessary duplication of effort. (See Chapter 2 for more information on command posts in a corps).

     4-90. Corps commanders position their command posts to facilitate their ability to command operations,
     integrate capabilities, and synchronize combat power. They must balance the placement of command posts
     with the risk of enemy interference or destruction of those command posts. Dispersion, emission control,
     force protection, and operations security are all critical considerations for determining where the corps
     establishes command posts and when to displace those command posts.

MOVEMENT AND MANEUVER

     4-91. Successful offensive and defensive operations depend on the corps’ ability to move and maneuver
     multiple large, division-sized elements into close combat. The movement and maneuver of the corps and
     subordinate formations is continuous. The corps is constantly moving forces into and out of close combat to
     prevent their culmination and maintain tempo.

     4-92. Corps maneuver requires sustained movement—often over extended distances—prior to the
     engagement of forces. Additionally, the quality of roads, terrain, and transportation infrastructure shape how
     quickly a corps can move formations forwards, rearwards, and laterally inside its AO. Detailed planning that
     accounts for route use, synchronization, maintenance, refueling, and staging points is critical. Corps engineer,
     military police, and sustainment forces are indispensable to operations. They enable the corps to clear, secure,
     and maintain routes, control the movement of subordinate units, and resupply and refuel units transiting an
     AO. (See Chapter 7 for more information on mobility).

74  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                       Integrating Corps Operations

4-93. The potential commitment of multiple corps and divisions with their supporting formations into AOs
with limited road infrastructure makes successful integration of movement operations with maneuver an
operational imperative. This places a premium on developing and maintaining LOCs and alternate movement
routes, sustainment, and detailed planning to deconflict movements. Corps do not need to solve maneuver
problems for their divisions; the corps ensures that divisions and BCTs are positioned to maneuver with the
necessary support. Successful planning accounts for the movement of field artillery, air defense artillery,
aviation support, engineers, intelligence, and sustainment elements to ensure uninterrupted support to the
commander’s scheme of maneuver. Limited and dispersed mobility corridors place a premium on a corps’
abilities to control the movement and sustainment of large units.

4-94. Corps maneuver demands anticipation, careful planning, and the synchronization of sustainment. Corps
sustainment depends on preparation well before movement and maneuver occurs.. A corps commander
considers the operational reach of the corps in terms of logistics capacity, distribution, evacuation, treatment
of casualties, and personnel services. A corps commander and staff continually—

     • Assess the sustainment posture of their formation.
     • Position and protect sustainment and medical units.
     • Establish priorities of support.
     • Secure and maintain lines of communications with Army and host-nation forces.
     • Manage movement of subordinate forces and coordinate across the formation for movement of

          sustainment units

4-95. Table 4-1 on page 73 provides general planning horizons for integrating sustainment and maneuver at
various echelons.
