INTELLIGENCE

             From adequate and timely military intelligence the commander is able to draw logical
             conclusions concerning enemy lines of action. Military intelligence is thus an essential
             factor in the estimate of the situation and in the conduct of subsequent operations.

                                                                                                    FM 100-5, Operations (1941)

     4-96. The commander and G-2 ensure the corps’ CPs are manned with intelligence staff and each of those
     staffs have access to information they need. Intelligence staff must be integrated into the intelligence
     architecture to share information between CPs, as well as to higher and lower echelon command posts. The
     intelligence staff must support situational understanding for current operations, support targeting, and support
     planning for future operations.

     4-97. In preparation for future operations, the corps G-2 determines the intelligence architecture; processing,
     exploitation, and dissemination activities; and information collection requirements associated with the
     transition. (See FM 2-0 for more information on intelligence support to deep, close, and rear operations.)

FIRES

     4-98. The corps FSE and the target working group begin target development to support planning for the next
     operation as they support current operations. The development, nomination, and initial prioritization of
     targets for the next operation becomes increasingly time sensitive as the corps approaches its objectives in
     the current operation, since the transition may be rapid.

     4-99. Fire support planners identify the sustainment and protection requirements to prepare fire support for
     the next operation. The planners begin to look at potential positions for artillery units and possible weapons
     locating radar coverage areas. This includes coordinating routes to move units to new locations that support
     future operations. It also requires the identification of locations to reposition CLV stocks.

     4-100. Underpinning the fire support planners’ efforts is the corps G-2 enemy assessment. The intelligence
     staff continues to develop an accurate picture of the enemy situation facing the corps. The intelligence staff
     identifies those long-range air defense and fires systems that remain operational and pose a threat to the next
     corps operation. The G-2, in conjunction with the corps AMD cell, also identify the enemy’s high-altitude,
     long-loiter unmanned aircraft systems (known as UASs) and their supporting systems, to include launchers
     and ground control stations, that remain operational.

76                                              ATP 3-92                        15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                             Integrating Corps Operations

SUSTAINMENT

     4-101. The CSC coordinates with the corps G-4 to provide input to necessary orders and assist in
     development of the sustainment support plan for future operations. The G-4 works closely with the chief of
     staff and G-3 to ensure shared understanding of priorities and all aspects of planning, preparation, execution,
     and assessment of sustainment support for operations. A corps must have sufficient support to sustain current
     operations and all planned branches and sequels.

     4-102. Sustainment planners at each echelon within the corps must collaborate with their supported
     headquarters during planning. The time and distances comprising the large and complex supply chains
     required to sustain large scale combat operations limit their responsiveness. As corps commanders determine
     future courses of action, sustainers maintain their running estimate, so the commander understands the
     potential for any critical sustainment capability shortfalls and the options available to mitigate them.

     4-103. Movement control is critical and requires detailed planning to ensure success. The corps G-3 approves
     the selection of MSRs and ASRs to ensure maneuver and other operational issues are considered and
     coordinates with the G-4 and CSC on movement plan development. The corps G-4 and CSC facilitate the
     movement of divisions and separate brigades along the MSR and ASRs. The corps G-4 employ all available
     transportation capabilities to enable operational maneuver. The G-3 and G-4 identify critical points where
     restrictions could slow down or stop movement (including bridges, tunnels, checkpoints, border crossings,
     steep inclines and declines, and MSR and ASR geospatial capabilities) along with congested areas or choke
     points.

     4-104. In the corps’ rear area, the CSC or MEB commander regulates movement control on the corps’ MSR,
     ASRs, and connecting networks. The corps G-3 (in conjunction with the G-2, corps engineer section,
     protection cell, and CTO) determines and publishes MSR and ASR status. Corps typically use the technique
     of designating MSRs and ASRs with a color-coded status of green, amber, red, or black. (See ATP 5-0.2-1
     for more information on designating MSR and ASR status.) Commanders and corps staff sections consider
     the placement of ambulance control points, refuel and rearm locations, and combat and field trains in the
     context of synchronizing BCT and division sustainment in AOs and timing between operations. (See Chapter
     3 for an expanded discussion of corps sustainment.)

Sustainment Synchronization

     4-105. At the tactical-level, sustainment remains the key to freedom of action. Properly integrated, the
     support provided by sustainment units allows corps formations to maneuver, concentrate their capabilities,
     and quickly adjust their task organizations. If poorly coordinated, sustainment becomes an unintended
     contributor to culmination. Corps staff sections integrate their sustainment units’ requirements into their
     concept of operations. This includes careful selection and security of support areas in their AOs and planning
     for their displacement based upon adjustments to the tactical AO, particularly during offensive operations.
     This displacement planning is based on movement, general engineering, security, and protection capabilities.
     The displacement of the support area normally entails more time and effort than shifting a subordinate
     formation. (See Chapter 3 for an expanded discussion of sustainment synchronization.)

Anticipating Future Requirements

     4-106. Corps planning horizons require planners to anticipate future requirements, balancing current needs
     against future plans. An example of such a balance is a corps commander establishing a controlled supply
     rate (known as CSR) for specific munitions. The controlled supply rate is the rate of ammunition
     consumption that can be supported, considering availability, facilities, and transportation. It is expressed in
     rounds per unit, individual, or vehicle per day (ATP 3-09.23). The corps’ controlled supply rate is influenced
     by its higher echelon headquarters’ controlled supply rate, the required supply rate reports from its
     subordinates, and the type of operations planned. The required supply rate is the amount of ammunition
     expressed in terms of rounds per weapon per day for ammunition items fired by weapons, in terms of other
     units of measure per day for bulk allotment, and other items estimated to be required to sustain operations of
     any designated force without restriction for a specified period (ATP 3-09.23). Each echelon is designed to
     carry a certain number of days of supply for Classes I, III, and V.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  77


---

Chapter 4

     4-107. Commanders typically designate priority for sustainment to units that they anticipate being the main
     effort. This helps maximize the combat power of a unit before it becomes the main effort. Planning for and
     anticipating which units are the main effort are essential to establishing required supply rates and controlled
     supply rates for corps operations. Shifting a priority of sustainment to a new main effort at the same time it
     is designated is generally too late because of the time it takes to adjust the volumes of supplies from one unit
     to another.

     4-108. The corps retains control of certain sustainment operations based on their complexity and scope. Two
     major operations of this type are thorough decontamination and reconstitution of large units. These operations
     will likely require a shift in priorities or the commitment of theater-level assets.

Sustainment in a CBRN-Contaminated Environment

     4-109. A CBRN-contaminated environment increases the sustainment burden. It requires resupply of filters,
     protective suits, and unique challenges for sustaining basic supplies like drinking water. Rapid assessment
     and decontamination are vital to regain the initiative and continue operations. Units are equipped and manned
     to conduct initial self-supported operational and thorough decontamination but may require support from a
     CBRN unit to sustain operations. When units transition out of CBRN environments, a thorough
     decontamination, including detailed troop and detailed equipment decontamination, is required. A thorough
     decontamination requires large volumes of water and supplies, including new CBRN protective clothing and
     equipment, replenished decontamination supplies, and basic tactic supplies to replace items that could not be
     decontaminated. (See ATP 3-11.32 for more information on CBRN protection and ATP 3-11.33 for
     information on CBRN contamination mitigation.)

Reconstitution and Regeneration

     4-110. Some percentage of corps units will be rendered combat ineffective during large-scale combat
     operations due to personnel and equipment losses. They require reconstitution when resources allow, and the
     corps is the lowest echelon capable of conducting reconstitution in most large-scale combat contexts.
     Reconstitution is an operation that commanders plan and implement to restore units to a desired level of
     combat effectiveness commensurate with mission requirements and available resources (ATP 3-94.4).
     Reconstitution transcends normal day-to-day force sustainment actions. Reconstitution is based on a
     command decision, typically made two echelons above the unit being reconstituted, and it is conducted across
     all warfighting functions, using existing systems and units to do so. Thus, a corps can be expected to make
     reconstitution decisions for the BCTs in its divisions. No resources exist solely to perform reconstitution.
     Reconstitution requires the full support of the commander and staff sections to achieve the necessary results
     commensurate with future mission success. (See Chapter 7 for an expanded discussion of corps reconstitution
     operations.)

PROTECTION

     4-111. The corps’ execution of deep, close, and rear operations during current operations and posturing forces
     to support future operations creates potential gaps in protection. These gaps include, but are not limited to,
     AMD, area security, electromagnetic protection, and populace control. Possible causes for these gaps include
     low-density capabilities supporting multiple operations, changes in main effort and supporting efforts, or
     changes in the commander’s protection priorities. As an operation progresses or is planned, the protection
     cell and protection working group remains proactive in identifying potential gaps and the threats or hazards
     that may exploit those gaps. (See ADP 3-37 for more information on protection.)

SECTION III -— SYNCHRONIZING DEEP, CLOSE, AND REAR OPERATIONS
    DURING OFFENSIVE OPERATIONS

     4-112. The application of the deep, close, and rear operations model during offensive operations enables the
     corps to maintain the desired tempo. The commander visualizes the conditions necessary for the corps and
     subordinate divisions to sustain momentum, retain the initiative, and exploit success. To accomplish these
     conditions generally requires the corps to attack or influence enemy forces and capabilities located out of
     contact with the divisions.

78  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                             Integrating Corps Operations

DEEP OPERATIONS

     4-113. Corps deep operations ensure subordinate success during close operations. Corps deep operations
     destroy or degrade enemy combat power, C2 capabilities, and morale while changing the tempo of enemy
     operations so that it favors friendly forces. Deep operations shift during offense, particularly as the corps
     advances. Initially the focus is on setting conditions for the first objectives it assigns for its divisions.

     4-114. Upon the commander’s decision, the corps shifts the focus of its deep operations to the next set of
     corps objectives. The shift in focus allows the corps to attrit the enemy second echelon’s line of defense and
     mobile reserves. The corps prevents, delays, or disrupts the ability of the second echelon to move and
     maneuver to reinforce the enemy's first operational echelon.

     4-115. Detailed planning, coordination, and synchronization across all warfighting functions by all forces
     involved (including joint and multinational) is required for the successful execution of deep attacks against
     the enemy’s second echelon forces. (See ATP 3-94.2 for more information on deep operations.)

CLOSE OPERATIONS

     4-116. The corps' close operations are primarily maneuvering its divisions while setting the conditions
     necessary for their employment. The divisions maneuver their BCTs while physically in contact with the
     enemy forces they intend to defeat and destroy. The corps weights its divisions in terms of support and
     priorities based upon whether they are main or supporting efforts. The corps form of maneuver affects how
     the divisions operate. (See Chapter 5 for additional information about corps forms of maneuver.)

     4-117. The corps may position other forces it controls within the division AOs, but that does not mean they
     are part of close operations. When elements of the corps' functional or multifunctional brigades are involved
     in close operations, they are usually temporarily task-organized to a division or have a supporting
     relationship. Normally, corps forces not involved in close operations are positioned forward to support the
     corps' deep operations more effectively. (See FM 3-0 for more information on close operations.)

REAR OPERATIONS

     4-118. The fluidity and quick tempo of corps offensive operations strongly influence corps rear operations.
     The forward movement of units and sustainment, both being essential parts of rear operations, are critical if
     the corps is to maintain the initiative. Enemy forces look for opportunities to counter the corps' attack and
     will target anything that could disrupt friendly forces in the corps’ rear area. If its offense is to be successful,
     the corps must keep supply routes open to ensure the sustainment of its attacking maneuver units, and it must
     detect and defeat enemy forces that intend to interrupt the corps' rear operations effort.

     4-119. Likely threats to the rear area during offensive operations include long-range rocket and artillery fires
     (including chemical munitions), manned and unmanned aircraft, short-range ballistic missiles, special
     operations forces, long-range reconnaissance units, and bypassed enemy elements. Their primary objectives
     will be C2, logistics facilities, and disruption or interdiction of LOCs. Additionally, the corps should
     anticipate a counterattack into its rear by a ground maneuver force from an adjacent enemy unit not within
     the corps' AO. This is likely if the corps' AO overlaps the lateral boundaries of different enemy units. Given
     this situation, the forces allocated to defend the rear area may only be able to contain or block such a force's
     attack. It may then be necessary to defeat or destroy the force by diverting some of the corps’ combat power
     or obtaining JFLCC support. The key consideration before diverting any combat power from the main effort
     is whether the corps can still accomplish its mission given the threat to its rear or not.

SECTION IV — SYNCHRONIZING DEEP, CLOSE, AND REAR OPERATIONS
    DURING DEFENSIVE OPERATIONS

     4-120. During defensive operations, the commander visualizes the conditions necessary to impact the tempo
     of the enemy forces’ attack and create advantages that subordination formations can exploit during close
     combat. As in offensive operations, the corps shapes enemy forces out of contact with friendly forces. By
     degrading, disrupting, or destroying parts of these enemy forces, the corps provides division commanders
     time and more favorable force ratios. Corps operations in depth also facilitate corps operations creating

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  79


---

Chapter 4

     advantages for a corps counterattack. The corps organizes its defensive operations in terms of time, space,
     and purpose by synchronizing deep, close, and rear operations with those of the divisions. It assigns
     subordinate formations areas to defend and task organizes them in accordance with their missions.

DEEP OPERATIONS

     4-121. A corps' defensive scheme in its deep area generally aims to isolate or delay part of the attacking force
     so that the defensive main effort can succeed. Corps coordinate and employ collection assets from organic,
     attached, and direct support units; SOF; and joint and multinational assets to gain contact with enemy forces.
     Deep operations begin before enemy forces close with a corps and continue throughout the battle. A corps
     uses organic and supporting surveillance assets to locate and track the first and second echelon divisions of
     an attacking enemy force within the corps' area of interest. As enemy formations approach a corps’ AO, the
     corps identifies and strikes high-payoff targets, disrupts or degrades follow-on forces, and disrupts or destroys
     support systems. The corps directs its information collection effort at identifying the locations, dispositions,
     movements, and intentions of specific units and activities that are essential to sustaining enemy momentum
     and providing support for enemy operations. The timing of these operations is important. For example, a
     deep operation that prevents enemy forces from being able to employ their reserve is best conducted before
     the reserve is deployed.

     4-122. As attacking follow-on echelon forces move toward the forward line of own troops (FLOT), the corps
     engages enemy forces with Army and joint fires, in accordance with the commander's intent. In some
     circumstances, commanders choose not to engage an acquired enemy immediately. The attack decision will
     depend on its payoff priority and desired result. A corps may also elect to disrupt or influence the target's
     movement as opposed to its destruction. The use of area denial munitions or terrain shaping can canalize or
     impede an enemy force's movement. A corps may restrict the engagement of some forces or physical targets
     within its AO if the corps' plan of defense calls for unimpeded advance of enemy forces on certain approaches
     or if it is in the interest of the corps to defer destruction of an enemy force as part of a deception effort.

     4-123. A corps will generally coordinate initial attacks by joint assets as part of deep operations. These
     missions will be preplanned, coordinated attacks, which often require the corps to provide suppression of
     enemy air defenses. A corps conducts battle damage assessment to determine the effectiveness of attacks and
     inform the decision to re-attack, continue to track, or seek other targets.

     4-124. Sustainment of deep operations requires placement of support units in proximity to the forces
     conducting them, which entails risk. Planners consider the recovery or destruction of damaged combat
     systems, pre-positioning of ammunition, and changes to the controlled supply rate due to fire support
     requirements. The duration of the operation, follow-on missions, and possible linkup all affect sustainment
     organization and execution.

CLOSE OPERATIONS

     4-125. Corps close operations mainly include the deep, close, and rear operations of their divisions and can
     include corps separate aviation, artillery, and engineer brigades. Corps commanders prescribe the tactics that
     the corps will use, assigning missions to the divisions and brigades with their concept. In all cases, they give
     their division commanders the greatest possible freedom of action. When visualizing the defense and
     developing the concept of operations, corps commanders may choose to further delineate defensive
     operations in their close area into a security area and a MBA.

SECURITY AREA

     4-126. A security area is that area occupied by a unit's security elements and includes the areas of influence
     of those security elements (ADP 3-90). It may be located as necessary to the front, flanks, or rear of a
     protected unit, facility, or location. Forces in a security area furnish information on an enemy force; delay,
     deceive, and disrupt that enemy force; and conduct counter-reconnaissance. Security areas should be deep
     enough to make enemy forces displace as much of their supporting forces as possible, such as cannon
     artillery, sensors, and air defense artillery gun systems, before attacking. All units perform security operations
     within their AOs, including the support area, to deny enemy reconnaissance and otherwise protect the force.

     4-127. A corps defense requires security operations to protect the force, deny enemy intelligence gathering,
     and support deception operations. This may be done primarily on the ground by the divisions with corps

80         ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                             Integrating Corps Operations

     support. However, the situation may require corps to task-organize security forces and employ them in their
     security area if the corps commander decides that the combination of division security areas does not provide
     sufficient depth. Since there are no organizations specifically designed for corps security area missions,
     commanders base the composition and capabilities of their security forces on mission variables. A corps can
     access multiple joint capabilities in addition to organic assets to support its security area operations.

     4-128. Units conduct security operations to provide their forces time and space to react to the approach of
     enemy forces by causing enemy forces to deploy prematurely. An enemy force's deployment reveals its main
     effort and choices of available avenues of approach. Friendly units also conduct security operations to conceal
     the location of main battle positions and reserves, prevent enemy observation of friendly preparations and
     positions, and prevent enemy forces from delivering observed fire on these positions. Planners can offset an
     attacker's inherent advantage of initiative regarding the time, place, plan, direction, strength, and composition
     of the attack by forcing enemy forces to attack into unexpected, prepared defenses. Friendly forces counter
     enemy space, ground, and air reconnaissance activities through both active and passive measures. (See
     Chapter 7 for more information on security operations.)

     4-129. Upon an enemy force's commitment of its main effort, and to preserve the security force for
     subsequent operations, a corps commander may designate a disengagement line or disengagement criteria
     for it to withdraw or reposition. Disengagement criteria are protocols that specify those circumstances where
     a friendly force must break contact with direct fire and observed indirect fire to prevent becoming decisively
     engaged or to preserve friendly combat power (FM 3-90). The commander's level of acceptable risk to
     security forces affects the disengagement criteria. The commander describes the criteria in quantifiable terms
     or measures that specify friendly conditions for initiating disengagement from an enemy force, such as
     friendly strength levels or a specific time or event.

MAIN BATTLE AREA

     4-130. The MBA is where the commander intends to deploy the bulk of the corps’ combat power and defeat
     an attacking enemy force. A defending commander's major advantage is the ability to select the ground on
     which the battle takes place. The natural defensive strength of a position determines the distribution of forces
     in relation to both frontage and depth. The major disadvantage is that holding ground simplifies enemy
     targeting and requires measures to protect the defending force from massed fires. Defending units typically
     employ field fortifications and obstacles to reinforce the terrain's natural defensive aspects; however, the
     resources for creating fortifications and obstacles are scarce. The corps therefore prioritizes the allocation of
     engineer assets and CL IV materiel to its divisions based upon which are main and supporting efforts.

     4-131. The corps’ MBA extends from the forward edge of the battle area (known as FEBA) to the rear
     boundary of the divisions. Planners place subordinate unit boundaries along identifiable terrain features and
     extend them beyond the FLOT by establishing forward boundaries. Division boundaries should not split
     avenues of approach or key terrain. The corps staff recommends the MBA based on the products of the IPOE
     process and their analysis using the mission variables. The IPOE process anticipates how an enemy force
     will use the available avenues of approach. (See ATP 2-01.3 for more information on the intelligence
     preparation of the operational environment process.)

     4-132. A corps must take measures to deceive enemy forces as to the location and organization of the MBA.
     Enemy forces employ sensors allowing them to observe friendly preparation of the defense in detail. A corps
     must remain as ambiguous as possible when positioning units, and it must build deception positions and
     obstacles to obscure the design of the MBA defense. (See Chapter 5 for an expanded discussion of corps
     defensive operations.)

REAR OPERATIONS

     4-133. Rear operations maintain freedom of action in the security and MBAs and prevent culmination. The
     corps’ rear CP enables this freedom of action by planning and directing sustainment, conducting terrain
     management, providing movement control, and ensuring area security of the rear area. The corps reserve is
     normally positioned in the rear area for maximum flexibility but must be dispersed enough to avoid
     presenting a lucrative target. This requires detailed planning to ensure cleared routes that allow for rapid
     movement of the reserve when committed.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  81


---

Chapter 4

     4-134. During defensive operations, the CPs in the corps’ rear area may be relatively static for extended
     periods of time. So, leaders must do everything possible to limit enemy detection, increase the survivability
     of critical nodes, and counter enemy attacks before they can inflict significant losses. Included within the
     corps’ protection measures is the need to conduct effective counter-unmanned aircraft system operations to
     prevent enemy forces from identifying high-payoff friendly targets.

82  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026
