                                 Chapter 5

Corps Offensive and Defensive Operations

This chapter addresses considerations for a corps when conducting offensive and defensive
operations. As the senior tactical headquarters, corps are responsible for sequencing battles and
engagements in support of a larger campaign. The information in this chapter pertaining to
corps operations is meant to augment FM 3-90, not summarize it. The chapter begins with an
overview of the offense and vignette, followed by a discussion of the operational framework
in the offense. Finally, it discusses the defense.

SECTION I – CORPS OFFENSIVE OPERATIONS

5-1. Corps commanders must be prepared to conduct offensive and defensive operations and ensure that the
selected tactics for a specific operation are appropriate to the situation and objectives. Tactics are the
employment, ordered arrangement and directed actions of forces in relation to each other (ADP 3-90). Corps
use tactics to maneuver and position subordinate formations to gain positions of advantage relative to their
enemy, reach or accomplish assigned objectives, and defeat the enemy.

5-2. The offense is the decisive form of war. The offense is the ultimate means commanders have of imposing
their will on enemy forces. Offensive operations provide opportunities for a corps to exploit the initiative to
achieve a variety of enemy- or terrain-oriented objectives. Corps offensive operations, either on their own or
as part of a larger campaign, should seek to retain the initiative, to attack the enemy in depth, to strike enemy
weaknesses, and create conditions preventing the enemy from organizing a coherent defense.

OVERVIEW OF CORPS OFFENSIVE OPERATIONS

5-3. Corps conduct the offense to—
     • Defeat or destroy enemy forces.
     • Secure decisive terrain, population centers, and resources.
     • Fix an enemy force in position.
     • Disrupt an enemy force’s attack.
     • Set the conditions for other future operations.

5-4. Tactically, the corps conducts offensive operations to achieve operational objectives for the land
component commander. Offensive operations allow the corps to seize, retain, and exploit the initiative.
Offensive operations require a corps to set conditions for the success of its subordinate divisions. Corps
synchronize the employment of joint capabilities to support offensive action by divisions and other lower
echelons. The corps employs combinations of joint and Army capabilities to achieve convergence, which
provides windows of opportunity for a division or more to exploit. (See Appendix D – XIV Corps and the
Battle for Manila for an example of the C2, logistic, and terrain challenges for a corps operating as a tactical
formation and as a tactical headquarters.)

5-5. The characteristics of the offense have some unique aspects at the corps level. Audacious attacks require

corps commanders to accept substantial risk. Since a JFC assigns corps

objectives essential to the success of a campaign, the commander’s Characteristics of the Offense

risk-taking should be commensurate with the stakes involved.             • Audacity.

Concentrating the effects of combat power without providing the          • Concentration.

enemy lucrative targets or acting predictably requires significant          • Surprise.
agility inside and among the corps’ formations. The corps is likewise
the best positioned and resourced echelon to achieve convergence,           • Tempo.
which requires thoughtful integration of joint and Army capabilities at  (See ADP 3-90 for an expanded
echelon, as well as their synchronized concentration in the right times  discussion of the offense.)

and places. Achieving corps-level surprise is difficult on an

increasingly transparent battlefield. Employing deception, like feints or demonstrations, can help divisions

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92                                                                  83


---

Chapter 5

     achieve surprise. Devising courses of action that conceal the actual main effort from the enemy for as long
     as possible can also create surprise. Corps commanders adjust tempo based on the situation. By deliberately
     controlling tempo, commanders maintain momentum and retain the initiative while avoiding culmination.
     Ultimately, it is the ability to sustain an advantageous tempo relative to the enemy that ensures offensive
     success.

     5-6. Ultimately, offensive operations end in a transition, either to different types of operations or a conclusion
     of conflict. Corps-level transitions between operations are extensive and can take hours to days to occur since
     they involve multiple divisions and separate brigades moving from one focus to another as part of a larger
     effort. Even as it manages current operations by its divisions, the corps plans and sets conditions for future
     operations.

FORMS OF MANEUVER

     5-7. The forms describe the arrangement of friendly forces in relationship to enemy forces. The Army has
     five forms of maneuver—envelopment, frontal attack, infiltration, penetration, and turning movement. Corps
     synchronize warfighting functions to execute these forms of maneuver. (See ADP 3-90 for more information
     on the five forms of maneuver.)

     5-8. Forms of maneuver are distinct tactical combinations of fire and movement with a unique set of doctrinal
     characteristics that differ primarily in the relationship between the maneuvering force and the enemy (ADP
     3-90). Corps commanders use the forms of maneuver as the basis for courses of action to create positions of
     advantage. A commander directs operations using the forms of maneuver and offensive operations.
     Employed sequentially and in combination, they effectively apply combat power in ways that create enemy
     dilemmas. A corps can task organize subordinate organizations for certain purposes, such as a penetration
     division and an exploitation division.

             Note. A corps would not conduct an infiltration, but it could task a subordinate division to conduct
             one with its forces. The corps would then support the infiltration with a supporting deception
             operation and electromagnetic attack to neutralize enemy sensors.

     5-9. Turning movements and envelopments tend to be the preferred forms of maneuver because historically
     they achieve decisive results at lower cost than penetrations and frontal attacks. Commanders may need to
     perform frontal attacks and penetrations, especially if the terrain provides few other options or they have an
     overall superiority of forces across the front. Corps main and supporting effort divisions may execute
     different forms of maneuver simultaneously.

ENVELOPMENT

     5-10. In an envelopment, a corps uses a division or brigade as a supporting effort to fix or deceive enemy
     forces to keep them from massing against the main effort seeking to attack an enemy flank. A corps can also
     use a supporting effort to make enemy forces reveal the location of high-value or high-payoff targets,
     allowing the corps to target them with fires. Depending on the size of the enemy force and its mobility, the
     corps may keep a brigade or a division as a reserve to maximize the corps’ ability to destroy the enemy or
     exploit other opportunities. Typical risks of this operation are culmination and a too shallow envelopment,
     which could create a friendly assailable flank. Double envelopments risk fratricide by converging forces and
     require significant control measures. A corps may mitigate the effects of potential risks with emphasis on
     sustainment during planning, detailed rehearsals in preparation, and assessment of key indicators during
     execution. (See figure 5-1 for a depiction of a single envelopment and figure 5-2 for a depiction of a double
     envelopment.)

84  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                           Corps Offensive and Defensive Operations

                 Figure 5-1. Single envelopment

                 Figure 5-2. Double Envelopment

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92                        85


---

Chapter 5

FRONTAL ATTACK

     5-11. A corps may attempt to destroy a division or smaller element with a frontal attack, but an envelopment
     or turning movement are preferred when possible. A frontal attack is used when time and urgency are among
     the most important factors. It is the easiest form of maneuver to organize and support, but it is the costliest
     form of maneuver since it is the one the enemy can most easily prepare to defend against. (See figure 5-3 for
     a graphic depiction of a frontal attack. See FM 3-90 for more information on frontal attacks.)

                                                  Figure 5-3. Frontal Attack

PENETRATION

     5-12. A penetration is necessary when there is no assailable enemy flank, and the corps commander must
     create one by creating a gap in one part of the enemy defense that elements of the corps can exploit to envelop
     or turn the other parts of the enemy defense. Creating a penetration requires the corps to mass overwhelming
     combat power against a single axis of advance while preventing the enemy from doing the same in response.
     Massing the combat power of a division through the depth of enemy battalion sized portion of the main
     defensive zone, while preventing the rest of the enemy division from effectively supporting enemy units in
     that area, would be an example. Corps normally employ supporting attacks and deep operations to fix or draw
     off enemy reserves. This includes enemy counterattack forces of regimental size or larger which are either
     adjacent to or in subsequent echelons outside of the assigned AO of the division conducting the penetration.
     After the main defensive zone is penetrated, the corps then commits trailing divisions to either further
     penetrate or envelop subsequent defensive zones of the defending enemy army. (See figure 5-4 for a graphic
     depiction of a penetration.)

86  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                           Corps Offensive and Defensive Operations

                                                   Figure 5-4. Penetration

TURNING MOVEMENT

     5-13. Like an envelopment, a corps will generally use a division or brigade in a supporting effort to fix or
     deceive the enemy main defense. Unlike an envelopment, a turning movement seeks to avoid the enemy’s
     prepared defenses, placing friendly force in positions that directly threaten the enemy’s line of
     communications and compelling the enemy to reposition or retreat to avoid encirclement. This can entail
     risk, as some corps subordinate units may not be mutually supportive during phases of the operation. If the
     corps employs a fixing attack as a supporting effort, the main and supporting effort divisions must be
     sufficiently strong and mobile to operate independently due to the distances separating them. (See figure 5-5
     on page 88 for a depiction of a turning movement.)

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  87


---

Chapter 5

                                              Figure 5-5. Turning Movement

PRIMARY OFFENSIVE OPERATIONS

     5-14. Corps conduct the four primary offensive operations: movement to contact, attack, exploitation, and
     pursuit. An offensive operation can progressively transition from one type to another; for example a
     movement to contact to an attack, then to an exploitation, and finally to a pursuit. However, not all offensive
     operations will necessarily include each type. Enabling operations are a critical component of offensive
     operations that corps commanders must also account for during planning offensive operations. (See Chapter
     6 for more information on enabling operations.)

     5-15. Corps offensive operations consume large amounts of supplies over ever-lengthening lines of
     communication that must be secured. Effective support requires forward-positioned sustainment units as
     mobile as the maneuver units they support, able to provide the volume of support necessary to support the
     desired tempo of operations. Commanders must synchronize the back-and-forth movement of sustainment
     units along their MSR and ASR to ensure effective support while accounting for all the other traffic that
     continuously moves along the routes. (See FM 4-0 and ATP 4-92 for more information on optimizing
     sustainment operations.)

MOVEMENT TO CONTACT

     5-16. A movement to contact is a type of offensive operation designed to establish or regain contact to
     develop the situation (FM 3-90). Properly executed, a movement to contact develops the situation while
     maintaining the corps’ freedom of action for subsequent operations. The corps commander is typically less
     focused on the individual movement and maneuver of subordinates, deferring their C2 to the division
     commanders. The corps allows the divisions to develop the situation within the commander’s intent, which
     should include guidance about risk and how the divisions should exploit opportunities. The corps focuses on
     reducing risks to the operation, generally related to intelligence, protection, sustainment, and fire support. To
     prevent culmination, the corps staff ensures the concept of sustainment provides the logistics necessary for

88         ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                         Corps Offensive and Defensive Operations

     prolonged movement and the expected results of the movement of contact. The corps likewise assigns
     responsibility for securing its LOCs. Corps FSEs plan and execute targets that support the divisions and
     should be prepared to focus on counterfires as enemy long-range artillery units reveal themselves.

     5-17. The corps may execute a movement to contact to develop the situation and create favorable conditions
     for subsequent tactical actions. This generally occurs because an enemy has successfully broken contact or
     has been able to prevent sufficient friendly information collection. During a movement to contact, the corps
     focuses on reducing risks to divisions by focusing on intelligence synchronization, protection, fire support,
     and sustainment. (See figure 5-6 on page 90 for a depiction of a corps movement to contact organization.)

Corps Considerations

     5-18. Command and control: The corps must strike acceptable balance between decentralized execution
     through mission command orders with the need to maintain situational awareness with its divisions. This
     requires in-depth communications planning that includes extending the tactical network as far forward as
     necessary to maintain contact with the divisions and support their networks if necessary. This helps with the
     task of maintaining an accurate corps-wide common operational picture. The corps commander must
     carefully consider the location and movement of corps command posts to best maintain control over the
     operation. As the divisions advance, their rear areas may expand beyond their ability to control. This requires
     the corps to plan for shifting division rear boundaries forward as the divisions move.

     5-19. Movement and maneuver: The biggest challenge for the corps with movement and maneuver will be
     ensuring that divisions maintain mutual support with each other and mitigating the risk when doing so is not
     possible. These plans must be flexible enough to account for the initiative that the corps commander expects
     division commanders to exercise.

     5-20. Fires: The corps creates a fire support plan that achieves effective balance between deliberate and
     dynamic targeting in the corps close and deep areas. Since a movement to contact means that the corps does
     not have extensive intelligence on enemy dispositions, the corps should anticipate weighting its focus
     dynamic targeting missions, particularly counterfires. The plan must also account for displacing artillery units
     and their logistics elements to maintain acceptable supporting ranges for both the advancing divisions and
     division deep operations.

     5-21. Sustainment: The sustainment concept of support must account for expanding lines of communication
     that increase security requirements and decrease throughput. It may also require the corps to establish new
     support areas to support anticipated branches and sequels that support different degrees of division success.
     Casualty evacuation, fuel, ammunition, and repair parts may require specific emphasis.

     5-22. Protection: The corps requires a plan to deal with bypassed enemy units and detainees. The movement
     to contact may deliberately bypass enemy units to maintain tempo and gain contact with the enemy main
     body. The corps should allocate sufficient combat power to deal with bypassed forces without employing its
     reserve. The corps submits updates to the JFC’s critical asset list if it repositions its support areas and
     command posts, since the JFACC may need to adjust the air defense coverage scheme to protect them.
     Depending on the enemy situation, it may be necessary to keep CBRN decontamination assets forward in the
     corps close area to support the divisions after they make contact.

Organizing the Corps for Movement to Contact

     5-23. A corps generally organizes for a movement to contact with forward and flank security forces, a main
     body, and a rear guard. The reserve, fires, engineers, and sustainment assets are included in the main body.
     Depending upon the situation, these assets may be placed under the tactical control of subordinate divisions
     for their movement and protection while the corps commander retains the authority for their employment.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  89


---

Chapter 5

                              Figure 5-6. Corps Movement to Contact Organization

Security Operations

     5-24. Corps security operations refine the intelligence picture and identify exploitable opportunities. A corps
     has the combat power to create a covering force due to accessibility to joint capabilities and other resources
     in addition to the divisions, such as its functional and multifunctional brigades. Depending on the mission
     variables of METT-TC (I), the corps employs a covering force and guard force. A corps may employ a screen
     to the flank to protect the main body. (See Chapter 6 for additional information on security operations.)

Main Body

     5-25. The main body contains the bulk of the corps combat power. The corps staff organizes the units and
     march columns to facilitate an immediate attack or possibly hasty defense from the march. Elements of the
     main body may also be committed to reduce pockets of resistance fixed or bypassed by the advance guard.

Reserve

     5-26. The commander organizes the reserve to exploit success or prevent enemy forces from destroying a
     portion of the force. During a movement to contact, the corps reserve is generally a brigade-sized element.
     Positioning of the reserve is critical, since it may need to move rapidly in any one of multiple directions.
     Upon commitment of the reserve, the corps commander may give it priority of fires.

ATTACK

     5-27. A corps conducts the attack until it creates conditions for exploitation or pursuit. The corps staff may
     describe an attack as hasty or deliberate, depending on the time available for assessing the situation, planning,
     and preparing. An enemy defense will likely have several echelons of defending forces arrayed in depth

90         ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                         Corps Offensive and Defensive Operations

     through which an attacking force must fight. (See TC 7-100.2 for more information on opposing force tactics.
     See figure 5-7 on page 92 for a depiction of a corps attack.)

Corps Considerations

     5-28. Command and control: Effective corps control measures synchronize operations while enabling
     maximum subordinate freedom of action. Detailed, in-depth planning of control measures maximizes
     flexibility during execution, allowing subordinate divisions to shift from known points to execute branches
     and sequels. Since the purpose of the attack is to create conditions for exploitation or pursuit, the corps must
     identify when and where that would occur and then plan for executing them. Corps plan how and when to
     shift the unit boundaries to accommodate shifts in main and supporting efforts or a transition to exploitation
     and pursuit. The corps locates its command posts where they will be most effective and displaces one or more
     of them forward as necessary to maintain C2 of the divisions as they advance.

     5-29. Movement and maneuver: The corps apportions additional aviation and engineer assets to divisions
     as required. Aviation may provide mobility to attacking units, thereby increasing the range of options
     available to the corps or division commanders. Attack aviation can support maneuver and provides the
     capability to attack additional decisive points to complement ground forces. Engineers provide mobility and
     gap crossing capabilities to the divisions. The location of the corps reserve is important. The corps
     commander can reduce risk to the attack by properly positioning the corps reserve where it can quickly
     support the main effort.

     5-30. Fires: The corps balances its fires capabilities between the corps’ deep and close areas. In particular,
     the corps will manage the counter-fires fight during the attack and must retain capabilities for that purpose.
     It also attacks the enemy’s air defenses with fires, in support of Army aviation and USAF strikes. While fires
     are not the sole means of achieving convergence, they provide a significant proportion of the lethal and
     non-lethal effects the corps can use to set conditions for the divisions to exploit.

     5-31. Sustainment: Medical evacuation and casualty evacuation will be a major consideration for the corps.
     For logistics, sustainment planners must carefully consider the Class III petroleum, oil, and lubricant
     requirements and the Class V ammunition requirements. The maintenance plan for battle damage will be
     critical to maintaining tempo. Depending on the scope of the attack, a robust Class VII major items resupply
     plan may be necessary to offset battle loss or battle damages requiring lengthy repairs. Additionally, water
     resupply will be critical to maintaining the effectiveness of the individual Soldier in combat operations.
     Finally, the corps must plan for the limited reorganization of subordinate units during the operation followed
     by larger reconstitution operations prior to the next one.

     5-32. Protection: The corps protection cell supports the commander by recommending shifts in protection
     priorities to support the tempo of operations and attacking force momentum. The corps protection cell
     anticipates changes and reassesses protection priorities throughout operations. In accordance with the corps
     commander’s priorities, the corps staff ensures that all operations are covered to some extent by lethal and
     non-lethal protection. The corps develops a detainee operations plan (see FM 3-63 for more information on
     detainee operations), which will require a corps-run detainee holding area. The plan should address how to
     transport detainees to the theater detention facility. The corps maintains AMD coverage of critical capabilities
     as they reposition in support of the divisions. During the transition to defense, the divisions will be vulnerable
     to counterattack and engineer support will be vital to establishing battle and other survivability positions.

     5-33. Intelligence: The corps G2 confirms the enemy course of action and coordinates to collect against the
     commander’s critical information requirements. Intelligence confirms the enemy disposition, composition,
     and strength for the corps’ attacking divisions. Additionally, the corps information collection plan should
     prioritize capabilities based upon the commander’s intent across the deep, close, and rear framework.

     5-34. Deception: Deceiving enemy forces is critical to preserving combat power and creating favorable force
     ratios. Attacking a comparable-sized or larger force requires the corps to accept risks in a part or parts of its
     AO to achieve concentration at decisive points. TAC-D plans are necessary to ensure that the corps can create
     ambiguity for enemy commanders seeking to determine the friendly course of action. (See Chapter 6 for
     more information on TAC-D.)

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  91


---

Chapter 5

                                                  Figure 5-7. Corps Attack

Variations of Attack

     5-35. Variations of the attack for a corps are counterattack, raid, and spoiling attack. The commander’s intent
     and the mission variables guide which of these variations of attack to employ. Corps can conduct the other
     variations, except for a raid, as either a hasty or a deliberate operation. Because of their complicated nature
     and necessary synchronization, forces always conduct raids as deliberate operations.

Counterattack

     5-36. Counterattack is a variation of attack by a defending force against an attacking enemy force (FM 3-90).
     Units plan and conduct counterattacks to attack enemy forces when and where enemy forces are most
     vulnerable. The two methods of counterattacks are major and local.

     5-37. A major counterattack seizes the initiative from an enemy force through offensive action after an
     enemy force launches an attack. Local counterattacks are attacks to retake positions lost to enemy action or
     to exploit targets of opportunity. These attacks may be executed by an entire corps as part of a JFLCC plan
     or by a subordinate unit of the corps. They may include both air and ground maneuver units.

Raid

     5-38. A raid is a variation of attack to temporarily seize an objective with a planned withdrawal (FM 3-90).
     In a corps assigned area, brigade combat teams and smaller-sized units normally conduct raids. Certain SOF

92         ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                         Corps Offensive and Defensive Operations

     units (including ranger units), attack helicopter units, and air assault units are ideal for conducting raids. (See
     the vignette Task Force Normandy for an attack helicopter raid example.) Raids within a corps AO may be
     planned as a phase of an operation or as branches or sequels to an operation. If a raid is preplanned, the
     raiding force should be an element of the corps reserve.

             Note. When raids are conducted using air assault or attack helicopter units, those operations must
             be coordinated with the JFACC and conducted under conditions of air parity, otherwise they
             should be planned as deliberate attacks.

                                        Task Force Normandy

          OPERATION DESERT STORM was the offensive mission designed to expel Iraqi
          forces from Kuwait. The offensive began with a massive allied air attack to destroy the
          Iraqis’ air defenses and communications, destroy 50 percent of the occupying Iraqi
          army, and complete the victory with a ground attack to push the surviving Iraqis out of
          Kuwait.

          The air campaign needed an element of surprise to ensure the first wave of bombers
          could penetrate Iraqi early warning radar sites undetected. The plan required the
          destruction of the enemy’s outer perimeter of radar sites and the anti-aircraft guns
          protecting them.

          The U.S. Central Command planners proposed a raid to precede the bombers and
          eliminate the two most critical sites. The task went to 1-101st Aviation Battalion of the
          101st Airborne Division, and the name of the force that executed the operation was
          Task Force Normandy. The task force consisted of eight AH-64 Apache helicopters
          from the division and four MH-53J Pave Low helicopters from the Air Force 20th
          Special Operations Squadron. The task force commander split the force into two
          groups, one group for each radar site. The plan called for the Air Force Pave Lows,
          with their precise navigation capability, to escort the Apaches. Then, at a precisely
          coordinated time, the Apaches, each armed with 8 Hellfire missiles, 19 Hydra 2.75-inch
          flechette rockets, and 800 to 1,000 rounds of 30-millimeter ammunition, would destroy
          the targets.

          The two groups flew nearly parallel routes at an altitude of 50 feet (15 meters) at a
          120-knot airspeed and arrived at their firing positions. Just before 0238 hours on 17
          January 1991, Task Force Normandy launched its first missiles. The raid was
          successful. The task force’s assault crippled enemy early-warning communications
          and prevented loss of coalition aircraft at the start of the air campaign.

Spoiling Attack

     5-39. A spoiling attack is a variation of an attack employed against an enemy preparing for an attack (FM
     3-90). The objective of a spoiling attack is to disrupt the enemy force’s offensive capabilities and timelines
     while destroying targeted enemy personnel and equipment. A spoiling attack is not to seize terrain or other
     physical objectives.

     5-40. The extensive number of units operating in a typical corps AO and the density of vehicular traffic
     sharing available routes can hinder the movement of an attacking ground maneuver unit. Consequently, the
     corps staff addresses movement during the planning stages, especially if using ground maneuver units in a
     counterattack or spoiling attack. The corps plan includes positioning of the attacking unit, routes, passage of
     lines, size of the attacking unit, and C2. (See FM 3-90 for more information on counterattacks and spoiling
     attacks.)

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  93


---

Chapter 5

EXPLOITATION

     5-41. A corps exploits the disruptive effects of a successful attack. An exploitation is a type of offensive
     operation following a successful attack to disorganize the enemy in depth (FM 3-90). Exploitations seek to
     disintegrate enemy forces to the point where they have no alternative but to surrender, retreat, or face
     complete destruction.

     5-42. Exploitations take advantage of tactical opportunities. Planning for exploitations will usually be
     provisional, normally as branches or sequels. It is difficult to foresee which forces are in position to lead an
     exploitation until the situation develops. When it does, the choice will be between exploiting with committed
     forces (which is faster) or using an uncommitted unit. Exploitation is the chief means of translating tactical
     success into operational advantage.

     5-43. When conducting an exploitation, the corps attacks the enemy’s cohesiveness as a system. The corps
     attacks by—

          • Securing objectives deep in the enemy rear.
          • Forcing critical enemy capabilities to reposition.
          • Cutting major LOCs.
          • Containing major enemy forces.
          • Disrupting, destroying, or dispersing major enemy reserves.
          • Destroying critical enemy logistic resources—especially fuel and ammunition.
          • Positioning itself to support the land component-level campaign plan against follow-on forces or

                subsequent objectives.

Corps Considerations

     5-44. A corps requires sufficient combat power to destroy organized enemy resistance bypassed by exploiting
     forces. In addition to the follow and support force, a corps retains under corps control only those follow and
     support forces necessary to ensure flexibility of operations, continued momentum of the advance, and
     minimum essential security. An exploitation force and a follow-and-support force must maintain direct
     communications. The corps staff ensures sustainment elements move and communicate to support the overall
     corps operation. (See figure 5-8 on page 96 for a depiction of a corps attacks followed by an exploitation.)

     5-45. Command and control: As during a movement to contact, the corps must control subordinates enough
     to prevent fratricide and premature culmination without inhibiting subordinate initiative. Proper graphical
     control and fire support coordination measures are vital to success. The conditions for conducting an
     exploitation may present themselves quickly, so the initial guidance to a division may be commander to
     commander, supplemented with fragmentary orders as the situation develops. The corps commander will use
     these fragmentary orders to establish the corps limit of advance, provide a framework for the scheme of
     maneuver (such as zones, routes, and objectives), adjust the operational framework boundaries, and
     communicate the conditions for transition to subsequent operations. One other critical consideration is the
     management of command posts and the overall corps C2 system. The corps must plan for the extension of
     tactical networks to support advancing corps units, as well as the movement of subsequent tactical command
     post locations.

     5-46. Movement and maneuver: Maximizing the effect of an exploitation requires accepting some risks for
     a significant reward. The corps may need to shift which division is the main effort and will likely need to
     reposition forces to reinforce success on a particular movement corridor. This may require one or more hasty
     passages of lines and rapid changes to task organization based upon the situation on the ground. Subordinate
     formations may find themselves outrunning their fire support and sustainment if the corps is not quickly
     anticipating requirements and shifting priorities to seize opportunities as the situation develops. Speed of
     movement can reduce risk during a successful exploitation, but commanders must account for the risk of
     units moving so far from their sources of support that they become vulnerable to counterattacks when they
     halt.

     5-47. Fires: The corps adjusts its employment of deep fires with its support to the divisions as the situation
     develops. Corps deep fires should focus on solving enemy problems that threaten exploitation objectives and
     reducing risk in places where the divisions are most exposed, which may be on the flanks of the penetration.

94  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                   Corps Offensive and Defensive Operations

Corps close fires in support of the divisions should focus on counter-fires and providing reinforcing mass.
Due to the fluidity of the situation, the corps should plan for more dynamic targeting during an exploitation.
The advance of subordinate divisions likely requires a displacement plan for corps artillery units to ensure
adequate support for the exploitation and good positioning for transitions to other operations.

5-48. Sustainment: The sustainment focus is supporting rapid movement and maneuver for the exploiting
forces. This includes emphasis on Class III, V, and IX, as well as a maintenance plan that prioritizes mobility
capabilities and conducting maintenance as far forward as possible. The corps may need to establish new
support areas to maintain throughput to exploiting forces. The medical support plan should be adjusted for
the transportation of patients along longer lines of communication than previously anticipated. Examples
include adjusting the ambulance shuttle system or establishing new Role 2 locations.

5-49. Protection: Withdrawing enemy forces will attempt to delay the friendly advance with blocking
positions supported by fires. As the exploitation develops, the corps must be prepared to dynamically shift
protection priorities to mitigate risks. The corps protects subordinate divisions by targeting enemy fires
capabilities and by providing air defense for lead elements of the corps that are beyond the original planned
AMD umbrellas.

5-50. An exploitation may result in large numbers of enemy prisoners placing a burden on lead friendly
maneuver forces. The corps should position military police as far forward as possible to take control of enemy
prisoners ensuring freedom of maneuver in the exploitation. Populace and resource control must also be
considered to protect civilian areas that were previously isolated from the FLOT that may suddenly be
exposed to retreating enemy and advancing friendly forces. The corps should plan for the positive control of
civilians to minimize interference in friendly operations, deny bypassed enemy forces from concealing
themselves among civilians, and sustainment of civilians as necessary when conditions allow. Civil affairs
units will be essential to this. (See FM 3-57 for more information on civil affairs operations.)

5-51. Intelligence: The nature of an exploitation requires the corps to prioritize intelligence capabilities
against providing the divisions situational awareness and supporting dynamic targeting. The corps and
division commanders must understand the rapidly changing enemy composition, disposition, and strength.
Enemy attempts to re-establish a coherent defense, the location of major obstacles and reserves, and the
repositioning of significant capabilities that can influence the depth of the penetration are examples of focus
areas. Corps should also have a detailed plan for collecting processing, exploiting, and disseminating
information from detainees, civilians, and recovered enemy equipment.

5-52. During exploitation or pursuit, corps units normally move much more rapidly over greater distances
than during an attack. Extended distances make it difficult for a corps-controlled reserve to provide
responsive support to leading elements during these operations unless it is trailing those elements within
supporting distance on the ground or they are air mobile (attack helicopter or air assault) forces. The
commander prioritizes the positioning of the reserve to reinforce the success of the main effort first, then to
counter the worst-case enemy counterattack.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  95


---

Chapter 5

                                Figure 5-8. Corps Attack Followed by Exploitation

PURSUIT

     5-53. Pursuits are conducted to catch or cut off enemy forces attempting to escape, with the intent to destroy
     or capture them and prevent their reorganization into an effective force. Pursuit along a wide front requires
     the complete collapse of the enemy’s defense. This normally follows a successful exploitation and completes
     the destruction of an enemy force that has lost the means or the will to defend and is attempting to retreat as
     quickly as possible. Unlike an exploitation, in which the corps seeks to avoid enemy combat units to defeat
     the enemy support systems, the corps conducting a pursuit focuses on destroying the fleeing enemy force.
     Although the plan may designate terrain objectives, destruction of the enemy force is the goal.

     5-54. A corps focuses on reducing the risks of a pursuit, since there is a high probability of outrunning
     sustainment support. Unless the corps staff planned the pursuit as a sequel, the commander may delay its
     execution until properly prepared. However, time spent on additional preparation gives the enemy time to
     get away and can result in a missed opportunity. If the corps staff planned the sequel, its goal would be to
     maintain pressure on the fleeing enemy formation by staying in contact. Successful completion of such a plan
     demands responsive sustainment and continuous fire and AMD support.

96         ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                   Corps Offensive and Defensive Operations

5-55. Pursuit operations require a direct pressure force and normally an encircling force. An encircling force
is the force that maneuvers to the rear or flank of the enemy to block the enemy’s escape (FM 3-90). Although
armored forces are normally preferred as encircling forces, motorized, attack helicopter, airborne, and air
assault forces can also encircle. During pursuit operations, a corps may encounter encircled enemy forces,
and it should plan for their defeat. The pursuing forces must clearly understand what size of an enemy force
to bypass and what size to destroy. (See figure 5-9 for a depiction of a corps pursuit.)

                                            Figure 5-9. Corps Pursuit

5-56. During pursuit operations, sustainment arrangements must be flexible and capable of rapid response.
Priority of support typically goes to the main effort, and fuel and ammunition are commonly the chief logistic
requirements. Planners can alleviate some problems created by road congestion and route disruptions through
other means of resupply such as air transportation. Nevertheless, securing LOCs suitable to support the
transportation of adequate supplies is essential to a successful pursuit. The pursuit, even more than the
exploitation, is a transportation-intensive operation.

5-57. During exploitation and pursuit, corps deep operations normally focus on preventing the introduction
of the enemy maneuver forces not already engaged by its divisions. They can also be used to prevent enemy

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  97


---

Chapter 5

     units out of contact with friendly divisions from establishing a coherent defense. At the conclusion of a
     pursuit, corps deep operations should focus on preventing any sort of effective enemy counter-attacks.

ENCIRCLEMENT OF AN ENEMY FORCE

     5-58. Corps conduct encirclement operations to isolate and destroy enemy forces. Encirclement is where one
     force loses its freedom of maneuver because an opposing force is able to isolate it by controlling all ground
     lines of communications and reinforcement (FM 3-90). Encircling operations seek to cut off evacuation and
     reinforcement routes. A corps may plan offensive operations with the express intent of encircling enemy
     forces. However, enemy forces may also become encircled by circumstance, such as when bypassed during
     an exploitation, rather than deliberate planning.

     5-59. An encircling command should identify and set the conditions of an encirclement before it develops to
     deny enemy forces as many advantages as possible before surrounding it. If an unexpected encirclement
     occurs, enemy forces may have the opportunity to occupy advantageous terrain or secure a better position.
     This would make reduction much more difficult for the encircling force.

     5-60. The corps staff should also understand that bypassed and encircled forces can behave in several ways.
     They may—

          • Surrender, remain stationary, and cause no interference.
          • Remain stationary and, by virtue of their position, interrupt friendly operations.
          • Move (attack) to rejoin their own lines.
          • Move (attack) with the intent to interrupt friendly operations.

     Enemy forces may also execute a combination of these options. (See figure 5-10 for a depiction of a corps
     encirclement.)

    Figure 5-10. Corps Encirclement

98  ATP 3-92                         15 January 2026


---

                                                             Corps Offensive and Defensive Operations

5-61. Encirclements can occur because of an operational action, a tactical action, or a combination of both.
Regardless of how an encirclement occurs or the size of the forces involved, its reduction is strictly a tactical
action. Once a commander decides to reduce an encirclement, the next decision is typically the reduction
method: reduction by fire or reduction by maneuver. Reduction by maneuver has four different techniques:
squeeze, hammer and anvil, wedge, and escape route. (See FM 3-90 for reduction techniques.)

SECTION II – DEFENSIVE OPERATIONS

5-62. The purpose of a corps defense is to create conditions for the offensive that allow the corps to regain
the initiative. A defensive operation is an operation to defeat an enemy attack, gain time, economize forces,
and develop conditions favorable for offensive or stability operations. The defense cannot be purely passive.
A defender must seek opportunities to attack enemy forces throughout the depth of their formations. Corps
commanders incorporate counterattacks and spoiling attacks into their overall plans. Plans should be flexible
enough in concept and task organization to permit rapid changes.

5-63. Corps usually conduct a combination of area and mobile defenses, even if part of a larger retrograde.

The reasons a corps typically conducts the defense include—

• Countering enemy action

• Retaining decisive terrain or denying a vital area to an enemy.

• Attriting or fixing an enemy as a prelude to the offense.

• Increase an enemy’s vulnerability by forcing an enemy                     Characteristics of the Defense
     commander to concentrate subordinate forces.                              • Disruption.

ORGANIZATION OF THE DEFENSE                                                    • Flexibility.
                                                                               • Maneuver.
     5-64. During large-scale combat operations, the corps is most likely      • Mass and concentration.
     to conduct a mobile defense due to the size of the AO, the number of      • Depth.
     subordinate formations, and the purpose of the corps’ operations. A       • Preparation.
     corps area defense (with all forces) is unusual. Normally a corps has     • Security.
     its divisions conduct different forms of the defense simultaneously.   (See ADP 3-90 for an expanded
     One division can conduct an area defense while another division        discussion of the defense.)
     conducts a mobile defense, based upon terrain and the composition

of the divisions. While one or two divisions are attacking, another

division could be conducting an area defense. One division could conduct a mobile defense, while another

conducts a retrograde. Organization of a corps defense is therefore situationally dependent, and it is critical

for the corps to understand that the corps’ approach will not mirror that of each subordinate echelon during

an operation. A division that is part of a corps mobile defense may have a brigade defending key terrain from

a fixed battle position to facilitate friendly maneuver.

AREA DEFENSE

5-65. An area defense is a type of defensive operation that concentrates on denying enemy forces access to
designated terrain for a specific time rather than destroying the enemy outright (ADP 3-90). A corps may
conduct an area defense when directed to hold key terrain by their higher headquarters or in the event of
becoming surrounded by an attacking enemy force. They may use an area defense to transition to offensive
operations, retrograde, or stability operations.

Corps Considerations

     5-66. Command and control: The corps provides general guidance to the divisions and ensures that their
     plans are integrated into the overall defensive scheme. The corps is responsible for synchronizing the area
     defense and accounting for any areas not assigned to a subordinate unit. Because the divisions are largely
     terrain focused, the commander and staff should focus on reducing vulnerabilities to massed enemy fires.
     The corps must also anticipate and plan for transition points to other types of enabling, defensive, offensive,
     or stability operations.

     5-67. Movement and maneuver: With an eye to retaining flexibility, the corps should ensure that the
     defense plan preserves desired maneuver corridors into and out of the MBA. The corps allocates engineer

15 January 2026            ATP 3-92                                         99


---

Chapter 5

     capabilities to build defense and engagement areas in support of corps and division requirements.
     Additionally, the corps needs to develop a relief-in-place plan for extended area defenses. Replacement
     operations are complicated, likely to be necessary during an extended defense, and especially difficult to
     conduct under contact.

     5-68. Fires: The corps’ focus for fires will be on attriting attacking forces and their supporting elements
     throughout the depth of their echelons. The corps must dynamically synchronize joint capabilities to provide
     advantages to its divisions. Additionally, the corps must have a positioning and protection plan for its firing
     units to ensure their survival while also ensuring that they can support the divisions.

     5-69. Sustainment: Large quantities of Class IV construction materials will be essential to preparing the
     defense. The corps should anticipate increased Class V ammunition consumption during a defense and plan
     for resupply. Depending on the duration of the area defense, Class VII major items may become critical to
     offset battle loss or battle damage capabilities. Since the area defense is relatively static, the corps has an
     opportunity to maximize the efficiency of Role 2 and Role 3 facilities but must protect them.

     5-70. Protection: Protection priorities in the defense will have an increased focus on survivability measures
     to avoid and defeat enemy detection, complicate enemy targeting, and withstand enemy attacks in multiple
     domains. Engineers can significantly enhance the survivability of command posts and viability of MSR and
     ASR. Depending on the threat, the corps may need a decontamination plan for the MBA to enable units to
     continue the defense after a CBRN attack. It must also allocate air defense resources to protect the MBA in
     accordance with the defended asset list and the corps’ prioritized protection list and interdict airborne threats,
     especially those originating from the deep area, such as ballistic missiles, large unmanned aerial vehicles,
     and aircraft. Protection operations must also account for enemy cyber or electromagnetic warfare attacks.

     5-71. Intelligence: Intelligence capabilities focus on understanding the enemy course of action. They also
     prioritize developing situational understanding in accordance with planned branches and sequels or other
     transitions as the corps commander directs.

     5-72. Based on what the terrain allows, the commander chooses a variation of area defense that is most
     appropriate: defense of a linear obstacle, perimeter defense, or reverse slope defense. The commander also
     has the option of defending forward or defending in depth. (See FM 3-90 for an expanded discussion of the
     area defense.)

MOBILE DEFENSE

     5-73. A mobile defense is a type of defensive operation that concentrates on the destruction or defeat of the
     enemy through a decisive attack by a striking force (ADP 3-90). A mobile defense defeats or destroys enemy
     forces by allowing them to advance to a point where the striking force can conduct a decisive counterattack.
     The corps’ focus for a mobile defense is ensuring the assigned area for its divisions is suitable for and
     prepared enough to support the mobile strike force. A mobile defense requires an assigned area with
     considerable depth. Corps planners set conditions with obstacles and fire support to cause enemy forces to
     overextend their LOCs, expose their flanks, and dissipate their combat power. Then, the corps maneuvers
     friendly forces to cut off and destroy them, typically from a flank or the rear. Employing part of the corps in
     an area defense to block the main attack and the remainder as the striking force is the most effective form of
     mobile defense since it makes enemy forces maneuvering against or around a fixed area vulnerable to
     counterattack.

     5-74. For the mobile defense, a corps assigns its divisions and BCTs one of three basic roles: striking force,
     fixing force, or reserve. A corps may use deception to lure enemy forces into attacking the defense at a place
     and time most advantageous to the corps’ plan. The corps maneuvers the striking force and fixing forces into
     the best possible terrain to execute the defense. The corps then supports these maneuvers with fire support
     and sustainment to prevent culmination. (See figure 5-11 on page 102 for a depiction of a corps mobile
     defense.)

Corps Considerations

     5-75. Command and control: A significant priority for the corps is identifying the conditions necessary for
     employing the strike force, then validating those conditions are met. The corps needs an accurate, real-time
     common operational picture (known as COP) shared with the divisions to synchronize the activities of the

100  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                   Corps Offensive and Defensive Operations

fixing and strike forces. The difficulties of maintaining an accurate common operational picture increase
once the strike force maneuvers. The corps needs a robust communications plan for the strike force, which
may include augmenting them with long-range communications. The corps should also develop a plan for
the fixing force to counterattack. This is a complicated part of the mobile defense as it involves a transition
from defense to offense, potentially while the strike force is also still maneuvering and friendly forces are
moving toward each other. Finally, the mobile aspect of this form of defense will require boundary
adjustment considerations for the divisions.

5-76. Movement and maneuver: Mobility is essential to the mobile defense. The corps must ensure that the
strike force has adequate mobility to move along the necessary timelines determined during planning. The
fixing force will rely on engineers to assist with countermobility and development of engagement areas. The
strike force will likely require mobility augmentation (such as obstacle reduction). Accurately predicting how
long it takes the strike force to maneuver into direct contact with the enemy is essential, as does ensuring that
it is prepared to move to the right place at the right time to meet the commander’s intent.

5-77. Fires: The corps will likely focus on disrupting enemy follow-on forces to prevent them from
interfering with the strike force. The corps may also assist divisions with disrupting the enemy attacking
force to assist the fixing force. A unique challenge in this situation is ensuring the enemy attacking force
maintains sufficient momentum to get deep enough to expose their flanks to the strike force. At the same
time, fires should degrade enemy attack force capabilities before they make full contact with the fixing force.

5-78. Sustainment: The strike force’s lines of communication may rapidly extend from its original position,
requiring additional sustainment capabilities and planning. In particular, the strike force will likely require
increased Class III and maintenance support in preparation for its attack and to maintain tempo. Meanwhile,
the fixing force will likely require additional Class IV construction materials before the enemy attack and
Class V ammunition during it.

5-79. Protection: The strike force will likely air defense along its movement routes. Air defense is also an
important aspect of the protection of the fixing force. This will likely be accomplished through a combination
of area air defense and destruction of enemy long-range strike capabilities. Engineer capabilities are required
to support survivability and countermobility efforts of any fixing force. Civil affairs and military police
capabilities will assist the corps with managing civilians on the battlefield and preserving the strike force’s
freedom of maneuver. Military police should take control of detainees from maneuver units as far forward as
possible to minimize their impact on maneuver forces.

5-80. Intelligence: Intelligence priorities are likely to be split between supporting the fixing force and
supporting the decision to employ the strike force. The corps may support the fixing force by identifying
enemy composition, disposition, and strength, along with confirming the enemy course of action. Intelligence
capabilities will also likely support the strike force by gaining information on the enemy’s capabilities along
the axis of advance. Finally, corps intelligence will maintain awareness of the corps’ deep area to prevent
surprise and enable long-range fires.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  101


---

Chapter 5

                                           Figure 5-11. Corps Mobile Defense

RETROGRADE

     5-81. A retrograde moves friendly forces away from the enemy to set conditions for another action. The
     subsequent action could be an operation, such as an attack, or a task, such as conducting reconstitution.
     During a retrograde, the corps’ focus is on maintaining control of subordinate units and protecting the force.
     There are three variations of the retrograde.

          • A delay is when a force under pressure trades space for time by slowing down the enemy’s
                momentum and inflicting maximum damage on enemy forces without becoming decisively engaged
                (ADP 3-90). In delays, units yield ground to gain time while retaining flexibility and freedom of
                action to inflict the maximum damage on enemy forces. (See figure 5-12 on page 104 for a visual
                depiction of a corps delay.)

          • To withdraw is to disengage from an enemy force and move in a direction away from the enemy
                (ADP 3-90). Withdrawing units, whether all or part of a committed force, voluntarily disengage
                from an enemy force to preserve the withdrawing force or release it for a new mission. (See figure
                5-13 on page 104 for a visual depiction of a corps withdrawal.)

          • A retirement is when a force out of contact moves away from the enemy (ADP 3-90).

Corps Considerations

     5-82. Command and control: Maintaining control of subordinate units and situational awareness is a
     primary focus for the corps. The retrograde plan incorporates shifting control measures over time. Close

102        ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                   Corps Offensive and Defensive Operations

coordination amongst echelons and between organizations is critical to success, which requires a shared
common operational picture and understanding of the corps’ intent. The corps provides clear engagement
and disengagement criteria to ensure forces have maximum flexibility and do not become decisively engaged.
Controlling the tempo of the divisions requires a robust commander’s dialogue between the corps and
division commanders, as well as any part of the corps acting as a covering or guard force between the corps
main body and the enemy. If retrograding under contact, ensuring security and support for subordinate units
as they maneuver will be essential to success. The slowest units in the corps ideally move first, while the
most mobile ones cover their movement.

5-83. Movement and maneuver: Lines of communication and movement corridors must be kept open long
enough and secured enough to prevent units being decisively engaged by enemy enveloping forces. The corps
closely manages movement control teams and transportation assets, ensuring units with limited mobility are
accounted for in the plan. The corps needs a transportation asset and movement common operational picture.
Routes in the corps AO require two-way traffic to ensure resupply and maintain flexibility. Engineers ensure
MSR and ASR remain usable.

5-84. Fires: The movement of subordinate forces away from the enemy reduces available combat power in
the corps close area. The corps should employ fires to mitigate unfavorable force ratios. The corps balances
its employment of fires between committed and uncommitted enemy forces, as well as the counterfire
mission. The displacement of artillery units should ensure continuous fires support across the depths the
corps commander requires.

5-85. Sustainment: Sustainers must be prepared to move support areas and may need to limit on-hand
commodities to quantities that can be kept mobile. Maintenance evacuation of vehicles is a significant
consideration, as performing maintenance forward will be limited during movement away from the enemy.
Prepositioning Class III and V for artillery resupply and aviation forward arming and refueling points, as
well as establishing a refueling plan for units on the move, is important. Retrograding units casualty
evacuation planning may require longer distances between ambulance exchange points, which should in turn
shift as the FLOT moves rearward.

5-86. Protection: The corps shifts protection priorities to support the retrograde of its forces. MPs or other
units minimize civilian interference along the retrograde movement routes and facilitate the movement of
detainees to a corps holding area or the joint security area (JSA) when directed. Engineers ensure that routes
remain usable. Air defense units should be echeloned and repositioned to ensure coverage of corps units in
accordance with the defended assets list.

5-87. Intelligence: Intelligence capabilities focus on confirming enemy locations and their courses of action.
Early warning about any enemy attempt to cut off elements of the corps or block the corps retrograde should
be considered a priority intelligence requirement .

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  103

104        ATP 3-92                          15 January 2026

                                                                                         Corps Offensive and Defensive Operations

FRIENDLY FORCE ENCIRCLEMENT

     5-88. A corps assesses that a unit is encircled when enemy action has cut all ground routes of evacuation and
     reinforcement. A unit may become encircled—

          • When ordered to remain in a strong position on key terrain to deny enemy forces passage through a
                vital chokepoint following an enemy breakthrough.

          • When given a mission of becoming encircled as part of a larger plan.
          • When cut off from friendly forces unintentionally.

     5-89. Enemy forces may not be aware that they have initially encircled a friendly element, so immediate
     action to break out of the unfavorable situation with support from the corps is imperative. The enemy is likely
     to be unprepared for a hasty attack.

CORPS ENCIRCLED

     5-90. When becoming encircled, the corps must determine whether the next higher commander wants the
     force to break out or to defend the position. If the force is free to break out, the corps should attempt to break
     out before enemy forces block all escape routes. Encirclement reduces flexibility, maneuverability, and
     options for the friendly commander. The corps sustainment situation immediately begins to degrade more
     quickly once friendly MSR and ASR are cut. Once encircled, there are three possible subsequent operations:
     perimeter defense, conduct a breakout, or exfiltrate subordinate units.

Perimeter Defense

     5-91. Encircled forces may be required to maintain their positions and execute a perimeter defense.
     Important considerations for encircled perimeter defense are—

          • Mission of unit and higher echelon headquarters.
          • Terrain available for a defense.
          • Availability of reinforcements or relief of the force before enemy forces can eliminate it.
          • Enemy forces being more mobile than friendly forces, allowing enemy forces to destroy the

                encircled forces during a breakout attempt.

     (See figure 5-14 on page 108 for a depiction of an encircled corps defense.)

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  105


---

Chapter 5

                                         Figure 5-14. Encircled Corps Defense

     5-92. The commander must assume control of all encircled forces and rapidly assess their overall defensive
     posture. In assuming an effective perimeter defense, the commander has the responsibility—

          • To reestablish the chain of command in subordinate echelons with heavy losses or in those that have
               been disorganized.

          • To establish a viable defense. The unit must quickly establish an all-round defense on defensible
               terrain. The force may have to attack to seize the ground. The commander must reorganize and
               consolidate the unit expeditiously—
               ▪ To establish security by assigning responsibility to divisions, positioning security forces as far
                     forward as possible to provide early warning, initiating vigorous reconnaissance, establishing
                     local security throughout, and insisting on passive measures to maintain operations security.
               ▪ To reestablish communications rapidly with higher echelon headquarters and internally
                     between units.

          • To continue to employ intelligence assets. The corps staff must stay informed about the battle
               outside the encirclement, which assists with planning the breakout and enabling the commander to
               provide information about the enemy’s rear area to higher headquarters.

          • To plan for detainees and dislocated civilians.
          • To establish a reserve. If mobile forces are available, the commander establishes a centrally located

               reserve to take advantage of interior lines. If only dismounted forces are available, the commander
               establishes local reserves to react to potential threats.

106        ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                         Corps Offensive and Defensive Operations

          • To reorganize fire support. The commander, aided by the staff, centralizes the control of all field
                artillery, positions aviation assets throughout the area to limit vulnerability to counterfire, and
                considers available fire support systems, including CAS, from outside the encirclement.

          • To reorganize logistic support. After assessing the corps logistics posture, the commander
                centralizes supplies and establishes strict rationing supply economy procedures. If possible, the
                commander arranges aerial resupply and casualty evacuation with aviation and established
                centralized mortuary affairs operations.

          • To reorganize medical support. After assessing the corps medical posture, the commander, aided by
                the corps surgeon section, arranges aerial resupply and medical evacuation with aviation and
                establishes centralized medical operations.

          • To limit vulnerability to CBRN hazards. This may include a plan to attack to break out based on
                anticipated enemy use.

          • To maintain morale.
          • To continue the defense. Commanders use engineers to improve the defended area. They preclude

                enemy forces from splitting the corps through penetrations of the perimeter. An energetic defense,
                rapid reaction by reserves, and defense in depth can defeat such attempts. Combat losses may force
                the commander to reduce the size of the perimeter.

Breakout Operations

     5-93. A breakout is an operation conducted by an encircled force to regain freedom of movement or contact
     with friendly units. An encircled force normally attempts to conduct a breakout when one or more of the
     following is true:

          • Opportunity exists to attack.
          • An encircled force does not have sufficient relative combat power to defend itself against enemy

                forces attempting the encirclement.
          • An encircled force does not have adequate terrain available to conduct its defense.
          • An encircled force cannot sustain itself long enough for forces outside the encirclement to relieve

                it.

     (See figure 5-15 on page 108 for a depiction of a corps breakout operation.)

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  107


---

Chapter 5

                                       Figure 5-15. Corps Breakout Operation

     5-94. The attack to break out only differs from other offensive operations in that the force must maintain a
     simultaneous defense in other areas of the perimeter. Critical breakout considerations include—

          • Deceive the enemy. If immediate breakout is not possible, friendly forces attempt to deceive enemy
               force about preparations.

          • Exploit gaps and weaknesses. Early in the encirclement, there will be gaps or weaknesses in the
               encircling force. Reconnaissance will reveal them, and an attack should capitalize on them. An
               attack over less favorable terrain may be the best course of action if it avoids enemy strength and
               increases the chance for surprise. The breakout should not take the obvious route toward friendly
               lines unless there is no other alternative.

          • Exploit limited visibility.
          • Organize the breakout force. The commander normally reorganizes the force so that armored units

               lead the attack while the remainder of the force fights a delaying action or defends the perimeter
               during the initial stage of the breakout. After penetrating the encirclement, the main body moves out
               of the area behind the attacking force, followed by a rear guard. Other organization considerations
               are—
               ▪ Ensure sufficient engineer mobility support of the breakout force.

108        ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                         Corps Offensive and Defensive Operations

                ▪ Integrate the sustainment elements into the main body.
                ▪ Organize a diversionary attack if sufficient forces are available.
          • Coordinate internal and external supporting attacks.
          • Concentrate combat power. The commander directs overwhelming combat power at the breakout
                point. Forces left in contact must fight a delaying action on the perimeter so enemy forces do not
                cut off any friendly forces. Fire support, including tactical air support, is concentrated at the breakout
                point. The rear guard may receive priority of fire support once the breakout occurs.
          • Provide for forces left behind. When it is not feasible to move patients, commanders leave medical
                personnel and sufficient supplies to attend to the wounded.
          • Prepare for linkup operations. As part of a breakout toward friendly forces, commanders plan where
                and how to regain contact with friendly forces outside of the perimeter to ensure support,
                deconfliction of fires, and mutually understood control measures.

     5-95. A corps or division that is temporarily encircled may be able continue the attack. The encircling force
     may not be capable of containing the friendly force. A corps could conduct such an operation similarly to the
     breakout toward friendly forces during the Korean War. (See Appendix D for an illustration of the Pusan
     breakout in 1950.)

Exfiltrate

     5-96. If there is no intention of a relief operation, or a breakout appears too risky, and there is significant
     risk to the defense being overwhelmed, exfiltration could preserve some of the corps. Exfiltrate is a tactical
     mission task in which a unit removes Soldiers or units from areas under enemy control by stealth, deception,
     surprise, or clandestine means (FM 3-90). Corps units returning from a raid, an infiltration, or a patrol behind
     enemy lines might also need to conduct an exfiltration. A corps or division exfiltrates an encircled force to
     preserve as much of it as possible. Prior to exfiltration, the commander of the encircled force ensures the
     destruction or incapacitation of left behind equipment (except medical equipment). If capture of an entire
     force is imminent, a commander may leave its casualties in place with supplies, chaplain support, and medical
     personnel.

     5-97. An exfiltrating force first establishes its rally points and exfiltration lanes. The corps staff designates
     exfiltration lanes as restrictive fire areas or no-fire areas. The exfiltrating force uses preparation fire to cover
     its movement and to expend stockpiled ammunition. Preparation fire is a brief, intense bombardment on
     selected targets or a prolonged effort over time covering a large number of targets (FM 3-09). Corps
     commanders and staffs plan how to support the encircled force with commitment of additional forces and
     fire support, including close air support provided by Army and other Service aviation elements. (See figure
     5-16 on page 110 for a depiction of a corps exfiltration with one division exfiltrating while the other two
     divisions continue to defend.)

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  109


---

Chapter 5

                                              Figure 5-16. Corps Exfiltration

TRANSITIONING TO THE OFFENSE

     5-98. Before a defensive battle begins, the corps staff plans for the eventual transition to the offense. The
     transition to offensive operations must be rapid to keep enemy forces from establishing prepared defenses
     and receiving reinforcements. A primary corps objective is to prevent enemy forces from breaking contact.
     The corps uses its security forces to maintain contact with enemy forces and its information collection
     capabilities to stay abreast of their movements and composition. If necessary, the corps commander may
     commit the reserve to maintain direct contact as the initial counterattack. These actions set the conditions for
     the subsequent offensive operations by the divisions.

     5-99. A defending corps commander anticipates when and where the enemy force will culminate and links
     the transition to offensive operations to a decision point supported by commander's critical information
     requirement. The staff then uses fragmentary orders to rapidly assign division objectives as they transition to
     offense.

TRANSITION TIMING AND CONSIDERATIONS

     5-100. There are several signs that can indicate an enemy force is becoming overextended, such as enemy
     forces become less aggressive and are defeated in most engagements. (See FM 3-90 for a more complete list
     of indicators.) As a force transitions from the defense to the offense, the commander directs several actions,
     such as designating the main effort or a shift in protection priorities. (See FM 3-90 for a more complete list
     of commander actions during transition.)

110        ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                         Corps Offensive and Defensive Operations

TRANSITION TECHNIQUES FOR THE OFFENSE

     5-101. Corps commanders generally have two options for designating the attacking element when
     transitioning to the offense. They can use uncommitted forces or committed forces. Each option or technique
     has pros and cons. The corps must initially determine the subordinate units that will be required for the attack.
     This will be dependent on the concept chosen to achieve the attack objectives.

Uncommitted Forces

     5-102. The generally preferred technique is to attack using forces not previously committed to the defense,
     such as reserves or reinforcements. There are several advantages of using uncommitted forces: defending
     MBA units may still be decisively engaged, and since uncommitted forces have not recently been actively
     involved in combat, they are more likely to—

          • Be at authorized strength levels.
          • Enjoy a higher combat system operationally ready rate.
          • Have leaders and Soldiers who are more likely to be rested and thus capable of prolonged,

                continuous operations.
          • Have a complete basic load of supplies.
          • Have the time and energy to plan and prepare for offensive action.
          • Be able to maneuver out of physical contact with the enemy.

     A drawback to the use of this technique is the requirement to conduct a forward passage of lines. Additionally,
     enemy reconnaissance and surveillance assets are likely to detect the arrival of significant reinforcements.

Committed Forces

     5-103. The second technique is to conduct offensive actions using the currently defending forces. This
     technique generally has the advantage of being more rapidly executed and thus more likely to catch enemy
     forces by surprise. Speed of execution in this technique results from not having to conduct an approach or
     tactical road march from reserve assembly areas or, in the case of reinforcements, move from other AOs and
     RSOI locations. Speed also results from not having to conduct a forward passage of lines and perform the
     liaison necessary to establish a common operational picture that includes knowledge of the enemy force’s
     patterns of operation. The primary disadvantage of this technique is the risk of early culmination and the need
     for quick replacement due to fatigue.

     5-104. If units in contact participate in the attack, the commander must retain sufficient forces in contact to
     fix enemy forces. The commander concentrates the attack by reinforcing select subordinate units so they can
     execute the attack and, if necessary, maintain the existing defense using economy of force approaches that
     assume some risk. The commander can also adjust the defensive boundaries of subordinate units so entire
     units can withdraw and concentrate for the attack.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  111
