                                                 Chapter 6

                           Enabling Operations

    This chapter covers enabling operations. It begins with an overview of enabling operations.
    Then, the chapter discusses reconnaissance, security, and troop movement, followed by relief
    in place. Next, the chapter discusses passage of lines, countermobility, mobility, and TAC-D.
    The chapter concludes with a discussion of linkup operations.

OVERVIEW OF ENABLING OPERATIONS

     6-1. An enabling operation is an operation that sets the friendly conditions required for mission
     accomplishment (FM 3-90). Enabling operations do not directly accomplish the commander’s end state and
     are not decisive. However, they are required to conduct successful offensive, defensive, and stability
     operations. Enabling operations are complex enough that they require either a deliberate planning effort or a
     well-developed standard operating procedure to execute.

     6-2. A corps may conduct independent enabling operations or combine them with other operations. Corps
     enabling operations include—

          • Reconnaissance.
          • Security.
          • Troop movement.
          • Relief in place.
          • Passage of lines.
          • Countermobility.
          • Mobility.
          • Deception.
          • Linkup.

     While the concept and basic goals of enabling operations remain constant, actual methods for their conduct
     will change based on the mission variables of METT-TC (I).

RECONNAISSANCE

     6-3. Reconnaissance is a mission undertaken to obtain information about the activities and resources of an
     enemy or adversary, or to secure data concerning the meteorological, hydrographic, or geographic
     characteristics of a particular area, by visual observation or other detection methods (JP 2-0). Reconnaissance
     occurs continuously at all levels. Corps may direct specific reconnaissance missions within its AO, but
     divisions and brigades should reconnoiter their own assigned areas aggressively and continually.
     Reconnaissance is one part of information collection, along with security, intelligence operations, and
     surveillance. The five forms of reconnaissance operations are—

          • Zone reconnaissance.
          • Area reconnaissance.
          • Route reconnaissance.
          • Reconnaissance in force.
          • Special reconnaissance (SOF only).

     6-4. Corps commanders depend on situational understanding to seize and retain the initiative. A corps
     requires reconnaissance formations capable of developing the situation to determine enemy intentions,
     strength, and disposition. The corps commander normally designates and task-organizes a BCT to support
     the requirement for reconnaissance, but the size of the force depends on the mission variables.

     6-5. Reconnaissance is usually executed prior to and as part of a corps attack or during a movement to contact.
     A corps’ primary objective is to acquire information regarding the enemy force’s situation and identify or
     confirm weaknesses in its defense. A reconnaissance may focus along select points of the enemy force’s

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  113


---

Chapter 6

     defense, to confirm the feasibility of attacking through or against those points, or along the entire front to
     determine the best points of attack. Reconnaissance in the corps AO includes repetitive checks to assure that
     roads and key infrastructure in the support area such as bridges, tunnels, and defiles remain secure and
     unobstructed.

     6-6. A corps may conduct reconnaissance or task subordinate divisions to conduct reconnaissance. In either
     instance, the corps must plan to either exploit success or extricate reconnaissance forces. The headquarters
     controlling the operation must have sufficient units (usually reserves) in position to accomplish either of
     these tasks. If the corps conducts the operation, its concept of operations normally assumes that the divisions
     executing the operation require all their forces to achieve the reconnaissance objectives. Consequently, the
     corps must plan for another division with suitable mobility and firepower to either exploit success or extricate
     the unit executing the reconnaissance operation.

     6-7. Aviation units are useful for corps reconnaissance operations because of their mobility. Employing
     manned and unmanned aviation capabilities as part of the reconnaissance force speeds movement and
     improves scouting. However, it requires a command relationship between aviation and ground commanders.
     (See paragraphs 6-10 through 6-13 for more information on security.)

     6-8. The corps needs to prioritize its reconnaissance objectives early in the planning process. This will enable
     reconnaissance forces to support the staff’s mission analysis during military decision-making process (known
     as MDMP).

     6-9. During a movement to contact, a corps covering force may also elect to develop the situation by
     reconnaissance in selected parts of its AO. A properly organized covering force should have the capabilities
     necessary to conduct reconnaissance. (See FM 3-98 and FM 3-90 for more information on reconnaissance.)

SECURITY

     6-10. Security operations are those operations performed by commanders to provide early and accurate
     warning of enemy operations, to provide the forces being protected with time and maneuver space within
     which to react to the enemy, and to develop the situation to allow commanders to effectively use their
     protected forces (ADP 3-90). The main difference between the performance of security operations and
     reconnaissance operations is that security operations orient on the force or an area, while reconnaissance
     operations orient on enemy and terrain.

     6-11. Corps conduct security operations to prevent surprise and reduce uncertainty. Security operations are
     dynamic efforts that anticipate and thwart enemy collection efforts. All maneuver forces can conduct security
     operations, and they usually perform security operations to the front, flanks, or rear of their own formations.
     Security operations prevent surprise and allow the corps to maintain the initiative. Commanders assign
     security missions based on the degree of security desired and the amount of combat power required. There
     are four types of security operations: screen, guard, cover, and area security. (See FM 3-90 for additional
     information on security operations.)

     6-12. As with reconnaissance, the corps must consider security operations at the beginning of planning. Corps
     units should already be executing security operations during the planning process since execution of security
     operations is inherent to transitions between most types of operations. For example, when completing an
     attack and transitioning to the defense, units typically deploy security forces while establishing the defense.
     Security operations are part of the preparation phase to execute all corps operations. For example, security
     forces protect the main effort and prevent enemy observation of friendly activities at the beginning of a corps
     attack.

     6-13. Commanders must be aware of the inherent risk of defeat in detail of corps units conducting security
     operations. When security operations are economy of force efforts, commanders typically minimize the
     amount of combat power apportioned for the mission. Security forces are often the first element to make
     significant contact with the enemy and, depending on the mission, may even fight the enemy’s main body.
     They also can make contact away from the direct support of the friendly main body. As a result, security
     forces may be at risk of being isolated or defeated in detail. Commanders mitigate this risk by assigning the
     type of security operation appropriate to the enemy situation. Different types of security operations provide
     more combat power to security forces. Commanders also mitigate risk by ensuring the corps has contingency
     plans for reacting to situations where the security forces are isolated or overwhelmed.

114  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                         Enabling Operations

TROOP MOVEMENT

     6-14. Troop movement is the movement of Soldiers and units from one place to another by any available
     means (FM 3-90). The essence of battlefield agility is the capability to conduct rapid and orderly movement
     to concentrate combat power at decisive points and times. Successful movement places troops and equipment
     at their destination at the proper time, ready for combat. The corps must successfully move large numbers of
     forces to conduct operations.

     6-15. There are two types of troop movement: nontactical and tactical. Units only conduct nontactical
     movements in secure areas, such as the continental United States. There are five methods of troop movements
     described in FM 3-90. The method employed depends on the situation, the size and composition of the
     moving unit, the distance the unit must cover, the urgency of execution, and the condition of the troops. It
     also depends on the availability, suitability, and capacity of the different means of transportation.

     6-16. The goal of all movement planning is to retain flexibility to execute a variety of plans to meet
     ever-changing conditions. A corps-size movement is inherently complicated. Detailed plans are necessary to
     maintain order and to account for the lack of lift to move all corps elements simultaneously.

     6-17. Movement planning should incorporate an extensive IPOE and be heavily influenced by terrain and the
     disposition of enemy forces, including aviation and long-range fires. Consequently, the plan should include
     contingencies for actions during the movement. The movement plan must also incorporate a fire support plan
     that includes joint capabilities, coordinated with the JFACC. Depending on the corps’ role, the corps will
     coordinate directly with the JFACC or submit requests through the land component commander. Corps
     artillery should be moved as far forward as possible during the move to ensure lead units have fire support
     when needed. Fluid movement and the ability to react to contingencies is critical to a large-unit movement.
     The corps employs echeloned logistics, tactical deception, and air missile defense along its routes.

     6-18. The corps must consider executing combat operations in its scheme of maneuver. The corps should
     anticipate locations along the movement route where contact with the enemy is more likely. It is critical that
     the corps arrive at anticipated points of contact prepared for maneuver and in formations suitable for combat.
     Corps units should end the movement in formations capable of conducting offensive or defensive operations.

     6-19. Security must be maintained during the movement and in assembly areas. This is accomplished through
     a combination of tactics and apportionment of critical enablers, such as air defense units. Mobile task forces
     should be capable of responding to the most dangerous anticipated enemy courses of action. This may require
     the corps to apportion corps-level capabilities to divisions most likely to make initial enemy contact.

     6-20. The corps should develop detailed understanding of the transportation network in its AO. In addition
     to the availability, suitability, and capacity relative to each means of corps transportation, the transportation
     network should be assessed for traffic regulation and control and the impact of civilian vehicles and
     dislocated civilians. Sound corps planning should prevent disruption to resupply of ongoing operations by
     other units using the transportation networks for the movement.

     6-21. The movement plan will also emphasize command and control. The corps must account for terrain
     management and the operational framework. Besides identifying the location of command posts, the plan
     must clearly designate the conditions for moving command posts and transferring command functions
     between them, if the plan requires corps CPs to displace as part of the movement. The corps is responsible
     for route designations, ground and air traffic control, and establishing timelines for the movement. The corps
     may need to employ additional signal capabilities to enable long-range communications. A corps-size
     movement can easily cover hundreds of kilometers. Maintaining communication from all ends of the
     movement formation is essential to the corps’ situational understanding.

     6-22. The corps should carefully consider the information aspects of a large-unit movement. The movement
     plan should consider techniques to protect friendly information by securing and obscuring it and conducting
     security activities. A common historical example of protecting friendly information is moving at night to
     reduce enemy observation of unit movements. (See ADP 3-13 for more information on the protect
     information activity.)

     6-23. Tactical deception reduces overall risk to the corps and preserves combat power. The corps commander
     should drive the tactical deception portion of the movement plan, which should include the six principles of

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  115


---

Chapter 6

     planning deception. Besides benefitting the movement itself, tactical deception can enhance the chances of
     success for the operation conducted after the movement. (See FM 3-90 for additional information on tactical
     deception.)

     6-24. Corps organization during a movement should facilitate rapid transitions to maneuver, change of
     direction of march, provide flank protection, and utilize all available roads and other transportation routes.
     The preservation of freedom of maneuver until the corps engages in close combat is essential. Consequently,
     the corps must designate the main and supporting efforts during the movement to preserve the ability to
     complete the move and transition to subsequent operations.

     6-25. Weather can impact the overall operation, the transportation network, and personnel. Weather may also
     impact the timing of the movement. The corps should be aware of severe weather that may damage the
     transportation network and require engineer support to repair.

     6-26. Typically, the corps positions command and control elements, logistics, maintenance, and route
     maintenance and route support capabilities to facilitate the move once it begins. If the plan directs the use of
     liaison elements, they should be in position prior to the beginning of the movement. Movement control teams
     and military police provide essential control over the movement routes. They enable maneuver units to focus
     on conducting offensive and defensive operations. Engineer assets support route improvement and
     maintenance. They are also necessary for improving assembly areas or attack positions at the end of the
     movement. The corps should carefully consider engineering requirements throughout the depth of the
     movement area and, if necessary, request additional support.

     6-27. Sustainment is essential to a successful corps-size move. Logistics should be pre-configured and pre-
     loaded onto vehicles before the move begins. Additionally, sustainment support, such as convoy support
     centers, are typically pre-positioned along the route when possible. These actions are critical to enabling
     throughput and maintaining the tempo of the movement, particularly by providing adequate fuel and
     maintenance support. (See FM 4-0 for additional information on sustainment of troop movements.)

     6-28. The route must receive thorough reconnaissance and may need to be improved before the execution of
     the movement. It may also be necessary to improve signs, or otherwise mark the route, before execution.

     6-29. The corps plan must permit adequate preparation time for subordinate units. Affected units require
     sufficient notification to enable them to complete their internal preparation activities, which may be extensive
     depending on the type of unit (especially those that are not fully mobile) and the extent of the movement.

     6-30. Initial execution may begin with seizure of key terrain to prevent enemy attempts to delay the
     movement. The corps may also employ specialized capabilities, such as SOF, or conduct airborne or air
     assault operations to establish conditions for the movement of the corps’ main body.

     6-31. March discipline will heavily impact the success of a corps-size move. The corps is focused on the
     planning aspect of the march, such as the synchronization matrix, but it must also clearly articulate the march
     standards to subordinate units. Failure to conform to the corps plan by a lower echelon unit can disrupt the
     entire corps movement.

     6-32. Execution requires aggressive supervision and precise reporting. Just as commanders observe named
     area of interest (known as NAI) in other operations, they make deliberate provisions to watch key points or
     areas during a march. Units must quickly and accurately report enemy interference or mobility problems on
     the route. Accurate, timely reporting enables the corps to maintain situational awareness.

     6-33. The corps commander must be continuously informed of the location, strength, dispositions, and
     movement of the enemy. The corps leverages Army and joint ISR capabilities to assist with this. The
     commander must also be kept apprised of the staff’s interpretation of enemy courses of action prior to and
     during the movement.

     6-34. The corps commander determines where to assume risk during movements. The overall risk decision
     addresses the tension between speed and security. Organizing for speed will simplify control, facilitate rapid
     movement, and reduce sustainment difficulties. However, organizing for speed reduces flexibility and,
     depending on the scheme of maneuver, likely increases some vulnerabilities to enemy action. Organizing for
     security presents the inverse of the benefits and challenges of organizing for speed.

116  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                         Enabling Operations

     6-35. There are multiple specific considerations for a corps-size movement that impact risk. The selection of
     different methods of transportation will affect speed and security. Capabilities that are loaded into trains,
     vessels, and aircraft are unavailable to the commander during movement and may not arrive to their
     destination on time or at all. Movement is dependent on enemy force capabilities. Historically, the U.S.
     enjoyed nighttime supremacy, but in the era of persistent surveillance and reconnaissance coverage this is no
     longer universally true. Night offers some level of concealment but typically slows the rate of march. March
     standards also impact risk. Commanders may sacrifice speed to increase tactical spacing, use alternate routes,
     and impose restrictions to how many units may move at a time.

     6-36. Security operations are inherent to tactical large-unit movements. The corps designates the main body
     for the movement. The corps will then assign and apportion security forces for the main body. Depending on
     the mission variables, the corps may employ a covering force, advance guard, flank or rear guards, and
     screening forces to support the main body. The covering force and advance guard (if used) will depart before
     the main body.

     6-37. Reconnaissance precedes unit movement. Reconnaissance units can conduct route and area
     reconnaissance for the movement, along with area reconnaissance of the assembly areas or attack positions
     at the termination of the movement route. The main body reconnaissance units will cross the line of departure
     prior to the main body. The main body then crosses the line of departure to execute its movement. The
     elements of the main body will be further subdivided into march columns, march serials, and march units.
     (See FM 3-90 for more information on reconnaissance operations.) The corps’ quartering party acts on behalf
     of the corps headquarters. Each subordinate unit is responsible for its own quartering party. The rear guard
     (if used) will then be the last element of the corps’ movement to cross the line of departure. Depending on
     the plan, the advance of the rear guard past predetermined locations or checkpoints may be the condition
     necessary to adjust the corps’ close and rear boundaries.

     6-38. The corps may employ any of the movement techniques from FM 3-90; however, they are most likely
     to deploy their divisions in columns along parallel axes based upon terrain and available maneuver corridors.
     Those columns may be organized in depth in formations that resemble a wedge, vee, or diamond to maintain
     flexibility with a smaller covering force out front to gain time and secure freedom of maneuver for the rest
     of the force.

     6-39. A corps movement requires the corps to coordinate with the field army or JFLCC for boundary
     adjustments in the same way that it coordinates for boundary shifts for its divisions. As the movement occurs,
     the lines of communication will increase (unless it is a rearward movement). The corps’ rear command post
     must oversee the security and management of the LOCs. The movement may also require establishing new
     support areas or closing existing ones. This requires detailed management to ensure minimal disruption to
     logistics to the forward elements and main body.

     6-40. The corps requires engineer gap crossing capabilities to facilitate movement in most theaters. These
     are unlikely to be permanently assigned to the corps. Their use must be anticipated, and their support
     requested. Movement and positioning of bridging units, as well as their security, requires extensive planning.
     Typically, a division conducts a wet gap crossing as part of a larger corps operation.

     6-41. Corps sustainment units may require additional support to manage the requirements of a movement.
     Depending on the methods of transportation employed, along with command and support relationships at
     areas of embarkation and debarkation, the corps may need additional materiel handling, transportation, and
     maintenance support. The corps should deconflict sustainment requirements with the land component
     command or the theater army.

RELIEF IN PLACE

     6-42. A relief in place is an operation in which, by direction of higher authority, all or part of a unit is
     replaced in an area by the incoming unit and the responsibilities of the replaced elements for the
     mission and the assigned zone of operations are transferred to the incoming unit. Units conduct a relief
     in place as part of a larger operation, primarily to maintain the combat effectiveness of committed forces.
     While a corps may be relieved by another corps, a corps will more often be focused on conducting reliefs in
     place between divisions.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  117


---

Chapter 6

     6-43. As an operation involving hand-off of combat responsibility from one force to another, relief operations
     are extremely complex and involve higher risk. To minimize risk and to ensure synchronization, successful
     relief in place operations require detailed, centralized planning and decentralized execution. Reliefs may be
     characterized as hasty or deliberate. The entire corps may participate in a relief in place mission as the
     incoming or relieved force. In addition, corps offensive and defensive operations often include subordinate
     division relief in place operations. There are three variations of a relief, which are described in FM 3-90. If
     forward elements can defend the AO, it is preferable to execute the relief in place from rear to front. This
     facilitates movement and terrain management.

     6-44. For a corps-level relief in place, a higher echelon will direct key aspects of the operation. When the
     corps is the headquarters directing subordinate relief in place, the corps will direct key aspects of the
     operation. Key aspects of a relief in place operation include—

          • Deception plan.
          • Priorities for routes.
          • Information collection plan.
          • Fire support plan.
          • Air defense plan.
          • Sustainment plan.
          • Control measures with effective times.
          • Rearward assembly areas.
          • Airspace control.
          • Command relationships with forces remaining in the transferred area.
          • Subsequent missions for the incoming and relieved forces.
          • Apportionment of enablers.

     6-45. When the corps is directed to conduct a corps-level relief by a higher echelon, it focuses its planning
     on coordination with the other force and guidance to subordinate units conducting the relief. These are done
     simultaneously. Any decisions made between coordinating corps are immediately passed to subordinates.
     However, it is typically at the division echelon or below that detailed planning and coordination occurs.

     6-46. Units conceal relief in place operations from enemy forces for as long as possible. As a result, the
     tactical deception plan is vital. As soon as units receive an order indicating a pending relief in place, they
     should initiate a deception plan. Central to deception will be operations security measures. The protect
     information activity will be the foundation of a tactical deception plan (See ADP 3-13 for more information
     on the protect information activity).

     6-47. The air defense plan must balance two competing requirements. The first is to maintain protection of
     forward committed forces. The second is to provide coverage over the primary relief routes. The incoming
     and relieved commands must jointly develop the air defense plan to ensure force protection. Whenever the
     plan impacts the coverage of the Joint Force Commander’s defended asset list, the corps must request a
     change through the Area Air Defense Commander.

     6-48. The relief plan may require the exchange of certain weapons, supplies, equipment, or vehicles between
     the incoming and relieved units. When necessary, the headquarters directing the relief in place operation must
     designate what is exchanged. Equipment exchanges should be controlled and documented to the extent the
     situation allows.

     6-49. Corps may conduct relief operations with or between multinational forces. Additional  planning
     consideration for such situations include—

          • Special adjustments of assigned sectors if the unit organizations are dissimilar.
          • Special liaison for fire support control.
          • Increased use of guides to overcome language challenges.
          • Special arrangements for using communications equipment and spectrum management.
          • Ammunition and equipment incompatibility may make exchange difficult.

     6-50. Command and control are facilitated by close coordination by all commanders involved. Properly
     locating command posts to support operations and the exchange of liaisons is critical. The liaisons must have

118        ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                   Enabling Operations

the ability to always communicate with their organic headquarters. (See FM 6-0 for more information on
liaison.)

6-51. Relief operations are vulnerable to enemy attacks. Any unnecessary delays during execution provide
the enemy additional time to acquire, target, and mass fires. Units must complete relief operations as quickly
as possible to protect the force. Units must consider their electromagnetic spectrum signature during a relief.
Whenever possible, the incoming unit should use the relieved unit’s networks and communications band to
minimize the risk of alerting the enemy of an ongoing relief operation.

6-52. The fire support plan for a relief in place should account for both units’ fires capabilities and ensure
that there is no interruption of fire support when the relieved units withdraw. This requires synchronization
of fire support responsibilities over time, decision points, shared fire support coordination measure (known
as FSCM), and the allocation of adequate position areas for artillery.

6-53. Commanders must carefully synchronize the transfer of command relationships. Units in the assigned
area must fall under the operational control of a single commander. Typically, the commander of the relieved
force retains TACON of the incoming force until a pre-designated time or condition is met. Then, the
incoming commander assumes TACON of the relieved force until the operation is complete. When command
is transferred, the units must be notified as quickly as possible to limit confusion. The commander’s use of
TACON over the other unit is typically limited to responding to enemy attacks and controlling movement
timing.

6-54. Commanders mitigate risk by executing the relief operation as quickly as possible. Units are vulnerable
to attack during a relief in place operation, especially if temporary gaps in defensive coverage occur due to
miscommunication. Commanders should mitigate this risk through their deception plans and fire support
plans. Apportionment and employment of joint capabilities is a significant risk mitigator during relief.

6-55. Commanders must also focus on risks associated with fratricide. Force intermingling is inherent in a
relief since units will be conducting forward and rearward passages of lines at some point during the relief.
Commanders establish risk mitigation primarily through control measures. The corps is responsible for
maintaining a common operational picture for corps or division relief in place operations. A significant
portion of this is accomplished through reporting and graphic control measures.

6-56. The corps will likely inherit the relieved corps-equivalent unit’s deep area during a relief. Transfer of
responsibility must be carefully coordinated between commanders. There may be actions the corps desires
to assist with the tactical deception plan, such as disrupting enemy long-range ISR and fires. However,
activities in the corps’ deep area remain generally unchanged from what the relieved unit was conducting
until the relief is complete.

6-57. During a defense, a corps would likely sequence activities to maintain OPSEC and defensive continuity.
A sequential relief in place that occurs from rear to front is preferred. This provides the best security for the
operation. Regardless of the sequencing of succession, a sequential relief or staggered relief maintains
security but requires more time than a simultaneous approach. For both sequential and staggered relief in
place operations, the incoming corps’ subordinate echelons occupy dispersed assembly areas prior to moving
forward into the forward unit’s assigned area. The relieved units then must conduct a rearward movement to
their new assembly areas. The corps command may use the tactical CP to control the operation. In the case
of sequenced operations, the tactical CP may move from one division AO to another.

6-58. A simultaneous relief in place is unlikely at the corps level. However, it may be possible to execute
this type of relief operation if the relieved corps is no longer in contact with the enemy and there are enough
movement corridors to support moving the divisions in brigade columns. In this case, the incoming corps
simultaneously departs its assembly areas in the rear area and executes a corps-size movement to the AO.
Once in place, the relieved corps will conduct a rearward corps-size movement out of the AO. Command and
control of this form of relief at this echelon is especially challenging because of the scope and speed of the
relief. (See figure 6-1 on page 120 for a relief in place in a corps AO. In the figure, divisions A and C are
relieved by divisions B and D, and they move rearward on ROUTES SILVER and GOLD to their assembly
areas in the corps rear. The corps commander placed the tactical CP between the two operations to enhance
control.)

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  119


---

Chapter 6

     6-59. The corps will likely inherit the relieved corps-equivalent unit’s rear area during a relief. The corps
     may assume the existing support areas. This will limit disruptions to sustainment, minimize protection lapses,
     and facilitate transfer of equipment and supplies (if required). However, this may increase the vulnerability
     of the support areas to enemy attacks. If the corps creates new support areas, the inverse of the preceding
     risks and benefits is true. If the higher echelon’s plan directs the relieved corps to occupy assembly areas in
     the incoming corps’ rear area, every effort should be made to adjust rear boundaries to minimize the presence
     of a second corps in the corps’ AO. If necessary, clear command and support relationships, along with control
     measures, must be established by the higher echelon.

                            Figure 6-1. Relief in Place in a Corps Area of Operations

PASSAGE OF LINES

     6-60. A passage of lines is an operation in which a force moves forward or rearward through another force’s
     combat positions with the intention of moving into or out of contact with the enemy (JP 3-18). Units may
     perform a passage of lines to sustain offensive tempo, maintain the viability of the defense, transition from
     the performance of delay or security operations to the defense, or transition a unit for another mission or task.
     Commanders may designate a passage of lines as a forward or rearward passage of lines. (See FM 3-90 for
     more information on passage of lines.) To minimize risk and to ensure synchronization of the operation,
     successful passage of lines operations require detailed, centralized planning and decentralized execution. The
     entire corps may participate in a passage of lines as the passing or stationary force. In addition, corps offensive
     and defensive operations often include subordinate passage of lines operations.

     6-61. Command and control are the driving consideration for a passage of lines. After receiving a warning
     order directing an operation that requires a passage of lines, the passing commander and staff establish liaison
     with the unit in contact. Close coordination and understanding between commanders and staff are essential
     for the smooth transfer of control during a passage of lines.

     6-62. Commanders must carefully synchronize the transfer of command relationships. Units in the assigned
     area must fall under the operational control of a single commander. During a forward passage of lines, the

120  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                         Enabling Operations

     commander of the stationary force typically retains TACON of the passing force until a pre-designated time
     or condition is met. Then, the passing commander assumes TACON of the stationary force until the operation
     is complete. When command is transferred, the units must be notified as quickly as possible to limit
     confusion. During a rearward passage of lines, passing unit commanders retain control over their forces.

     6-63. Typically, the fire support capabilities of the stationary corps will support the passing corps until
     completion of the operation. For a forward passage of lines, this allows the passing corps’ fire support assets
     to continue to move forwards into firing positions that support an attack and maximizes the amount of artillery
     available for counterfire during a hazardous operation. For a rearward passage of lines, this ensures coverage
     during the displacement of the passing corps’ artillery.

     6-64. Units are vulnerable during a passage of lines to attack, especially if temporary gaps in the defensive
     front occur. Commanders mitigate risk with intelligence, deception, and as many passage points as possible.
     In a forward passage of lines, the stationary force maintains contact with the enemy and shares situational
     understanding for the passing unit commander. In a rearward passage of lines, if the passing unit is in contact
     with the enemy, maintaining a rear guard force is important to prevent enemy direct fire against passing units
     and penetrations at the passage points.

     6-65. A passage of lines presents significant risk of fratricide. A forward passage of lines exposes the passing
     force to the risk of fratricide, especially when initially passing forward of the passage point. A rearward
     passage of lines exposes both forces to the risk of fratricide during initial contact at the passage point.
     Commanders establish risk mitigation for fratricide, primarily through control measures. Commanders must
     ensure that they deconflict direct and indirect fires with graphic control measures.

     6-66. The stationary corps continues to conduct deep operations during the passage of lines until battle
     handover. In a forward passage of lines, corps employ fires to protect the passage of lines and set conditions
     for the passing corps’ subsequent attack. The stationary force intelligence collection plan primarily supports
     the passage of lines by providing situational understanding between the passage points and the objectives of
     the passing force. For a rearward passage of lines, the passing corps hands over its deep operations
     responsibilities in stages to the stationary force. If it is in contact with enemy forces and executing a
     retrograde, the passing corps focuses its deep operations on disrupting follow-on enemy forces to create
     enough stand off to ensure it can break contact. The stationary corps should provide fire support that
     facilitates the passing corps movement, based upon a mutually agreed upon division of responsibility for
     counterfire and specific essential fire support tasks.

FORWARD PASSAGE OF LINES

     6-67. In a forward passage of lines, the passing corps conducts a troop movement from assembly areas
     through the stationary corps through passage lanes without halts or bunching up along the routes. This
     requires considerable planning and significant gaps between march units and march serials to account for
     friction, but gaps should be small enough to preclude units from becoming unsupported on the far side of the
     passage lanes. Divisions execute their respective passage of lines, synchronized and supported by the corps.
     The corps determines timing and sequencing of the division passages and provides specific guidance about
     when corps-controlled assets supporting the divisions move. Once predetermined conditions agreed to by the
     two corps commanders are met, the passing corps assumes responsibility of the AO and continues operations.

     6-68. In a forward passage of lines, the stationary corps must accommodate the passing corps’ movement
     from their assembly areas to the passage point. This may require adjusting sustainment operations from the
     support areas, as the passing corps will have priority on the planned movement routes. If necessary, the
     stationary corps adjusts its protection activities in the rear area prior to departure of the passing corps from
     their assembly areas to ensure adequate coverage of the passing corps. The passing and stationary corps will
     conduct rear operations in close proximity to each other and likely share MSR and ASR, depending upon the
     number and quality of routes available, until the passing corps expands its AO enough to no longer be
     intermingled with the stationary corps. Support areas will likely be in close proximity to one another, since
     the passing corps should consider establishing its support area forward in the stationary corps rear area before
     it begins forward movement.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  121


---

Chapter 6

REARWARD PASSAGE OF LINES

     6-69. In a rearward passage of lines, the passing corps most likely executes a retrograde operation through a
     stationary corps. The passing corps maintains contact with advance enemy forces and continues its operation
     while simultaneously executing the passage of lines. The passing corps commander oversees the rearward
     passage of lines, which is primarily executed by subordinate divisions. The corps will likely transition to a
     large-unit movement that terminates in assembly areas after passing through the stationary corps. (See
     paragraph 6-14 to 6-41 for more information on troop movement.). The corps usually passes its sustainment
     units through the passage point first. The passing corps continues to manage the retrograde fight with the
     enemy until the predesignated battle handover. This includes controlled displacement of its fire support
     capabilities to enable them to support their forces in contact while simultaneously retrograding. At battle
     handover, the stationary corps assumes responsibility for fighting advancing enemy formations, which
     includes control of fire support.

     6-70. In a rearward passage of lines, the passing corps’ units conducting rear operations displace first, while
     those responsible for support operations follow to the degree that they can continue to support in preplanned
     locations along the routes. The stationary corps will retain control over its rear area during the passage
     operation, although priority of designated routes will shift to accommodating the requirements of the passing
     corps. Once the passing corps completes the passage of lines and occupies its assembly areas, the passing
     corps may re-establish a rear area coordinated with the LCC to ensure it doesn’t hinder the stationary corps
     lines of communication. In this case, the passing corps must establish new support areas and assume
     protection and security activities.

     6-71. Ideally, a passage of lines does not interfere with the stationary unit’s operations. In figure 6-2,
     Divisions A and C are assembled in the corps rear area and then moved forward to the division areas using
     ROUTES SILVER and GOLD in preparation for passage through Divisions B and D. The corps commander
     can use the tactical CP to control the operation.

     Figure 6-2. Passage of Lines in a Corps Area of Operations

122  ATP 3-92                                                    15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                         Enabling Operations

COUNTERMOBILITY

     6-72. Countermobility is a set of combined arms activities that use or enhance the effects of natural and man-
     made obstacles to prevent the enemy freedom of movement and maneuver. (ATP 3-90.8). Countermobility
     supports the execution of corps offensive and defensive task operations. Corps units capable of conducting
     countermobility include combined arms maneuver forces, military police, AMD, and engineer units.

     6-73. Countermobility adds depth for the corps by denying an enemy force’s ability to maneuver. Enemy
     forces are more vulnerable when their movements are disrupted, fixed, or blocked, since doing so provides
     additional time for engagement by direct and indirect fire systems. Turning an enemy force makes it
     vulnerable to attack from a flank.

     6-74. The corps engineer advises the commander on how to integrate obstacles, coordinates for the obstacle
     emplacement authority, establishes obstacle control, recommends directed obstacles, supervises the
     employment of obstacles, and maintains obstacle status throughout the operation. The corps engineer must
     have representation on the targeting board to prevent destruction of key infrastructure essential to corps future
     operations. The corps engineer develops the corps obstacle plan and refines it based on the commander’s
     guidance and feedback from the divisions.

     6-75. Combatant commanders have the authority to emplace obstacles in their area of responsibility. This
     authority is delegated down to an appropriate level to maximize flexibility to execute tactical missions.
     Depending on the context, the corps may or may not receive authority to emplace obstacles. Even when
     authority is delegated to the corps, there may be specific restrictions on types of obstacles or methods of
     employment. The corps commander must also carefully consider implications for obstacle emplacement if
     delegating authority to division commanders.

     6-76. Corps countermobility considerations include balancing risks to the friendly mission and forces with
     those to neutral actors, especially civilians. When the corps seeks to increase security for friendly forces
     through lethal obstacle emplacement, it should mitigate the risk to neutral actors from the measures it takes.
     For example, the corps may need to use command-detonated, self-deactivating, or nonexplosive obstacles in
     certain situations.

     6-77. The corps must also consider future operations whenever conducting countermobility operations.
     Denying enemy access to routes and avenues of approach with obstacles can deny future friendly access as
     well. For example, if conducting countermobility operations in support of a defense with the expectation of
     launching a counterattack, the corps may need to preserve mobility along their anticipated movement
     corridors.

MOBILITY

     6-78. Mobility is a quality or capability of military forces which permits them to move from place to place
     while retaining the ability to fulfill their primary mission (JP 3-36). The focus of mobility operations is to
     enable the corps to move and maneuver freely on the battlefield.

     6-79. A corps focuses on different aspects of mobility depending on its role. When acting in a joint role, a
     corps considers how the combatant command (known as CCMD) has set the theater and what capabilities it
     must improve the mobility within its JOA. When acting as a tactical headquarters, corps mobility
     considerations focus on its assigned AO.

     6-80. The quality of roads, terrain, and transportation infrastructure shape how quickly a corps can move
     formations. Improving and maintaining routes is critical. Detailed planning that accounts for route use,
     maintenance, and management is a fundamental corps responsibility.

     6-81. Corps engineer, military police, and sustainment units enable mobility operations. They allow the corps
     to clear, secure, and maintain routes; control the movement of subordinate units; and resupply and refuel
     units transiting an AO. Engineers conduct infrastructure assessments of existing bridging and roads,
     obstacles, and terrain against the corps’ capabilities. Engineering support may include major construction,
     designing and building roads and road networks, repairing and clearing routes, and improving trafficability
     to facilitate movement. The corps provides haul capacity augmentation to its engineers for these tasks.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  123


---

Chapter 6

GAP CROSSING

     6-82. Corps-level gap-crossings have been historically important operations. An example is XII Corps’
     crossing of the Moselle River during World War II near the town of Nancy, France, as discussed in the
     Multiple Bridgeheads vignette and shown in figure 6-3. The ability of the corps to manage multiple, widely
     dispersed division crossing sites was instrumental in Third Army’s advance across France. Although gap-
     crossings are not historically decisive, they are still foundational to corps-level maneuver that supports
     offensive and defensive operations.

     6-83. A gap crossing is the projection of combat power across a linear obstacle (wet or dry gap) (ATP 3-90.4).
     Examples of gaps include ravines, mountain passes, and rivers. Gap crossing enables friendly forces to move
     and maneuver freely on the battlefield across terrain typically not trafficable by most corps’ units. There are
     three types of gap crossings: deliberate, hasty, and covert. Larger gap crossings are typically conducted by
     divisions and planned in detail at division level as part of a larger corps operation. The corps performs
     fundamental planning and resourcing responsibilities while setting conditions for division success before and
     during execution. A critical part of setting conditions is to ensure that the enemy cannot effectively employ
     fires against friendly forces crossing the gap. (See ATP 3-90.4 for further information on gap crossing
     operations.)

124  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                             Enabling Operations

                                Multiple Bridgeheads

Over a week-long period in September 1944, Lieutenant General George S. Patton’s
Third Army attempted to cross an assault force at several points across the Moselle
River to establish a bridgehead and then encircle three German Army divisions in the
city of Nancy, France. With a plan that closely followed U.S. river-crossing doctrine,
Third Army’s XII Corps executed a wet-gap crossing south of the town of Nancy with
the 35th Infantry Division as the bridgehead force and 4th Armored Division as the
breakout force. As planned, the successful wet-gap crossing opened the southern
route to Nancy and allowed the 80th Infantry Division to initiate a similar crossing north
of the city. The establishment of multiple bridgeheads at Nancy demonstrates how
mobility played a significant role in the Third Army’s successful march across France.
(See figure 6-3 for a map of the operations.)

                                 Figure 6-3. Corps Mobility in World War II

6-84. Corps seize existing bridges and fording sites whenever possible since it reduces the amount of bridging
its engineer brigade must emplace. Speed is important since the enemy understands the importance of
crossing sites in the same way friendly forces do. The corps typically controls an engineer brigade with
bridging companies and other enablers that it allocates to support its divisions, in accordance with the roles
of its main and supporting efforts inside a specific scheme of maneuver.

6-85. The corps determines if its subordinate divisions will cross the river on a wide front, involving two or
more divisions, or on a narrow front, involving one division. While a crossing on a wide front is preferred
because it projects combat power more rapidly across the obstacle and keeps the corps more dispersed, terrain
and the scarcity of bridging assets may limit options. The corps might cross on a narrow front if the scheme

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  125


---

Chapter 6

     of maneuver requires it or if the corps does not have the equipment it needs for multiple division crossing
     points. The fewer the crossing points, the more important protecting the crossing points becomes.

     6-86. The corps determines whether subordinate units can execute simultaneous or sequential crossings based
     on the enemy situation and available resources. The corps assigns divisions mission objectives and
     bridgeheads, also providing the deception plan and terrain and enemy analysis. The corps commander clearly
     articulates the main and supporting efforts and priorities of support, so limited resources, such as engineering,
     fire support, air defense, sustainment, and movement control assets, are distributed appropriately.

     6-87. The corps ensures that the divisions do not crowd their assigned crossing sites by allocating them AOs
     large enough to disperse forces outside of medium artillery range. The corps should help the divisions
     synchronize crossings as continuous movements across the gap. This allows the divisions to move crossing
     units without stopping at a series of assembly areas or attack positions that are vulnerable to massed fires.

     6-88. The corps designates the bridgehead(s) for an offensive gap crossing, which is graphically depicted
     with a bridgehead line. The bridgehead must provide space for all units needed on the far side of the gap for
     the corps to continue the attack. The bridgehead should be defensible and large enough to allow dispersion
     and maneuver for the bridgehead commander. If the division(s) conducting the crossing continues the attack
     beyond the bridgehead, the corps establishes a shallow bridgehead. If the corps intends to pass a follow-on
     division through the bridgehead, it requires more depth. In instances where the bridgehead force is expected
     to defeat substantial enemy forces short of the bridgehead line, the corps establishes intermediate division
     objectives.

     6-89. During a retrograde gap crossing, planning considerations remain generally the same. However,
     command and control will be more difficult if the corps is executing retrograde operations in contact with
     the enemy. Units unable to break direct fire contact with enemy forces are unlikely to survive a crossing
     attempt, so the corps should set conditions for the bulk of its forces to cross out of direct fire contact. The
     crossing sites available to friendly forces will likely be limited, making the corps more vulnerable while
     crossing. Tactical deception should be employed to conceal the location of crossing sites and the timing of
     the crossings.

     6-90. Corps and division reconnaissance forces assist with the selection of crossing sites. They gather and
     refine information about the nature of the gap. For example, reconnaissance forces measure the width, depth,
     and flow rate of a river. They develop the situation on the opposite side of the gap to inform the commander’s
     decision-making. Reconnaissance forces can perform initial security operations during the crossing of a gap.
     The corps further ensures that all available joint and Army reconnaissance capabilities are employed early in
     the gap crossing planning process.

     6-91. In a retrograde gap crossing, the commander must get all nonessential support forces across the gap
     early and disperse them in locations that can support the remainder of the operation. Units may need to
     destroy equipment they cannot quickly recover and that is in danger of being captured. When possible, units
     should recover bridging equipment early and use other means to cross the last units on the far side.

     6-92. The corps must develop contingency plans for the gap crossing operation. An example of a corps-level
     contingency is the loss of a crossing site, whether through friendly or enemy actions. These plans should
     include specific conditions that commit to different branches of the plan. For example, if a small percentage
     of the force is across the gap when a crossing site is interdicted, whether by accidental friendly actions or
     enemy attack, then the corps executes branch A. If most of the force is across the gap when it is closed, then
     the corps executes branch B.

     6-93. The primary risk for the corps commander is the failure of a gap crossing that is partially complete,
     isolating a portion of the corps on the far side of the obstacle. To mitigate this risk, the corps prepares
     contingency plans and expends maximum effort to complete the crossing once forces are committed. Speed
     is essential to the operation. Corps commanders enhance the tempo of operations by using multiple crossing
     sites, higher capacity crossing capabilities, and limiting the complexity of follow-on objectives in the
     bridgehead to enable divisions to mass combat power on the far side of the gap. Corps commanders also pay
     close attention to the mobility of subordinate units, maximizing mounted movement and maneuver however
     possible. Commanders also mitigate the risk of partial corps isolation by ensuring the security of the crossing
     sites. It may be more important to allocate protection capabilities to crossing sites than to support areas, the
     bridgehead, or some command posts. Corps commanders also direct divisions to aggressively execute

126        ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                   Enabling Operations

offensive operations in the bridgehead, forcing the enemy to focus resources in places other than the crossing
sites.

6-94. Friendly forces are most vulnerable while in the crossing lanes. The corps mitigates this risk by
allocating air defense capability to the crossing site(s), suppressing the enemy’s long-range fires capabilities,
ensuring security operations are prioritized in the scheme of maneuver, and developing a tactical deception
plan.

6-95. Corps artillery may need to be positioned near the crossing sites early in the operation to enable support
to the divisions. The corps may also need to reposition air defense capabilities to protect the operation before
the divisions begin main body crossing. The corps reconnaissance forces will likely cross early in the
operation to allow them to conduct operations on the far side of the crossing and develop the situation. The
corps commander will position a corps command post where they can best influence the operation. This will
depend on a combination of the mission variables and the scheme of maneuver, particularly on simultaneous
or sequential crossing by divisions. The corps tactical command post (known as TAC) may either be
positioned to oversee a crossing site, if conducting simultaneous crossings, or cross into the bridgehead early
to oversee operations on the far-side of the gap. In a retrograde crossing, the TAC will likely oversee the
continuation of the retrograde while the main CP manages the gap crossing. Intelligence activities in the
corps’ close area prioritize the crossing sites and developing the far-side situation. Sustainment activities
during the crossing remain mostly the same as the offensive or defensive operation, depending on whether
the corps is attacking or retrograding. (See figure 6-4 on page 128 for a depiction of corps C2 for multiple
gap crossings.)

6-96. Operations in the corps’ rear area remain generally the same as during offensive or defensive
operations. Depending on the scheme of maneuver and follow-on missions, the corps may need to establish
new support areas across the gap once the operation concludes. The rear CP oversees the reduction of any
temporary gap crossing materials (such as bridges) that are not required to sustain future operations. It will
also manage the reconsolidation and reorganization, when required, of units committed to the crossing
operation, such as bridging units. In a retrograde gap crossing, the rear CP may be the first corps CP across
the gap and manage far-side operations for the corps commander. This will include dispersing sustainment
capabilities and establishing support areas.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  127


---

Chapter 6

                                Figure 6-4. Example of a Corps Wet Gap Crossing

     6-97. Bridging units are essential to corps gap-crossing operations, especially the multi-role bridge
     companies. Due to their low-density, their apportionment must be carefully managed by the corps. The corps
     may also retain OPCON over these units during gap-crossing operations, delegating TACON to the divisions,
     to help the corps manage their use over time. The corps needs to consider subsequent gap crossings when
     making apportionment decisions, retaining some capability for the next anticipated gap.

     6-98. Military police and movement control units assist with managing the flow of traffic at the crossing
     site(s). The corps will make division apportionment decisions for corps-level units. The corps is ultimately
     responsible for ensuring the development of the traffic control plan with the assistance of these units. The
     divisions will execute the plan.

     6-99. The corps must closely manage air defense capabilities for gap-crossings. Risk mitigation may require
     the corps to coordinate with the Area Air Defense Commander to adjust the defended asset list during a
     crossing operation to ensure crossing sites are defended with upper-tier capabilities. The corps will also
     apportion short-range air defense (known as SHORAD) capabilities to the divisions to support the crossing
     site(s), especially by defending against level 1-3 unmanned aerial vehicles, indirect fires, and missiles.

TACTICAL DECEPTION

     6-100. Tactical deception is a friendly activity that causes enemy commanders to take action or cause inaction
     detrimental to their objectives (FM 3-90). TAC-D operations are designed to support the corps commander’s
     plan by enhancing the probability of mission success. TAC-D allows corps to create windows of opportunity
     for subordinate units.

     6-101. TAC-D concepts do not tie into an overall military deception operation. TAC-D differs from MILDEC
     in three ways—

          • MILDEC is centrally planned and controlled through combatant-command derived authorities, but
                TAC-D is not. Tactical commanders (at division and corps echelons) with an approved plan can
                employ TAC-D unilaterally.

128        ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                         Enabling Operations

          • TAC-D actions are tailored to tactical requirements of the local commander and not always linked
                or subordinate to a greater MILDEC plan.

          • The TAC-D approval process differs from the MILDEC approval process. The corps receives
                approval for division TAC-D from its higher echelon command.

     6-102. TAC-D is a commander-driven activity that is structured to set favorable conditions to achieve
     friendly objectives. Corps commanders decide the location and time to employ deception in support of
     operations. Corps use the principles, types, means, and variations of TAC-D to conceal friendly locations,
     capabilities, or intent until a time and place of the friendly commander’s choosing. (See FM 3-13.4 for further
     details.)

     6-103. The corps incorporates tactical deception into its planning from the beginning. The corps deception
     officer, who normally resides in the information operations section, needs to be involved with TAC-D
     planning at the outset. Deception planners are trained on deception planning process, types and principles of
     deception, deception policy, and deception security considerations. Deception planners are trained on
     deception planning process, types and principles of deception, deception policy, and deception security
     considerations. Each course of action developed during military decision-making process should address the
     employment of tactical deception. Depending on the scope of the commander’s desired deception, tactical
     deception operations may be force intensive and have an impact on the available combat power for the corps’
     main effort or other supporting efforts. For example, a feint to support a corps-level operation may require
     an entire division to complete.

     6-104. The timing of tactical deception activities is critical to success. The corps may need to sequence its
     course of action to enable tactical deception operations to occur prior to the execution of the operation, such
     as moving forces to deceive the enemy. The corps develops its understanding of the enemy’s intelligence
     collection capabilities and routines and then uses that information to drive the timing of tactical deception
     operations.

     6-105. Commanders must consider the risk of tactical deception operations when planning them. A
     significant risk is building a plan that is dependent on a certain degree of success from tactical deception to
     make the risk of the main effort acceptable. To mitigate this risk, commanders may allocate additional
     resources to the tactical deception operation to ensure success. They must also develop indicators to assess
     the effectiveness of deception efforts, then apportion resources to collect information against those indicators.

     6-106. Tactical deception also creates the risk of unintended consequences. Since tactical deception
     inherently depends on understanding the enemy commander’s decision-making process, success depends on
     actions outside the control of friendly forces. Planners need to evaluate risks associated with potential
     compromise of the deception plan, risk of the deception plan failing, and risk that successful deception results
     in unintended action by the enemy commander. Deception planners need to develop a security plan to prevent
     the enemy from discovering any planned deception – to include limiting details of the plan to commanders
     and enablers with a need-to-know. Planners need to identify specific termination criteria for TAC-D,
     accounting for maintaining security of TAC-D efforts, addressing risks associated with compromise of the
     TAC-D, and conditions under which TAC-D is terminated due to success.

LINKUP OPERATIONS

     6-107. A linkup is a meeting of friendly ground forces, which occurs in a variety of circumstances (ADP
     3-90). Linkups are conducted to complete the encirclement of an enemy force, assist the breakout of an
     encircled friendly force, join an attacking force with a force operating adjacent to it, or to join an attack force
     with airborne or air assault elements following a vertical envelopment.

     6-108. A corps may be required to conduct a linkup with another force as part of a directed operation from
     the joint task force or multinational land force command. A corps may also be required to direct a linkup of
     subordinate units as a phase of a larger corps operation. In either case, the physical linkup is likely to be
     conducted initially by one of the corps’ divisions. (See figure 6-5 on page 130 for a depiction of a linkup
     operation of two moving forces.)

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  129


---

Chapter 6

                                     Figure 6-5. Corps Linkup of Two Divisions

     6-109. The corps staff develops minimum control measures for units conducting a linkup. The commander
     assigns each unit an AO defined by lateral boundaries and a restrictive fire line that also acts as a limit of
     advance (known as LOA). The restrictive fire line should be as close to the stationary force (if applicable) as
     possible to allow maximum maneuver space for the moving force. The corps designates a no-fire area around
     one or both forces and establishes a coordinating fire line beyond the area where the forces linkup. The linkup
     forces use the linkup points established by the commander to initiate physical contact.

     6-110. The headquarters directing the linkup establishes the command relationships between the converging
     forces both during and after completing the linkup. At some point during the operation or after the linkup,
     higher headquarters may elect to combine both forces into a single force under the control of either
     commander, or both forces can continue to operate separately under the control of the higher commander.
     The headquarters directing the linkup will also assign the converging forces subsequent missions after the
     linkup.

     6-111. Converging forces will maintain command and staff liaison during the planning phase and throughout
     the duration of the operation. Command liaison elements must be capable of continuous operations and of
     communicating directly back to their organic headquarters. This is especially critical during joint or combined
     operations when communications equipment may not interface. Converging forces also need to establish any
     additional communications necessary to support the operation, including the exchange of signal operating
     instructions and physical recognition procedures.

     6-112. The corps must reduce the risks associated with fratricide during a linkup. Ideally, one force will be
     stationary while the linkup force is moving. This will assist with fratricide mitigation. Regardless of whether
     both forces are moving or not, an important risk mitigator is constant communication with the other force

130        ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                   Enabling Operations

and, whenever possible, the exchange of liaisons. This enables situational awareness and common graphical
control measures. However, the commander seeks acceptable balance between necessary restrictive measures
and enabling freedom of action for subordinate units.

6-113. Subordinate units need to have sufficient sustainment to ensure they can reach the linkup point. The
corps must also ensure its support areas and sustainment plan support the tempo and depth of operations
necessary for the linkup. The corps should employ its reconnaissance forces to fight for information and
maneuver its forces with audacity to defeat any enemy counterattacks. If the corps is directing the linkup, it
should employ lethal and non-lethal effects to fix or isolate enemy forces and mitigate risk to the flanks of
friendly units conducting the attack.

6-114. If the corps conducts a linkup with a formation that is not part of the corps, elements of one division
will most likely act as the main effort. The headquarters directing the linkup may dictate conditions for
adjusting the respective operational frameworks of the corps and the other unit. If not, then the corps must
coordinate with the other linkup force to synchronize operations, maintain a common operational picture,
and deconflict fires and airspace.

6-115. If the corps is assisting a friendly breakout, their close area activities are largely METT-TC (I)
dependent. Examples include leading reconnaissance and security forces that will affect the linkup,
employing fires to destroy enemy forces, and organizing the main body to be able to conduct a defensive
operation after linkup. If the corps is the force breaking out from an encirclement, activities in the close area
may include a focus on supporting a penetration of enemy defenses, reinforcing the penetration and
conducting flank security operations, supporting the defense within the encirclement, and sending lead
reconnaissance and/or security forces along the axis of advance to the linkup point.

6-116. When the corps is joining an adjacent attacking force or in a follow and assume role with a friendly
force, for example, linking up with airborne or air assault units that have seized objectives, the dynamics of
the linkup may have other factors to consider. If there is zero or limited enemy resistance between the corps
and the linkup point, the focus will likely be on organizing for the subsequent operation and be executed like
a movement to contact. If there is significant enemy resistance between the linkup forces, the corps will
organize to defeat or isolate the intervening enemy forces, and then transition to the linkup, followed by the
directed subsequent operation.

6-117. Rear operations during a corps linkup operation focus on supporting rapid movement and maneuver.
Since linkup operations generally have specific timelines informed by enemy dispositions or the need to
relieve friendly forces experiencing difficult conditions, most linkups require corps units to move quickly.
The corps must be ready to push sustainment, expand lines of communication, establish new support areas,
and displace CPs when necessary. The corps must also be concerned with security, particularly if the linkup
forces expect to bypass enemy units to maintain their tempo.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  131
