                                                 Chapter 7

                 Consolidating Gains While Fighting

    This chapter addresses what a corps is required to do to start consolidating gains while still
    fighting. This chapter begins with an overview of stability operations and consolidating gains
    followed by a discussion of the challenges inherent in consolidating gains during combat
    operations. It then discusses the types of activities and operations that a corps is responsible for
    in their AO. The chapter concludes with some planning considerations when planning for these
    operations.

CONSOLIDATING GAINS AND ITS RELATION TO OPERATIONAL
SUCCESS

     7-1. Successfully consolidating gains is fundamental to Army operations. Consolidating gains in combat can
     be challenging, requiring careful planning, execution, and the ability to adapt to changing circumstances. A
     corps initially focuses on the exploitation of tactical success as it begins to consolidate gains in its AO. It
     does this by reorganizing its forces and defeating any remaining enemy forces to exploit the success of its
     divisions. Additionally, the corps identifies what is required to perform the Army’s two minimum-essential
     stability operations tasks in their area of operation. These two minimum-essential stability operations tasks
     are—

          • Establish civil security which requires Army units to protect the population from violence and
                restore public order.

          • Provide immediate needs which requires Army units to ensure the population has food, water,
                shelter, and emergency medical treatment.

     7-2. When consolidating gains, commanders balance the competing demands of current and future combat
     operations with the requirement to begin reestablishing stability for the local population as quickly as
     possible. Although stability operations to consolidate gains may be economy of force efforts during the initial
     stages of any operation, they are critical to the long-term success of the corps’ and, by extension, the joint
     forces’ success. The corps must deliberately plan for consolidating gains because it can be resource intensive.
     Depending on the circumstances and assigned objectives, it will also require extensive coordination with
     unified action partners.

     7-3. When consolidating gains during operations, there are several challenges that the corps and its units may
     encounter. In large-scale combat, the most significant challenge is often enemy forces that continue to resist
     or counterattack. Consolidating gains in the face of continued enemy resistance can be difficult and may
     require additional resources. Bypassed enemy forces, special operations forces, and irregular forces can
     threaten lines of operations for future corps operations. The corps will need to conduct operations to defeat
     bypassed forces and reduce any remaining enemy fortifications in their AO. Consolidating gains involves
     occupying, or at least controlling, populated areas which can present additional security challenges. Troops
     must protect the local population while maintaining control over the area. The enemy might try to regain lost
     territory by launching counterattacks in the corps’ AO.

     7-4. The physical features of the terrain can also pose challenges to consolidating gains. For example,
     mountainous or heavily forested areas can provide concealment for enemy forces, making it difficult to secure
     and hold. Similarly, urban environments are particularly challenging due to the presence of buildings, streets,
     and other structures that can be used to hide in or fortify. Maritime environments characterized by limited
     land areas separated, or surrounded, by large bodies of water can make it difficult for the corps to move the
     units and equipment necessary to consolidate gains.

     7-5. The corps may have to reallocate available forces from other operations to consolidate gains effectively.
     This is especially true for engineers, civil affairs, military police, medical, and logistics units that provide
     specialized capabilities but are limited in numbers and availability. The corps may be forced to divert combat
     power in the form of BCTs and/or functional brigades from active combat operations to conduct area security

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  133


---

Chapter 7

     and stability tasks until the required units arrive in theater. Additionally, a corps may require additional assets
     and capabilities, including—

          • Additional forces to provide area security tasks.
          • Host-nation, partner, and allied security forces.
          • ISR assets.
          • Engineers.
          • Military police.
          • Explosive ordnance disposal units.
          • Medical units.
          • Logistics units.
          • Civil affairs units.
          • Psychological operations units.
          • CBRN units.

     7-6. Many of the specialized units required for operations reside in the National Guard or Reserve forces.
     This presents force projection challenges for their employment that the corps commander and staff must
     consider during planning. The corps MCP-OD and TLDs are critical for operations because they provide
     necessary, specialized staff functions. So are various specialized operational medical, engineer, and civil
     affairs units. Mobilization timelines for these units means that they may not be available in theater for several
     months. Planners must identify the need for these specialized skills and request them as early as possible.

     7-7. Consolidating gains requires effective logistics, including the ability to resupply troops, evacuate
     casualties, and maintain communications. After prolonged combat operations, many corps’ formations will
     require immediate resupply and maintenance support to consolidate local gains and prepare for future
     operations. The expeditionary nature of corps operations will place a premium on the corps’ ability to
     prioritize where and how it provides logistics support. The ability of the corps to leverage available means
     of transportation and lines of communication will be critical to balancing competing demands.

     7-8. Large-scale combat operations will almost inevitably result in large numbers of enemy prisoners of war
     requiring the corps to conduct detainee operations. Detainee operations are logistically demanding and
     resource intensive, but the failure to secure defeated enemy forces quickly poses the risk that they will
     reorganize and continue to fight. Large numbers of detainees may require the corps to divert combat power
     to process and secure them. Large-scale combat operations can also result in large, displaced populations,
     particularly from areas that were damaged in the fighting. The corps needs to be prepared to address the
     immediate needs of the local population by ensuring access to food, water, and shelter at a minimum and
     limited medical care when possible.

     7-9. On an increasingly transparent battlefield, protecting units and capabilities performing operations to
     consolidate gains is a challenge. Constant maneuvering during offensive operations provides a measure of
     protection that will be largely absent as units slow down and occupy terrain during consolidation. Medical
     and logistics units are particularly vulnerable. It is impractical to position all the corps critical capabilities far
     enough behind the FLOT to be out of range of all the enemy’s precision munitions. Instead, they should be
     placed in locations at the outer range limit of the enemy’s preponderance of fire support assets whenever
     possible, dispersed over wide areas, and in positions that offer cover and concealment. Dispersion coupled
     with concealment and a detailed deception plan is the key to the survivability of critical assets busy
     consolidating gains. However, the increased distances between formations and capabilities creates additional
     challenges that the corps staff must account for during planning and execution of any operation.

     7-10. Finally, consolidating gains can be time-consuming. It may take time to clear mines, establish defensive
     positions, and build up logistical support. It also takes time to defeat bypassed forces and establish area
     security in the corps AO. A corps commander must balance the need to maintain a rapid tempo to defeat the
     enemy in a timely manner against the risk of overextending lines of communication and culminating before
     achieving their operational objectives. Operations to contain or defeat bypassed enemy forces can be time-
     consuming and resource intensive. The corps commander must balance the need to defeat bypassed forces to
     protect friendly forces and secure terrain with the requirements of current and future operations.

134  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                    Consolidating Gains While Fighting

DEFEATING BYPASSED ENEMY FORCES AND ESTABLISHING
AREA SECURITY

     7-11. Often the corps must continue offensive operations to completely defeat bypassed enemy forces and
     secure key terrain. After conducting offensive operations, a corps may find its units intermingled with enemy
     forces as the result of deliberate action and the vagaries of combat with a peer enemy. For example, when
     lead friendly echelons fix and bypass enemy forces to maintain momentum, follow-on friendly forces must
     defeat the bypassed enemy forces to secure future operations, key terrain, and infrastructure.

     7-12. During attacks of considerable depth, follow and support forces must be provided to protect lines of
     communications, facilitate the forward movement of supplies, and defeat or contain bypassed enemy forces.
     Defeating bypassed enemy forces can pose a significant challenge to the corps, as these forces can continue
     to pose a threat to future corps operations.

     7-13. Defeating bypassed enemy forces is the first step towards establishing area security. Area security is a
     type of security operation conducted to protect friendly forces, lines of communications, and actions within
     a specific area (FM 3-90). During the offense, the corps may task a variety of forces to conduct area security
     operations to protect lines of communication, protect critical capabilities, secure critical infrastructure, and
     control populations and key terrain. At a minimum, the corps must protect lines of communication, reserves,
     and displacing support elements as they are logical targets for the enemy’s own deep operations. (See FM
     3-90 for an expanded discussion of area security.)

     7-14. The failure to establish adequate area security during combat operations can have significant negative
     impacts on corps operations. Friendly forces may be more vulnerable to surprise by enemy forces, which can
     result in increased casualties and lost/damaged equipment. Finally, security is required to transition
     responsibility to a legitimate governing authority and the successful completion of combat operations. If the
     corps is unable to establish this security, they risk prolonging operations, possibly jeopardizing the overall
     success of operations. At a minimum, it will lead to the increased expenditure of resources and commitment
     of forces.

RECONSTITUTION OPERATIONS

     7-15. Reconstitution is essential to maintain combat readiness and effectiveness. Reconstitution refers to the
     process of restoring military capabilities after they have been degraded due to combat or other operational
     reasons. It’s critical after a unit has been engaged in combat operations. Large-scale combat operations
     against capable enemy forces will result in significant losses requiring corps to conduct significant
     reconstitution operations to rapidly rebuild forces necessary to the subsequent phases of the overall operation.
     Reconstitution can be a complex process involving multiple interconnected operations including logistical
     support, personnel management, training, and maintenance. Balancing competing demands requires the corps
     commander to establish clear priorities for reconstitution operation balancing short-term requirements with
     long-term requirements.

     7-16. Reconstitution has three elements: assessment, reorganization, and regeneration. In most instances,
     units will undergo some form of reorganization before requiring regeneration. Assessment refers to a
     commander’s estimate of the unit’s ability to accomplish its mission. The assessment must include the
     commander’s judgment of all factors. While some factors, such as personnel and equipment availability, are
     easily and objectively defined, the unit commander is in a unique position to judge the less quantifiable
     aspects of the unit.

     7-17. Reorganization is all measures taken by the commander to maintain unit combat effectiveness or return
     it to a specified level of combat capability. At any level, reorganization is accomplished internal to an
     organization, and it does not require significant resources from higher echelons to accomplish.
     Reorganization does not significantly increase combat power within a degraded unit. An effective
     reorganization does, however, make the most productive use of available resources and leadership.

     7-18. Regeneration is the rebuilding of a unit through large-scale replacement of personnel, equipment, and
     supplies, including the reestablishment of essential C2 and the conduct of mission-essential training for the
     unit. Regeneration of a BCT by the corps can require significant external support and resources from the
     theater and national level. Personnel and materiel must be integrated into the formation, and the unit must be

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  135


---

Chapter 7

     trained before being employed in combat. Because of the time required for regeneration activities and the
     space and resources needed for units to train, regeneration usually occurs at the corps echelon and above, out
     of contact with enemy forces.

     7-19. Reconstituting forces in combat can also be challenging for several reasons:
          • Operational tempo: Reconstituting forces in combat often needs to be done quickly to deny the
                enemy the opportunity to gain an advantage. However, the process of regenerating forces can be
                time consuming if it involves training new personnel or repairing damaged equipment.
          • Logistical constraints: Reconstituting forces in combat requires a steady supply of personnel,
                equipment, and supplies.
          • Personnel issues: Combat takes a significant toll on soldiers, both physically and mentally.
          • Technological constraints: Modern equipment can be complex and expensive, and it may not be
                possible to repair or replace equipment losses quickly or easily. This can limit a corps’ ability to
                reconstitute capabilities.
          • Tactical and strategic considerations: Joint force commanders and the corps as the senior tactical
                formation may need to make difficult decisions about where to allocate resources, which units to
                reconstitute first, and how to balance the need for immediate combat power with the need to prepare
                for future operations.

     7-20. The resources required to regenerate a unit will likely place severe constraints on a commander’s
     decision to regenerate and the degree to which those units will be restored. To regenerate a severely attrited
     unit, a commander may have to divert resources from elsewhere in the command, change priorities, or request
     assistance from higher echelon headquarters. Due to resource constraints, commanders probably will be
     unable to replace losses in a one-for-one fashion or replace losses in all units. During large-scale combat
     operations, units that are still combat effective continue to fight understrength. The corps prioritizes units
     that are no longer combat effective for reconstitution.

     7-21. A unit may be attrited to such a point that the resources required to regenerate sufficient combat power
     and return the unit to an effective fighting force can exceed the resources available to the commander. In
     such a case, the commander may decide to redistribute the remaining combat power of that unit to other units
     based upon coordination between corps and division commanders. Redistribution is the reassignment of
     personnel and equipment among other formations within the command, preferably as units when possible.

     7-22. Unit status is key to initiating reconstitution. The corps commander decides to reconstitute a unit when
     it has become combat ineffective and resources exist to do so. Reconstitution restores the unit to the required
     level of combat effectiveness to accomplish its future mission. Besides normal support actions, reconstitution
     includes—

          • Removing a unit from combat.
          • Using external assets to assess the unit.
          • Reestablishing the chain of command.
          • Training the unit for future operations.
          • Reestablishing unit cohesion.

     7-23. Division, corps, and theater army headquarters should designate regeneration task forces in standard
     operating procedures. While performing regeneration tasks, regeneration task force elements are not
     performing their normal missions. The commander determines whether scarce resources required to
     regenerate a unit would be better used elsewhere in the command to accomplish its overall mission. (See
     ATP 3-94.4 for additional information on reconstitution operations.)

PERFORMING MINIMUM ESSENTIAL STABILITY OPERATIONS
TASKS

     7-24. Army operations are always conducted with the long-term consolidation of gains in mind. In large-
     scale combat operations, a corps will most likely be the senior tactical formation and Army headquarters
     during operations. As such, the corps will be responsible for integrating stability operations across their areas
     of operations, delegating tasks, and identifying those mission essential stability tasks, and ensuring unity of
     effort with organizations outside of their formation operating in the same spaces. Corps tactical formations
     initially limit their stability efforts to the minimum-essential stability tasks. As operations progress and major

136  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                    Consolidating Gains While Fighting

     combat operations cease in portions of their assigned AOs, stability operations conducted by the corps may
     increase, requiring additional forces and capabilities to reestablish civil security and begin the tasks of
     rebuilding as directed.

             Note: When conducting operations inside the territory of an ally or partner, the corps should
             coordinate for as much host nation support for stability tasks as possible.

     7-25. Performing minimum essential stability operations tasks during combat operations can be challenging,
     requiring the corps to balance the need to defeat the enemy with the need to establish a secure and stable
     environment for the local population. Army forces are morally and legally required to conduct
     minimum-essential stability operations tasks during all operations. These tasks provide for the immediate
     protection and well-being of civilian populations to the greatest extent possible based upon the mission.
     Besides providing immediate assistance, these tasks also set the conditions for future stability operations.

     7-26. Generally, during large-scale combat operations, corps retain responsibility to conduct these
     minimum-essential stability operations allowing their subordinate divisions to focus on close combat. Corps
     are responsible for identifying and coordinating with local civilian and host nation organizations to ensure
     that these tasks are being conducted. The corps is also responsible for providing support as necessary to
     ensure those organizations can complete these two tasks. Where there are no civilian or host-nation
     organizations present to conduct these tasks, corps commanders are responsible for identifying the
     requirements and task organizing forces to conduct these tasks in their areas of operation.

     7-27. Limitations in resources, security, and logistics challenge the corps’ ability to perform these tasks
     without significant augmentation. Stability tasks often require significant resources, which may not be
     available during ongoing combat operations. A corps will generally establish a civil-military operations
     center led by either the corps G9 or the senior civil affairs headquarters assigned to the corps, to coordinate
     corps support for stability operations. (See FM 3-57 for more information.) A corps may require several
     additional brigades, or an additional division, depending on the situation. Reestablishing security may be
     difficult due to bypassed enemy forces that continue to pose a threat or if there are ongoing combat operations
     in the vicinity of local population. Stability operations also require significant logistical support, competing
     with other corps priorities for limited logistical support. Finally, the corps commander will have to balance
     the need to protect their forces with the need to protect the local population.

TRANSITIONING TO POST CONFLICT STABILIZATION

     7-28. Transitions are critical events. While planning for transitions is necessary for all operations, it takes on
     some added significance for stability planning because many of the specialized units required for stability
     operations reside in the National Guard or Reserve forces, requiring significant mobilization timelines. The
     corps must identify and request the required force early during operations well before the end of combat
     operations. Transitions during stability operations have long-term political and practical components that
     must be accounted for in planning and during execution. Early identification of situations that require the
     commitment of corps assets to conduct stability tasks is essential.

     7-29. As large-scale combat operations transition to a post-combat phase, the corps first focuses on the two
     minimum-essential stability operations tasks discussed above. Corps planning during this transition must
     account for the possibility that units engaging in stability tasks can become re-engaged in combat operations.

     7-30. Consolidating gains during operations requires detailed planning during all phases of the operation.
     They may include transitions of authority and control between different military forces, civilian agencies and
     organizations, and host nations. The role of the corps may also change from leading the conduct of stability
     operations to supporting a new organization executing stability operations. These transitions can create
     vulnerabilities to the force, to the mission, and to the local population as enemy forces seek to take advantage
     of any disruptions. The corps commander and staff identify these transitions through deliberate, detailed
     planning tied to decision points to mitigate risks while continuing the transition back to civilian oversight
     and host nation control of operations.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  137


---

Chapter 7

TRANSITIONAL GOVERNANCE

     7-31. Transitional governance is essential for maintaining stability in periods of competition, promoting
     resilience in periods of crisis, and assuring continuity of governance during armed conflict. Transitional
     governance is the actions taken to assure appropriate control and continuity of government functions
     throughout the range of military operations (FM 3-57). The introduction of foreign military forces into
     sovereign territories often has a destabilizing effect on the integrity of local governance structures and their
     ability to control activities within defined political boundaries. When U.S. forces deploy to remove threat
     forces from a friendly country (or to defeat the security forces and governance structures of a belligerent
     country or non-state actor), they must be prepared to assume certain governance responsibilities over newly
     liberated or occupied territories until those responsibilities are able to be transferred to another authority.

     7-32. A corps must prepare to support joint and Army forces as they establish transitional governments to
     assure appropriate control and the continuation of government functions during times of destabilization.
     Army forces assure the effective transition of governance from civilian control to military control and then
     back to civilian control as smoothly and efficiently as possible. Transitional governance has two primary
     missions, which are to provide transitional military authority and to provide support to civil administration.
     Since a corps does not have the resident expertise to conduct these missions, it will typically focus on
     reestablishing local security and providing forces to perform specific functions as required. Civil affairs
     formations and staff planning capabilities are critical to these operations. (See FM 3-57 for more information
     on CAO.)

UNITY OF EFFORT

     7-33. Maintaining unity of effort during transitions is critical. The corps commander and staff will devote a
     significant amount of time communicating with the various stakeholders and organizations involved in the
     operation to ensure that competition among organizations and diverging objectives doesn’t threaten the
     overall unity of effort. This communication is critical to ensuring continuity and understanding as forces
     draw down and are replaced, units are task organized into new configurations, and different
     intergovernmental or host nation organizations assume the lead for operations. Finally, the corps needs to be
     in continuous communication with the theater army to ensure that they are prepared to receive responsibility
     for enduring military tasks when the corps redeploys.

138  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                Appendix A

                                     ARFOR

    This appendix discusses the role of the ARFOR. While an ARFOR controls forces
    operationally, this appendix discusses the ARFOR’s administrative and support responsibilities
    to subordinate Army forces and to other Services in a joint force.

ARFOR OVERVIEW

     A-1. The ARFOR is the Army component and senior Army headquarters of all Army forces assigned or
     attached to a combatant command, subordinate joint force command, joint functional command, or
     multinational command. The ARFOR is the Army component of any joint force. Army doctrine
     distinguishes, however, between the Army component of a combatant command and that of a joint force
     formed by the combatant commander. The Army component of a geographic combatant command is called
     the Army service component command (ASCC), and the Army component of the subordinate joint force is
     an ARFOR.

             Note. The Army’s designation for an ASCC assigned to a geographic combatant command is the
             theater army.

ARMY SERVICE COMPONENT COMMAND

     A-2. The ASCC is the senior Army command assigned to a combatant command. It consists of the Army
     Service component commander and all those Army forces, such as individuals, units, detachments,
     organizations, and installations under that command, including the support forces assigned to a combatant
     command or further assigned to a subordinate unified command.

     A-3. ASCC commanders exercise command authorities, normally OPCON, of assigned and attached Army
     forces as delegated by the combatant commander, and ADCON as delegated by the Secretary of the Army.
     ADCON is the Army’s responsibility to administer and support Army forces even while in a combatant
     command AOR. OPCON is the basic authority for command and control of the same Army forces.

     A-4. ADCON for Army units in a combatant command normally extends from the Secretary of the Army
     through the ASCC, through an ARFOR, and then to the Army units assigned or attached to an Army
     headquarters in that command. However, ADCON is not tied to the operational chain of command. The
     Secretary of the Army may redirect some or all Service responsibilities outside the normal ASCC channels.
     In similar fashion, the ASCC may distribute some administrative responsibilities outside the ARFOR.

ARFOR IN A SUBORDINATE JOINT FORCE

     A-5. All joint force commands that include Army forces have an ARFOR. JTFs are the organizations most
     often used by a combatant commander for contingencies. The typical JTF has a combination of service and
     functional components (see figure A-1 on page 142). JTF commanders normally exercise OPCON of all U.S.
     forces through functional component commands, Service components, subordinate JTFs, or a combination
     of these. The senior Army officer assigned to a JTF, other than the JFC and members of the JTF staff,
     becomes the ARFOR commander. The ARFOR commander answers to the Secretary of the Army through
     the ASCC for most ADCON responsibilities.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  139


---

Appendix A

                               Figure A-1. Possible Joint Task Force Components

ARFOR RESPONSIBILITIES

     A-6. The theater army provides sustainment to all Army forces stationed in, transiting through, or operating
     in an AOR. It also provides most ASOS, common-user logistics, and Army executive agent support to unified
     action partners in an AO. The theater army executes these sustainment responsibilities through its assigned
     TSC.

     A-7. The ARFOR in a subordinate joint force provides administrative and logistics support to all Army
     forces and retains OPCON over Army units not subordinate to another component of the JTF. Since the
     preferred approach to establishing a JTF headquarters is use of an existing Service headquarters, the JTF
     commander (if an Army headquarters) retains all responsibilities associated with both headquarters (the
     ARFOR and JTF). This can overload the JTF headquarters unless the commander delegates authority for
     Service-specific matters to another commander. For example, when a corps headquarters transitions to a JTF
     headquarters, the corps commander becomes the JTF commander. The corps retains ARFOR responsibilities
     through the ASCC back to the Army, unless the corps commander shifts Service responsibilities to another
     headquarters. The corps commander normally designates a subordinate Army commander and staff as the
     deputy ARFOR commander to perform those duties.

             Note. Generally, an Army headquarters that transitions to a JTF headquarters designates a
             subordinate Army commander and staff as the deputy ARFOR commander for performing
             ARFOR duties.

140         ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                                            ARFOR

ARMY SUPPORT TO OTHER SERVICES

     A-8. In addition to controlling Army forces, the ARFOR coordinates ASOS. ASOS includes provisions of
     common-user logistics and executive agent support to the JTF as required by the JTF establishing authority.
     ASOS is directed through Title 10, DOD executive agent, and combatant commander directed authorities.
     The ARFOR headquarters manages support to other Services including, but not limited to—

          • Missile defense.
          • Fire support.
          • Base defense.
          • Transportation.
          • Fuel distribution.
          • General engineering.
          • Intra-theater patient movement.
          • Veterinary services.
          • Logistics management.
          • Communications.
          • CBRN defense.
          • Consequence management capability.
          • Explosive ordnance disposal.
          • Detainee operations.

     A-9. ASOS affords support external to Army forces to increase or improve capabilities. Paragraphs A-10
     through A-26 provide descriptions focused on a few key capabilities provided through ASOS. A few of the
     key examples of ASOS provided at theater and operational levels include sustainment, general engineering,
     protection, chemical, biological, radiological, and nuclear, AMD, and signal. The ARFOR commander
     advises the JFC on the employment of Army organizations and their capabilities and ensures a clear
     understanding of capability requirements and gaps.

Sustainment

     A-10. Sustainment operations are critical to enabling operational reach. The Army’s sustainment capability
     provides crucial theater support that further enables the strategic and operational reach of the joint force.
     Much of this support is provided through ASOS.

     A-11. In all joint operations, sustainment is a Service responsibility except as specified by DOD directives,
     combatant commanders’ lead Service designations, or inter-Service support agreements. Each Service retains
     its responsibility for sustainment. Combatant commanders direct theater army commanders to provide
     common-user logistics and ASOS, agencies, or multinational forces, as required. However, shared
     sustainment responsibility or common-user logistics is more effective, especially for joint operations. Title
     10 authorizes combatant commanders to assign common-user logistics responsibilities that overlap the
     military department’s prescribed functions. Additionally, directive authority for logistics is the additional
     authority used by combatant commanders to eliminate duplicated or overlapped sustainment responsibilities.
     The theater army coordinates with the combatant command staff to determine joint sustainment requirements,
     identify responsibilities, and enable commanders to exercise command and control for sustainment. Theater
     army commanders focus on operational-level theater support involving force generation and sustainment
     during campaigns and joint operations.

General Engineering

     A-12. Specialized engineer units are typically employed at theater and operational levels to augment
     capabilities. The general engineering support is fully coordinated with combat operations, while planners
     consider and anticipate the impact of geography, force projection infrastructure, and available assets. General
     engineers perform engineering tasks, including support to construction management, lines of communication,
     infrastructure development, theater opening and closing, and protection.

     A-13. Engineer assets providing support to the theater and field armies focus on those operations that help to
     set the theater. General engineer units such as echelon above brigade engineer construction companies and

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  141


---

Appendix A

     specialized units enable force projection, expeditionary logistics, and the development of the infrastructure
     needed to support the joint force. Military construction in an AO is characterized by using the minimum
     necessities when possible, and maximizing the use of host-nation facilities such as ports, airfields, roads, and
     barracks. Missions include base camps and improving ports and airfields. Engineer assets in the corps and
     divisional support areas are primarily focused on mobility and limited protection tasks to improve
     survivability. Theater commanders decide on construction standards early in the planning process. (See ATP
     3-34.40 for information on constraints or planning considerations for construction missions.)

Protection

     A-14. Combatant commanders must take protection into consideration as large-scale combat operations entail
     significant operational risk. Protection emphasizes the importance of planning and expanding protection
     priorities, including protecting mission partners, civilian populations, equipment, resources, infrastructure,
     and cultural landmarks across the range of military operations. The synchronization, integration, and
     organization of protection capabilities and resources to preserve combat power from the effects of threats and
     hazards are essential.

     A-15. The ARFOR plays a significant role in setting the theater, which includes conducting activities to
     ensure protection of the force while preserving combat power and increasing the probability of mission
     success. Combatant commanders must consider protection requirements for assets such as aerial ports of
     debarkation, seaports of debarkation, lines of communication, and critical infrastructure at theater level, and
     requirements such as support areas, command posts, and signal nodes at corps and division level. The
     ARFOR anticipates changes to protection prioritization and prepares for reassessment and transition of assets
     throughout operations to coincide with the commander’s priorities.

     A-16. Protection occurs throughout all operations, and ARFOR responsibilities include executing key
     protection tasks such as survivability, coordination of AMD support, CBRN operations, police operations,
     detainee operations, and populace and resource control.

     A-17. If the JFC elects to establish a JSA in the JOA, the ARFOR normally determines its structure and its
     controlling headquarters. The options for the JSA depend on the threat and the mission variables, particularly
     the forces available. If the threat to the JSA is low to moderate, the theater army commander may tailor the
     ARFOR with a MEB specifically for controlling the JSA. The MEB may include additional military police
     and intelligence assets. If the threat to theater bases and lines of communications is significant, the theater
     army may tailor the ARFOR with an additional BCT to control that AO. A third option, in the case of very
     high threat levels, is to assign the mission to an Army division with BCTs and one or more MEBs.

Chemical, Biological, Radiological, and Nuclear

     A-18. CBRN capabilities support operations conducted across the range of military operations by assessing
     CBRN threats and hazards, providing protection against CBRN hazards, mitigating CBRN incidents, and
     providing hazard awareness and understanding. All CBRN functions share a common fundamental purpose
     that fits within the protection warfighting function to achieve or contribute to national objectives.

     A-19. The ARFOR staff integrates CBRN considerations as part of protection. They are responsible for
     understanding the current CBRN conditions and integrating this understanding into the intelligence
     preparation. Ultimately, the ARFOR commander identifies the need and directs protection considerations
     and guidance concerning CBRN hazards to the force.

Conduct Detention Operations

     A-20. Army forces are responsible for conducting DOD detainee operations. When Air Force, Marine Corps
     or Navy forces encounter detainees as a result of their operations, they will transfer those detainees to Army
     military police at the earliest opportunity. Army commanders are responsible for the transfer, backhaul, and
     safeguarding of all detainees taken by the joint force.

     A-21. Although other Services normally play smaller roles than the Army in conducting detainee operations,
     they can still make significant contributions because of their considerable experience in training their
     counterparts. The Marine Corps, Navy, and Air Force should be used, when possible, for training their host-

142  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                           ARFOR

     nation counterparts. Standard joint practices should ensure that the training conducted by all Services is in
     concert with one another. In the case of corrections officer training, training teams may consist of trainers
     from more than one Service.

Signal

     A-22. As commanders operate across the range of military operations, signal capabilities must be tailorable
     to meet the commander’s requirements. The way signal formations deliver communication services varies
     based on the mission. Communications requirements during theater security cooperation activities from fixed
     posts, camps, or stations differ from a BCT commander’s requirements during a movement to contact mission
     in large-scale combat operations. Signal staff officers and leaders need to articulate to commanders what
     communications capabilities are available during critical points of an operation and how these capabilities
     support the exercise of command and control.

     A-23. Networks enable the exercise of command and control and the conduct of support operations through
     the wide dissemination of data and relevant information. Networks allow commanders to communicate
     information and control forces, and they are key enablers of successful operations.

     A-24. Signal support is critical to flexibility and redundancy. While SOPs, various communications
     prioritization efforts, and trained staff personnel can mitigate some of the challenges with C2, effective signal
     support remains central to the operational success of armies, corps, and divisions. When planning to operate
     in a degraded or austere environment, these echelons, especially the corps and division, need to build plans
     from the bottom up. They need to start with single-channel tactical radios and retransmission, and then scale
     up to beyond line of sight systems and command post networks as conditions permit.

     A-25. Operations in degraded environments require units to develop redundant means of communication that
     are not solely reliant on space-based assets to function properly. Truly redundant primary, alternate,
     contingency, and emergency communication plans mix space- and terrestrial-based systems and networks
     and non-technical communication means to ensure the greatest possible flexibility.

Air and Missile Defense

     A-26. Air and missile defense mutually supports all Services by contributing capabilities necessary for
     mission success. Unity of effort, centralized planning and direction, and decentralized execution are key
     elements in countering air and missile threats. Typically, air and missile engagements will be conducted in
     accordance with guidelines and rules established by area air defense commander who is normally the joint
     force air component commander. A joint force air component commander is a commander from the Service
     with the preponderance of air assets and the capability to plan, task, and control joint air operations in an AO,
     generally from either the Air Force or Navy.

     A-27. The AAMDC is the Army’s lead organization for Army AMD forces. The Army AMD commander
     in the roles of the theater army AMD coordinator and deputy area air defense commander is involved in this
     development process by assisting the ARFOR commander and area air defense commander in the planning
     and coordination of the critical asset list and creating the defended asset list.

     A-28. The ARFOR plays a significant role in identifying the priorities for AMD operations. As part of the
     planning and development of the Air and Missile Defense Appendix of the operations order, the ARFOR
     details a prioritized list of critical assets in coordination with the AAMDC. This coordination extends to
     collaborative and exhaustive planning with joint and multinational AMD partners.

THEATER ARMY SUPPORT TO THE ARFOR

     A-29. The theater army assists the combatant command in developing support priorities, including those
     affecting other Services. To support the ARFOR, the theater army also coordinates the projection of
     additional required support from the continental United States, another theater, or an intermediate staging
     base using air lines of communication and sea lines of communication. (See ATP 3-93 for more information
     on theater army support.)

     A-30. The theater army is the ASCC and has the responsibility to control attached and assigned Army forces
     in the AOR through ADCON and OPCON or TACON. As the Army component of the combatant command

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  143


---

Appendix A

     the ASCC is the ARFOR for the theater, and once delegated to a subordinate Army formation, the Army
     component of the subordinate joint force is an ARFOR for the JOA.

     A-31. The theater army commander will specify the ADCON responsibilities of the ARFORs with the theater
     army normally retaining control of RSOI; logistic support of the deployed force; personnel support; finance
     operations support; and medical support. Administrative responsibilities normally retained by the ARFOR
     include internal administration and discipline, training in the JOA, and Service-specific reporting. The theater
     army normally retains OPCON of Army sustainment and medical commands operating in the JOA. The
     theater army commander establishes an Army support relationship between the ARFOR and these units. It
     commands all Army forces in the region until the combatant commander attaches selected Army forces to a
     JFC.

     A-32. The theater army initially maintains control of all Army units assigned to an AOR until that control is
     passed to the senior Army force or ARFOR in a subordinate JOA. As part of the controlling of Army forces,
     the ARFOR maintains ADCON of Army forces and addresses Service responsibilities such as coordinating
     ASOS. (See ADP 4-0 for more information on ASOS.)

FIELD ARMY AS AN ARFOR

     A-33. Large-scale combat operations often require the Army to conduct land operations with multiple corps
     sized formations as part of a joint or multinational command. Large-scale combat operations could require a
     theater army headquarters to expand and transform into an operational land headquarters (field army
     equivalent) exercising command over multiple Army corps and a Marine expeditionary force.

     A-34. A field army (when constituted) performs operational ARFOR tasks and is the Army component of the
     joint force to which it is assigned. A field army, specifically tailored to mission requirements, may be
     assigned to a joint force command with an enduring operational requirement. Typically, a subordinate unified
     command is established instead of a JTF when a military operation is anticipated to be enduring. In this case,
     a field army would be appropriate as the Army component or ARFOR to the subordinate unified command.
     The theater army exercises ADCON over the field army and its subordinate Army forces, and it provides the
     field army and its joint force command with all Army Service functions.

CORPS AS AN ARFOR

     A-35. The corps headquarters is organized, trained, and equipped to serve as the ARFOR during campaigns
     and major operations. This is done when commanding two or more Army divisions, while also coordinating
     with supporting theater-level organizations, across the range of military operations. As the ARFOR for the
     JFC, the corps serves as an operational-level headquarters, conducting land operations as the Service
     component. A corps commander and staff should not attempt to simultaneously perform the roles of a JTF
     and an ARFOR headquarters during major operations. The workload associated with simultaneously
     conducting these two roles can overwhelm the commander and staff. The corps can control up to five Army
     divisions in large-scale combat operations. The corps normally has an assigned ESC and one MEDBDE in
     direct or general support. Other theater-level assets are attached as required. The corps allows other theater-
     level assets to adapt to operational- or tactical-level roles depending upon the combatant commander’s
     requirements.

     A-36. In addition to operational requirements, the corps has extensive ADCON responsibilities as the
     ARFOR. When it serves as the ARFOR, the corps is responsible through the theater army commander for
     the Service-specific support of all Army forces in a JOA and for providing any ASOS with forces deployed
     in a JOA. The Army force package attached to the ARFOR receives the sustainment necessary to conduct
     land operations effectively and indefinitely. The corps commander must balance responsibility for Service
     component actions while communicating requests for support from various theater-level commands and
     units.

     A-37. The JTF commander sets priorities for Army sustainment and support to other Services while
     delegating most Title 10 tasks and ASOS to these commands. Sustainment is assigned to the corps ESC.
     Echelons above brigade medical units remain under the C2 of a MEDBDE or theater medical command in a
     direct or general support role to all forces in an AO. Air units require centralized control of AMD
     engagements through the joint air operations center, and signal units must integrate into an AOR-wide

144         ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                                            ARFOR

     network. Therefore, these units may remain attached to the theater commands and provide direct or general
     support. Attachment of units—such as the civil affairs, engineers, military police, and aviation—allows the
     corps commander to allocate their capabilities among the division, corps, and multinational units.

     A-38. A corps as an ARFOR organizes the assigned force to accomplish three operational-level tasks in
     support of the JTF mission. While subordinate Army commanders can perform all or part of these tasks
     within a JOA, they remain the responsibility of the ARFOR commander. The three tasks are—

             ⚫ Conduct operations in support of the joint headquarters.
             ⚫ Establish linkages among unified action partner organizations.
             ⚫ Conduct sustainment operations.

CONDUCT OPERATIONS IN SUPPORT OF THE JOINT HEADQUARTERS

     A-39. The first task of the ARFOR commander in a JOA is to conduct operations in support of the joint
     headquarters. The ARFOR commander advises the JFC on the employment of Army organizations and their
     capabilities. The ARFOR commander ensures that subordinate commanders and staffs are trained, agile, and
     versatile to meet this requirement. The JFC seeks the nomination and selection of Army-apportioned or
     assigned units for assignment to subordinate joint commands from the ARFOR commander.

     A-40. A corps headquarters, when serving as the ARFOR for a JTF, includes the headquarters controlling
     multiple subordinate tactical formations and the Army forces placed under a joint or multinational
     headquarters. The ARFOR within a JOA exercises OPCON over all Army maneuver, fires, and maneuver
     support forces. The Army commander is responsible to the JTF commander for these operational
     requirements. The corps, as the ARFOR for a JTF, establishes what is needed first—for example, construction
     engineers or infantry, and tanks or trucks—connected to the mission’s success or failure. These analyses
     require a full assessment of the operational and mission variables and an understanding of risk considerations.
     The ARFOR commander designates, sustains, and shifts priorities of subordinate ground forces to support
     the joint or multinational plan. The corps staff’s understanding of operational art is essential when the corps
     is the ARFOR.

     A-41. The ARFOR commander’s guidance includes subordinate commander’s missions and tasks expected
     to contribute to the JFC’s plan. This guidance includes the assignment of forces and sequencing of
     subordinate units’ assigned missions and tasks. The ARFOR commander’s guidance also includes any
     delegated authority, other information pertinent to the situation, and any changes that modify subordinate
     missions and tasks.

ESTABLISH UNIFIED ACTION PARTNER LINKAGES

     A-42. The second task of the ARFOR commander in a JOA is to collaborate with the JTF headquarters to
     establish linkages to unified action partners and organizations operating within that area. The ARFOR
     commander leverages existing relationships and partnerships established by the theater army commander
     with unified action partners.

     A-43. Army forces coordinate with unified action partner organizations located within the JOA. This liaison
     can also include organizations located outside the JOA that have the capability to influence activities within
     the ARFOR’s AO, which includes establishing lateral liaison with other Services, and higher and lower
     echelon liaison with the appropriate joint or multinational force staffs and any subordinate joint or
     multinational organizations as required. The ARFOR commander needs to understand the capabilities that
     other organizations can provide and any support they require from the ARFOR to enable their operations.
     This understanding enhances the opportunity for synergy within the force.

     A-44. Like the exchange of liaison teams, the ARFOR augments a joint force staff or receives augmentation
     from joint forces when Army forces form the core of a joint staff headquarters. The ARFOR interfaces with
     joint force command’s information systems. These systems require specific hardware that are unique to the
     joint force headquarters and require special Army efforts for effective joint coordination.

     A-45. Army intelligence elements closely coordinate with unified action partner organizations to establish
     the mutual supporting intelligence structure required to support the ARFOR and JTF’s operations. The
     intelligence staff assigns collection capabilities consistent with available assets, conducts timely all-source
     analysis, and provides rapid dissemination of available combat information and intelligence.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  145


---

Appendix A

CONDUCT SUSTAINMENT OPERATIONS

     A-46. The third task of the ARFOR commander is to execute Title 10 responsibility by supporting operations.
     Most operational considerations are logistics, but they can include significant general engineering efforts,
     depending on existing infrastructure. In the force-projection mode, decisions made early are highly
     significant as the time for combat operations approaches. Decisions such as the sequencing of arriving forces
     and equipment are often not reversible.

     A-47. The JTF commander is not responsible for Service-specific matters involving administration and
     support of Army forces. The ARFOR commander answers to the Secretary of the Army through the theater
     army for Service-specific matters, whether it is a theater army or functional command (for example, the
     Surface Deployment and Distribution Command of the U.S. Transportation Command). A theater army
     provides ADCON or Title 10 authorities and responsibilities for all Army units in a JTF, including those not
     under OPCON of the headquarters. In certain circumstances, such as geographic separation between the
     ADCON headquarters and the intermediate tactical headquarters, the theater army commander can delegate
     authority to execute specified administrative tasks to Army component commanders under OPCON of
     different JTFs operating in JOAs within the supported CCDR’s AOR.

     A-48. The ARFOR in a JOA identifies requirements, establishes priorities of support for Army forces, and
     coordinates with the JTF providing sustainment. The theater army is always the ARFOR to the combatant
     command. In this case, each subordinate commander potentially has subordinate Army forces, while the
     theater army exercises ADCON of all Army forces across the AOR. The theater army provides ASOS,
     (including common-user logistics), assignment eligibility and availability, and sustainment to interagency
     elements and Army, joint, and multinational forces in a JOA.

     A-49. Each joint or multinational organization with Army forces has an ARFOR commander who ensures
     that delegated ASOS requirements are met. These support requirements, which include logistics, personnel
     services, finance and comptroller, and health service support flow through the chain of command.

     A-50. In some limited contingency operations, the JTF commander may elect to employ Marine Corps and
     Army forces as Service components, instead of creating a functional joint land component headquarters. In
     that case, the ARFOR and Marine Corps forces would exercise OPCON and ADCON over their respective
     Service forces. This arrangement may occur in a forcible entry operation due to the complexity of vertical
     insertion, or during amphibious operations with limited time available for joint integration. As soon as the
     JOA matures with the arrival of follow-on forces and headquarters, the JTF commander may establish a land
     component command.

DIVISION AS AN ARFOR

     A-51. A division commands multiple Army brigades and is one of the Army’s tactical headquarters for
     decisive action. When required it may serve as a JTF or joint force land component headquarters in a limited
     contingency operation. As required, a division may be the ARFOR and the joint force land component in a
     JTF. When the division headquarters serves as a JTF headquarters or a joint force land component
     headquarters, it requires joint augmentation.

     A-52. A division headquarters may serve as a joint force land component headquarters with staff
     augmentation in a limited contingency operation. With extensive joint augmentation, it may serve as a JTF
     for a limited contingency operation. When serving as the ARFOR or joint force land component, a division
     is primarily concerned with the conduct of operational tasks. The theater army provides most of the ADCON
     and Army support to forces deployed in a JOA. Joint manning documents specify other Service augmentation
     according to the role determined by the combatant commander, either as a JTF or as a joint force land
     component headquarters. When serving as a JTF headquarters, the division headquarters organizes and
     operates in accordance with joint doctrine. (See JP 3-33 for doctrine for the JTF. See JP 3-31 for doctrine for
     the joint force land component command.)

     A-53. When it is the joint force land component, an Army division becomes the ARFOR. As the ARFOR,
     the division retains OPCON of all Army forces not subordinated to another component of the JTF. To
     function effectively as both the joint force land component and the ARFOR, the division headquarters
     requires strong coordination with the TSC. This enables the division to oversee logistic and administrative

146  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                                      ARFOR

support to all Army forces while also providing logistic support to other Services and multinational units.
Without it, the ARFOR responsibilities for sustainment would degrade the operational focus of the division.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  147

                                                Appendix B

        Transitioning the Corps to a Joint Headquarters

    This appendix describes the types of Joint Task Forces, the formation authority, and planning
    considerations when transitioning a corps headquarters into a Joint Task Force Headquarters.

  SECTION I — JOINT TASK FORCE HEADQUARTERS

                                                          Joint Formations

             The individual Services have evolved capabilities and competencies to maximize their
             effectiveness in their respective domains. Even more importantly, the ability to integrate
             these diverse capabilities into a joint whole that is greater than the sum of the Service
             parts is an unassailable American strategic advantage.

                                                                                                               Admiral M. G. Mullen
                                                                                Former Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff

     B-1. Joint task forces operate in a complex strategic environment that consists of a variety of national,
     international, and global factors. As such, the formation authority for JTFs is relegated to senior levels of the
     DOD. Combatant commanders have the authority and are the most probable headquarters to establish a JTF
     within their AOR. However, the Secretary of Defense, a subordinate unified command commander, and a
     commander, joint task force (known as CJTF) may also establish subordinate JTFs.

     B-2. During limited contingency and crisis response operations on land, a corps headquarters is the preferred
     Army unit to form a JTF headquarters. Corps have senior general officers and staff with extensive military
     experience. Additionally, corps are trained and ready to respond rapidly, but they require significant
     personnel and equipment augmentation from the other service departments, the CCDR, multinational forces,
     and other United States Government agencies. The scope of augmentation is largely dependent on the mission
     for which the JTF was formed.

             Note. Joint and Army doctrine states that the Army corps is the preferred option for forming a
             JTF, while CCDRs often see theater armies as the preferred option. The Army has limitations in
             terms of the number of corps available and in the required pre-deployment preparation.

TYPES OF JOINT TASK FORCES

     B-3. Operational JTFs are the most common types of JTF and are established in response to a Secretary of
     Defense approved military operation or crisis. The establishing authority normally disestablishes an
     operational JTF when the mission is complete; however, an operational JTF can transition to a standing JTF.

     B-4. Contingency JTFs are identified and designated, as needed, to support operation plan (known as
     OPLAN) execution or specific on-call missions, such as national special security events. These JTFs
     normally remain in a cadre or reduced manning status for planning purposes until activated via a SecDef
     execute order (known as EXORD).

     B-5. Standing JTFs were established as an operational JTF and remained in place due to the enduring nature
     of their mission. Examples include JTF Bravo (USSOUTHCOM), JTF-N (USNORTHCOM), and
     JIATF-West (USINDOPACOM).

     B-6. Combined Joint Task Force Headquarters are multinational, joint force headquarters created by
     agreement through Allied and partner nations. The purpose of creating a commander, joint task force is to
     provide the Alliance with flexible and efficient means to generate coordinate military and governmental
     efforts across an array of nations, and military services, under one, unified headquarters.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  149


---

Appendix B

     B-7. Joint Task Force-State is a National Guard headquarters that can provide C2 over all National Guard
     forces in the establishing state for the governor and can act as a joint HQ for national-level response efforts
     during contingency operations.

     B-8. A land component command is a component of a unified command, subordinate unified command, or
     JTF. It is responsible to the establishing commander for recommending the proper employment of assigned,
     attached, and available land forces; planning and coordinating land operations; or accomplishing such
     operational missions as may be assigned. An annually updated memorandum from the Secretary of Defense
     assigns forces to CCDRs. For limited contingencies, the corps is the preferred echelon to form the land
     component command. The land component headquarters performs the staff actions necessary to translate the
     operational directives of the JTF commander into definitive tactical actions for subordinate unit execution.

  SECTION II — FORMING THE JOINT HEADQUARTERS

     B-9. Once the corps is designated in a joint role, the commander has the authority to form a joint
     headquarters under a named operation. Through this authority, the commander establishes subordinate
     commands, assigns responsibilities, establishes or delegates appropriate command relationships, and
     establishes coordinating instructions for the component commanders. This contributes to the forming of a
     joint headquarters.

     B-10. Forming a joint headquarters provides for unity of command, centralized planning, and direction. Unity
     of command is necessary for effectiveness and efficiency. Centralized planning and direction are essential
     for controlling and coordinating the efforts of friendly forces. Decentralized execution is essential because
     no one commander can control the detailed actions of many units or individuals.

     B-11. The ability to task-organize a corps based on the scope and scale of the operation makes it suitable to
     serve as the nucleus for JTFs. A corps habitually trains personnel and exercises missions to perform C2 in
     large-scale combat operations. Therefore, with augmentation, a corps has the expertise, knowledge, and staff
     organization to form and transition to a JTF headquarters. (See JP 1-0, JP 3-0, and JP 3-33 for additional
     information on JTFs. (See figure B-1 for a depiction of a notional JTF headquarters established from an Army
     corps.)

     B-12. The corps headquarters can assume the role as a joint headquarters for joint operations but requires
     augmentation to expand the operational effectiveness of the headquarters. Placement of Air Force, Navy, and
     SOF personnel into key staff positions will make that staff less capable of conducting land operations but
     more capable of conducting joint operations. Identifying joint gaps and capabilities early sets conditions for
     assets and enablers necessary to handle the scope of a joint mission and identifies critical capabilities,
     timelines, and requirements to reorganize the Army headquarters into a joint headquarters.

     B-13. When a corps organizes as a joint headquarters, the commander incorporates multinational forces into
     planning, executing, and assessing areas of training and operations. Corps train to share information and
     understanding across multinational forces and echelons, and they prepare tactical formations to conduct
     operations as part of a multinational force. The corps staff utilizes LNOs to best understand and employ the
     capabilities of multinational forces, and other mission partners, across all domains to support mission
     requirements within the Joint Operation Area. The corps then allocates resources to multinational
     organizations or synchronizes the application of those multidomain effects in its main or supporting efforts.

150  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                           Transitioning the Corps to a Joint Headquarters

Figure B-1. Corps Staff Efficacy While Aligned as a Joint Task Force

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92                                             151


---

Appendix B

  SECTION III — JOINT HEADQUARTERS AUGMENTATION

     B-14. A Corps staff may not have the required expertise to fill all JTF positions based on the mission. The
     JTF mission dictates the skills required by the staff. Some skill sets are readily available from the CCDR and
     Army sources, but others require lead time and must be formally requested, especially one-of-a-kind,
     national-level assets such as the Joint Communications Support Element. (See JP 3-33 for more information
     on joint force headquarters. (See figure B-2 for a depiction of corps augmentation to form a joint
     headquarters.)

                         Figure B-2. Corps Augmentation to Form a Joint Task Force

     B-15. The JTF determines the augmentation requirements needed for the task and coordinates support
     through the establishing headquarters. Augmentation of the corps staff is essential in transitioning the corps
     to a JTF structure. Augmentation is organized using a modular concept to address the various staff entities
     such as—

          • Command and staff.
          • Headquarters support and sustainment (life support functions).
          • Signal support.
          • Security support for the joint headquarters.
          • Augmentation in technical areas, such as civil affairs and psychological operations.

     B-16. Augmentation in these areas ensures linkage between the JTF staff and the combatant command staff
     concerning access to information and capabilities available at the combatant command level. Mission
     analysis conducted during pre-deployment or deployment identifies personnel requirements. These
     requirements are documented on joint manning documents with the personnel requirements filled based on
     priority. The Joint Force Headquarters Training website provides JMD examples with a variety of situations
     such as disaster relief, counterterrorism, noncombatant evacuation, and humanitarian assistance.

     B-17. A notional JMD for small-scale contingency operations requires roughly 650 personnel. A corps
     headquarters can fill roughly 480 of these positions. U.S. Army Forces Command and other Army resources
     provide other required resources, such as regional language and cultural experts, in addition to public affairs,
     civil affairs, and operational contract support experts. However, there are roughly 35 positions that must be
     filled by subject matter experts from the other Services, in addition to other governmental agencies operating
     within the JOA.

     B-18. Augmentation for each JTF staff section is available from agencies and organizations that support the
     joint community. Each staff section has two key tasks for mission analysis. First, it forecasts the required fill
     of its authorized positions. Second, it identifies the vacancies to be filled by external sources. Corps
     Headquarters can, as a pre-crisis planning effort, identify the gaps within their organization that would need
     augmentation to assume responsibility as a JTF during a large-scale contingency operation. This would
     generate the running estimate needed by the supported CCDR’s staff to determine resourcing solutions.

INDIVIDUAL AUGMENTATION

     B-19. Newly formed JTF headquarters receive individual augmentees, including Army personnel identified
     and requested by the corps commander and principal staffs to fill slots in the JMD. Once individual
     augmentees are identified and join the corps staff, they may require additional training or certification before
     executing their duties. Frequently, individual augmentees are functional experts who chair, provide guidance,
     or serve in meetings (including working groups and boards), centers, cells, and planning teams associated

152  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                  Transitioning the Corps to a Joint Headquarters

     with joint operations. (See CJCSI 1301.01G for additional information on joint individual augmentation
     procedures.)

JOINT ORGANIZATIONAL AUGMENTATION

     B-20. Several joint organizations exist to augment the joint community in the execution of military
     operations. In addition to deployment support, many of these organizations support Service training exercises
     as resources allow. They also support other forecasted training exercises to include in the Chairman of the
     Joint Chiefs of Staff exercise program. Some joint enabling capability entities are self-supporting, while
     others require support from the supported headquarters. The entities listed in JP 3-33 are valuable assets to a
     newly formed corps JTF headquarters.

JOINT ENABLING CAPABILITIES COMMAND

     B-21. The Joint Enabling Capabilities Command (JECC) is a subordinate command of the U.S.
     Transportation Command (USTRANSCOM). It provides mission-tailored, ready, joint capability packages
     to CCDRs to facilitate rapid establishment of joint force headquarters and, as available, provides support to
     other validated CCDR requirements. This command provides the commander of a JTF with short-duration
     (less than 120 days) support to rapidly establish, organize, and operate a JTF headquarters. The command
     consists of two subordinate elements: the Joint Communications Support Element and the Joint Planning
     Support Element. The U.S. Transportation Command deploys forces from this command in the form of a
     joint enabling capability package. By design, this package is modular, scalable, and tailored to the mission.
     It consists of active and Reserve Component forces, and it includes elements from one or more of the
     command’s subordinate elements.

     B-22. The JECC combines the capabilities of seven unique functional areas to deliver tailored,
     mission-specific support: operations, plans, logistics, intelligence support, knowledge managements,
     communications, and public affairs. These capabilities are embedded in the JECC’s two subordinate joint
     commands. While a standard ready package can include up to 74 personnel (a combination of active, Reserve,
     and civilian personnel), the JFC can tailor the package to meet specific needs. (See JP 3-61 for more
     information on joint public affairs.)

IMMEDIATE RESPONSE FORCE

     B-23. The Immediate Response Force (formerly known as the Global Response Force), consists of forces
     ready to deploy to proactively shape the strategic environment, rapidly transition to combat, and, in conflict,
     contest enemy objectives until follow-on forces arrive. The Immediate Response Force is built around a BCT
     of the 82d Airborne Division and U.S. Air Force Air Mobility Command assets. This rotational organization
     is kept at a high alert to allow it to deploy on 18 hours’ notice without warning. The Secretary of Defense
     delegated to the commander of the U.S. Transportation Command the authority to deploy JECC capabilities
     with the Immediate Response Force Executive Order. (See CJCSI 4120.02F for more information on the
     DOD transportation movement priority system.)

CONTINGENCY RESPONSE FORCE

     B-24. The contingency response force is composed of personnel and assets that are trained and equipped to
     secure after seizure, assess, open, and initially operate airfields. The contingency response force provides the
     engineer, security, communications, and airfield support assets needed to support the forcible entry effort.
     Special capabilities that are not present in every contingency response force include airborne, air assault, and
     pathfinder elements; expanded combat communications; and expeditionary engineering elements. (See JP
     3-18 for additional information on forcible entry and the contingency response force.)

JOINT EXERCISE PROGRAM

     B-25. Each JECC subordinate command participates in priority combatant command exercises. JECC
     support for joint exercise program events is requested through the Joint Training Information Management
     System process. The commander, U.S. Transportation Command, in accordance with the Chairman, Joint
     Chiefs of Staff exercise priorities, approves requests for JECC exercise support on a case-by-case basis. JECC
     forces may be requested for other joint operational requirements via routine temporary duty travel orders.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  153


---

Appendix B

     These requests are approved on a not-to-interfere basis with JECC primary mission requirements. The
     requesting combatant command may use informal channels for this type of support.

JOINT COMMUNICATIONS SUPPORT ELEMENT

     B-26. The Joint Communications Support Element provides rapid, reliable, and interoperable
     communications that link the JTF commander and staff to the President and the Secretary of Defense,
     CCDRs, their component headquarters, joint special operations task forces, and multinational partners. This
     element’s tactical communication packages vary in capability from small initial-entry and early-entry teams
     that enable rapid formation of a JTF headquarters to significantly larger deployable joint C2 systems. They
     can provide en route communications support. As a low-density, high-value asset, the Joint Communications
     Support Element may not remain in support of a JTF headquarters for the duration of its mission. (See JP
     3-33 for more information on) joint communications support elements.)

THEATER PUBLIC AFFAIRS SUPPORT ELEMENT

     B-27. A theater public affairs support element (known as a TPASE) provides direct public affairs support
     through planning, coordination, execution, and supervision of public affairs operations in support of theater
     army, joint and unified action partner operations. This element, when assigned to the corps JTF headquarters,
     provides the public affairs staff with media operations center capabilities. It provides advice and counsel,
     conducts public affairs planning, conducts public affairs training, facilitates media relations, and facilitates
     public communication and community engagement. The theater public affairs support element is the lead
     element for the corps to facilitate media operations in theater. The corps commander may task and deploy
     the theater public affairs support element in teams according to the mission and gaining command public
     affairs augmentation requirements. (See FM 3-61 for more information on the communication strategy and
     public affairs operations.)

NATIONAL INTELLIGENCE SUPPORT TEAM

     B-28. A national intelligence support team (known as a NIST) provides a rapidly deployable and mission
     tailored national intelligence reachback capability to provide a national-level, deployable, all source
