     intelligence team to meet a JTF’s intelligence requirements. National intelligence support teams are
     nationally sourced and composed of expert intelligence and communications analysts, communicators, and
     managers from the Defense Intelligence Agency, Central Intelligence Agency, National Security Agency,
     National Geospatial-Intelligence Agency, and other agencies. Requests for this national asset are coordinated
     with the Director of National Intelligence in cooperation with the supported geographic CCDR.

DEFENSE THREAT REDUCTION AGENCY

     B-29. The Defense Threat Reduction Agency (known as DTRA) is a DOD agency providing subject matter
     expertise on countering weapons of mass destruction (known as CWMD) and CBRNE operations. This
     agency provides capabilities to reduce, eliminate, and counter weapons of mass destruction threats and
     mitigate their effects. It supports CCDRs and JTF staffs with 24/7 reachback via the Defense Threat
     Reduction Agency operations center or forward deployed counter weapons of mass destruction experts.

JOINT COMMUNICATIONS SECURITY MONITORING ACTIVITY

     B-30. The joint communications security monitoring activity (known as JCMA) provides information
     security monitoring and analysis support to a JTF. This activity is a Joint Chiefs of Staff-sponsored
     organization operating under the auspices of the National Security Agency. Its mission is to conduct
     communications security monitoring (including collection, analysis, and reporting) of DOD-encrypted and
     unencrypted telecommunications signals and automated information systems and monitoring of related
     noncommunications signals. The purpose is to identify vulnerabilities exploitable by potential adversaries
     and to recommend countermeasures and corrective actions. (See DODI 8560.01 for more information on
     communications security monitoring and JP 3-55 for more information on operations security.)

JOINT PERSONNEL RECOVERY AGENCY

     B-31. The Joint Personnel Recovery Agency is a Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff-controlled activity
     that reports to and receives guidance from the Joint Staff Directorate for Joint Force Development. This

154  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                           Transitioning the Corps to a Joint Headquarters

     agency provides joint guidance, develops and conducts joint training, provides support to deployed forces,
     collects joint lessons learned, and supports joint policy and doctrine development. The agency provides
     exercise support to JFCs and staffs as requested and supports operational implementation of joint personnel
     recovery policy. (See CJCSI 3270.01C and JP 3-50 for more information on personnel recovery.)

JOINT WARFARE ANALYSIS CENTER

     B-32. The Joint Warfare Analysis Center assists in preparation and analysis of joint operation plans and
     Service chiefs’ analysis of weapons effectiveness. It supports both crisis operations and contingency
     planning. The center provides this support to JTFs through the supported combatant command. It is a
     functional component of the U.S. Strategic Command.

DEFENSE LOGISTICS AGENCY

     B-33. The Defense Logistics Agency supports the JTF by providing various capabilities, including Defense
     Logistics Agency contingency support teams and other experts embedded physically or virtually with the
     JTF. These teams provide LNOs and functional experts with logistics planning experience, logistics surge,
     and sustainment expertise to the agency. Team capabilities include logistics assistance teams to address
     supply management issues, disposal remediation teams to manage disposal of hazardous waste, distribution
     operations teams to provide expertise in distribution management, and fuel support teams to serve as a liaison
     in bulk fuel operations.

U.S. CYBER COMMAND

     B-34. U.S. Cyber Command plans, coordinates, integrates, synchronizes, and conducts activities to direct the
     operations and defense of specified DOD information network. It conducts, when directed, military
     cyberspace operations to enable actions in an OE and to facilitate U.S. and unified action partner freedom of
     action in cyberspace while denying freedom of action to adversaries.

POSSIBLE ARMY AUGMENTATION FROM THEATER ASSETS

     B-35. Wherever deployed, the corps headquarters serving as a JTF headquarters falls under the ADCON of
     a theater army. Depending on the type and duration of the mission, the supported geographic combatant
     command may direct certain Army organizations. These Army organizations, in the combatant command’s
     AOR, provide individual or unit augmentation to enable the JTF headquarters to accomplish its mission.
     These Army organizations include—

          • Theater sustainment command.
          • Signal command (theater).
          • Military intelligence brigade–theater.
          • Military police brigade.
          • Psychological operations battalion.
          • Civil affairs brigade or command.
          • Theater medical command.
          • Theater aviation command.

     B-36. Other potential augmenting organizations include a battlefield coordination detachment, AAMDC or
     air defense artillery brigade, and other functional organizations. Corps often work with theater assets
     including contracting elements, CBRN elements, EOD elements, engineering assets, military police
     elements, information operations elements, aviation elements, and space operations elements. (See Chapter
     2 for additional information on provided forces. See ATP 3-09.13 for information on the battlefield
     coordination detachment. See ATP 4-10 and ATP 4-71 for more information on operational contracting and
     contract requirements development.

OTHER SERVICE AND LIAISON OFFICERS

     B-37. The JTF headquarters provides trained and knowledgeable liaison teams, and it receives liaison teams.
     Both the required number and skilled composition of these teams are maintained throughout the mission.
     Liaison detachments have four functions: monitor, coordinate, advise, and assist. Each team sent to superior,
     subordinate, adjacent, or supporting organizations has enough staff members to provide 24-hour coverage
     and sufficient highly ranked personnel to gain access to the appropriate echelon at the receiving command.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  155


---

Appendix B

     Each liaison team received from a sending unit is integrated into the staff and provided workspace,
     communications, protection, life support, and sufficient access to the commander and appropriate staff
     members to accomplish its mission. Each corps liaison team experiences challenges when assigned to distant
     locations. Often, liaison teams have limited transportation assets, and they need assistance to receive
     guidance, supplies, mail, and morale support.

     B-38. Upon designation as a JTF headquarters, during either a short- or no-notice contingency or upon
     activation in support of a planned, deliberate operation, the corps commander deploys liaison teams to the
     supported CCDR. The primary tasks of the liaison team are to coordinate operational plans and orders,
     determine joint communications requirements and gaps, and coordinate joint staff augmentation to the corps
     headquarters. The JTF commander may also deploy liaison teams to supporting or subordinate Service
     commands assigned to the JTF.

     B-39. The JTF establishes liaison with any theater special operations command or task force currently or
     projected to have forces operating within the JTF’s JOA. (The theater special operations command has a
     support relationship with its geographic CCDR, but it is assigned to the U.S. Special Operations Command.
     It does not have a command relationship with the supported CCDR.) This liaison is in the form of the JTF
     headquarters receiving a liaison team for the special operations command or task force. In some rare
     situations, the JTF sends a liaison team to the special operations command or task forces headquarters.

     B-40. Current doctrine identifies the chief of staff as the central point of contact for liaison operations.
     However, the number of liaison teams in joint operations with a large military, intergovernmental, and
     multinational force may make this requirement impractical. An alternative solution is to establish a liaison
     office in the main CP associated with the movement and maneuver cell. This office contains a director and a
     small staff to control liaison operations which is sent from and received by the corps headquarters. An
     individual of sufficient rank directs and staffs the office to control the operation. This individual has ready
     access to corps senior leaders to facilitate information exchange. Liaison office functions include maintaining
     liaison rosters, managing status reporting and accountability, serving as an information clearinghouse, and
     providing communications systems. The liaison office also acts as the point of contact for life support and
     other liaison support operations, such as protection, medical assistance, and security.

     B-41. A corps headquarters transitioning to a joint headquarters during critical situations requires liaisons.
     Liaisons are required during pre-deployment academic and training exercises, during RSOI, before new
     equipment training, and during the first few weeks as a joint headquarters.

OTHER AUGMENTATION OR COLLABORATIVE CAPABILITIES

     B-42. Modern OEs contain more than just military organizations, especially if the JTF is engaged in the
     conduct of stability or defense support of civil authority tasks. These organizations provide the JTF with
     capabilities not found in the military. Capabilities are augmented or collaborative. The joint enabling
     capabilities are a type of augmentation. Interagency enabling capabilities, such as linguistic, interpreter, and
     cultural support, do not augment the JTF staff. Rather, they are collaborators focusing on the same objectives.
     They often join a corps headquarters serving as a JTF headquarters after it arrives in the joint AO.

     B-43. Reaching out to joint and unified action partners during the initial formation of the JTF headquarters
     is important, so those partners have the time they need to provide these collaborative capabilities. The corps
     leverages pre-existing relationships where possible to expedite the inclusion of these partners. When working
     with these partner organizations, the commander influences their personnel selection processes when and
     where necessary. Maintenance of current and accurate billet descriptions helps ensure that the personnel
     provided by those joint and unified action partners have the right qualifications.

Interagency Enabling Capabilities

     B-44. Interagency enabling capabilities provide skills that military forces lack to support mission
     accomplishment. The interagency representative provided by non-DOD activities of the U.S. Government
     may not have the skills needed for the mission. JTF leaders must identify knowledge shortfalls and provide
     orientation and training to JTF members. This orientation and training may involve providing equipment to
     facilitate access to the common operational picture and JTF databases.

156  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                  Transitioning the Corps to a Joint Headquarters

     B-45. An Army corps headquarters designated as a JTF headquarters can expect to interact with, receive, and
     provide direct support to other government agencies. These agencies include the American embassy country
     team, the Department of State, Department of Homeland Security, Department of Agriculture, Department
     of Commerce, and Department of Transportation. The list expands considerably when state and local
     government entities are included.

     B-46. Overseas, the JTF headquarters encounters representatives of international organizations, such as the
     United Nations, International Committee of the Red Cross, International Red Cross and Red Crescent
     Movement, and hundreds of nongovernmental organizations. While many of these organizations try to
     remain neutral by not associating directly with the JTF, many interact with it as they pursue their specialized
     missions. Host-nation governments, whether they welcome U.S. presence or not, do interact with the JTF
     headquarters, and they need accommodations. (See JP 3-08 for further information on interagency,
     intergovernmental, and nongovernmental coordination.)

Linguist, Interpreters, and Cultural Support

     B-47. The corps coordinates with the theater army G-2 or combatant command intelligence directorate of a
     joint staff for appropriate linguist support. Interpreters transfer the meaning of one spoken language into
     another spoken language. Translators render the meaning of one written language into another written
     language. Linguists can provide valuable training to the JTF staff and key personnel for a specific JOA. Early
     in the planning and forming stages of the JTF’s lifecycle, staff members identify and resource the
     requirements for linguists, interpreters, and translators.

     B-48. Integrating interpreters and translators into the JTF occurs as soon as possible to obtain security
     clearance and finalize contractual agreements. The corps headquarters develops procedures to vet interpreters
     and translators to uncover biases. The corps staff considers the possibility that they may not have the best
     interests of the JTF foremost in their minds. Other positions are sourced through vetted, contracted
     interpreters. In the event of a shortage of linguists, translators, and interpreters, JTFs may use machine foreign
     language translation devices as a stop-gap measure for translation needs.

     B-49. Cultural support goes beyond the use of local interpreters and local hires to perform life support tasks.
     Inserting U.S. forces into a different culture creates a dynamic situation for commanders to address. Each
     JTF requires cultural understanding to successfully deal with the local population. Knowledge of the local
     culture in terms of the social, political, economic, and demographic factors contributes to understanding a
     people’s culture or a nation’s history, institutions, psychology, religious beliefs, and behaviors. Increasing
     cultural awareness using Department of State regional experts, assigned civil affairs forces and staff sections,
     or SOF and civilian academics with regional expertise, language, and cultural understanding facilitates the
     planning, preparation, and execution of military operations. Additionally, foreign area officers from the
     security cooperation division of the theater army headquarters or internal staff personnel from the corps
     assistant chief of staff, civil affairs operations, may serve as political or cultural advisors to the corps or
     subordinate commanders.

JOINT TASK FORCE STAFF EQUIPMENT AUGMENTATION

     B-50. A joint headquarters formation also involves acquisition and distribution of joint mission-essential
     equipment. Equipping a JTF headquarters, formed around an Army corps headquarters, is closely associated
     with augmentation. A corps headquarters designated as a JTF headquarters works with the theater army and
     the joint community to meet shortfalls through requisitions and redistribution. The joint enabling capabilities
     command can supply shortfalls in equipment, either permanently or on a temporary basis. Most of the
     equipment necessary to give corps headquarters full JTF capabilities is acquired from other sources
     (including commercial sources).

     B-51. The joint mission-essential equipment list includes equipment and capabilities the JTF needs to
     function. Additional equipment is added in incremental blocks to the JTF headquarters to meet the projected
     mission demands. For a corps, the foundation block consists of its organic Service-provided capabilities,
     followed by equipment received from the theater army and the theater signal command or brigade. Usually,
     these capabilities are internal systems that consist of computers with software applications, operating
     systems, office applications and security, associated peripherals, and servers. To fill equipment gaps, units

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  157


---

Appendix B

     identify any continuing shortfall between needs and capability, refine a joint mission-essential equipment
     list, and submit a request through joint or Army channels

     B-52. The theater army for the supported CCDR is partially responsible for equipping a transitioning corps
     headquarters. The equipment available in theater comes from three sources: theater property, Army
     pre-positioned stocks (with Secretary of Defense-level authorizations in those locations where they exist),
     and theater sustainment stocks. Some equipment may not be available. In these cases, the corps staff initiates
     and processes an operational needs statement through the theater army.

     B-53. The JTF logistics directorate is responsible for the sustainment of the JTF in coordination with the
     combatant command logistics directorate. The Military Departments’ contributing forces to the JTF are
     responsible for the sustainment of their forces within the JTF. Therefore, the theater army has Title 10
     responsibilities for Army forces within the JTF. (See JP 4-0 for further joint logistics information and
     requirements.)

     B-54. A corps headquarters often uses its own information systems when designated as the base for a JTF
     headquarters. The corps G-6 considers numerous unified action partner information systems and their
     associated databases. Integrating these systems and establishing interoperability or workarounds with the
     corps’ currently fielded information systems presents training, technical, and operations security challenges
     for the corps exercise of C2. Corps planners account for the security challenges (for example, foreign
     disclosure and network access) with integrating unified action personnel and systems into the corps
     headquarters. A significant part of the pre-deployment preparation in the corps’ CPs involves training in
     using these systems. (See JP 6-0 for information on the joint communications system and planning and
     operational considerations associated with joint communications.)

     B-55. With hundreds of potential applications and thousands of potential users, the JTF staff establishes and
     monitors the corps and joint network infrastructure architectures for reliability. Operations security is an
     important consideration for systems using an air gap to transfer voice and data from one control node to
     another. Commanders and staffs understand the levels of security and security classifications of all the
     systems in the corps CPs.

158  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                Appendix C

                        The Corps in a Joint Role

         This appendix expands on the discussion from chapter 1 by describing corps
         considerations when performing other roles. The chapter begins with an overview and
         then discusses the corps headquarters when serving as joint task force. Next, it
         discusses the corps as the land component command. The chapter concludes with a
         discussion of operational considerations in support of joint operations.

  SECTION I — OVERVIEW OF THE CORPS IN A JOINT ROLE

     C-1. During competition and crisis, an Army corps is uniquely designed to perform joint roles—when
     required—such as a land component headquarters or JTF headquarters. A corps can fill these roles during
     conflict for limited periods, but it requires significant augmentation to be effective during continuous
     operations. During crises, the Army may deploy a corps headquarters and select units as part of a U.S.
     response.

     C-2. The corps is the preferred Army echelon for use as a JTF headquarters because of the seniority of its
     staff and its deployability. Additionally, corps are trained and ready to respond rapidly, but they require
     augmentation. CCDRs transform a corps into a JTF or LCC by augmenting it with personnel and units from
     SOF and other Services. This augmentation does not include the personnel needed to augment the battalion
     network capability for the corps headquarters.

             Note. Joint and Army doctrine states that the Army corps is the preferred option for forming a JTF
             or LCC, while CCDRs often see component commands as the preferred option. The Army has
             limitations in terms of the number of corps available and in the required pre-deployment
             preparation.

     C-3. For limited contingency operations, a corps may find itself serving in a joint role depending on the size
     and duration of operations. These operations typically have a multinational component like those in
     operations in Iraq and Afghanistan. Depending upon violence levels, a corps may not have many nonmilitary
     organizations operating in its assigned operational areas, although there will usually be some.

     C-4. A corps headquarters designated as a JTF headquarters requires a separate ARFOR headquarters
     because of the differing roles and responsibilities inherent in each. For example, a corps JTF would focus on
     the operational level of warfare and use joint doctrine whereas a division ARFOR headquarters would focus
     on tactical operations and use Army doctrine.

  SECTION II — CORPS AS A JOINT TASK FORCE

     C-5. A corps headquarters designated as a JTF headquarters requires a separate ARFOR headquarters
     because of the differing roles and responsibilities inherent in each. For example, JTFs focus on the operational
     level of warfare and use joint rather than Army doctrine. The JTF commander normally exercises OPCON
     over most forces and other resources in the JOA. A JTF can include functional and Service components and
     multinational forces from allied and coalition partners.

JOINT TASK FORCE HEADQUARTERS

     C-6. The corps headquarters can assume the role as a joint headquarters in joint operations, but
     augmentation is necessary to limit degradation of capabilities in directing land operations. Placement of Air
     Force, Navy and SOF personnel into key staff positions will make that staff less capable of conducting land
     operations. Identifying joint gaps and capabilities early sets conditions for assets and enablers necessary to
     handle the scope of a joint mission and identifies critical capabilities, timelines, and requirements to
     reorganize the Army headquarters into a joint headquarters.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  159


---

Appendix C

     C-7. A JTF headquarters is constituted and designated by an establishing authority, such as the Secretary of
     Defense, a CCDR, a subordinate unified command commander, or a state governor (for JTF-state) in
     accordance with the National Security Act of 1947, Title 10, U.S. Code, and Title 32, U.S. Code. A JTF
     commander establishes a JTF headquarters when the scope, complexity, or other factors of an operation
     require capabilities from at least two military departments operating under a single JTF commander. JTF
     commanders are responsible for establishing command relationships between subordinate commanders and
     accomplishing any operational missions.

     C-8. When a corps organizes as a JTF headquarters, the commander incorporates multinational forces into
     planning, executing, and assessing areas of training and operations. Corps train to share information and
     understanding across multinational forces and echelons; and they prepare tactical formations to conduct
     operations as part of a multinational force. The corps staff understands and employs the capabilities of
     multinational forces and other mission partners across all domains to support mission requirements in the
     corps AO. The corps then allocates those resources to multinational organizations or synchronizes the
     application of those multidomain effects in its main or supporting efforts.

     C-9. Based upon the decision of the establishing authority, the commander may exercise OPCON or
     TACON over assigned and attached forces. Establishing authorities for subordinate unified commands and
     JTFs normally direct the delegation of OPCON over forces attached to those subordinate commands. A JTF
     commander with OPCON forces (assigned or attached) can task-organize these forces to accomplish the
     mission. The commander’s intent, concept of operations, and other Service considerations dictate
     organization of these forces. If commanders have TACON of assigned or attached forces, they can task, but
     not reorganize these forces. (See FM 3-0 for additional information on command relationships.)

     C-10. Mission requirements drive the JTF headquarters’ responsibilities. When a corps operates as a JTF
     headquarters, these additional responsibilities drive the required capabilities of the staff, and ultimately the
     organization and staffing needed to support the CCDR. For example, the JTF staff must have awareness of
     joint terminology and familiarity with joint staff processes.

             Note. The establishing authority (the CCDR) designates a Service headquarters as a JTF-capable
             headquarters once that authority is satisfied with its level of training and readiness.

     C-11. The ability to task-organize a corps based on the scope and scale of the operation makes it suitable to
     serve as the nucleus for JTFs of various sizes and durations. A corps habitually trains personnel and exercises
     missions to perform C2 in operations requiring unified action. Therefore, with augmentation, a corps has the
     expertise, knowledge, and staff organization to form and transition to a JTF headquarters. (See JP 1-0, JP 3-
     0, and JP 3-33 for additional information on JTFs

  SECTION III — CORPS AS A LAND COMPONENT COMMAND

     C-12. A land component command is a component of a unified command, subordinate unified command, or
     JTF. It is responsible to the establishing commander for recommending the proper employment of assigned,
     attached, and available land forces; planning and coordinating land operations; or accomplishing such
     operational missions as may be assigned. (For simplicity, this chapter refers to the JTF as the land component
     establishing authority, but it acknowledges that a CCDR or subordinate unified command commander can
     also be the establishing authority.)

     C-13. A JTF commander establishes a Service headquarters as the land component headquarters if a single
     Service (for example, the Army or Marine Corps) will conduct land operations in support of JTF operations.
     A JTF commander may establish a functional land component headquarters when the scope, complexity, or
     other factors of an operation require capabilities from two military departments or a multinational force
     operating in the land domain. When designated as a JFLCC, the corps commander exercises TACON or
     OPCON over Marine Corps forces, as required by the JFC, in addition to the attached Army forces. The land
     component headquarters, at the direction of the JTF, is responsible for establishing command relationships
     for subordinate forces to accomplish assigned operational missions and coordinating with other JTF
     component commands (for example, the JFACC and joint force maritime component commander).

160  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                         Corps in a Joint Role

     C-14. When organizing joint forces, simplicity and clarity are critical. A JTF can enhance synchronization
     of operations not only between U.S. ground components, but also with multinational land forces by providing
     a single commander for land operations. Forming a land component headquarters builds unity of effort, an
     integrated staff, a single voice for land forces and land control operations, a single concept and focus of effort
     for land control operations, and a synchronized and integrated land force planning and execution.

     C-15. A JTF’s land operations support the commander’s operation or campaign objectives or support other
     components of the joint force. Joint land operations require synchronizing and integrating the instruments of
     national power to achieve strategic and operational objectives. The forces conducting joint land operations
     include the Army, Marine Corps, and Navy forces operating on or from land to accomplish missions and
     tasks. Joint land operations also involve multinational land forces. Joint land operations include land control
     operations which employ land forces, supported by or supporting naval and air forces (as appropriate), to
     accomplish military objectives in vital areas of an AO.

     C-16. During crisis planning, forces allocated to CCDRs may differ from those apportioned for contingency
     planning. The CCDR or an established JTF commander selects an Army or USMC organization with which
     to form the land component headquarters.

FORMING THE LAND COMPONENT HEADQUARTERS

     C-17. A corps will require significant augmentation to form an LCC. The headquarters that provides a base
     for the LCC headquarters may retain its Service Title 10 responsibilities (or delegate to one of its assigned
     divisions) as the senior Service headquarters for all Army or USMC forces assigned to the JTF. In rare cases,
     the LCC is resourced with a separate senior Army or USMC commander, and the LCC’s headquarters is
     responsible for the ADCON of the respective Army or USMC forces in the LCC. If this does not occur, the
     LCC maintains responsibility over its parent Service component functions (ARFOR in the case of a corps
     headquarters), which requires the corps staff to split its focus. This arrangement has the potential to
     overwhelm the LCC’s staff as it performs a dual role. An LCC (who is also the corps commander) can
     delegate as many ARFOR duties as practical to a subordinate Army headquarters to facilitate the corps’
     operations.

     C-18. Within the headquarters, the corps commander, deputy corps commander, chief of staff, and key
     members of the staff fully integrate with representation from the forces and capabilities made available to the
     LCC. The LCC’s staff includes the work force and personnel directorate of a joint staff through the
     communications system directorate of a joint staff in addition to personal and special staff sections and the
     standing and on-call meetings as required by the situation. A corps commander, when designated as the LCC,
     provides the core elements of the corps staff to assist in planning, coordinating, and executing functional land
     component operations. (See CJCSI 3100.01F for information on the joint strategic planning system and how
     the DOD assigns forces.)

     C-19. The corps organizes as an LCC’s headquarters in accordance with the JTF’s implementing directive.
     The directive establishes the roles and responsibilities of the LCC and designates the LCC’s mission and the
     forces that constitute the LCC’s battle rhythm. Commanders usually build the LCC’s staff around the corps
     staff and augment it with members of other Service components and interorganizational and multinational
     partners. As required by the JTF, the LCC’s staff receives a special operations liaison element. The LCC and
     chief of staff allocate key staff billets, so the staff appropriately represents all Services. Ideally, for an LCC’s
     headquarters built around an Army corps headquarters, the deputy LCC or chief of staff comes from the
     USMC. Replicating this construct throughout the LCC’s staff ensures all leaders understand the distinct
     capabilities of the Army and USMC to optimize employment of the LCC’s forces.

     C-20. The primary functions of the LCC and staff are planning, preparing, executing, and assessing the
     conduct of offensive, defensive, and stability tasks that achieve the JTF’s campaign plan. In addition, the
     LCC plans large-scale operations, coordinating with the JTF’s other functional and Service components to
     establish necessary conditions within the JOA. The LCC also directs the maneuver of subordinate forces.
     The LCC plans for offensive tasks to secure or retain the initiative, to exploit or pursue enemy forces, and to
     prevent enemy forces from regrouping and regaining the initiative. The LCC plans defensive tasks to gain
     time or space to conduct decisive offensive actions. Even in the defense, the LCC seizes opportunities and
     plans for offensive maneuver, counterattack, and other operations that enable the tactical success of

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  161


---

Appendix C

     subordinate commanders whenever possible. Simultaneously, the LCC conducts stability tasks to meet the
     immediate needs of civilians in the AO until the LCC transfers responsibility for the conduct of tasks to
     appropriate authorities.

When designated as a joint force land component command, the corps follows joint doctrine. As a joint force land
component headquarters, the corps staff has tasks that include, but are not limited to—

             ⚫ Coordinating the planning and execution of joint land operations with the other components and
                  supporting agencies.

             ⚫ Designating the target priorities, effects, and timing for joint land operations.
             ⚫ Providing mutual support to other components by conducting operations within the JOA.
             ⚫ Coordinating with other functional and Service components in support of achieving the JTF

                  commander’s objectives.
             ⚫ Providing a deputy to the area air defense commander (normally the commander of the AAMDC)

                  for land-based joint theater AMD operations as determined by the JFC. The AAMDC provides
                  inputs to the JTF commander’s joint area air defense plan and the airspace control plan for the
                  joint force land component command.
             ⚫ Supporting the JTF commander’s information operations by developing requirements that support
                  land control and synchronize land force information operations assets when directed.
             ⚫ Integrating the joint and Army networks within the DOD information networks throughout the
                  AO. Performing this function may require joint and Service augmentation.
             ⚫ Performing joint security functions.
             ⚫ Establishing standard operating procedures and other directives based on the JFC’s guidance.
             ⚫ Supporting SOF throughout the land AO.

     C-21. To facilitate joint security operations, the JFC may establish a joint security element with a designated
     joint security coordinator to coordinate operations in a joint security area. If the JTF commander gives the
     LCC responsibility for a joint security area, the corps commander may elect to delegate some corps
     headquarters functions to subordinate commanders. The deputy corps commander—with part of the corps
     staff, augmentation from theater echelon units, and an Army maneuver enhancement brigade—receives
     TACON of the theater units in the joint security area for protection and movement. The deputy corps
     commander becomes the joint security coordinator. Alternatively, the commander may designate and
     organize a subordinate task force to focus solely on operations in the joint security area.

     C-22. An associated requirement of the corps headquarters controlling U.S. land forces is for the corps
     headquarters to function as a multinational joint force headquarters for units operating in its AO. In most
     cases, the corps exercises TACON over multinational brigades and divisions. However, in this case, the corps
     commander becomes a multinational force LCC. National caveats often exist on the use of multinational
     forces. Commanders and staff operating as a multinational headquarters must be aware that multinational
     brigades normally lack compatible digital communication capabilities. The digital liaison detachment is
     uniquely adapted to bridge this communication gap with liaison personnel at the corps headquarters. The
     corps also trains in multinational exercises as part of theater engagement and security assistance managed by
     the theater army.

             Note. National caveats are political directives regarding the employment of allied or partner forces
             during multinational security operations. Nations offer their forces, but they use caveats to limit
             their forces to approved activities. Countries can have specific caveats for their operations or
             forces.

COMMAND RELATIONSHIPS WITH THE JOINT TASK FORCE

     C-23. The LCC reports directly to the JTF and advises on the proper employment of land forces. A JTF
     commander has the full authority to assign missions, redirect efforts, and direct coordination among JTF
     subordinate commands. The JTF commander allows Service tactical and operational assets and groupings to
     function as designed. The intent is to meet the JTF’s needs while maintaining tactical and operational integrity

162  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                   Corps in a Joint Role

of Service organizations. (See figure C-1 for a depiction of land component command relationships. See
figure 2-3 on page 27 for a broader example of the corps forces and enablers.)

                             Figure C-1. Land Component Command Relationships

SUPPORT RELATIONSHIPS WITH JOINT TASK FORCE
FUNCTIONAL COMPONENTS

     C-24. The JTF also establishes supporting and supported relationships between components. The CCDR can
     determine not only how to organize the joint force into components, but also how the components relate to
     each other. The CCDR directs support between Services for other than maneuver functions. Support
     relationships are an effective way to achieve unity of effort and to weight the main effort properly.
     Components receive and provide support at the same time. CCDRs designate the range and scope of the
     provided support in the directive or order that established the support relationships. However, the personal
     relationships between the supporting and supported commanders are more important than the doctrinal
     support relationship established.

     C-25. The JTF directs support authority within the joint force. A JTF can direct four different support
     relationships: general support, mutual support, close support, and direct support. Regardless of the
     organizational and command arrangements within joint commands, the ARFOR commander is responsible
     for Service-specific functions and other matters affecting Army forces, including internal administration,
     training, logistics, and Service intelligence operations. (The directive authority for logistics can override this
     Title 10 responsibility. See JP 1, for additional information on support relationships.)

  SECTION IV — OPERATIONAL CONSIDERATIONS

     C-26. As described earlier, the corps can function at the operational level as a JTF headquarters or as a land
     component command. Joint doctrine covers the structure and operations of joint force headquarters in detail.
     This appendix discusses some of the main offense-related aspects when the corps is in the role of a land
     component command.

ASSEMBLE OPERATIONAL FORCES IN THE JOA

     C-27. The assembly of operational forces involves the concentration of designated large forces with the
     necessary sustainment support into those areas where the land components’ launch major operations.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  163


---

Appendix C

     Operational concentration enables the land component to assemble the necessary forces required to support
     the JTF’s campaign plan before the initiation of actual operations.

     C-28. Along with the physical grouping of forces, operations involve the assembly of any special technical
     means that enhances the probability of success. Such means include the assembly of new weapons and
     equipment, capabilities, and organizations (for example, thermobaric warheads, EW operations, and a Stryker
     BCT). The JFLCC employs available capabilities decisively at the time and place necessary to gain maximum
     advantage and surprise during the land operations portion of the JTF’s campaign. Occasionally, a JFLCC
     directs using a new weapon or organization alone to produce a psychological effect intended to destroy the
     enemy’s will or capability to resist.

     C-29. The JFLCC establishes a solid sustainment foundation in the desired concentration area early in the
     JTF’s campaign. Staff planners coordinate sustainment support using Army resources and available joint,
     multinational, host-nation, or contract facilities. Speed and secrecy in concentration, followed by bold, rapid
     execution of the plan may be a decisive factor in the defeat of the enemy. By concentrating forces, the JFLCC
     takes the first step in executing the JTF’s campaign plan.

     C-30. When selecting locations of assembly areas and their distances from the enemy, planners consider
     mission variables, including physical factors such as the transportation system capacity and enemy
     capabilities. Additionally, planners control the assembly of the operational force through early arrival of
     echelon tactical CPs, information systems, signal communications systems, and using parallel and
     collaborative planning. (See JP 3-31 for additional information on joint land operations.)

     C-31. The land component headquarters reviews its task organization daily as forces flow into and throughout
     the theater to ensure that contingency plans reflect current available resources. The commander protects
     ground LOCs, their supporting infrastructure, and critical installations, in addition to providing for the
     security of assembling forces. This protection is enhanced by—

             ⚫ Selecting operational assembly areas that protect (wholly or in part) land component LOCs from
                  ground attack with natural obstacles.

             ⚫ Locating operational assembly areas between potential sources of danger and LOCs.
             ⚫ Denying an enemy time to offer serious interference through rapid assembly, followed

                  immediately by decisive action.
             ⚫ Adhering strictly to camouflage discipline, emission control, and antenna and emitter offset.
             ⚫ Placing troop units and operational force protection assets where they can secure LOCs and critical

                  installations.
             ⚫ Developing an integrated AMD system that defends corps assets against enemy air and missile

                  reconnaissance and attacks throughout the AO.

     C-32. Conducting constant and intensive information collection operations throughout the assembly process
     is essential to force security efforts. Accurate knowledge of enemy capabilities, dispositions, and intentions
     forms the basis for effective friendly security operations.

ASSEMBLY OF LAND COMPONENT COMMAND FORCES IN THE
JOA

     C-33. Troops march into assembly areas or reach them by rail, water, motor, or air transport-or a combination
     of these methods. The methods employed are determined by—

             ⚫ Locations and types of forces assembled.
             ⚫ Capabilities of transportation facilities.
             ⚫ Time and space factors.
             ⚫ Proximity of enemy forces and the danger of interference with the concentration.

     C-34. The mission variables govern the sequence in which JFLCC forces arrive in their assembly areas.
     Normally, each echelon’s forward or tactical CP elements arrive early in this process. Other considerations
     affecting the sequence of arrival are security, phasing, unit integrity, and adequate sustainment assets.
     Convenience, efficient use of transportation assets, and the need to provide support to arriving combat units

164  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                         Corps in a Joint Role

     largely govern the order of arrival of elements—other than CP elements—when assembly areas are secure,
     and time is not pressing.

POSTURE FORCES FOR OPERATIONS

     C-35. Before conducting offensive, defensive, and stability tasks, the land component headquarters, in
     conjunction with the JTF, positions its forces to influence the enemy. A JFLCC—

             ⚫ Positions forces and sustainment assets to support the JTF’s next campaign or major operation
                  plan before assembly of the land component is complete.

             ⚫ Visualizes the JTF’s campaign or major operation from the beginning of the operational assembly
                  to the completion of the mission.

             ⚫ Selects its final objectives and determines a concept of operations for the obtainment of these
                  objectives.

             ⚫ Determines the different operational and tactical tasks that its forces need to perform, including
                  determination of the forms of maneuver or defense necessary to accomplish its assigned missions.

             ⚫ Arrays available forces within the operational assembly areas to support the preferred course of
                  action yet maintain flexibility to respond to enemy actions and changes in the operational and
                  mission variables. In rare cases, this occurrence requires the JFLCC to designate the combat
                  formations used by subordinates.

             ⚫ Postures forces for not only the initial operations but also subsequent operations based on the
                  visualization and understanding of the JTF’s intent and operational end state.

             ⚫ Conducts ISR operations to refine planning and achieve positional advantage.

     C-36. The land component’s initial force deployments support the JTF’s entire campaign because it is
     difficult to change the lines of operation of operational forces after their establishment. The JFLCC evaluates
     the following factors that can impact operations either favorably or unfavorably in the planning process:

             ⚫ Probable weather conditions.
             ⚫ Terrain and hydrography.
             ⚫ Presence or absence and the condition and carrying capacity of railroads, roads, waterways,

                  pipelines, airfields, and communications nodes used to establish land LOCs.
             ⚫ Enemy situation, including location of main enemy forces, where and how to meet enemy forces,

                  and when contact is expected.
             ⚫ Complex terrain, such as mountains, major urban areas, and fortified areas.
             ⚫ Capabilities of enemy forces to interfere with friendly operations.
             ⚫ Means for attaining and maintaining air superiority.
             ⚫ Time and space to effectively employ available forces.
             ⚫ Organization and employment of assigned, attached, or supporting forces, including air and naval.
             ⚫ Reconnaissance and security measures.
             ⚫ Sustainment support and the means, methods, and routes by which it flows to support land

                  component combat forces.

     C-37. The land component constantly updates previously conducted troop-to-task analysis since it will
     continuously receive new tasks, which also requires the JFLCC to reassess subordinate unit command and
     support relationships. The JFLCC and staff need to think and operate at the operational level of warfare. They
     need to know and understand the elements of operational design in addition to—

             ⚫ Time and distance factors associated with both inter- and intra-theater movement.
             ⚫ Joint and Army doctrine so that they use appropriate doctrinal terms without needlessly inventing

                  new ones when interacting with other components.
             ⚫ Advantages and disadvantages of collaborative versus parallel planning.
             ⚫ Operational risk assessment and mitigation.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  165


---

Appendix C

CONDUCT OPERATIONAL MANEUVER

     C-38. The land component moves and maneuvers large forces to obtain a positional advantage or in reaction
     to an enemy operational maneuver. These activities include directing the movement of subordinate forces
     during each phase of a major operation. This positioning enables land component forces to perform a rapid
     transition to subsequent phases of the JTF’s major operation or campaign. These tasks make their maximum
     contribution when the JTF synchronizes its conduct with air superiority and interdiction operations. (See
     Chapter 3 for more on operational movement and maneuver.)

COORDINATE TRANSITION OF JOINT FORCES TO AND FROM
TACTICAL BATTLE FORMATIONS

     C-39. The land component’s ability to posture forces to conduct operations depends on the command’s ability
     to effectively move the forces from their assembly areas into combat formations and employ these formations
     in the various types of offensive and defensive tasks and forms of maneuver. Combat formations allow a unit
     to move on the battlefield in a posture suited to its commander’s intent and mission. There are seven combat
     formations—column, line, echelon (left or right), box, diamond, wedge, and vee. The land component uses
     these combat formations to—

             ⚫ Overwhelm enemy forces with one or more unexpected blows before they have time to react in an
                  organized fashion.

             ⚫ Make contact with the smallest possible force in situations when and where the command’s
                  common operational picture does not clearly show the enemy situation.

             ⚫ Mass the effects of combat power against designated small portions of the known enemy forces
                  to defeat them.

     C-40. The basis of the land component’s ability to use combat formations is the force’s mastery of the forms
     of tactical and nontactical troop movement. Commanders link troop movement with rapid transition into
     situationally appropriate combat formations. The advantages and disadvantages of combat formations at the
     tactical level also apply at the operational level. A JFLCC initiates changes in the tactical battle formations
     of subordinate units only when such change is necessary to facilitate the execution of current operations, with
     due consideration to the impact on future operations. (ADP 3-90 discusses combat formations and FM 3-90
     discusses troop movement.)

     C-41. When at the operational level, the corps directs offensive-focused operations against an operational
     center of gravity, such as a visible mass of enemy forces or something more abstract, such as the cohesion of
     enemy forces. The commander may also direct operations at a critical vulnerability of the enemy, such as the
     boundary between two of its major combat formations, a vital C2 center, a logistic base, LOCs, or the mental
     and psychological balance of a key enemy commander.

     C-42. Types of joint force maneuvers include forcible entry, sustained action at and from the sea, sustained
     action on land, and sustained air operations. Joint doctrine addresses Navy and Marine Corps sustained action
     at and from the sea and USAF sustained air operations. (See JP 3-32 and JP 3-30 for more information on
     joint operations.)

     C-43. Forcible entry is the seizing and holding of a military lodgment in the face of armed opposition or
     forcing access into a denied area to allow movement and maneuver to accomplish the mission (JP 3-18). In
     some situations, forcible entry is the only method of gaining access to an operational area or for introducing
     decisive forces into a region. Forcible entry operations are normally joint operations, and they may include a
     combination of airborne, amphibious, and air assault operations. A corps typically takes part in forcible entry
     operations with airborne and air assault forces that seize a lodgment. A corps may also conduct amphibious
     operations to seize a lodgment when required. A corps also contributes protective and sustainment
     capabilities to support the forcible entry.

166  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                Appendix D

                      Corps Operations in History

         This appendix is organized to provide additional context for specific content contained
         within the manual. The vignettes contained herein are presented in the order in which
         their related content is presented in the manual.

PURPOSE OF THE VIGNETTES

     D-1. The purpose of these vignettes is to provide context for specific content in the manual and illustrate
     key concepts. They are purposely short, descriptive historical examples. Some will have maps where
     necessary to add to the discussion. They are included here to provide practical examples of corps operations
     and assist the reader’s understanding by providing deeper context. The individual sources for this material
     are provided at the back of the manual. Military professionals are encouraged to conduct deeper self-study
     as needed.

OPERATION IRAQI FREEDOM 2003 — OVERTAXING THE ARMY
CORPS

     D-2. C2 of land forces during the initial days of OPERATION IRAQI FREEDOM was just as tumultuous
     as it had been in previous conflicts. At the outset of crisis, Lieutenant General David McKiernan, Commander
     of Third U.S. Army, was dual hatted as an Army Service component commander and the LCC. For these
     tasks, McKiernan’s staff grew to some 1,300 personnel. Following the successful invasion and removal of
     Saddam Hussein’s regime, McKiernan briefly became commander of Combined Joint Task Force-Iraq and
     was responsible for overseeing the military occupation of Iraq.

     D-3. Unfortunately, in June 2003, Lieutenant General Ricardo Sanchez, V Corps commander with only
     about 328 staff members, transitioned his headquarters to Combined Joint Task Force-7 (CJTF-7). This task
     force replaced the Third U.S. Army as the operational headquarters because the U.S. Central Command
     commander directed McKiernan to focus on the entire theater of operations. Although not manned, trained,
     or equipped for the mission, Sanchez found himself in command of all U.S. and multinational forces in Iraq.
     Virtually overnight, Sanchez’s staff grew to roughly 1,300 personnel. Tasked to continue planning and
     conducting combined arms operations and wide area security at the tactical-level, Sanchez had the additional
     responsibility of managing the war at operational and strategic levels. Sanchez and his staff had immense
     responsibility managing three levels of warfare. Sanchez’s staff not only dealt directly with the host nation
     to train and certify the Iraqi Armed Forces, but it also reported to the Coalition Provisional Authority, U.S
     Central Command, and politicians in Washington.

     D-4. The political-military aspects consumed the staff, and six months passed before Lieutenant General
     Sanchez could convince Washington to establish an adequate C2 structure. Moreover, the rotation of U.S.
     combat forces and the employment of III Corps as the operational and tactical headquarters slowed the
     transition from CJTF-7 to the four-star Multinational Force-Iraq for another six months. By that time, the
     war had changed from a conventional war to a counterinsurgency, and the first year of combat was essentially
     lost. Although ground-centric, CJTF-7 desperately needed a subordinate land component to plan and
     synchronize land operations. Had Lieutenant General Sanchez had a subordinate land component to assume
     all the operational and tactical tasks associated with securing Iraq, it is probable that CJTF 7 could have better
     managed its resources and responded more quickly to its allies, partners, and politicians in Washington.

OFFENSIVE OPERATIONS — BATTLE FOR MANILA, 1945

     D-5. XIV Corps, commanded by Major General Griswold, initially consisted of the 37th and 40th Infantry
     Divisions when tasked to seize Clark Airfield and the ports of Manila. On 18 January, XIV Corps moved
     south facing little resistance. On 23 January, XIV Corps reached the outskirts of Clark Field, and on 24
     January, Griswold regrouped his divisions for a coordinated advance. 17 days after landing, XIV Corps had
     advanced 59 miles (95 kilometers [km]) to reach forward positions extending 124 miles (200 km); 77 miles

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  167


---

Appendix D

     (124 km) held by the 40th ID and 47 miles (76 km) controlled by the 37th Infantry Division. After a week of
     intense fighting, the 40th Infantry Division remained at the U.S. controlled airfield to control enemy advances
     from the north or west, and the rest of XIV Corps continued toward Manila.

     D-6. To support the advance, the 11th Airborne Division was attached to the corps on the 26th of January
     and inserted 70 miles (113 km) to the south of Manila at Nasugbu Beach. The 1st Cavalry Division was
     attached on 31 January joining the 37th Infantry Division to advance toward Manila from the north to seize
     the city. The 37th Infantry Division and 1st Cavalry Division moved at glaringly different paces. The
     Japanese had destroyed almost every bridge and roadway along 37th Infantry advance, whereas 1st Cavalry
     advanced rapidly at times reaching speeds of 40-50 miles per hour (63-80 km per hour). Synchronizing the
     pace of the divisions was critical to protect XIV Corps flanks. Constant C2, mobility and engineer support
     and intelligence updates from XIV Corps staff were essential. The 11th Airborne Division moved north
     quickly but faced Japanese resistance in Imus (five miles [8 km] south of Manila). The division secured the
     bridge over the Zapote River to Las Piñas and attempted to advance further. On 4 February, the 11th Airborne
     Division reached the Japanese main defenses on the south side of Manila near Nichols Field and were stalled.
     (See figure C-1 for a map of the operations.)

     D-7. Manila was a large city with reinforced concrete buildings, huts, and stone fortresses. Over 800,000
     people and roughly 18,000 enemy troops were entrenched in the city. On 4 February, 1st Cavalry to the north
     of Manila, and 11th Airborne to the south reported increased Japanese resistance. On 5 February, Griswold
     divided the northern section of the city into two parts: 37th Infantry was responsible for the western half, and
     1st Cavalry was responsible for the eastern half. By 8 February, 37th Infantry had cleared most of the
     resistance from its sector, but there was extensive damage to the residential districts. 1st Cavalry cleared the
     suburbs east of Manila, and on 10 February 1st Cavalry extended control south of the river. On 10 February,
     the 11th Airborne moved under XIV Corps control. The 11th Airborne had been halted at Nichols Field since
     4 February under a constant barrage of artillery. On 11 February, the airfield was secured and on 12 February,
     the 11th Airborne Division completed the U.S. encirclement of Manila.

     D-8. For the next few weeks, Griswold’s XIV Corps cleared out remaining enemy forces, and on 4 March
     Manila was liberated. Securing Manila was important to extend the logistics operations for supply lines that
     were at a breaking point. XIV Corps, along with I Corps and XI Corps were critical in subsequent operations
     in Luzon. (See figure D-1 and D-2 on pages 169 and 170 for a map of the operations.)

                                                                                                               Corps Operations in History

RETROGRADE (WITHDRAWAL) OF THE X CORPS IN KOREA, 1950

     D-9. Lieutenant General “Ned” Almond’s X Corps included the 1st Marine Division, 3d Infantry Division,
     7th Infantry Division, and the 1st Republic of Korea Corps. The X Corps focused on destroying North Korean
     and Chinese forces in support of 8th Army’s northern advance.

     D-10. General Ridgway ordered Almond to advance toward the Chosin Reservoir along 8th Army’s East
     Flank to facilitate 8th Army’s operations. The Chinese forces, including the 9th Chinese Army with eight
     divisions, intended to destroy the allied forces at Yudami-ni and Sinhung-ni (near the reservoir); the 9th
     Chinese Army planned this after assuming that X Corps would move into the garrisons, and the Peoples
     Volunteer Army could destroy X Corps in the reservoir. Greatly outnumbered, taking extensive casualties,
     and with horrible weather conditions, General Ridgway directed Lieutenant General Almond to plan for a
     withdrawal and retrograde from the port of Hungnam on 30 November. Almond directed Major General
     Soule (of the 3d Infantry Division) to withdraw from Wonson and link up with X Corps in Hungnam. Major
     General Smith (of the 1st Marine Division), and Major General Barr (of the 7th Infantry Division) were to
     conduct a withdrawal from the reservoir and subsequent movement to the port of Hungnam. Barr’s 7th
     Infantry Division and Smith’s 1st Marine Division faced continuous attacks in and surrounding the Chosin
     Valley terrain from several division-sized Peoples Volunteer Army elements. These commanders would have
     to conduct a phased withdraw from the reservoir to prevent X Corps, and adjacent Corps boundaries, to avoid
     being overrun. (See figure D-3 for a map of the operations.)

     D-11. X Corps deployed to Korea through three ports over a period of two months and then had to depart
     through one port in two weeks. To facilitate C2, X Corps headquarters in Hamhung facilitated communication
     between the ground units and the amphibious commander and staffs in the port of Hungnam. On 9 December,
     X Corps established an embarkation control group, including control group members from each division, to
     provide supervision of the corps withdrawal. X Corps divisions provided a readiness for loading report to the
     embarkation control group to synchronize operations with capacity, resourcing, and shipping availability.
     Once consolidated in Hungnam, the 1st Marine Division loaded out of Hungnam first, while the 3d Infantry
     Division and elements of the 7th Infantry Division provided perimeter and port security for the retrograde
     until the 1st Republic of Korea Corps cleared the port. The 7th Infantry Division commenced loading on 15
     December, and the 3d Infantry Division assumed primary security operations in the port. On 20 December,
     3d Infantry Division forces began loading their final personnel and supplies, and naval gunships assumed
     force protection to support final loading operations before destroying the port on 24 December.

     D-12. The X Corps retrograde was a successful joint-multinational operation supported by airpower, naval
     assets, and extensive C2 collaboration. In total, over 105,000 U.S. and Republic of Korea Soldiers, 17,500
     vehicles, 350,000 tons of supplies and equipment, and 91,000 civilians were evacuated.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  171

BUSAN (PUSAN) BREAKOUT IN KOREA, 1950

     D-13. Lieutenant General Walton H. (Bulldog) Walker led Eighth Army in the summer of 1950. Following
     the North Korean surprise attack on 25 June south of the 38th parallel, on 7 July, the United Nations Security
     Council recommended the establishment of a unified command in Korea, under a U.S. commander. President
     Truman appointed General Douglas MacArthur as commander in chief, United Nations Command and
     Walker as commander of all United Nations ground forces in the Republic of Korea.

     D-14. The United Nations forces were initially unprepared, saw many difficult battles, and had to fight a
     series of delaying actions, but by early August, Walker ordered a final stand along a 140-mile perimeter
     around the well-stocked port of Pusan. A combination of great courage and defensive tactics, a somewhat
     weakened North Korean force, and friendly reinforcements resulted in the United Nations forces holding the
     line. Their success allowed MacArthur’s staff to remain focused on preparing for OPERATION
     CHROMITE, launched on 15 September.

     D-15. Key to that plan was for Walker’s forces to launch a breakout soon after the landing to linkup with the
     forces that landed at Inchon. CHROMITE was a success. The North Korean forces were split and forced to
     retreat north. (See figure D-4 for a map of the operations.)

Appendix D

XII CORPS CROSSING THE MOSELLE RIVER IN FRANCE, 1944

     D-16. Over a week-long period in September 1944, Lieutenant General George S. Patton’s Third Army
     attempted to cross an assault force at several points across the Moselle River to establish a bridgehead and
     then encircle three German Army divisions in the city of Nancy, France. With a plan that closely followed
     U.S. river-crossing doctrine, Third Army’s XII Corps executed a wet-gap crossing south of the town of Nancy
     with the 35th Infantry Division as the bridgehead force and 4th Armored Division as the breakout force. As
     planned, the successful wet-gap crossing opened the southern route to Nancy and allowed the 80th Infantry
     Division to initiate a similar crossing north of the city. The establishment of multiple bridgeheads at Nancy
     demonstrates how mobility played a significant role in the Third Army’s successful march across France.
     (See figure D-5 for a map of the operations.)

                                      Figure D-5. Corps Mobility in World War II

RECONSTITUTION OF XIV CORPS ON GUADALCANAL, 1942-1943

     D-17. Guadalcanal was primarily a Navy and USMC operation. Major General Vandegrift (of the First
     Marine Division) commanded the landing forces, and Army troops were commanded by Major General
     Harmon (Commanding General, South Pacific). In October 1942, the 164th Infantry Regiment arrived at
     Guadalcanal, and it reinforced the First Marine Division until the remaining units under the command of
     Major General Patch arrived. Major General Vandergrift initiated the Matanikau offensive on 1 through 4
     November 1942 against forces of the Japanese 17th Army around the Matanikau River and Point Cruz. As a
     result of this operation, U.S. Marines and Soldiers inflicted heavy losses on the Japanese. The Japanese forces
     suffered over 400 casualties, compared to 70 casualties for U.S. forces. In December 1942 reinforcements
     were supposed to arrive, but sickness and disease were rampant in the First Marine Division, and it required
     immediate reconstitution. This left all American ground forces under the command of Major General Patch.

174  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                               Corps Operations in History

     D-18. Army forces continued to grow on Guadalcanal, and the Americal Division was formed. Commanded
     by Major General Patch, the Americal Division included the 132d, 164th, and 182d infantry regiments, in
     addition to artillery, engineer, and other support units. During three months of fighting, most of these units
     became combat ineffective from disease or injuries. Near the end of 1942 to early 1943, reinforcements
     arrived. Major General Harmon activated XIV Corps (including the 25th Infantry Division, Second Marine
     Division, and the Americal Division) under the command of Major General Patch. XIV Corps prepared for
     offensive operations while it was short of supplies and personnel. Every division was suffering from combat
     losses and malaria. To remain effective, Major General Patch reorganized the remaining regiments of XIV
     Corps into the Composite Army-Marine division consisting of two Army regiments, one USMC regiment,
     and artillery battalions from the Americal Division and Second Marine Division.

     D-19. Several factors contributed to the reconstitution and reorganization of XIV Corps. XIV Corps Soldiers
     were enthusiastic but hesitant and inexperienced in combat. Supply delivery required extensive transfers that
     consumed valuable time. As fighting progressed, the distance between supply dumps lengthened; ship-to-
     shore logistics could not keep up with the demands. Disease was rampant; for every casualty to combat, five
     fell to malaria. These problems notwithstanding, joint Service collaboration among Army, Navy, and USMC
     units was necessary to successfully consolidate and reorganize.

VIII CORPS AREA DEFENSE AT BASTOGNE, FRANCE 1944

     D-20. The World War II Battle of Bastogne provides an example of a corps area defense. VIII Corps was
     responsible for an 88-mile front that stretched along eastern Belgium and Luxembourg when the German
     army initiated its offensive in December 1944. Due to the German breakthrough, VIII corps, headquartered
     in Bastogne, needed reinforcements to defend this vital node in the highway system. 12th Army Group,
     through the First Army, reassigned the 101st Airborne Division and the 10th Armored Division from adjacent
     corps, as well as the 705th Tank Destroyer Battalion to VIII Corps to augment its defenses. The use of these
     reinforcements to hold critical supply routes limited further German advances in Belgium and enabled the
     Allied counterattack in January 1945. (See figure D-6 on page 176 for a map of the defense of Bastogne.)
