XVIII CORPS AND THE AFTERMATH OF OPERATION JUST CAUSE

     D-21. Ongoing disagreement between the United States and General Manuel Noriega culminated in the fatal
     shooting of U.S. Marine Corps First Lieutenant Robert Paz on 18 December 1989, and the violent harassment
     of Navy personnel by members of the Panamanian Defense Force. As a result of escalating tensions,
     OPERATION JUST CAUSE had been approved as a contingency operation by President Bush a month
     earlier. It was unique because it was a unilateral military operation conducted entirely by U.S. forces. This
     simplified the planning and execution of operations, but it created issues later in the transition to stability
     operations. Additionally, interagency partners such as the U.S. Department of State or U.S. Agency for
     International Development, were not involved in the contingency planning and this complicated military
     efforts later. The final operation plan included U.S. Army and Marine Corps forces commanded by the XVIII
     Airborne Corps, designated JTF-South, and it was supported by a joint special operations task force.

     D-22. OPERATION JUST CAUSE began with a combined airborne and ground assault on 20 December
     1989. Major combat operations were over by 3 January 1990, although some units began transitioning to
     stability operations earlier. The transition from combat operations to stability was beset with problems.
     Contingency planning provided a concept for stability operations, but no detailed planning, and it did not
     account for the needed Army Reserve forces, primarily civil affairs elements, early enough for them to be
     available. The larger stability plan, OPERATION PROMOTE LIBERTY, was not approved until 21
     December 1989, the day after the invasion began. Because civilian agencies had not been involved in the
     planning, they were not prepared to assume responsibility for post-combat stability operations. All of this
     resulted in the initial combat forces having to conduct stability operations which they had not planned for,
     including traffic control; garbage disposal; and providing food, water, and medical care to the local
     population. Efforts to establish the Military Support Group in late January 1990 helped to focus subsequent
     efforts, although rapidly changing command relationships continued to create challenges. JTF-South shut
     down in early February 1990, and responsibility for the military support group, along with the fractured

176         ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                               Corps Operations in History

     stability plan, transitioned to U.S. Army South. By the time Reserve forces and civilian agencies were able
     to operate in force throughout Panama, crime was rampant and there was an almost complete breakdown of
     civil authority. Stability operations focused on combating this crime while slowly rebuilding Panamanian
     governmental institutions and overseeing free elections in 1994.

     D-23. Stability operations can include several types of transitions that require detailed planning. They may
     include transitions of authority and control between different military forces, civilian agencies and
     organizations, and host nations. The role of a corps may also change from leading the conduct of stability
     operations to supporting a new organization executing stability operations. These transitions can create
     vulnerabilities to the force, to the mission, and to the local population as enemy forces seek to take advantage
     of any disruptions. The corps commander and staff identify these transitions through deliberate, detailed
     planning tied to decision points to mitigate risks while continuing the transition to civilian oversight and host-
     nation control of operations.

     D-24. Maintaining unity of effort during transitions is critical. The corps commander and staff spend a
     significant amount of time communicating with the various U.S., host-nation, and foreign agencies and
     organizations involved in the operation to ensure that friction between organizations or diverging objectives
     does not threaten the overall unity of effort. This communication is critical to ensuring continuity and
     understanding as new forces relieve U.S. forces, units are task-organized into new configurations, and
     different intergovernmental or host-nation organizations assume the lead for operations. Finally, the corps
     must continuously communicate with the theater army to ensure it is prepared to receive responsibility for
     enduring military tasks as the corps redeploys.

OPERATION POWER PACK, 1965-1966

     D-25. The Dominican Republic experienced three years of turmoil starting in 1962 after elections and
     culminating on 24 April 1965, when communist guerrilla forces deposed the Dominican leader and
     overwhelmed Dominican security forces. President Lyndon Johnson ordered the U.S. military deployments
     to intervene. The purpose of the deployment was to stabilize the country and prevent its fall to the
     communists.

     D-26. Lieutenant General Palmer, commander of the XVIII Airborne Corps, commanded a JTF composed of
     U.S. Marines and elements of the 82d Airborne Division tasked with restoring and supporting the duly elected
     government of the Dominican Republic. Additional SOF from 7th Special Forces Group and psychological
     operations forces also participated.

     D-27. Initial forces deployed on 28 April 1965 to secure critical infrastructure, link up with local government
     forces, and regain control of the capital, Santo Domingo. These initial forces included Marines from the USS
     Boxer and elements of the 82d Airborne Division. By the end of May, the entire 82d Airborne Division along
     with a battalion of Marines was deployed in country. Palmer directed the start of stability operations in
     support of host-nation forces. They focused on security and alleviating human suffering, the two minimum-
     essential stability tasks mandated by doctrine and law today. U.S. military forces conducted constabulary
     operations in Santo Domingo and continued distributing food, water, and medical supplies to both factions.

     D-28. U.S. military forces continued these operations while the Organization of American States helped to
     establish a provisional government in preparation for elections to reestablish a legitimate government. By
     September 1965, most security operations had been transferred to a Latin American military task force led
     by Brazil, and U.S. military forces began to redeploy to the United States. By September 1966, all American
     forces had departed the Dominican Republic.

     D-29. During crises, the Army may deploy a corps headquarters and select units as part of a U.S. response.
     The corps is the preferred Army echelon for use as a JTF headquarters because of the seniority of its staff
     and its deployability. A corps acting as a JTF headquarters requires a separate formation to assume the role
     of ARFOR because of the differing roles and responsibilities inherent in each. The emphasis on the other
     instruments of national power during these operations requires increased integration of nonmilitary and
     multinational partners in operations. Ideally, a corps partners with local host-nation forces whenever possible
     in the conduct of operations.

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  177


---

Appendix D

     D-30. A corps deployed as part of a U.S. crisis response operates as part of a joint command and in most
     instances a multinational one. This requires an awareness of political and military considerations for
     operations. The corps rapidly integrates liaisons and staffs from multiple sources while simultaneously
     beginning operations. This places a tremendous burden on staff processes that must be accounted for during
     initial planning. The nature of crises means the involvement of multiple nations in the development of long
     term solutions even if they may not have been involved in initial operations to stabilize the operational area.
     Clearly identifying the requirements for future solutions, where they may be coming from, and integrating
     them into the transition efforts is critical for success. (See OPERATION POWER PACK for an example of
     corps operations during crisis.)

     D-31. In many respects, stability operations conducted during crises are the most difficult to plan and execute.
     Because speed is important, a corps will often deploy during the initial stages of planning with broad
     directives and missions from its higher headquarters. Those broad directives can be difficult to translate into
     action with limited resources. The stability needs of a developing crisis can be difficult to determine in
     advance, and they may change during operations. Additionally, other nations may also respond to the crisis
     with military forces. These forces may or may not be working towards the same objectives, presenting an
     additional challenge.

     D-32. International crises also attract the attention of nongovernmental organizations seeking to help; all of
     which are competing for workspace and access to supplies and resources. Finally, international media
     attention creates the demand for immediate action and quick resolution which does not always account for
     long-term impacts in the region. All this places unique demands on the corps commander and staff as they
     deploy corps formations, develop requirements, and execute their assigned mission.

OPERATION DESERT DRAGON – ESTABLISHING THE DEFENSE
AND BUILDING COMBAT POWER

     D-33. On 8 August 1990, the XVIII Airborne Corps assault CP and the lead elements of the 82d Airborne
     Division deployed to Saudi Arabia initiating OPERATION DESERT SHIELD. The corps deployed three
     divisions within 60 days (the 82d Airborne, 101st Air Assault, and the 24th Infantry Division [Mechanized]).
     The remainder of the corps (3d Armored Cavalry Regiment, 1st Cavalry Division, 75th Field Artillery
     Brigade, and the 212th Field Artillery Brigade) arrived by 1 November. The corps defense increased in
     capability with the arrival of each unit in theater. As additional units arrived, corps plans (called DESERT
     DRAGON I, II, and III) were revised.

     D-34. DESERT DRAGON I was the initial corps lodgment defense to protect the air and seaports of
     debarkation at Dhahran and Ad-Dammam with a reinforced airborne brigade. DESERT DRAGON II
     expanded the lodgment area to include the port of Al-Jubayl to protect the arrival of the 7th Marine
     Expeditionary Brigade. During DESERT DRAGON I and II, the lodgment defense was heavily outnumbered
     by the Iraqi’s armored forces. The airborne brigades optimized four factors to enhance their defensive combat
     power:

             ⚫ They situated their defenses at points along the coast roads closely bordered by sabkhas (coastal
                  salt flats), which minimized off-road maneuver by enemy armored forces. (See figure D-7 for a
                  depiction of the defense.)

             ⚫ They optimized their direct-fire range advantage over the Iraqis with tube-launched, optically
                  tracked, wire-guided missiles and Sheridan tanks.

             ⚫ The defense was supported by attack helicopters and joint and multinational air component aircraft
                  flying CAS and air interdiction sorties.

             ⚫ The Soldiers were disciplined and sufficiently confident in their units to withstand the shock of an
                  armored attack.

178  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                           Corps Operations in History

                     Figure D-7. OPERATION DESERT DRAGON Phase I and II

D-35. Shortly after the arrival of the lead elements of the 24th Infantry Division (Mechanized), the corps
transitioned to Desert Dragon III. This also signaled an end to any reasonable chance of success for an Iraqi
attack into Saudi Arabia. The plan took its final form with the arrival of the 1st Cavalry Division. The corps
conducted a mobile defense in sector with a division-size striking force. A provisional Arab mechanized
division conducted the initial defense of Saudi Arabia forward of the corps covering force. The Marines
conducted an area defense of the Al-Jubayl coastal region. (See figure D-8 on page 180 for a depiction of the
AO.)

D-36. The 101st Airborne (Air Assault) Division, augmented by the 3d Armored Cavalry Regiment and the
12th Aviation Brigade, conducted covering force operations in AOs Carentan and Normandy. The 24th
Infantry conducted an area defense within the MBA. The 82d Airborne Division conducted an area defense
of critical installations and facilities.

D-37. The 1st Cavalry Division was designated as the corps striking force (called the reserve at that time)
with the principal mission to counterattack into flanks and rear of the trailing echelon brigades and divisions,
presumably the Republican Guard forces. Corps deep operations focused on attacking select high-value
targets and follow-on Iraqi echelons to attrit and disrupt them.

D-38. On 17 January 1991, corps units transitioned to offensive operations. Attack helicopters from the 10lst
Airborne (Air Assault) Division conducted a deep attack in conjunction with joint forces and destroyed a
critical ground control intercept radar in Western Iraq to open the air campaign of OPERATION DESERT
STORM.

D-39. The corps remained in eastern Saudi Arabia until shortly before the initiation of the ground offensive
to deceive the Iraqis as to the true location of the coalition’s attack.

                 TSC   theater sustainment command

  SECTION II — TERMS

adversary
          A party acknowledged as potentially hostile to a friendly party and against which the use of force may
          be envisaged. (JP 3-0)

air tasking order
          A method used to task and disseminate to components, subordinate units, and command and control
          agencies projected sorties, capabilities, or forces for targeting and specific missions. (JP 3-30)

airspace control order
          An order implementing the airspace control plan that provides the details of the approved requests for
          airspace coordinating measures. (JP 3-52)

area defense
          A type of defensive operation that concentrates on denying enemy forces access to designated terrain
          for a specific time rather than destroying the enemy outright. (ADP 3-90)

area of influence
          An area inclusive of and extending beyond an operational area wherein a commander is capable of
          direct influence by maneuver, fire support, and information normally under the commander’s
          command or control. (JP 3-0)

area of interest
          That area of concern to the commander, including the area of influence, areas adjacent to it, and
          extending into enemy territory. (JP 3-0)

area security
          A type of security operation conducted to protect friendly forces, lines of communications, installation
          routes and actions within a specific area. (FM 3-90)

ARFOR
          The Army component and senior Army headquarters of all Army forces assigned or attached to a
          combatant command, subordinate joint force command, joint functional command, or multinational
          command. (FM 3-94)

Army corps
          An echelon of command and tactical formation that employs divisions, multifunctional brigades, and
          functional brigades to achieve objectives on land. (JP 3-31)

assured mobility
          A framework—of processes, actions, and capabilities—that assures the ability of a force to deploy,
          move, and maneuver where and when desired. (ATP 3-90.4)

close area
          close area is the portion of the commander’s area of operations where the majority of subordinate
          maneuver forces conduct close combat (ADP 3-0).

15 January 2026        ATP 3-92                     185


---

Glossary

close operations
          Tactical actions of subordinate maneuver forces and the forces providing immediate support to them,
          whose purpose is to employ maneuver and fires to close with and destroy enemy forces. (FM 3-0)

combat power
          The total means of destructive and disruptive force that a military unit/formation can apply against an
          enemy at a given time. (JP 3-0)

command post cell
          A grouping of personnel and equipment organized by warfighting function or by planning horizon to
          facilitate the exercise of command and control. (FM 6-0)

consolidate gains
          Activities to make enduring any temporary operational success and to set the conditions for a
          sustainable security environment, allowing for a transition of control to other legitimate authorities.
          (ADP 3-0)

controlled supply rate
          The rate of ammunition consumption that can be supported, considering availability, facilities, and
          transportation. It is expressed in rounds per unit, individual, or vehicle per day. (ATP 3-09.23)

counterair
          A mission at the theater level that integrates offensive and defensive operations to attain and maintain a
          desired degree of control of the air and protection by neutralizing or destroying enemy aircraft and
          missiles, both before and after launch. (JP 3-01)

counterattack
          A variation of attack by a defending force against an attacking enemy force. (FM 3-90)

countermobility
          A set of combined arms activities that use or enhance the effects of natural and man-made obstacles to
          prevent the enemy freedom of movement and maneuver. (ATP 3-90.8)

crisis
          An emerging incident or situation involving a possible threat to the United States, its citizens, military
          forces, or vital interests that develops rapidly and creates a condition of such diplomatic, economic, or
          military importance that commitment of military forces and resources is contemplated to achieve
          national and/or strategic objectives. (JP 3-0)

cyberspace electromagnetic activities (CEMA)
          The process of planning, integrating, and synchronizing cyberspace and electronic warfare operations
          in support of unified land operations. (ADP 3-0)

deep area
          Where the commander sets conditions for future success in close combat. (ADP 3-0)

deep operations
          tactical actions against enemy forces, typically out of direct contact with friendly forces, intended to
          shape future close operations and protect rear operations. (FM 3-0)

defeat
          To render a force incapable of achieving its objectives. (ADP 3-0)

defeat mechanism
          A method through which friendly forces accomplish their mission against enemy opposition. (ADP
          3-0)

delay
          When a force under pressure trades space for time by slowing down the enemy’s momentum and
          inflicting maximum damage on enemy forces without becoming decisively engaged. (ADP 3-90)

186  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                                          Glossary

disengagement criteria
          Protocols that specify those circumstances where a friendly force must break contact with direct fire
          and observed indirect fire to prevent becoming decisively engaged or to preserve friendly combat
          power. (FM 3-90)

early-entry command post
          A lead element of a headquarters designed to control operations until the remaining portions of the
          headquarters are deployed and operational. (FM 6-0)

enabling operation
          An operation that sets the friendly conditions required for mission accomplishment. (FM 3-90)

encirclement
          Where one force loses its freedom of maneuver because an opposing force is able to isolate it by
          controlling all ground lines of communications and reinforcement. (FM 3-90)

encircling force
          The force that maneuvers to the rear or flank of the enemy to prevent the enemy’s escape. (FM 3-90)
          Exfiltrate is A tactical mission task in which a unit removes Soldiers or units from areas under enemy
          control by stealth, deception, surprise, or clandestine means. (FM 3-90)

exploitation
          A type of offensive operation following a successful attack to disorganize the enemy in depth. (FM
          3-90)

fire support
          A rapid and continuous integration of surface-to-surface indirect fires, target acquisition, armed
          aircraft, and other lethal and nonlethal attack/delivery systems that converge against targets across all
          domains in support of the maneuver commander’s concept of operations. (FM 3-09)

force field artillery headquarters
          A battalion size or higher unit designated by the supported commander who specifies its duration,
          duties, and responsibilities. (FM 3-09)

force projection
          The ability to project the military instrument of national power from the United States or another
          theater in response to requirements for military operations. (JP 3-0)

forms of maneuver
          Distinct tactical combinations of fire and movement with a unique set of doctrinal characteristics that
          differ primarily in the relationship between the maneuvering force and the enemy. (ADP 3-90)

gap crossing
          The projection of combat power across a linear obstacle (wet or dry gap). (ATP 3-90.4)

information advantage
          A condition when a force holds the initiative in terms of situational understanding, decision making,
          and relevant actor behavior. (ADP 3-13)

information collection
          An activity that synchronizes and integrates the planning and employment of sensors and assets and
          the processing, exploitation, and dissemination systems in direct support of current and future
          operations. (FM 3-55)

interoperability
          The ability to act together coherently, effectively, and efficiently to achieve tactical, operational, and
          strategic objectives. (JP 3-0)

joint fires
          Fires delivered during the employment of forces from two or more components in coordinated action
          to create desired effects in support of a common objective. (JP 3-0)

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  187


---

Glossary

knowledge management
          The process of enabling knowledge flow to enhance shared understanding, learning, and decision
          making. (ADP 6-0)

large-scale combat operations
          Extensive joint combat operations in terms of scope and size of forces committed, conducted as a
          campaign aimed at achieving operational and strategic objectives. (ADP 3-0)

lethality
          The capability and capacity to destroy. (FM 3-0)

linkup
          A meeting of friendly ground forces, which occurs in a variety of circumstances. (ADP 3-90)

main command post
          A portion of a unit headquarters containing the majority of the staff designed to command and control
          current operations, conduct detailed analysis, and plan future operations. (FM 6-0)

military deception
          Activities intended to mislead and cause adversary actions or inactions that are beneficial to the
          friendly force. (JP 3-54)

military information support operations (MISO)
          Planned operations to convey selected information and indicators to foreign audiences to influence
          their emotions, motives, objective reasoning, and ultimately the behavior of foreign governments,
          organizations, groups, and individuals in a manner favorable to the originator’s objectives. (JP 3-53)

mobile defense
          A type of defensive operation that concentrates on the destruction or defeat of the enemy through a
          decisive attach by a striking force. (ADP 3-90)

mobility
          A quality or capability of military forces which permits them to move from place to place while
          retaining the ability to fulfill their primary mission. (JP 3-36)

movement control
          The dual process of committing allocated transportation assets and regulating movements according to
          command priorities to synchronize distribution flow over lines of communications to sustain land
          forces. (ADP 4-0)

movement to contact
          A type of offensive operation designed to establish or regain contact to develop the situation. (FM
          3-90)

multidomain operations
          The combined arms employment of joint and Army capabilities to create and exploit relative
          advantages that achieve objectives, defeat enemy forces, and consolidate gains on behalf of joint force
          commanders. (FM 3-0)

mutual support
          That support which units render each other throughout joint operations, because of their assigned tasks,
          their position relative to each other and to the enemy, or their inherent capabilities. (JP 3-31)

node
          1. A location in a mobility system where a movement requirement is originated, processed for onward
          movement, or terminated. (JP 3-36)

operation
          1. A sequence of tactical actions with a common purpose or unifying theme. (JP 1, Volume 1)

188  ATP 3-92  15 January 2026


---

                                                                                                                                          Glossary

operational framework
          A cognitive tool used to assist commanders and staffs in clearly visualizing and describing the
          application of combat power in time, space, purpose, and resources in the concept of operations. (ADP
          1-01)

passage of lines
          An operation in which a force moves forward or rearward through another force’s combat positions
          with the intention of moving into or out of contact with the enemy. (JP 3-18)

preparation fire
          A brief, intense bombardment on selected targets or a prolonged effort over time covering a large
          number of targets. (FM 3-09)

raid
          A variation of attack to temporarily seize an objective with a planned withdrawal. (FM 3-90)

rear operations
          Tactical actions behind major subordinate maneuver forces that facilitate movement, extend
          operational reach, and maintain desired tempo. (FM 3-0)

reconnaissance
          A mission undertaken to obtain information about the activities and resources of an enemy or
          adversary, or to secure data concerning the meteorological, hydrographic, or geographic characteristics
          of a particular area, by visual observation or other detection methods. (JP 2-0)

reconstitution
          An operation that commanders plan and implement to restore units to a desired level of combat
          effectiveness commensurate with mission requirements and available resources. (ATP 3-94.4)

*relief in place
          An operation in which, by direction of higher authority, all or part of a unit is replaced in an area by
          the incoming unit and the responsibilities of the replaced elements for the mission and the assigned
          zone of operations are transferred to the incoming unit.

required supply rate
          The amount of ammunition expressed in terms of rounds per weapon per day for ammunition items
          fired by weapons, in terms of other units of measure per day for bulk allotment, and other items
          estimated to be required to sustain operations of any designated force without restriction for a specified
          period. (ATP 3-09.23)

retirement
          When a force out of contact moves away from the enemy. (ADP 3-90)

security area
          That area occupied by a unit's security elements and includes the areas of influence of those security
          elements. (ADP 3-90)

security operations
          Those operations performed by commanders to provide early and accurate warning of enemy
          operations, to provide the forces being protected with time and maneuver space within which to react
          to the enemy, and to develop the situation to allow commanders to effectively use their protected
          forces. (ADP 3-90)

spoiling attack
          A variation of an attack employed against an enemy preparing for an attack. (FM 3-90)

stability mechanism
          The primary method through which friendly forces affect civilians in order to attain conditions that
          support establishing a lasting, stable peace. (ADP 3-0)

15 January 2026  ATP 3-92  189


---

Glossary

staff section
          A grouping of staff members by area of expertise. (FM 6-0)

support area
          The portion of the commander’s area of operations designated to facilitate the positioning,
          employment, and protection of base sustainment assets required to sustain, enable, and control
          operations. (ADP 3-0)

support area operations
          The tactical actions securing lines of communications, bases, and base clusters that enable an echelon’s
          sustainment and command and control. (FM 3-0)

supporting distance
          The distance between two units that can be traveled in time for one to come to the aid of the other and
          prevent its defeat by an enemy or ensure that it regains control of a civil situation. (ADP 3-0)

supporting range
          The distance one unit may be geographically separated from a second unit yet remain within the
          maximum range of the second unit’s weapons systems. (ADP 3-0)

sustainment
          The provision of logistics, financial management, personnel services, and health service support
          necessary to maintain operations until successful mission completion. (ADP 4-0)

synchronization
          1. The arrangement of military activities in time, space, and purpose to produce maximum relative
          combat power at a decisive place and time. (JP 2-0)

tactical deception
          A friendly activity that causes enemy commanders to take action or cause inaction detrimental to their
          objectives. (FM 3-90)

tactics
          The employment, ordered arrangement and directed actions of forces in relation to each other. (ADP
          3-90)

targeting
          The process of selecting and prioritizing targets and matching the appropriate response to them,
          considering operational requirements and capabilities. (JP 3-0)

troop movement
          The movement of Soldiers and units from one place to another by any available means. (FM 3-90)

warfighting function
          A group of tasks and systems united by a common purpose that commanders use to accomplish
          missions and training objectives. (ADP 3-0)

withdraw
          To disengage from an enemy force and move in a direction away from the enemy. (ADP 3-90)
